### [1] [Architectural and Regularization Components in Deep Learning Medical Image Registration: Systematic Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05484)
*Nabira Rashid*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A controlled ablation study separating architectural enhancements (affine pre-alignment) from regularization losses in deep learning brain MRI registration. Regularization alone accounts for most gains (21.3% MSE improvement, 99% reduction in unrealistic deformations), while the combined model provides the largest accuracy gain (25.8%) at moderate computational cost (+9.8% inference time).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning registration papers routinely combine architectural additions (e.g., affine pre-alignment stages) with training-objective additions (e.g., regularization losses) simultaneously, making it unclear which component actually drives performance improvements. This confounds understanding of what makes these methods work.

Method: Controlled ablation on OASIS brain MRI dataset (394 training, 20 test subjects). Four variants of the same 3D U-Net registration pipeline were trained: (1) baseline with basic similarity losses, (2) baseline + full regularization suite, (3) affine-plus-deformable architecture with basic losses, and (4) affine architecture with full regularization suite. Evaluated registration accuracy (MSE, NCC, SSIM), deformation quality (Jacobian determinant, displacement statistics, anatomical plausibility score), and computational cost.

Result: Regularization alone delivered 21.3% relative MSE improvement (1.78% to 2.16%, P<.001), 21.8% NCC improvement, and reduced maximum deformation by 99.0% (53.1 to 0.51 units) at no computational cost (-0.06% inference time). The combined model achieved the largest accuracy gain (25.8%, 1.78% to 2.24%) and raised anatomical plausibility from 0.596 to 0.930, with +9.8% inference-time cost. Gradient correlation improved from 0.742 to 0.980. All enhanced variants achieved sub-voxel accuracy under plausible deformation constraints.

Conclusion: Regularization losses are the primary driver of performance in this setting, delivering accuracy gains and deformation control essentially for free at inference time. The affine architecture adds a smaller complementary benefit at acceptable cost. The 99% reduction in unrealistic deformations addresses a key barrier to clinical deployment.

Abstract: Deep learning registration methods routinely stack two kinds of enhancement on a base network: architectural additions such as affine pre-alignment stages, and training-objective additions such as regularization losses. Papers tend to adopt both at once, so it is unclear which is doing the work. I ran a controlled ablation to separate them. Using the OASIS brain MRI dataset (394 training subjects, 20 test subjects), I trained four variants of the same registration pipeline: a baseline 3D U-Net with basic similarity losses, the same U-Net with a full regularization suite, an affine-plus-deformable architecture with basic losses, and the affine architecture with the full suite. I evaluated registration accuracy (MSE, NCC, SSIM), deformation quality (Jacobian determinant preservation, displacement statistics, an anatomical plausibility score), and computational cost. Regularization alone accounted for most of the gain: a 21.3% relative gain on the MSE-improvement metric (1.78% to 2.16%, P<.001) and a 21.8% relative gain in NCC improvement, while cutting maximum deformation from 53.1 to 0.51 units, a 99.0% reduction, at essentially no computational cost (-0.06% inference time). The combined model produced the largest accuracy gain, 25.8% (1.78% to 2.24%), and raised anatomical plausibility from 0.596 to 0.930, at a moderate +9.8% inference-time cost. Gradient correlation rose from 0.742 at baseline to 0.980 for the fully enhanced model. All enhanced variants reached sub-voxel accuracy under plausible deformation constraints. Regularization losses are the primary driver in this setting, delivering the accuracy gains and almost all of the deformation control for free at inference time, while the affine architecture adds a smaller complementary benefit at acceptable cost. The 99% reduction in unrealistic deformations addresses a known barrier to clinical deployment.

</details>


### [2] [A Survey on Adversarial Attacks and Defenses for Diffusion Models Across Multiple Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05503)
*Ozgur Kara,Tarik Can Ozden,Furkan Horoz,Zeqian Long,Haotian Xue,Yipu Chen,Oguzhan Akcin,Yongxin Chen,James Matthew Rehg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This survey provides the first unified review of adversarial attacks and defenses on diffusion models across three visual modalities: image, video, and 3D. It introduces a task-centric taxonomy organizing the literature by modality, then separating attacks from defenses, and grouping by generative task chronologically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The widespread public availability of diffusion models enables misuse at scale, motivating research on adversarial attacks and defenses. Despite rapid growth in this field, no unified review existed to consolidate this fragmented literature across different visual modalities.

Method: The survey organizes literature using a comprehensive, task-centric taxonomy: divided by modality (image, video, 3D), then separated into attacks and defenses, grouped by targeted generative task, and presented chronologically within each task. It also provides in-depth analysis of evaluation settings including datasets, metrics, and benchmarks.

Result: The survey consolidates the rapidly growing body of research on adversarial attacks and defenses for diffusion models across visual modalities, identifying evaluation practices and existing literature patterns.

Conclusion: The paper concludes by identifying several open challenges and outlining concrete future research directions in the field of adversarial attacks and defenses on diffusion models.

Abstract: Diffusion models have become the dominant family of generative models in the visual domain. However, their widespread public availability enables misuse at scale, motivating a rapidly growing body of research on adversarial attacks and defenses. This survey provides, to our knowledge, the first unified review of this literature across three visual modalities: image, video, and 3D. We introduce a comprehensive, task-centric taxonomy: we first divide the literature by modality; within each modality, we separate methods into attacks and defenses, and then group them by the generative task they target, presenting them chronologically within each task. Moreover, we provide an in-depth analysis of their evaluation settings, consolidating the datasets, metrics, and benchmarks used to assess them. We conclude by identifying several open challenges and outlining concrete future research directions. Project Webpage: https://github.com/ozgurkara99/awesome-adv-attack-defense-on-diffusion

</details>


### [3] [An Exploratory Study of Frequency-Aware Task Weighting for YOLOv8-Based Unified Driving Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05516)
*Zhiyuan Nie,Zixi Zhou,Xianbin Gu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Frequency-aware Task Weighting (FTW), a dynamic task-balancing method for multi-task learning in unified autonomous driving perception. FTW uses the low-frequency energy ratio of recent loss histories as a stability proxy to assign appropriate weights to different tasks (object detection, drivable-area segmentation, lane segmentation) during training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-task learning in autonomous driving perception faces challenges because different tasks (object detection, drivable-area segmentation, lane segmentation) exhibit varying convergence rates, loss scales, and optimization stability. Existing task-weighting methods rely on loss magnitude, learned uncertainty, short-term loss changes, or gradient statistics, leading the authors to explore frequency structure of loss-history as a complementary signal.

Method: FTW estimates loss-trajectory stability from the low-frequency energy ratio of recent loss histories within a sliding window. Tasks whose mean-centered loss trajectories contain larger proportion of low-frequency power receive larger weights. Experiments use a unified YOLOv8-based perception framework with three task-specific heads, comparing FTW against fixed and uncertainty-based weighting under two configurations: full-network static training and progressive freezing.

Result: Experiments on Mapillary Vistas dataset show mixed results across six single-run configurations: static FTW achieves the largest overall score and lane mIoU, progressive FTW has the largest detection mAP, while static uncertainty weighting achieves the largest drivable-area mIoU. No single method dominates all metrics.

Conclusion: The evidence supports the feasibility of loss-frequency-based weighting in unified perception pipelines, but does not establish clear improvement over baselines or generalization beyond the reported runs. Limitations include single-run configurations without repeated-seed estimates, absence of single-task baselines, and no FTW ablations.

Abstract: Unified perception enables autonomous driving systems to perform object detection, drivable-area segmentation, and lane segmentation within a single network, improving efficiency and reducing deployment complexity. Jointly optimizing multiple perception tasks remains challenging because tasks exhibit different convergence rates, loss scales, and optimization stability. Existing task-weighting methods use loss magnitude, learned uncertainty, short-term loss changes, or gradient statistics; here, we explore the frequency structure of a recent loss-history window as a complementary signal.
  We implement and examine Frequency-aware Task Weighting (FTW), a dynamic task-balancing rule that estimates a loss-trajectory stability proxy from the low-frequency energy ratio of recent loss histories. FTW assigns larger weights to tasks whose mean-centered loss trajectories contain a larger proportion of low-frequency power. We document FTW and two baselines under full-network static training and progressive freezing using a unified YOLOv8-based perception framework with three task-specific heads.
  Experiments on Mapillary Vistas compare FTW with fixed and uncertainty-based weighting under both configurations. Final holdout metrics are reported for the checkpoint with the lowest per-epoch validation loss in each run. Across six single-run configurations, static FTW has the largest derived overall score and lane mIoU, progressive FTW has the largest detection mAP, and static uncertainty weighting has the largest drivable-area mIoU. Without repeated-seed estimates, single-task baselines, or FTW ablations, these rankings are descriptive. The evidence supports the feasibility of loss-frequency-based weighting in this pipeline, but does not establish improvement over the baselines or generalization beyond the reported runs.

</details>


### [4] [RelightFormer: Feed-forward Generative Transformer for Multiview Object Relighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07414)
*Hejun Wang,Jinxi Li,Junwei Jiang,Shiwei Mao,Hu Cheng,Shouwang Huang,Bo Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a feed-forward generative Transformer for single- and multi-view image relighting that bypasses explicit intrinsic property estimation. The method uses a latent illumination module with cross-attention to inject target environment maps into spatial features, and permutation-invariant positional encodings to handle unordered multi-view inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional image relighting methods suffer from two key limitations: (1) inverse rendering pipelines face ill-posed optimization problems, and (2) single-image generative models ignore crucial multi-view cues necessary for understanding 3D geometry and material interactions.

Method: The proposed approach uses a feed-forward generative Transformer adapted from a video foundation model. It features a latent illumination module that dynamically injects target environment maps into spatial features via cross-attention. The method employs permutation-invariant positional encodings to symmetrically process unordered multi-view inputs without sequential bias. Training is performed on the massive Laval Objaverse Dataset (LOD) comprising 90K objects and 39K unique illuminations.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art visual quality, photorealistic relighting quality, and strong zero-shot generalization across single-view, multi-view, and novel-view relighting tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed Transformer-based approach successfully addresses the limitations of both inverse rendering and single-image generative models, achieving superior relighting results through a data-driven approach that effectively leverages multi-view cues while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Image relighting is traditionally tackled via complex inverse rendering pipelines, which suffer from ill-posed optimization, or single-image generative models that ignore crucial multi-view cues necessary for understanding 3D geometry and material interactions. To address these limitations, we introduce a feed-forward generative Transformer for direct single- and multi-view image relighting that entirely bypasses explicit intrinsic property estimation. Adapted from a video foundation model, our architecture features a latent illumination module that dynamically injects target environment maps into spatial features via cross-attention. Furthermore, we employ permutation-invariant positional encodings to symmetrically process unordered multi-view inputs without sequential bias. To train this robust data-driven model, we construct the massive Laval Objaverse Dataset (LOD), comprising 90K objects and 39K unique illuminations. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art visual quality, photorealistic relighting quality, and strong zero-shot generalization across single-view, multi-view, and novel-view relighting tasks.

</details>


### [5] [Emergent Goal-Directed Attention in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05517)
*Han Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The study investigates whether vision-language models (VLMs) can exhibit goal-directed attention similar to humans, without being specifically trained on gaze data. Two VLMs were tested on 4,887 images under both visual-search and free-viewing conditions, with their predictions compared to human eye-tracking data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most computational models of naturalistic viewing are trained on gaze data from free viewing, but it remains unclear whether goal-directed attention can emerge in systems without such gaze supervision. This research addresses whether general-purpose VLMs can generate human-like spatial priorities aligned with different task goals.

Method: Two off-the-shelf VLMs (Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking and Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it) were tested on 4,887 naturalistic scenes under visual-search and free-viewing instructions. Model predictions were compared with human fixations on the same images under corresponding tasks. Analysis included decoder-layer readouts and model-thinking traces.

Result: Both models showed stronger alignment with human fixations under matching goals (visual-search instructions matched with search task human data) compared to mismatched goals. This crossover effect persisted in target-absent scenes and appeared in decoder-layer readouts. Model-thinking traces were grounded in target semantics during search and visual prominence during free viewing.

Conclusion: General-purpose VLMs can generate human-aligned, goal-directed spatial priorities without gaze-specific training. This finding informs theories of goal-directed attention and provides scalable tools for predicting human gaze across different tasks.

Abstract: Human observers prioritize visual information according to task goals. Most computational models of naturalistic viewing are gaze-trained for free viewing, leaving open whether goal-directed attention can emerge in systems without gaze supervision. We tested two off-the-shelf vision-language models (VLMs), Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking and Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it, on 4,887 naturalistic scenes under visual-search and free-viewing instructions. Model predictions were compared with human fixations on the same images under corresponding tasks. Both models aligned more closely with human fixations under matching goals than under mismatched goals. This crossover persisted in target-absent scenes, where alignment could not be explained by simple visual grounding, and appeared in decoder-layer readouts. Furthermore, model-thinking traces were grounded in target semantics during search and in visual prominence during free viewing. These findings show that general-purpose VLMs can generate human-aligned, goal-directed spatial priorities without gaze-specific training, informing theories of goal-directed attention and offering scalable tools for predicting where people look across tasks.

</details>


### [6] [SoftRerank: Hierarchical Soft Fusion with Candidate-Label Reranking for Long-Tailed Micro-Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08221)
*Yichi Zhang,Zhichao Xia,Yanjun Chi,Lingsi Zhu,Yuefeng Zou,Jun Yu,Qingsong Liu,Jianqing Sun,Shengping Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a fine-grained micro-action recognition method addressing challenges of subtle, low-intensity behaviors with weak visual changes and similar motion patterns. The approach combines full fine-tuning of InternVideo2.5, hierarchical soft fusion, and a lightweight candidate-label reranker, achieving 79.99% F1-mean on MA-52 and ranking 1st in the 3rd Micro-Action Analysis Grand Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Micro-actions are subtle, low-intensity non-verbal behaviors that provide cues to fine-grained human states (emotions, intentions). Recognizing them is difficult because they are brief, contain weak visual changes, and often exhibit similar motion patterns across categories.

Method: The method combines three key components: (1) Full fine-tuning of InternVideo2.5 end-to-end; (2) Hierarchical soft fusion with coarse and group-conditional fine-grained classification heads attached to shared video representation; (3) Lightweight candidate-label reranker using hard samples and video-label matching for ambiguous samples. For long-tailed label distribution in MA-52, class-balanced sampling and inverse-frequency reweighting are used to reduce the effect of frequent classes.

Result: The method achieves 79.99% F1-mean on MA-52 and ranks first in the 3rd Micro-Action Analysis Grand Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026.

Conclusion: The proposed fine-grained micro-action recognition method, combining InternVideo2.5 fine-tuning, hierarchical soft fusion, and candidate-label reranking, effectively addresses the challenges of recognizing subtle micro-actions and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: Micro-actions are subtle, low-intensity non-verbal behaviors that provide cues to fine-grained human states, including emotions and intentions. Recognizing them remains difficult because they are brief, contain weak visual changes, and often exhibit similar motion patterns across categories. This paper addresses these challenges with a fine-grained micro-action recognition method that combines full fine-tuning of InternVideo2.5, hierarchical soft fusion, and a lightweight candidate-label reranker. For the long-tailed label distribution in MA-52, we use class-balanced sampling and inverse-frequency reweighting to reduce the effect of frequent classes during training. We fine-tune InternVideo2.5 end to end and attach coarse and group-conditional fine-grained classification heads to the shared video representation, improving the consistency between coarse and fine predictions. For ambiguous samples, the candidate-label reranker uses hard samples and video-label matching to focus on easily confused fine-grained actions. Experiments validate the proposed method, which achieves a 79.99% F1-mean on MA-52 and ranks first in the 3rd Micro-Action Analysis Grand Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026.

</details>


### [7] [CrossModalQA: A Cross-modal and Multi-hop Benchmark for Multimodal Retrieval-augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05518)
*Jiacheng Cai,Zijin Hong,Zheng Yuan,Huachi Zhou,Qinggang Zhang,Xiao Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CrossModalQA is an open-domain benchmark for evaluating multimodal retrieval and reasoning over heterogeneous corpora, containing 1,863 QA pairs from 4,987 Wikipedia articles and 4,431 Wikimedia Commons images. It covers five reasoning paths and requires an average reasoning depth of 3.50 hops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite strong capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), their parametric knowledge remains incomplete and difficult to update, necessitating multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to ground responses in external text and images. Existing benchmarks have two major limitations: (i) emphasis on single-hop retrieval rather than open-domain evidence discovery; and (ii) fragmented coverage of cross-modal reasoning paths, leaving complex multi-hop and multi-image reasoning underexplored.

Method: The benchmark is constructed through multimodal knowledge graph-guided subgraph sampling, with rule-based consistency checking and LLM verification to ensure multimodal dependence and traceable evidence.

Result: Existing multimodal RAG systems struggle to recover complete evidence chains and can underperform closed-book models when incomplete retrieval introduces distracting context. Complete cross-modal retrieval contributes more to answer accuracy than generator scaling, while multi-image retrieval and reasoning remain the primary bottlenecks.

Conclusion: CrossModalQA reveals critical gaps in current multimodal RAG systems. The study shows that complete cross-modal retrieval is more impactful than scaling the generator model, and multi-image reasoning represents the main bottleneck limiting end-to-end performance.

Abstract: Despite the strong capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), their parametric knowledge remains incomplete and difficult to update, motivating multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to ground responses in external text and images. However, existing benchmarks face two major limitations: (i) they typically emphasize single-hop retrieval or reasoning over a small set of provided contexts rather than open-domain evidence discovery; and (ii) they provide fragmented coverage of cross-modal reasoning paths, leaving complex multi-hop and multi-image reasoning underexplored. In this paper, we introduce CrossModalQA, an open-domain benchmark for evaluating multimodal retrieval and reasoning over heterogeneous corpora. CrossModalQA contains 1,863 question-answer pairs constructed from 4,987 Wikipedia articles and 4,431 Wikimedia Commons images. It covers five complementary reasoning paths: vision-to-text, text-to-vision, vision-to-text-to-vision, multi-image intersection, and image-set reasoning. Every question requires retrieving and composing distributed textual and visual evidence, with an average reasoning depth of 3.50 hops. We construct the benchmark through multimodal knowledge graph-guided subgraph sampling and apply rule-based consistency checking and LLM verification to ensure multimodal dependence and traceable evidence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that existing multimodal RAG systems struggle to recover complete evidence chains and can underperform closed-book models when incomplete retrieval introduces distracting context. Further analysis reveals that complete cross-modal retrieval contributes more to answer accuracy than generator scaling, while multi-image retrieval and reasoning remain the primary bottlenecks limiting end-to-end performance.

</details>


### [8] [Srijika: OpenType-Layout-Reusing Font Restyling for Nine Indic Scripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05661)
*Anil Pai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Srijika is a system that generates installable OpenType fonts for nine Brahmic scripts by restyling glyph outlines from template fonts while preserving OpenType shaping data (cmap, GSUB, GPOS). It uses a latent diffusion model conditioned on reference fonts for style transfer, with a retrieval system (Lipika) for natural-language style selection. The system produces 66 TTFs and includes a comprehensive audit of 80,915 glyphs and 54,812 anchors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central challenge in Indic font generation is maintaining consistency among hundreds to thousands of conjuncts, half forms, and matra variants under OpenType shaping. Existing font generation methods often produce incomplete fonts that fail shaping, requiring manual intervention. This work addresses the need for complete, shaping-consistent fonts by reusing layout data from template fonts.

Method: Srijika restyles glyph outlines from shaping-complete template fonts using a reference-conditioned latent diffusion model. It preserves the template's cmap and GSUB closure and GPOS data under a documented metric policy. Natural-language style selection uses Lipika, a retrieval index over ~650 open-license font families. The pipeline includes content gating, harmonization, and shaped-cluster verification with fallback to template outlines. The system produces 66 TTFs: 57 curated presets and nine open-vocabulary showcase fonts.

Result: All 66 generated TTFs pass OpenType Sanitizer validation. HarfBuzz and CoreText successfully reproduce template glyph-ID sequences on conjunct-heavy probes. A full-closure audit covers 80,915 glyphs and 54,812 anchors. Evaluation against no-learning baselines shows template copying outperforms diffusion-based generation on 50 of 56 faces (SSIM metric). Style movement is measurable only with an internal embedding trained on the held-out families, requiring caution in interpretation.

Conclusion: The layout-reusing formulation enables production of complete, shaping-consistent Indic fonts without generating from scratch. The negative results catalogue (failed conditioning, objective choices, data-hull limits) provides valuable insights for reference-guided restyling. The key limitation is that learned style transfer shows no advantage over template copying in this evaluation, with style movement only detectable using internal embeddings. Future work includes independent style metrics and human studies.

Abstract: We present Srijika, a system for producing installable OpenType fonts for nine Brahmic scripts: Devanagari, Tamil, Bengali, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Gujarati, Gurmukhi, and Odia. Rather than generating fonts from scratch, Srijika restyles glyph outlines from shaping-complete template fonts. It preserves the template's cmap and GSUB closure and its GPOS data under a documented metric policy, making every output a complete font by construction. This addresses a central challenge of Indic font generation: hundreds to thousands of conjuncts, half forms, and matra variants must remain mutually consistent under OpenType shaping.
  Srijika produces 66 TTFs: 57 curated presets and nine open-vocabulary showcase fonts. All pass the OpenType Sanitizer, while HarfBuzz and CoreText reproduce the template glyph-ID sequences on conjunct-heavy probes. A full-closure audit covering 80,915 glyphs and 54,812 anchors quantifies metric changes. Natural-language style selection uses Lipika, a retrieval index over approximately 650 open-license font families. A reference-conditioned latent diffusion model redraws template glyphs in the selected style, followed by content gating, harmonization, and shaped-cluster verification with fallback to template outlines.
  We evaluate against no-learning baselines. On diffusion-training-family-held-out SSIM gates, template copying outperforms generation on 50 of 56 faces. Style movement is measurable only with an internal same-model embedding whose training corpus includes the held-out families, so these results require caution. A learned baseline, independent style metric, and human study are outside this report's scope. Our contributions are the layout-reusing formulation and pipeline, its nine-script audit and benchmark, and a negative-results catalogue covering failed conditioning, objective choices, and data-hull limits of reference-guided restyling.

</details>


### [9] [Contrastive Knowledge Distillation for Anomaly Detection in Multi-Illumination/Focus Display Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05520)
*Jihyun Lee,Hangil Park,Yongmin Seo,Taewon Min,Joodong Yun,Jaewon Kim,Tae-Kyun Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Multiresolution Contrastive Distillation (MCD), a novel contrastive learning method for automatic anomaly detection in multi-illumination and multi-focus display images. The method extends Multiresolution Knowledge Distillation (MKD) by using teacher-student feature distance manipulation instead of traditional positive/negative pairs, and includes a blending module for multi-channel information aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Minute defects on display surfaces are difficult to detect in RGB images and by models trained only with normal data, necessitating a more sophisticated approach for multi-illumination and multi-focus display anomaly detection.

Method: The proposed MCD method extends MKD baseline by: (1) Using contrastive learning that pulls/pushes distance between teacher and student features without requiring explicit positive/negative pairs with an anchor; (2) Implementing a blending module that transforms and aggregates multi-channel information into three-channel input for MCD.

Result: The proposed method significantly outperforms competitive state-of-the-art methods in both AUROC and accuracy metrics on the collected Multi-illumination and Multi-focus display image dataset for Anomaly Detection (MMdAD).

Conclusion: The MCD approach effectively addresses the challenge of detecting minute display defects through contrastive knowledge distillation, achieving superior performance over existing methods on the MMdAD benchmark.

Abstract: In this paper, we tackle automatic anomaly detection in multi-illumination and multi-focus display images. The minute defects on the display surface are hard to spot out in RGB images and by a model trained with only normal data. To address this, we propose a novel contrastive learning scheme for knowledge distillation-based anomaly detection. In our framework, Multiresolution Knowledge Distillation (MKD) is adopted as a baseline, which operates by measuring feature similarities between the teacher and student networks. Based on MKD, we propose a novel contrastive learning method, namely Multiresolution Contrastive Distillation (MCD), which does not require positive/negative pairs with an anchor but operates by pulling/pushing the distance between the teacher and student features. Furthermore, we propose the blending module that transforms and aggregate multi-channel information to the three-channel input layer of MCD. Our proposed method significantly outperforms competitive state-of-the-art methods in both AUROC and accuracy metrics on the collected Multi-illumination and Multi-focus display image dataset for Anomaly Detection (MMdAD).

</details>


### [10] [RenderFormer-V2: Neural Rendering with Heterogeneous Scene Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05738)
*Chong Zeng,Yue Dong,Pieter Peers,Lvmin Zhang,Maneesh Agrawala*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RenderFormer-V2 is a unified transformer-based neural rendering model that handles diverse light-transport effects (caustics, volumetric scattering, environment lighting, displaced surfaces, out-of-distribution materials) without per-scene training or specialized code, modeling global light transport as a sequence-to-sequence transformation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current physics-based rendering systems require specialized code for different light-transport effects, and existing neural rendering methods lack versatility across diverse effects. The goal is to create a unified model that can handle various rendering phenomena without per-scene optimization.

Method: Two-stage transformer-based approach: (1) View-independent stage using combined windowed-attention and rendering-informed attention sink to resolve intra-scene primitive-to-primitive transport with improved scalability; (2) View-dependent stage transforming neural scene representations to image pixels. Supports heterogeneous primitives (environment maps, participating media) and uses material encoding via neural embedding independent of surface reflectance models.

Result: Demonstrated versatility across various scenes with extensive ablation studies validating the improved attention mechanism, showing improved scalability while maintaining render accuracy.

Conclusion: RenderFormer-V2 successfully provides a unified, versatile neural rendering framework that handles multiple light-transport effects without per-scene training, with the novel attention mechanism improving both efficiency and accuracy.

Abstract: We present 'RenderFormer-V2', a unified learned transformer-based neural rendering model, complementary to modern physics-based rendering systems, that can handle diverse light-transport effects such as caustics, volumetric scattering, environment lighting, textured and displaced surfaces and out-of-distribution materials without per-scene training or specialized code. RenderFormer-V2 models global light transport as a sequence-to-sequence transformation. Following its predecessor, RenderFormer-V2 also employs a two stage process: a view-independent stage that resolves intra-scene primitive to primitive transport, and a view-dependent stage that transforms the internal neural scene representation into image pixels. Different from RenderFormer, our model employs a novel combined windowed-attention and rendering-informed attention sink in the view-independent stage to improve scalability while maintaining render accuracy. To further improve versatility, RenderFormerV2 supports heterogeneous scene primitives, including environment maps and participating media, and it employs a material encoding independent of the underlying surface reflectance model that encodes material appearance via a novel neural embedding. We demonstrate the versatility of RenderFormer-V2 on a variety of scenes and perform an extensive ablation of the improved attention mechanism.

</details>


### [11] [Diffusion models for eye-gaze trajectory generation using position and velocity representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05522)
*Laxman Basnet,Alexander Szorkovszky,Pedro G. Lind,Anis Yazidi,Shailendra Bhandari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper addresses the challenge of expensive and privacy-restricted eye-tracking data by developing two denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) for unconditional generation of synthetic eye-gaze dynamics. One model generates 2D gaze-position sequences while the other generates velocity sequences, both using a FiLM-conditioned 1D U-Net architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eye-tracking data collection is expensive (requires specialized hardware and controlled laboratory conditions) and difficult to share due to privacy constraints. The authors aim to generate realistic synthetic eye-gaze data to overcome these limitations.

Method: Two complementary DDPMs with identical FiLM-conditioned one-dimensional U-Net architecture (19.35M parameters) trained on 8-second sliding-window sequences from 28 participants. The position-space model generates raw 2D gaze-position sequences, while the velocity-space model generates two-component velocity sequences. Each uses representation-specific preprocessing, training settings, data partitions, and evaluation protocols. Evaluated across three independent training seeds.

Result: Position-space model achieved mean JS divergence of 0.016±0.004 across nine kinematic features, with fixation duration within 2% of real data. Fréchet Gaze Distance was over an order of magnitude below baselines. Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real protocol achieved R²=0.66±0.02 (82.7% of real-data R²). Velocity-space model achieved mean JS divergence of 0.0065 with maximum of 0.015±0.005.

Conclusion: Unconditional diffusion models can capture local gaze kinematics and short-range temporal/directional structure, but long-range properties like saccade counts and cumulative path geometry remain challenging and require future conditioned models.

Abstract: Eye-tracking data are expensive to collect, requiring specialized hardware and controlled laboratory conditions, and difficult to share because of privacy constraints. We address this using two complementary denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) for unconditional generation of eye-gaze dynamics from visual-search data. Both use an identical FiLM-conditioned one-dimensional U-Net with self-attention (19.35,M parameters), trained on 8,s sliding-window sequences from 28 participants. One model generates raw two-dimensional gaze-position sequences, while the other generates two-component velocity sequences; each uses representation-specific preprocessing, training settings, data partitions, and evaluation protocols. Both are evaluated across three independent training seeds, with aggregated metrics reported as mean,$\pm$,SD. The position-space model achieves a mean Jensen-Shannon (JS) divergence of $0.016\pm0.004$ across nine kinematic features, with the highest feature-wise mean below $0.030$, fixation duration within 2% of real data, and a Fr'echet Gaze Distance more than an order of magnitude below statistical and Markovian baselines. Under a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real protocol, synthetic-only training achieves $R^2=0.66\pm0.02$, or 82.7% of the real-data $R^2$ point estimate. The velocity-space model achieves a mean JS divergence of $0.0065$ across velocity components, speed, log-speed, and turning angle, with a maximum of $0.015\pm0.005$. Reconstructed path length is less accurate ($0.21\pm0.02$ versus $0.03\pm0.01$ in position space), although the protocols differ. Overall, unconditional diffusion captures local gaze kinematics and short-range temporal and directional structure, while long-range properties such as saccade counts and cumulative path geometry remain targets for future conditioned models.

</details>


### [12] [PLSR: Progressive and Localized Super-Resolution of 3D Objects via Localized Latent Voxel Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06436)
*Yuxin Liu,Minshan Xie,Jiawen Liang,Runsong Zhu,Chi-Wing Fu,Tien-Tsin Wong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PLSR is a progressive and localized 3D super-resolution framework that enhances high-resolution 3D asset generation by decomposing global SR tasks into localized sub-tasks via associative input decomposition, adapting a flow-based 3D generator through low-cost finetuning, and using iterative patch-wise denoising for seamless output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing state-of-the-art diffusion-based 3D generation models are constrained by fixed resolutions, limiting their ability to produce fine details in high-resolution 3D objects.

Method: PLSR introduces three key technical components: (1) an associative input decomposition scheme that decomposes the global SR task into localized sub-tasks, (2) low-cost finetuning to adapt a flow-based 3D generator into a localized super-resolution model, and (3) an iterative patch-wise denoising pipeline for seamless high-resolution output.

Result: The approach generates 3D details with new strong fine-detail fidelity while significantly reducing computational cost compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: PLSR offers a new and practical solution for high-resolution 3D asset generation, effectively achieving detailed and memory-efficient super-resolution.

Abstract: High-resolution 3D asset generation is vital in various 3D applications. Existing state-of-the-art diffusion-based models remain constrained by fixed resolutions, limiting their ability to produce details. In this paper, we tackle the challenge of generating more detailed, higher-resolution 3D objects by introducing a 3D super-resolution (SR) framework built on existing 3D generative foundation models. To this end, we design PLSR, a progressive and localized super-resolution solution to achieve this goal effectively and memory efficiently. Technically, given a coarse geometry from a pretrained 3D generator, we decompose the global SR task into localized sub-tasks via an associative input decomposition scheme, adapt a flow-based 3D generator into a localized super-resolution model through low-cost finetuning, and unify them in an iterative patch-wise denoising pipeline for seamless high-resolution output. Experiments on challenging objects show that our approach is able to generate 3D details with new strong fine-detail fidelity while significantly reducing the computational cost, offering a new and practical solution for high-resolution 3D asset generation.

</details>


### [13] [Infrastructure-based Monocular 3D Vehicle Localization Framework with Experimental Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05523)
*Akos T. Kopeczi-Bocz,Tian Mi,Gabor Orosz,Denes Takacs*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A one-stage learning framework that directly estimates vehicle states (position, dimensions, yaw angle) from monocular roadside camera images, eliminating the need for separate geometric post-processing by jointly leveraging visual features from a pretrained object detector.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional infrastructure-based perception approaches require detecting vehicles in the image plane first, then applying geometric post-processing to recover ground-plane states. This two-stage approach is complex and potentially less scalable. The authors aim to develop a more direct, end-to-end approach that can recover vehicle trajectories and orientations from monocular roadside imagery.

Method: The framework uses features from a pretrained object detector to jointly estimate each vehicle's ground-plane position, dimensions, and yaw angle in a single stage. Data collection uses synchronized video from roadside camera and UAV (acting as temporary top-view sensing platform) to generate ground-truth labels by transforming UAV vehicle trajectories, dimensions, and orientations into the ground-fixed coordinate frame.

Result: The proposed method can recover vehicle trajectories and orientations from monocular roadside imagery without a separate geometric post-processing stage, demonstrating potential as a scalable approach to infrastructure-based perception at urban intersections.

Conclusion: The one-stage approach successfully achieves direct spatial and orientation estimation from monocular roadside images, showing promise for scalable infrastructure-based perception. The method eliminates the traditional detect-then-geometric-post-processing pipeline while maintaining accuracy in vehicle state estimation.

Abstract: This paper presents a one-stage learning framework that maps monocular roadside-camera images directly to vehicle states in a ground-fixed coordinate frame. Unlike conventional approaches that first detect vehicles in the image plane and subsequently apply geometric post-processing, the proposed method leverages features from a pretrained object detector to jointly estimate each vehicle's ground-plane position, dimensions, and yaw angle. The framework therefore uses visual features not only for vehicle detection but also for direct spatial and orientation estimation. To support model training and evaluation, we develop a data-collection and label-generation pipeline based on synchronized video from a roadside camera and an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV). Acting as a temporary top-view sensing platform, the UAV provides vehicle trajectories, dimensions, and orientations, which are transformed into the ground-fixed coordinate frame and temporally aligned with the roadside-camera images to generate ground-truth labels. The framework is evaluated using data collected during multiple experiments at the Mcity Test Facility. Results show that the proposed method can recover vehicle trajectories and orientations from monocular roadside imagery without a separate geometric post-processing stage, demonstrating its potential as a scalable approach to infrastructure-based perception at urban intersections.

</details>


### [14] [ADELE - Adaptive Delaunay Grids for High-Fidelity Mesh-Native Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06723)
*Johannes Weidenfeller,Shaofei Wang,Philipp Fürnstahl,Siyu Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces an adaptive mesh-based optimization framework for high-quality 3D mesh reconstruction. It combines an optimizable Delaunay-triangulated tetrahedral grid with a multi-resolution hash grid, using volumetric rendering for coarse geometry and mesh-based rendering for fine details. A differentiable rasterization-based depth-offset rendering formulation is proposed to reduce geometric artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mesh reconstruction methods struggle to produce high-quality meshes. State-of-the-art approaches use NeRF/3DGS as intermediate representations, treating mesh extraction as post-processing, which leads to oversmoothed surfaces or poor quality meshes with excessive triangle counts. Mesh-native optimization methods suffer from fixed-resolution discretizations and unstable optimization behavior.

Method: The proposed representation combines: (1) An optimizable Delaunay-triangulated tetrahedral grid refined through point pruning and insertion; (2) A multi-resolution hash grid providing latent features for SDF/appearance predictions. The approach uses volumetric rendering to bootstrap coarse geometry while leveraging mesh-based rendering for recovering fine-grained details. Additionally, a differentiable rasterization-based depth-offset rendering formulation is proposed.

Result: The method significantly outperforms existing mesh optimization approaches across various object-centric benchmarks while being competitive with state-of-the-art NeRF/3DGS methods.

Conclusion: The adaptive mesh-based optimization framework with practical mesh rendering technique successfully addresses the challenges of existing methods, achieving superior reconstruction quality with reduced geometric artifacts.

Abstract: Meshes remain the most practical representation for geometry reasoning and integration into graphics pipelines, yet existing reconstruction methods struggle to produce high-quality meshes. Most state-of-the-art approaches initially learn an intermediate representation (NeRF/3DGS) and treat mesh extraction as a post-processing step, which often leads to oversmoothed surfaces or poor quality meshes with excessive triangle counts.Existing mesh-native optimization methods alleviate some of these issues but suffer from fixed-resolution discretizations and unstable optimization behavior. In this paper, we introduce an adaptive mesh-based optimization framework and a practical mesh rendering technique to address these challenges. Our representation combines an optimizable Delaunay-triangulated tetrahedral grid with a multi-resolution hash grid. The former is refined through point pruning and insertion, while the latter provides latent features for SDF/appearance value predictions. We use volumetric rendering to bootstrap a coarse geometry while leveraging mesh-based rendering for recovering fine-grained details. Additionally, we propose a differentiable, rasterization-based depth-offset rendering formulation, reducing geometric artifacts and improving reconstruction quality. Our method significantly outperforms existing mesh optimization approaches across a variety of object-centric benchmarks while being competitive with state-of-the-art NeRF/3DGS methods.

</details>


### [15] [DIVA: Exploiting Cross-Step Conditional Propagation for Visual Jailbreaks in Discrete Diffusion Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05525)
*Guorui Song,Runqing Tang,Jingye Zhang,Luyuan Zhang,Feice Huang,Cong Ray,Guocun Wang,Dake Zhong,Choo Sin Wai,Bingquan Dai,Chuming Wang,Tongxu Lin,Wanyu Guo,Haoqian Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces DIVA, the first visual jailbreak attack specifically targeting discrete diffusion vision-language models (dVLMs). It discovers a unique vulnerability called "cross-step conditional propagation" where adversarial visual semantics are amplified across denoising steps, unlike autoregressive models. DIVA achieves 58.8-69.1% attack success rates across three dVLMs, outperforming prior visual jailbreak methods designed for autoregressive architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual jailbreak research focuses exclusively on autoregressive VLMs, leaving the emerging family of multimodal discrete diffusion VLMs unstudied despite their deployment in safety-critical settings. This gap motivated investigation of a potentially new attack surface specific to diffusion generation.

Method: The authors propose DIVA, a white-box visual jailbreak framework combining (1) cross-modal intent obfuscation to mislead safety mechanisms and (2) diffusion-aware multi-timestep adversarial optimization that exploits the cross-step conditional propagation phenomenon where visual embeddings condition every reverse denoising step.

Result: Across three dVLMs, DIVA achieves 58.8%, 67.7%, and 69.1% HADES ASR (Attack Success Rate) under the Beaver reward-model metric, significantly outperforming visual jailbreak baselines designed for autoregressive models.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion VLMs have a unique vulnerability (cross-step conditional propagation) not present in autoregressive VLMs, requiring specialized attack methods. DIVA successfully exploits this new attack surface, demonstrating the need for diffusion-specific safety measures in multimodal generation systems.

Abstract: Large vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical settings, yet existing visual jailbreak research has focused almost exclusively on autoregressive architectures, leaving an important emerging family unstudied: multimodal discrete diffusion vision-language models (dVLMs). We identify a vulnerability specific to diffusion generation: because the visual embedding conditions every reverse denoising step rather than acting as a one-time prefix, adversarial visual semantics are repeatedly propagated and amplified across the generation trajectory, a phenomenon we term cross-step conditional propagation. We provide empirical evidence through stage-sensitivity analysis, prompt-level switch rates, and pairwise denoising-bin disagreement metrics, confirmed by bootstrap resampling. We propose DIVA (Discrete-diffusion Vision-language model Attack), a white-box visual jailbreak framework using cross-modal intent obfuscation and diffusion-aware multi-timestep adversarial optimization. Across three dVLMs, DIVA reaches 58.8%, 67.7%, and 69.1% HADES ASR under the Beaver reward-model metric, outperforming visual jailbreak baselines designed for autoregressive models. Code: https://github.com/loststars2002/DIVA

</details>


### [16] [Sub-Pixel Affine Registration of Space Debris Images via the Radon Point Spread Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06929)
*Shenshen Luan,Miaomiao Tian,Shuai Jiang,Yan Yang,Shuguo Xie,Zezhou Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method using Radon Point Spread Function (RPSF) to estimate inter-frame translation and rotation for point target imagery in optical surveillance, requiring only four scalar samples per frame pair without iterative optimization or feature extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inter-frame affine misalignment from platform jitter and attitude adjustments poses a fundamental challenge for multi-frame point target analysis, as conventional registration methods fail due to absence of spatial intensity correlations or distinctive features in low-SNR point target imagery.

Method: The paper introduces RPSF to characterize point targets in the Radon-transformed domain, deriving a closed-form framework that jointly estimates inter-frame translation and rotation from as few as four scalar RPSF samples per frame pair, requiring no iterative optimization, feature extraction or interpolation.

Result: Simulation confirms sub-pixel translation accuracy and mean rotation error of 0.2556° at 1° Radon angular resolution. Validation on five real space debris datasets (ground-based and in-orbit) yields mean calibration error below 0.5 pixels.

Conclusion: The RPSF-based method substantially exceeds the precision required for reliable multi-frame processing, and is suitable for resource-constrained onboard processing due to its computational efficiency.

Abstract: Inter-frame affine misalignment caused by platform jitter and attitude adjustments poses a fundamental challenge for multi-frame analysis of point targets in optical surveillance. Conventional registration methods rely on spatial intensity correlations or distinctive image features, both of which are largely absent in low-signal-to-noise-ratio point target imagery. We introduce the Radon Point Spread Function (RPSF) to characterize point targets in the Radon-transformed domain, and derive a closed-form framework that jointly estimates inter-frame translation and rotation from as few as four scalar RPSF samples per frame pair. The method requires no iterative optimization, feature extraction or interpolation, which is suitable for resource-constrained onboard processing. Simulation results confirm sub-pixel translation accuracy and a mean rotation error of 0.2556° at 1° Radon angular resolution. Validation on five real space debris datasets including both ground-based and in-orbit observations yields a mean calibration error below 0.5 pixels, substantially exceeding the precision required for reliable multi-frame processing.

</details>


### [17] [A Specialized Large Multimodal Model for Interpreting PET/CT in Head and Neck Cancer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05532)
*Haengbok Chung,SunGyu Kim,Joo hyun Lee,Sangjin Bae,Min Jeong Cho,Minseok Suh,Jae Sung Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study develops a specialized Large Multimodal Model (LMM) for automated PET/CT interpretation in head and neck cancer, fine-tuning LLaVA-NeXT with a two-level curriculum using radiologist-curated data. The specialized model significantly outperforms generalist models like ChatGPT and LLaVA-NeXT in both primary tumor classification (83.14% internal, 69.03% external) and lymph node metastasis localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing head and neck cancer using PET/CT is clinically challenging due to anatomical complexity, time-consuming interpretation, and limitations of generalist LMMs (insufficient medical knowledge, privacy concerns, verbosity). This motivates development of specialized standalone LMMs for automated PET/CT interpretation.

Method: Fine-tuned LLaVA-NeXT using a two-level curriculum with image-conversation pairs curated by two radiologists. Level 1 (28,000 pairs) learned basic modality information and hypermetabolism. Level 2 (12,975 pairs) learned primary tumor presence and cervical lymph node metastasis location. External validation across four institutions with diverse imaging devices.

Result: The specialized LMM substantially outperformed ChatGPT and LLaVA-NeXT. Level-2 external validation: ROUGE-L=0.8751, ROUGE-S=0.8794, Cosine Similarity=0.8324, Precision=0.8794, Recall=0.8711, F1=0.8751 (generalist models <0.1). Primary tumor classification: 83.14%±1.15% internal, 69.03%±0.81% external. Lymph node localization: 0.6389, 0.6257, 0.5287, 0.5782, 0.6371, 0.6648.

Conclusion: Specialized LMMs show promising results for fast, accurate PET/CT-based diagnostic support and medical education, highlighting potential for clinical translation in head and neck cancer imaging.

Abstract: Background: Diagnosing head and neck cancer using PET/CT is clinically challenging and time-consuming due to the anatomical complexity of the region, motivating computer-aided diagnosis (CAD). Generalist Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) remain limited in medical contexts by insufficient domain-specific knowledge, privacy and security concerns, and verbosity, motivating specialized standalone LMMs. Purpose: We evaluated the feasibility of a specialized LMM for automated PET/CT interpretation in head and neck cancer using a large-scale multi-institutional PET/CT dataset, a tailored training curriculum, and autoregressive training. Methods: LLaVA-NeXT was fine-tuned using a two-level curriculum with image-conversation pairs curated by two radiologists from public data. The dataset included clinically important annotations such as primary tumor presence and metastatic lymph node location. Level 1 used 28,000 image-conversation pairs to learn basic information, including modality type and hypermetabolism. Level 2 used 12,975 pairs to learn primary tumor presence and the existence and anatomical location of cervical lymph node metastases. External validation included four institutions with diverse imaging devices. Results: The specialized LMM substantially outperformed ChatGPT and LLaVA-NeXT. In Level-2 external validation, ROUGE-L, ROUGE-S, Cosine Similarity, Precision, Recall, and F1 were 0.8751, 0.8794, 0.8324, 0.8794, 0.8711, and 0.8751, while generalist models consistently scored below 0.1. Primary tumor classification accuracy was 83.14 +/- 1.15% internally and 69.03 +/- 0.81% externally. For lymph node localization, the corresponding scores were 0.6389, 0.6257, 0.5287, 0.5782, 0.6371, and 0.6648. Conclusion: Specialized LMMs show promising results for fast, accurate PET/CT-based diagnostic support and medical education, highlighting their potential for clinical translation.

</details>


### [18] [SimpleMemVLA: A Simple but Effective Native-Video Memory for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05533)
*Cheng Yin,Wang Xu,Junpeng Yang,Sikyuen Tam,Hanyu Liu,Yuan Yao,Xiangrui Zeng,Junbo Cui,Yequan Wang,Zhouping Yin,Yankai Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SimpleMemVLA removes dedicated memory modules from Vision-Language-Action models by directly passing intact history as timestamped video to pretrained backbones, achieving state-of-the-art on four memory benchmarks while maintaining general control performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory mechanisms (retrieval banks, learned compressors, recurrent states) must decide what to keep from the past before knowing what future decisions will require. This is problematic because the assumption that minute-scale history is too large to process is no longer true with modern VLM backbones.

Method: SimpleMemVLA keeps sampled history intact and passes it to the backbone in timestamped video format (which the backbone was pretrained to process). Hidden states of a generated sub-task form the only channel from history to a standard flow-matching action head. Since consecutive decisions share most of their history, prefilling the shared prefix during action execution keeps latency close to a single-frame VLA.

Result: Sets new state of the art on four memory benchmarks without cost on general-purpose control. Outperforms retrieval, compression, and recurrent-state mechanisms by a wide margin. Causal interventions confirm the policy genuinely reads its history.

Conclusion: Directly processing intact history through pretrained VLM backbones is more effective than dedicated memory mechanisms. The approach achieves superior performance on long-horizon manipulation tasks while maintaining low latency through prefix prefilling optimization.

Abstract: Long-horizon manipulation is partially observable: the information needed to choose the next action may appear only in observations from minutes earlier. Existing memory mechanisms: retrieval banks, learned compressors, recurrent states must decide what to keep from the past before knowing what a future decision will require. This was motivated by the assumption that minute-scale history is too large to process directly, which modern VLM backbones no longer make true. In this work, we introduce SimpleMemVLA, a VLA without a dedicated memory module. It keeps the sampled history intact and passes it to the backbone in the timestamped video format the backbone was pretrained to process; the hidden states of a generated sub-task then form the only channel from history to a standard flow-matching action head. Since consecutive decisions share most of their history, prefilling the shared prefix during action execution keeps latency close to a single-frame VLA. SimpleMemVLA sets a new state of the art on four memory benchmarks without cost on general-purpose control. Holding the backbone and training setup fixed, it outperforms retrieval, compression and recurrent-state mechanisms by a wide margin, and causal interventions confirm that the policy genuinely reads its history. Code available at https://github.com/wadeKeith/SimpleMemVLA

</details>


### [19] [Heat Kernel Textures: the Geodesic Gaussians That Do Not Splat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07557)
*Simone Foti,Caner Korkmaz,Stefanos Zafeiriou,Tolga Birdal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Heat Kernel Textures (HKTex) is a novel texture representation that eliminates UV mapping issues by using anisotropic heat kernels defined on triangular mesh surfaces, reducing memory footprint while integrating with physically-based rendering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional UV mapping suffers from wasted UV space, seams, distortions, vertex-duplication, and varying resolution. These persistent issues need a fundamental rethinking of texture representation.

Method: HKTex uses discrete Riemannian geometry with anisotropic heat kernels as geodesic equivalents to Gaussians. The optimization of kernel positions and adaptive densification strategies operate directly on the surface manifold. The representation integrates with a physically-based renderer and can be optimized from existing textures or multi-view images.

Result: HKTex eliminates UV unwrapping and its associated problems while significantly reducing memory footprint compared to traditional UV mapping.

Conclusion: This approach provides a seamless, distortion-free texture representation that operates intrinsically on any manifold surface, with applications in novel view synthesis and 3D vision.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting has recently revolutionised novel view synthesis as well as many other 3D vision methods and applications. Drawing inspiration from this representation, we now rethink textures to overcome the main issues of UV mapping while considerably lowering their memory footprint. Heat Kernel Textures (HKTex) eliminate UV unwrapping as well as their persistent issues of wasted UV space, seams, distortions, vertex-duplication, and varying resolution. Grounded in discrete Riemannian geometry and intrinsically defined on any manifold surface discretised as a triangular mesh, HKTex uses anisotropic heat kernels as geodesic equivalents to Gaussians. Like our kernels, also the optimisation of their position and the adaptive densification strategies were redefined to operate on the surface of the object to be textureised. Our novel representation is also fully integrated with a physically based renderer and can be optimised either from existing textures or multi-view images. Our project page and code are available at circle-group.github.io/research/HeatKernelTextures.

</details>


### [20] [Beyond the Verdict: Evidence-Aligned Evaluation of Visual Prompt-Injection Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05535)
*Suyoung Lee,Myungsub Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical gap in evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) for web-agent guardrails: current verdict-only evaluation fails to reveal whether models actually use visual evidence to support their decisions. The authors introduce Mind2Web-Injection, a benchmark with 9,954 instruction-screenshot pairs, and propose new evaluation metrics like Evidence-Aligned Detection (EAD) to assess both detection and localization of conflicts between on-screen text and user instructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verdict-only evaluation in VLM safety assessment is insufficient because it doesn't reveal whether models actually use visual evidence to support their decisions. This is particularly critical in web-agent guardrails where VLMs must judge whether on-screen text conflicts with user instructions. The authors discovered that models with nearly identical average precision can differ ninefold in their ability to correctly localize evidence, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation metrics.

Method: The authors created Mind2Web-Injection benchmark with 9,954 instruction-screenshot pairs featuring instruction-relative labels, pixel-exact evidence boxes, and matched image-side counterfactuals. They evaluated six VLMs and introduced Evidence-Aligned Detection (EAD) as a new metric. Two training-free interventions were proposed: ReadGate (improves grounding without changing verdicts) and CmdCompare (tests whether explicit instruction-command comparison resolves instruction-side inconsistency). They also tested whether verdicts depend on the command cited as evidence by replacing instructions with ones that endorse the cited command.

Result: Key findings: (1) Six VLMs showed that models with nearly identical average precision can differ ninefold in EAD; (2) Qwen3-VL-32B (strongest open-weight localizer) returned aligned responses in only 58.7% of cases, while GPT-5.6-luna achieved 99.9%; (3) ReadGate improved grounding without changing verdicts; (4) CmdCompare tested whether explicit instruction-command comparison resolves inconsistency.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that current evaluation metrics are insufficient for assessing VLM guardrail performance. They motivate reporting verdict correctness, evidence localization, and counterfactual responsiveness as separate metrics. The proposed interventions (ReadGate and CmdCompare) offer training-free solutions to improve grounding and detect instruction-side inconsistency. The significant gap between Qwen3-VL-32B (58.7%) and GPT-5.6-luna (99.9%) in alignment performance suggests substantial room for improvement in VLM grounding capabilities.

Abstract: Verdict-only evaluation does not reveal whether a vision-language model (VLM) used the visual evidence that should support its decision. We study this problem in web-agent guardrails, where a VLM judges whether on-screen text conflicts with a user instruction. We introduce Mind2Web-Injection, a benchmark of 9,954 instruction-screenshot pairs with instruction-relative labels, pixel-exact evidence boxes, and matched image-side counterfactuals. Across six VLMs, two models with nearly identical average precision differ ninefold in Evidence-Aligned Detection (EAD), the fraction of attacks both detected and correctly localized. To test whether a verdict depends on the command cited as evidence, we replace the instruction with one that endorses that command. Qwen3-VL-32B, the strongest open-weight localizer, returns aligned in only 58.7% of cases, whereas GPT-5.6-luna does so in 99.9%. To diagnose these failures, we propose two training-free interventions. ReadGate improves grounding without changing verdicts, while CmdCompare tests whether explicit instruction-command comparison resolves instruction-side inconsistency. These results motivate reporting verdict correctness, evidence localization, and counterfactual responsiveness separately.

</details>


### [21] [Flexible Motion Generation from Language and Style References](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08032)
*Kai Weixian Lan,Bodie Criswell,Briana Fedkiw,Zhan Zhang,Joseph Teran,Daniel Holden*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FlexMoGen is a novel human motion synthesis framework that generates high-quality motions conditioned on both natural language descriptions and motion style references. It uses a variational style encoder combined with a text-to-motion latent diffusion model to preserve semantic content while faithfully reproducing target stylistic traits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text prompts alone are limited in capturing fine-grained style details such as timing, limb articulation, and expressive dynamics. Prior methods rely on discrete style labels and do not generalize to long or multi-style generation. A style example clip can supplement text by conveying nuanced motion characteristics directly.

Method: FlexMoGen jointly pre-trains a variational style encoder (without style supervision) and a text-to-motion latent diffusion model within a unified architecture. It uses a lightweight adaptation module to modulate motion style, integrates an efficient relative positional encoding scheme, and is trained on both stylized and non-stylized datasets.

Result: FlexMoGen achieves the best balance between content fidelity and style reflection, with strong generalization to unseen text-style combinations. It supports long, time-varying, multi-style synthesis.

Conclusion: FlexMoGen offers users greater control over the animation generation process by enabling flexible synthesis conditioned on both text prompts and style example clips, addressing limitations of prior discrete label-based approaches.

Abstract: We introduce FlexMoGen, a novel framework for flexible human motion synthesis conditioned on both natural language descriptions and motion style references. Text prompts are effective at defining semantic content, but they are often limited in capturing fine-grained style details such as timing, limb articulation, and expressive dynamics. A style example clip supplements the text by conveying these nuanced motion characteristics directly, enabling the model to preserve high-level intent while reproducing the desired stylistic traits. Given a text prompt and a style example clip, FlexMoGen generates high-quality motions that preserve semantic content while faithfully reflecting the target style, offering users greater control over the animation generation process. Unlike prior methods that rely on discrete style labels and do not generalize to long or multi-style generation, FlexMoGen learns a variational style encoder without style supervision and supports long, time-varying, multi-style synthesis. Our framework jointly pre-trains the style encoder and a text-to-motion latent diffusion model within a unified architecture, modulating motion style through a lightweight adaptation module. It integrates an efficient relative positional encoding scheme and is trained on both stylized and non-stylized datasets, enabling strong generalization to unseen text-style combinations. Experiments show that FlexMoGen achieves the best balance between content fidelity and style reflection.

</details>


### [22] [VIS-DICT: A Visual Dictionary for Missing Modality Imputation in Social Network Depression Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05537)
*Hamed Marvi,Mohammad Mehdi Keikha,Abolfazl Nadi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Tracking social media posts can help spot early signs of depression. Recent studies show that combining text and images works better for detecting depression than using text alone. However, many social media posts do not have images, which makes it hard to use multimodal models. Most existing methods fill in missing images using retrieval or generative models that need extra training. In this paper, we introduce Vis-Dict, a dictionary-based method that builds missing visual features by linking words to average image vectors from complete training posts. These estimated visual features are then combined with text to track changes in user behavior over time. We tested Vis-Dict on a social media dataset using user timelines of up to 512 posts and compared it with other missing-data methods. The results show that Vis-Dict performs on par with generative networks, reaching an F1-score of 0.9454 and an ROC-AUC of 0.9890. Most importantly, Vis-Dict achieves this strong performance with zero trainable parameters for image generation. These findings show that directly connecting words to visual features is an effective and practical way to handle missing images in depression detection systems.

</details>


### [23] [Dual-Latent Memory Routing for Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05539)
*Hao-Xuan Ma,Jin-Fei Qi,Yicheng Xiao,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DLMR proposes a parameter-efficient mechanism with Dual Latent Memories (visual and reasoning) to address performance degradation in multimodal LLMs during long generations. A dynamic router decides which memory to reuse, preserving visual grounding while maintaining coherent reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs suffer performance degradation with longer generations because they lose track of earlier visual evidence and intermediate constraints under monolithic growing context. The solution is inspired by human cognitive behavior of separately recalling visual evidence vs. inferences.

Method: DLMR uses Dual Latent Memories: 1) Visual memory compresses image evidence, 2) Reasoning memory tracks intermediate conclusions/constraints. A Router dynamically decides which memory and how much to reuse during inference. Training occurs in three stages (latent memory construction → selective router learning) while keeping the base MLLM frozen.

Result: Substantial gains on both general and reasoning benchmarks with only a small number of additional trainable parameters. Shows interpretable state-dependent routing with specialized memory roles and reduced decoding tokens over long generations.

Conclusion: DLMR provides an effective solution for long-horizon vision-language reasoning in MLLMs through dual memory architecture and dynamic routing, achieving improved performance with minimal parameter overhead.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have recently made strong progress in vision-language reasoning, yet their performance often degrades as generations grow longer. A key factor is that they frequently lose track of earlier visual evidence and intermediate constraints under a monolithic growing context. Inspired by how humans separately recall what they see and what they infer when solving complex tasks, we propose DLMR, a parameter-efficient mechanism that equips MLLMs with Dual Latent Memories: a visual memory that compresses image evidence and a reasoning memory that tracks intermediate conclusions and constraints. A Router then dynamically decides which memory and how much to reuse during inference, preserving visual grounding while maintaining coherent long-horizon reasoning. DLMR is trained in three stages, from latent memory construction to selective router learning, while keeping the base MLLM frozen, yielding substantial gains on both general and reasoning benchmarks with only a small number of additional trainable parameters. Analyses further show interpretable, state-dependent routing with specialized memory roles and reduced decoding tokens over long generations. Code is available at https://github.com/Hunter-Wrynn/DLMR.

</details>


### [24] [Knowing When Not to Answer: Abstention and Refusal Reasoning in Vision--Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05540)
*Karan Dua,Amit Agarwal,Hitesh Laxmichand Patel,Hansa Meghwani,Jyotika Singh,Ranjeet Gupta,Graham Horwood,Tao Sheng,Avi Sil,Sujith Ravi,Dan Roth*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper audits whether vision-language models (VLMs) appropriately abstain from answering unanswerable medical diagnostic queries (ASD diagnosis from facial photographs). The authors introduce PARITY, a synthetic dataset of identity-controlled portrait pairs, and find that models split into refusal-first and speculative categories, with certain expressions disproportionately triggering harmful attributions in speculative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical conditions like ASD require behavioral/developmental assessment, not visual appearance. Yet VLMs are increasingly queried for medical diagnostic judgments from images, raising safety concerns when such inferences are unsupported. The paper aims to audit whether VLMs abstain from these unanswerable queries and whether expressions influence non-abstaining choices.

Method: The authors introduce PARITY (Paired Assessment with Reused Identity), a synthetic, demographically balanced dataset of identity-controlled neutral/expression portrait pairs with neutral-neutral controls. All identities are synthetic with no ASD status. Since the query is unanswerable from images, any non-abstaining selection is treated as harmful attribution. They test across contemporary VLMs and evaluate the effect of clinical guardrails and single-image framing.

Result: Across contemporary VLMs, a clear split is observed between refusal-first models and speculative models. In speculative models, certain expressions disproportionately trigger harmful selections. Clinical guardrails and single-image framing substantially increase abstention rates.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a critical safety issue in VLMs regarding medical diagnostic queries. There is a clear split between models that refuse unanswerable queries and those that speculate. Clinical guardrails and single-image framing serve as actionable mitigations in both prompting and interface design to increase appropriate abstention.

Abstract: Many medical conditions require diagnosis through detailed, multi-context clinical assessment rather than from visual appearance alone. Despite this, vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly queried to interpret images in ways that touch on medical or diagnostic judgments, raising safety concerns when such inferences are unsupported. ASD diagnosis requires behavioral and developmental evidence, not static facial photographs. We audit whether VLMs abstain from this unanswerable paired-image query, and whether expressions sway non-abstaining choices.
  We introduce PARITY (Paired Assessment with Reused Identity), a synthetic, demographically balanced set of identity-controlled neutral/expression portrait pairs with neutral-neutral controls. All identities are synthetic and have no ASD status; because the query is unanswerable from images, any non-abstaining selection is treated as a harmful attribution. Across contemporary VLMs, we find a clear split between refusal-first models and speculative models; in the latter, certain expressions disproportionately trigger harmful selections.
