Clinical guardrails and single-image framing substantially increase abstention, suggesting actionable mitigations in both prompting and interface design

</details>


### [25] [Video Compression with Graph-inspired Neural Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05541)
*Changqi Wang,Ge Gao,Fan Zhang,Yue Li,Kai Zhang,Li Zhang,David Bull*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: G-NeRV is a graph-inspired Implicit Neural Representation (INR) for video compression that explicitly exploits temporal redundancy through message passing between frame embeddings and a memory bank mechanism, outperforming state-of-the-art INR and conventional video codecs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing INR-based video compression methods exploit temporal redundancy implicitly through shared network parameters and frame-indexed embeddings, which leads to sub-optimal compression performance. The authors aim to address this limitation by explicitly modeling temporal relationships in the latent space.

Method: The proposed G-NeRV constructs a temporal neighborhood over frame embeddings and performs message passing to aggregate reusable information from neighboring frames. An adaptive gate controls the injection of neighboring information. Additionally, a memory bank mechanism (inspired by reference frame buffers in conventional video coding) enables efficient temporal-neighbor retrieval under random frame-index sampling during INR training.

Result: On the UVG dataset, G-NeRV outperforms NVRC (state-of-the-art INR-based codec) by 8.86% and VVC VTM (latest standard video codec) by 14.68% in BD-rate measured by PSNR.

Conclusion: The graph-inspired approach with explicit temporal redundancy exploitation and memory bank mechanism significantly improves INR-based video compression performance, surpassing both neural and conventional video codecs.

Abstract: Implicit Neural Representations (INR) provide a compact and content-adaptive paradigm for video compression, typically representing a video through shared network parameters and frame-indexed embeddings. Compared to conventional or autoencoder-based codecs, these approaches exploit temporal redundancy within videos in an implicit manner, which potentially results in sub-optimal compression performance. In this paper, we propose G-NeRV, a graph-inspired INR that explicitly improves temporal redundancy exploitation in the implicit latent space. Motivated by the total correlation principles in information theory, we construct a temporal neighborhood over frame embeddings and perform message passing to aggregate reusable information from neighboring frames through an adaptive gate controlling the injection of neighboring information. Inspired by the reference frame buffer in conventional video coding, a memory bank mechanism has been further designed to enable efficient temporal-neighbor retrieval under random frame-index sampling in INR training. This new representation model has been integrated into an advanced representation compression framework and compared with existing conventional and neural video codecs. The results show that the G-NeRV codec outperforms the state-of-the-art INR-based codec, NVRC, and the latest standard video codec, VVC VTM, by 8.86\% and 14.68\% (in BD-rate), respectively, measured by PSNR on the UVG dataset.

</details>


### [26] [Do Depressive Facial Patterns Transfer Across Cultures and Contexts? Evidence from a German RCT and E-DAIC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05543)
*Misha Sadeghi,Robert Richer,Lydia Helene Rupp,Lena Schindler-Gmelch,Marie Keinert,Farnaz Rahimi,Malin Hager,Bernhard Egger,Matthias Berking,Bjoern M. Eskofier*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates cross-corpus generalization of automated depression assessment from facial dynamics, pairing an RCT (EmpkinS-EKSpression) with a clinical interview corpus (E-DAIC). Binary classification transfers more robustly than continuous severity regression, with functional context alignment (passive vs. active phases) determining generalization success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-corpus generalization of learned biomarkers for depression assessment remains an open challenge, limiting scalability of automated mental health monitoring systems.

Method: Systematic bidirectional transfer study using two corpora (EmpkinS-EKSpression RCT, N=256; E-DAIC, N=275) to predict depression severity (PHQ-8) and binary diagnostic status from facial action units, head pose, and gaze.

Result: Cross-corpus binary classification (AUC=0.70) is more robust than continuous PHQ-8 regression. Functional context alignment governs regression transfer: passive observation phases yield most transferable models, while active emotion regulation phases elicit stronger within-corpus signals.

Conclusion: Functional context alignment is the primary determinant of cross-corpus generalization. Passive elicitation contexts offer the best trade-off between within-corpus sensitivity and cross-corpus robustness for depression detection from facial dynamics.

Abstract: Automated assessment of depression from facial dynamics holds promise for scalable mental health monitoring, yet cross-corpus generalization of learned biomarkers remains an open challenge. We present a systematic bidirectional transfer study pairing the EmpkinS-EKSpression randomized controlled trial (RCT; N = 256, SCID-5-CV diagnoses) with the Extended Distress Analysis Interview Corpus (E-DAIC; N = 275, semi-structured clinical interviews), predicting depression severity and binary diagnostic status from facial action units, head pose, and gaze. Cross-corpus binary classification proves more robust than continuous PHQ-8 severity regression, with forward transfer achieving AUC = 0.70. Regression transfer is governed by functional context alignment: passive observation phases yield the most transferable models, while active emotion regulation phases elicit stronger within-corpus signals. These findings establish functional context alignment as the primary determinant of cross-corpus generalization, with passive elicitation contexts offering the best trade-off between within-corpus sensitivity and cross-corpus robustness.

</details>


### [27] [Subject-Relative Micro-Motion and Sleep Dynamics for Near-Infrared Video Sleep Staging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05550)
*Kunmin Jang,You Rim Choi,Hun Heo,Heonjun Lee,Suahn Bae,Dongik Park,Hyun-Woo Shin,Hyung-Sin Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ViNUSS is a framework for NIR video-only sleep staging without physiological proxy reconstruction, achieving 0.80 accuracy and 0.78 macro-F1 on 475 overnight recordings by combining subject-relative micro-motion learning with full-night sleep dynamics modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether NIR video itself can provide informative sleep-stage evidence, rather than only serving as input for recovering physiological proxies. Current methods rely on video as a route to reconstructed respiratory/cardiac signals, but this work investigates if video alone can directly infer sleep stages defined by polysomnography.

Method: ViNUSS combines: (1) Spatially anchored pre-spatial micro-motion encoding preserving localized temporal variation with spatial context; (2) Within-subject stage contrast learning stage cues relative to each subject's night-specific baseline; (3) Two-scale sleep dynamics modeling capturing within-epoch motion evolution and organizing epoch-level evidence into full-night sleep-stage trajectory.

Result: On 475 overnight NIR recordings (~3,250 hours), ViNUSS achieves 0.80 accuracy and 0.78 macro-F1 for four-class sleep staging. Interpretability analysis shows attention to thoraco-abdominal periodic motion and gross body movements associated with arousals and position changes.

Conclusion: NIR video can serve as an independently informative and complementary modality for PSG-defined sleep-stage estimation, providing direct sleep-stage evidence without requiring explicit physiological proxy reconstruction.

Abstract: Near-infrared (NIR) video is a promising modality for contactless sleep monitoring, but recent video-based sleep staging methods often use it as a route to reconstructed respiratory/cardiac proxies or cross-modal physiological representations. We study video-only sleep staging under labels defined by polysomnography (PSG), where the model infers sleep stages from NIR video alone without explicit physiological proxy reconstruction or auxiliary physiological signal supervision. This tests whether NIR video itself can provide informative sleep-stage evidence, rather than only serving as an input for recovering physiological proxies. We propose ViNUSS (Video-Native Unmediated Sleep Staging), a framework that combines subject-relative micro-motion learning with full-night sleep dynamics modeling. Spatially anchored pre-spatial micro-motion encoding preserves localized temporal variation together with its spatial context. Within-subject stage contrast learns stage cues with respect to each subject's night-specific baseline. Two-scale sleep dynamics modeling captures within-epoch motion evolution and organizes epoch-level evidence into a coherent full-night sleep-stage trajectory. On 475 overnight NIR recordings (~3,250 hours), ViNUSS achieves 0.80 accuracy and 0.78 macro-F1 for four-class sleep staging. Interpretability analysis suggests attention to thoraco-abdominal periodic motion and gross body movements associated with arousals and position changes. These results support NIR video as an independently informative and complementary modality for PSG-defined sleep-stage estimation

</details>


### [28] [ViT3Flow: A Test-Time Training Transformer MeanFlow for Postoperative Radiograph Synthesis in Scoliosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05579)
*Rui Tang,Sicheng Yang,Moxin Zhao,Hongqiu Wang,Guankun Wang,Lei Zhu,Hongliang Ren,Menglin Cong,Nan Meng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents ViT³Flow, a novel framework for predicting postoperative spinal morphology from preoperative radiographs for scoliosis surgical planning. It introduces ScoliSurg, the first paired dataset with 632 preoperative-postoperative radiograph pairs, and uses a conditional MeanFlow approach with test-time-training token mixers and diagnosis-routed cross-attention to achieve anatomically faithful synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting postoperative spinal morphology from preoperative radiographs could provide valuable support for scoliosis surgical planning, but remains challenging because surgical correction induces large spatial changes while anatomical structures must be faithfully retained.

Method: The authors propose ViT³Flow, a single-NFE conditional MeanFlow framework that models surgical correction as finite-interval generative transport. It uses test-time-training token mixers for sample-specific inner adaptation to anatomy and deformity patterns. A Spinal Morphology Extraction Agent extracts distributions of dominant-curve region and direction from preoperative radiographs, which guide Diagnosis-Routed Interval Cross-Attention (DRICA) for interval-dependent retrieval from preoperative token streams.

Result: ViT³Flow achieves the best performance among compared methods in perceptual image quality, anatomical fidelity, and clinically relevant geometric accuracy, while requiring only a single network evaluation.

Conclusion: ViT³Flow demonstrates potential for efficient and anatomically faithful postoperative radiograph synthesis in scoliosis surgical planning, offering a promising tool for pre-operative planning.

Abstract: Predicting postoperative spinal morphology from preoperative radiographs could provide valuable support for scoliosis surgical planning, but remains challenging because surgical correction induces large spatial changes while anatomical structures must be faithfully retained. We formulate this problem as postoperative scoliosis radiograph synthesis and construct ScoliSurg, the first paired dataset for this task, comprising 632 preoperative--postoperative whole-spine radiograph pairs with structured morphology information. We further propose ViT$^{3}$Flow, a single-NFE conditional MeanFlow framework for efficient postoperative radiograph synthesis. ViT$^{3}$Flow models surgical correction as finite-interval generative transport and replaces conventional self-attention with test-time-training token mixers that perform sample-specific inner adaptation to the anatomy and deformity pattern of each case. In addition, a Spinal Morphology Extraction Agent extracts distributions of dominant-curve region and direction from the preoperative radiograph. These distributions guide Diagnosis-Routed Interval Cross-Attention (DRICA), which performs interval-dependent vertical, horizontal, joint, and global retrieval from a separate preoperative token stream. This design enables the evolving postoperative representation to incorporate spatially corresponding anatomical evidence throughout the transport process. Extensive experiments on ScoliSurg demonstrate that ViT$^{3}$Flow achieves the best performance among the compared methods in perceptual image quality, anatomical fidelity, and clinically relevant geometric accuracy, while requiring only a single network evaluation. These results highlight the potential of ViT$^{3}$Flow for efficient and anatomically faithful postoperative radiograph synthesis in scoliosis surgical planning.

</details>


### [29] [An overview of 3D Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05583)
*Márcus Lobo,Vitor Matias,Afonso Paiva,Jeová Farias,Tiago Novello,Moacir Ponti*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This abstract describes a tutorial on 3D Vision-Language Models (3D VLMs), which extend CLIP-based methods to align 3D embeddings with pretrained image and text representations, enabling zero-shot classification, cross-modal retrieval, and open-vocabulary recognition of 3D shapes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional 3D deep-learning models are trained for specific tasks (classification, segmentation, detection) and do not support cross-modal retrieval using text or images as queries. There is a need to align 3D embeddings with visual and textual representations to enable more flexible 3D understanding.

Method: The paper reviews CLIP-based methods that align 3D embeddings with pretrained image and text representations, creating 3D VLMs. It covers contrastive learning for multimodal embedding alignment, language-guided 3D Gaussian splatting, 3D shape generation, and embodied AI for robotics.

Result: 3D VLMs support zero-shot classification, cross-modal retrieval, and open-vocabulary recognition of 3D shapes, enabling new capabilities in 3D computer vision.

Conclusion: This tutorial provides a comprehensive overview of 3D VLMs, from basic definitions of 3D representations to modern multimodal frameworks and 3D Vision-Large Language Models (3D VLLMs), highlighting recent advances in the field.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are reshaping computer vision by aligning visual and textual embeddings, allowing models to recognize visual concepts and reason about them using natural language. Traditional 3D deep-learning models, however, are typically trained for specific tasks, such as classification, segmentation, or detection, and do not naturally support cross-modal retrieval from their embedding spaces using text or images as queries. To address this issue, Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP)-based methods align 3D embeddings with pretrained image and text representations, giving rise to 3D Vision-Language Models (3D VLMs) that support zero-shot classification, cross-modal retrieval, and open-vocabulary recognition of 3D shapes. This tutorial provides an overview of 3D VLMs, ranging from basic definitions of 3D representations and their encoding into embeddings to cross-modal contrastive alignment, modern multimodal frameworks, and 3D Vision-Large Language Models (3D VLLMs). We present the main definitions of contrastive learning for multimodal embedding alignment and highlight recent advances in language-guided 3D Gaussian splatting, 3D shape generation, and embodied AI for robotics.

</details>


### [30] [SceneMosaic: Efficient and Diverse Simulation-Ready Scene Generation via Hybrid Agentic Layout Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05594)
*Xingjian Ran,Xiaoye Mo,Sihao Liu,Jianyu Zhang,Li Luo,Bo Dai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SceneMosaic is a framework that combines image-based prior models with VLM agents for efficient and physically valid indoor scene generation. It decomposes scenes into independent local units for evolution before composing the global scene via Cartesian product.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-3D scene generation pipelines either rely on costly iterative VLM-based refinement (high fidelity but expensive) or efficient but imprecise parametric image-to-3D models. Both paradigms struggle to generate diverse scenes for a single input and produce physically invalid scenes.

Method: SceneMosaic combines merits of both paradigms: (1) obtains initial candidates from learned image-based priors for efficiency, (2) evolves results through VLM agents for physical validity. It exploits scene locality by decomposing scenes into independent local units, allowing separate evolution within each unit before composing the global scene via Cartesian product.

Result: On SceneEval-100, SceneMosaic matches the strongest agentic baseline in semantic layout quality with 24x speedup, substantially reduces physical violations, and receives the highest human ratings.

Conclusion: SceneMosaic successfully bridges the gap between efficient image-based priors and accurate VLM-based refinement, achieving both speed and quality in indoor scene generation while maintaining physical validity and diversity.

Abstract: Diverse and simulation-ready indoor scenes are essential for interactive entertainment and embodied AI, yet their scalable generation remains challenging. Recent agentic text-to-3D scene pipelines that rely on vision-language models (VLMs) can generate scenes of high fidelity but require costly iterative object placement and refinement. Another mainstream paradigm, parametric image-to-3D scene models, produces scenes efficiently from strong priors learned from 2D images but often leads to imprecise and physically invalid scenes. More importantly, both paradigms struggle to output diverse scenes for a single input, making it hard for them to reflect the dynamically changing nature of real scenes. In this paper we propose \textbf{SceneMosaic}, a framework that combines the merits of both paradigms. It obtains the initial candidate from the learned image-based prior, and subsequently evolves the result through VLM agents, ensuring both efficiency and physical validity. Within the evolution process, SceneMosaic exploits the locality of natural scenes and decomposes a scene into independent local units, allowing separate evolution within each unit before composing the global scene via Cartesian product. On SceneEval-100, SceneMosaic matches the strongest agentic baseline in semantic layout quality with a 24x speedup, substantially reduces physical violations, and receives the highest human ratings. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/rxjfighting/SceneMosaic.

</details>


### [31] [Time-Aware Assistive Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05596)
*Masaki Kuribayashi,Zhongkai Shangguan,Eshed Ohn-Bar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses a fundamental challenge in multimodal large language models (MLLMs): learning not just what to say but when to say it in real-time assistive navigation. The authors introduce a large-scale benchmark for egocentric assistive navigation in complex outdoor environments, revealing that off-the-shelf MLLMs struggle with time-sensitive instructions even after fine-tuning. A simple modification involving direct supervision to predict the underlying reason for each instruction yields significant performance gains, though challenges remain in temporal reasoning, safety-critical object awareness, and relational/distance understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language models rarely plan over whether and when to provide real-time responses, which is critical for assistive applications like guiding visually impaired individuals through urban environments. Poorly timed responses can distract users or add unnecessary cognitive load, making timing a crucial but understudied aspect of human-AI interaction in time-sensitive scenarios.

Method: The authors create a large-scale multimodal benchmark for egocentric assistive navigation in complex outdoor environments. They evaluate off-the-shelf MLLMs and then test a simple modification: direct supervision to predict the underlying reason for each instruction. Experiments are conducted across open-loop, closed-loop, and sim-to-real generalization settings.

Result: Off-the-shelf MLLMs show fundamental limitations in delivering safe and time-sensitive navigation instructions, even with substantial fine-tuning data. However, the proposed modification (predicting the underlying reason for each instruction) yields significant performance gains across all tested settings. Persistent challenges remain in temporal reasoning, safety-critical object awareness, and relational/distance understanding.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that reasoning about the "when" of communication is essential for safe and effective assistive navigation. The proposed approach improves performance but significant challenges remain in temporal reasoning, safety awareness, and spatial understanding. The authors will release their simulation, benchmark, and code to advance development of scalable assistive agents.

Abstract: Can interactive vision-and-language agents learn not just what to say but also \textbf{\textit{when}} to say it? Current language models rarely plan over whether and when to realize a real-time response to a user. However, providing accurate and timely support for human decision-making, such as when guiding visually impaired individuals through urban environments, requires careful real-time responsiveness--poorly timed responses can distract users or add unnecessary cognitive load. As a machine intelligence challenge for Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM)-based agents, we introduce a large-scale multimodal benchmark for an egocentric, assistive navigation task in complex outdoor environments. Using this benchmark, we uncover a fundamental limitation of off-the-shelf MLLMs in delivering safe and time-sensitive navigation instructions, even with model fine-tuning on substantial amounts of data. We then demonstrate that a simple yet effective modification of the model, including direct supervision to predict the underlying reason for each instruction, yields significant performance gains across open-loop, closed-loop, and sim-to-real generalization settings. However, our analysis highlights persistent challenges in temporal reasoning, safety-critical object awareness, and relational and distance understanding. To advance the development of scalable assistive agents, we will release our simulation, benchmark, and code (available at the project website: https://timeli-icra.github.io/).

</details>


### [32] [Unsupervised Transfer Clustering for Mitigating Cold Start in Active Prompt Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05636)
*André Camargo Portella,Samuel Felipe dos Santos,Jurandy Almeida*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the cold-start problem in Active Prompt Learning (APL) for Vision-Language Models by proposing UTC+SQ, which uses unsupervised transfer clustering with projection-based clustering instead of traditional distance-based clustering to improve sample selection representativeness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cold-start problem in APL methods causes initial query performance to be worse than random sampling. Current state-of-the-art methods rely on rigid, distance-based clustering which struggles to capture complex, high-dimensional semantic distributions inherent to VLMs, leading to suboptimal query representativeness.

Method: The proposed UTC+SQ framework enhances a recent APL approach by leveraging state-of-the-art unsupervised transfer models. These models generate high-fidelity pseudo-labels that establish semantically meaningful clusters, allowing for projection-based clustering instead of distance-based clustering for more relevant sample selection.

Result: Experimental evaluations demonstrate that shifting from distance-based to projection-based clustering improves the representativeness of the queried subset, achieving accuracy gains in 6 of the 8 datasets tested.

Conclusion: The UTC+SQ framework successfully improves zero-shot classification performance for VLMs by using unsupervised transfer clustering, demonstrating that projection-based clustering is more effective than distance-based clustering for sample selection in active prompt learning scenarios.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are able to achieve impressive zero-shot classification performance by aligning visual and textual representations, but each new task still demands handcrafted prompts. Active Prompt Learning (APL) combines Active Learning (AL) and Prompt Learning (PL) into a single framework, allowing for the usage of the VLM prior knowledge for iteratively querying the most informative images to be labeled. However, the cold-start problem is still relevant for APL methods, where the performance of the initial query can be worse than random sampling. While recent state-of-the-art APL methods mitigate with balanced sampling and multimodal features, they rely on rigid, distance-based clustering to group these features. This simplistic approach can struggle to capture the complex, high-dimensional semantic distributions inherent to VLMs, leading to suboptimal query representativeness. Unsupervised transfer can be applied to these features as a possible alternative, since it is capable of inferring the underlying human labeling of a task without any form of supervision. This way, samples can be grouped in semantically coherent clusters. This paper proposes Unsupervised Transfer Clustering with Selective Querying (UTC+SQ), a framework that enhances a recent APL approach by leveraging state-of-the-art unsupervised transfer model. These models generate high-fidelity pseudo-labels that establish semantically meaningful clusters, allowing for the selection of more relevant samples. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that shifting from distance-based to projection-based clustering improves the representativeness of the queried subset, achieving accuracy gains in 6 of the 8 datasets tested.

</details>


### [33] [When Speech Meets Lips: Interpretable Audio-Visual Synchronization for L2 Pronunciation Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06788)
*Bowen Yu,Mingyu Huang,Yishen Liu,Yue Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses a gap in Automatic Pronunciation Assessment (APA) systems by proposing an audio-visual synchronization framework that models speech-lip temporal alignment, rather than relying solely on acoustic signals. The framework introduces lag trajectories and a Lag Stability Index (LSI) to quantify synchronization robustness, validated through user interviews with 30 participants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing APA systems rely only on acoustic signals and overlook temporal synchronization between speech and articulatory movements (lip movements), which limits diagnostic feedback on timing mismatches critical for L2 pronunciation training.

Method: The proposed framework consists of five components: (1) Feature encoding for audio and visual inputs, (2) Cross-attention fusion to combine audio-visual features, (3) Lag estimation to measure temporal alignment, (4) Stability quantification using the Lag Stability Index (LSI), and (5) Visualization of frame-level lag trajectories. The framework was evaluated through interviews with 30 participants (10 instructors and 20 students with diverse L1 backgrounds).

Result: The framework transforms implicit alignment into interpretable representations, connecting automatic scoring with actionable Computer-Aided Pronunciation Training (CAPT) feedback. Specific quantitative results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The audio-visual synchronization framework successfully addresses the limitation of acoustic-only APA systems by explicitly modeling speech-lip temporal alignment, providing interpretable feedback for pronunciation training.

Abstract: Automatic Pronunciation Assessment (APA) systems have achieved strong performance with transformer-based models and self-supervised speech representations. However, most methods rely only on acoustic signals and overlook temporal synchronization between speech and articulatory movements, limiting diagnostic feedback on timing mismatches important for L2 pronunciation training. We propose an interpretable audio-visual synchronization framework that explicitly models speech-lip temporal alignment through feature encoding, cross-attention fusion, lag estimation, stability quantification, and visualization. The framework introduces frame-level lag trajectories and a Lag Stability Index (LSI) to quantify synchronization robustness. We also interviewed 30 participants, including 10 instructors and 20 students with diverse first-language backgrounds, to assess its effectiveness. By transforming implicit alignment into interpretable representations, the framework connects automatic scoring with actionable Computer-Aided Pronunciation Training feedback. Datasets and supplemental materials are available at https://www.robots.ox.ac.uk/~vgg/data/lip_reading/.

</details>


### [34] [Facial Age Estimation for Age Fraud Detection in National ID Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05638)
*Sharib Athar,Arka Koner,Chetan Naik,Barada P. Sabut,Tanusree Deb Barma,Anoop M. Namboodiri,Anil K. Jain*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SwinAge is a facial age estimation system developed for the Aadhaar national identity program to detect age-related fraud during enrollment and updates. Built on SwinFace architecture with landmark-based alignment, it was trained on 1.45 million images and evaluated on 283K independent test images. The system achieves 2.94 years MAE and provides FAR/FRR metrics at three operational thresholds (5, 18, 60 years) to flag suspected cases for manual review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identity fraud during biometric enrollment, particularly age misrepresentation to access age-restricted services or welfare schemes, is a major challenge for large-scale national identity systems like Aadhaar (world's largest with 1.5 billion identities, 22.4M new enrollments and 283M updates annually). There was a need for a system to assist QC operators in flagging potential age-related fraud.

Method: Built upon SwinFace architecture with landmark-based similarity (warp affine) alignment. Trained on a large in-house dataset of 1.45 million face images and evaluated on an independent, age-stratified test set of 283K images, both from an ethnically diverse population of 716K unique subjects. Investigated three Aadhaar-specific operational thresholds (5, 18, and 60 years) and proposed a deployment triage framework that flags suspected cases for manual review. Following NIST FATE guidelines, reported FAR/FRR at each threshold.

Result: At 1% FAR, the model achieves FRR of 3% (<5yrs), 0.4% (>18yrs) and 11.0% (>60yrs). SwinAge achieves a mean absolute error (MAE) of 2.94 years, outperforming three zero-shot vision language models on all benchmarks, and improving state-of-the-art on 5 out of 7 public benchmark datasets. Per-gender errors were also reported.

Conclusion: SwinAge provides an effective solution for detecting age-related fraud in national identity systems. The system successfully balances false acceptance and rejection rates at key operational thresholds, outperforming existing methods. The paper provides valuable lessons for implementing similar age estimation systems in national identity programs, with the triage framework enabling efficient manual review of flagged cases.

Abstract: Identity fraud during biometric enrollment and updates remains a major challenge for large-scale national identity systems. A common fraud vector is misrepresenting one's age to access age-restricted services or welfare schemes. In this work, we present SwinAge, a facial age estimation system designed for use within the Aadhaar biometric enrollment pipeline, to assist quality-check (QC) operators to flag potential age-related fraud. This is critical for a system like Aadhaar (the world's largest national identity programme), that holds about 1.5 billion unique identities, with 22.4 million new enrollments and 283 million updates in the last year. Building upon the SwinFace architecture with landmark-based similarity (warp affine) alignment, we train on a large in-house dataset of 1.45 million face images and evaluate on an independent, age-stratified test set of 283K images, both drawn from an ethnically diverse population of 716K unique subjects. We investigate three Aadhaar-specific operational thresholds (5, 18, and 60 years) and propose a deployment triage framework that flags suspected cases for manual review. Following NIST FATE, we report false acceptance/rejection rates (FAR/FRR) at each threshold rather than aggregate accuracy: at 1% FAR the model achieves an FRR of 3% (<5yrs), 0.4% (>18yrs) and 11.0% (>60yrs). SwinAge achieves a mean absolute error (MAE) of 2.94 years on the same test set, outperforming three zero-shot vision language models on all benchmarks, and improving the state-of-the-art on 5 out of 7 public benchmark datasets. We further report per-gender errors and distill lessons for national identity programs.

</details>


### [35] [Full-Page Optical Music Recognition of Handwritten Monophonic Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05662)
*Adrian Rosello,Antonio Ríos-Vila,David Rizo,Jorge Calvo-Zaragoza*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses full-page end-to-end Optical Music Recognition (OMR) for handwritten monophonic music, exploring how synthetic pretraining impacts performance on handwritten scores. The study introduces a synthetic data generator capable of producing both typeset and handwritten-style full-page scores, and evaluates multiple full-page pipelines on three real handwritten datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional OMR pipelines rely on accurate staff segmentation, which limits their effectiveness. While Transformer-based architectures have achieved strong results on typeset scores using large-scale synthetic data, their applicability to handwritten music remains largely unexplored. This work aims to fill this gap by systematically studying full-page transcription on handwritten collections and understanding the role of synthetic pretraining.

Method: The authors introduce a synthetic score generator capable of producing visually coherent full-page scores in both typeset and handwritten styles. They conduct comparative experiments evaluating several full-page pipelines and different synthetic pretraining strategies on three real handwritten datasets, focusing on understanding which factors during pretraining are most relevant.

Result: The experiments reveal that the benefits of synthetic pretraining are primarily associated with learning structural layout conventions (e.g., staff positioning, notation spacing, system layout) rather than achieving visual similarity to the target handwriting style.

Conclusion: Synthetic pretraining for handwritten OMR is effective mainly because it teaches the model structural layout conventions of music notation, not because it mimics handwriting visual characteristics. This suggests that for handwritten music transcription, emphasis should be placed on learning layout structure rather than visual style matching during pretraining.

Abstract: Full-page end-to-end Optical Music Recognition seeks to transcribe entire music pages directly into symbolic notation, avoiding the limitations of traditional pipelines that rely on accurate staff segmentation. Recent Transformer-based architectures have achieved strong performance on typeset scores, relying on large-scale synthetic data for pretraining. However, their applicability to handwritten music remains largely unexplored. In this work, we study full-page transcription on handwritten monophonic collections and analyze the impact of synthetic pretraining in this setting. To investigate which factors are most relevant during pretraining, we introduce a generator capable of producing visually coherent full-page scores in both typeset and handwritten styles. Experiments on three real handwritten datasets provide a comparative evaluation of several full-page pipelines and different synthetic pretraining strategies. The results suggest that the benefits of synthetic pretraining are primarily associated with learning structural layout conventions rather than with visual similarity to the target handwriting.

</details>


### [36] [Bigger Text Encoders Can Hurt CLIP Zero-Shot Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05730)
*Samir Char,Carles Domingo-Enrich,Randall Balestriero*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates how the capacity split between vision and text encoders affects CLIP model performance. The researchers discovered that scaling text encoders beyond an optimal size actually degrades zero-shot performance, even with increased total parameters. They propose using modality-specific weight decay to recover and improve performance, and identify embedding uniformity and cross-modal alignment as predictive metrics for zero-shot performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior CLIP scaling laws treat total model size as a single variable, without exploring how capacity allocation between vision and text encoders impacts downstream performance. This gap limits efficient resource allocation in large-scale CLIP training.

Method: The researchers trained multiple CLIP models with varying vision and text encoder sizes. They analyzed performance degradation using geometric metrics (embedding uniformity and cross-modal alignment) and tested modality-specific weight decay coefficients as a solution.

Result: The study revealed an optimal text encoder size for most vision encoders, beyond which zero-shot performance degrades despite increased total parameters. This degradation stems from overfitting caused by oversized text encoders. Using modality-specific weight decay coefficients recovered and improved performance across all degraded configurations. Efficient configurations matching ViT-B/16 performance were achieved with up to 55% fewer parameters. Cross-modal alignment and embedding uniformity were found to be predictive metrics of zero-shot performance.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that CLIP scaling requires careful consideration of encoder capacity allocation rather than just total parameter count. Modality-specific training techniques can counteract performance degradation. These insights provide a foundation for more efficient and reliable CLIP architecture design and training methods.

Abstract: Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) is a building block of many machine learning applications. Scaling laws have guided resource allocation for large-scale training, yet prior work treats total CLIP model size as a single variable, without exploring how the capacity split between encoders impacts downstream performance. Here, we train multiple CLIP models with different vision and text encoder sizes, revealing that for most vision encoders, there is an optimal text encoder size beyond which zero-shot performance degrades---even as total parameter count increases. Exploiting this behavior yields efficient configurations that match the zero-shot performance of the standard ViT-B/16 architecture with up to 55% fewer parameters. We further show that this degradation stems from overfitting induced by the oversized text encoder, and that using modality-specific weight decay coefficients not only recovers but improves performance across all degraded configurations. A geometric analysis reveals a trade-off in which scaling the text encoder improves embedding uniformity but worsens cross-modal alignment; we further show that these metrics are predictive of zero-shot performance. We hope these findings motivate CLIP architectures and training methods that counteract this degradation, a prerequisite for scaling CLIP reliably and efficiently.

</details>


### [37] [SeRV: Semantic-Aligned Residual Vector Quantization for American Sign Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05742)
*Hongyu Wu,Xu Wu,Tianhao Wu,Jiawei Yu,Phuc Nguyen,Jian Liu,Yi Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SeRV is a novel semantic-aligned residual vector quantization tokenizer for ASL generation that addresses limited paired text-ASL motion data and reconstruction-predictability trade-offs by combining sentence-level motion-text alignment with token-level text-conditioned supervision, achieving state-of-the-art pose accuracy on 375 hours of ASL video across How2Sign and YouTube-ASL datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: American Sign Language (ASL) generation is challenging due to (1) limited paired text-ASL motion data and (2) the difficulty of learning motion representations that are both precise for reconstruction and predictable from linguistic input. Existing methods rely on motion tokenizers optimized for reconstruction without explicit semantic supervision from paired text, resulting in tokens that fail to support semantically consistent and fine-grained ASL motion generation.

Method: SeRV (Semantic-Aligned Residual Vector Quantization) learns a semantically structured residual token space by combining sentence-level motion-text alignment with token-level text-conditioned supervision. A Hierarchical GPT then predicts residual motion tokens in a coarse-to-fine manner. Additionally, a large-scale reconstructed 3D ASL motion-text benchmark was constructed by recovering paired 3D motion from YouTube-ASL videos.

Result: SeRV achieves state-of-the-art pose accuracy on both How2Sign and YouTube-ASL datasets. It produces semantically consistent 3D ASL motion directly from text across 375 hours of ASL video.

Conclusion: SeRV successfully addresses the limitations of existing ASL generation methods by introducing semantic-aligned residual vector quantization, enabling structurally coherent and semantically aligned 3D ASL motion generation from text input.

Abstract: American Sign Language (ASL) generation remains challenging due to limited paired text-ASL motion data and the difficulty of learning motion representations both precise for reconstruction and predictable from linguistic input. Existing methods rely on motion tokenizers optimized for reconstruction, without explicit semantic supervision from paired text. As a result, the learned tokens remain limited in supporting semantically consistent and fine-grained ASL motion generation. To address this limitation, we propose SeRV (Semantic-Aligned Residual Vector Quantization), a semantic-aligned RVQ tokenizer for ASL generation. SeRV learns a semantically structured residual token space by combining sentence-level motion-text alignment with token-level text-conditioned supervision. Building on this tokenizer, a Hierarchical GPT predicts residual motion tokens in a coarse-to-fine manner, generating structurally coherent and semantically aligned 3D ASL motion. We further construct a large-scale reconstructed 3D ASL motion-text benchmark by recovering paired 3D motion from YouTube-ASL videos. Experiments across 375 hours of ASL video show that SeRV achieves state-of-the-art pose accuracy on both How2Sign and YouTube-ASL datasets, while producing semantically consistent 3D ASL motion directly from text.

</details>


### [38] [Gaussian Linear Functional Manifold Method for Massive Point Cloud Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05744)
*Hong Zhao,Tonglin Zhang,Baijian Yang,Jin Wei-Kocsis,Songlin Fei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Gaussian Linear Functional Manifold (GLFM), a physics-informed statistical framework for reconstructing continuous terrain manifolds from massive airborne LiDAR point clouds in Wildland-Urban Interface environments. It uses deterministic linear functional bases for surface topography and isotropic Gaussian process for laser backscatter modeling. A linear-time SVD rank-reduction algorithm enables efficient parameter estimation. The method achieves 0.9933 ARI on 35.2 km² of real data, outperforming four baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address challenges in reconstructing terrain from unstructured LiDAR point clouds in complex WUI environments: deep neural networks require expensive point-wise annotations, while nonparametric methods lack structural interpretability. There is a need for a scalable, interpretable, and annotation-free approach.

Method: GLFM combines deterministic linear functional bases (for continuous surface topography) with isotropic Gaussian process modeling (for microscale diffuse laser backscatter). To achieve computational efficiency, an algebraic SVD rank-reduction algorithm enables linear-time parameter estimation and closed-form quadric classification, avoiding the quadratic cost of exact constrained maximum likelihood estimation.

Result: Evaluated on 35.2 km² of real-world aerial LiDAR data, GLFM automatically filters ground points and extracts morphological features, achieving an adjusted Rand index (ARI) of 0.9933 against field-verified ground truth. It outperforms four leading baselines while maintaining an out-of-core memory footprint (scalable to large datasets).

Conclusion: GLFM provides a rigorous, interpretable, and scalable foundation for large-scale point cloud analytics in WUI environments. It successfully balances accuracy (0.9933 ARI), computational efficiency (linear-time), and memory efficiency (out-of-core), making it suitable for practical deployment on massive LiDAR datasets.

Abstract: Reconstructing continuous terrain manifolds from massive, unstructured airborne LiDAR point clouds remains challenging in complex Wildland-Urban Interface (WUI) environments, where deep neural networks require costly point-wise annotations and nonparametric surface reconstruction methods often lack structural interpretability. This paper introduces the Gaussian Linear Functional Manifold (GLFM), a physics-informed statistical framework that represents continuous surface topography using deterministic linear functional bases while modeling microscale diffuse laser backscatter as an isotropic Gaussian process. To avoid the quadratic computational cost of exact constrained maximum likelihood estimation, we develop an algebraic singular value decomposition (SVD) rank-reduction algorithm that enables linear-time parameter estimation and closed-form quadric classification. Evaluated on 35.2 km^2 of real-world aerial LiDAR data, GLFM automatically filters ground points and extracts morphological features, achieving an adjusted Rand index (ARI) of 0.9933 against field-verified ground truth and outperforming four leading baselines while maintaining an out-of-core memory footprint. The framework provides a rigorous, interpretable, and scalable foundation for large-scale point cloud analytics.

</details>


### [39] [GeoContext: One Context Ladder, Two Failure Modes in Vision-Language Geolocation: Flat Reliance on User-Provided Location Context and False Confirmation of Location Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05761)
*Yifan Zhang,Kai Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoContext introduces two new geolocation tasks: GeoHint (localization given coarse location hint) and GeoVerify (binary verification within 150m). The benchmark covers 109 sites in 30 cities with 21,933 GeoHint and 6,270 GeoVerify responses, evaluating five vision-language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visual geolocation benchmarks ask models to identify where an image was captured without considering location context that users typically provide. This limits real-world applicability.

Method: Constructs a "context ladder" by stratifying nearby reference points according to distance and referenceability, keeping the image fixed while varying supplied context.

Result: Three main patterns: (1) Hint repetition varies ≤3% across conditions but localization error increases with hint distance; (2) Behavior depends on no-context performance—at low-accuracy sites median error/hint-distance ratio ≈1.00, at high-accuracy sites 0.24-0.69; after bias correction only 1/5 models retains negative accuracy with nearby hints; (3) In GeoVerify, no model achieves d'=1 for decoys beyond 150m, rankings change when sensitivity separated from bias, and 83.8% of false acceptances have confidence ≥0.8.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals significant limitations in current vision-language models for geolocation tasks, particularly in handling location context and verification accuracy. Resources released: benchmark, construction pipeline, audit decisions, and scoring code.

Abstract: Visual geolocation benchmarks typically ask a model where an image was captured without accounting for the location context that users often provide. We introduce GeoContext, a resource supporting two complementary tasks: GeoHint, open-ended localization given a true but coarse location hint, and GeoVerify, binary verification of whether an image was taken within 150 m of a claimed place. GeoContext constructs a context ladder by stratifying nearby reference points according to distance and referenceability, allowing the image to remain fixed while the supplied context varies. The benchmark covers 109 sites in 30 cities and evaluates five vision-language models using 21,933 GeoHint responses and 6,270 GeoVerify responses.
  Our evaluation reveals three main patterns. First, hint repetition varies by only 1.5 percentage points across referenceability tiers and by less than 3 points across distance bands, while the resulting localization error increases steadily with hint distance. Second, behavior depends strongly on no-context performance: at sites with low no-context accuracy, the median ratio between localization error and hint distance is approximately 1.00, whereas at higher-accuracy sites it ranges from 0.24 to 0.69. After correcting for bias introduced by the site grouping procedure, only one of the five models retains a negative accuracy estimate when given a nearby hint. Third, in GeoVerify, no model reaches d' = 1 for decoys immediately beyond the 150 m tolerance. Model rankings also change when sensitivity is separated from response bias, and 83.8% of false acceptances are reported with confidence of at least 0.8. We release the benchmark, construction pipeline, audit decisions, and scoring code.

</details>


### [40] [AAMBERS-UAV: Acquisition-Aware Multimodal Backbone Evaluation and Ranking for UAV Weedy Rice Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05762)
*Tarek Rahman,Nazim-E-Alam,Md Kishor Morol,Jannatun Noor*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical evaluation flaw in UAV semantic segmentation benchmarks: standard image-level splitting allows samples from the same acquisition flight to appear in both training and testing sets, obscuring true transfer performance to new surveys. The authors propose acquisition-aware evaluation protocols and demonstrate that exposure to target acquisition data significantly affects measured performance and modality rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current semantic segmentation benchmarks for UAV imagery split data at the image level, ignoring the spatial and temporal relationships within acquisition sequences. This practice can inflate performance metrics by placing samples from the same survey in both training and testing, failing to reflect true model generalization to genuinely new aerial surveys.

Method: The authors used the WeedyRice-RGBMS-DB dataset (734 samples) with a fixed 124-image target acquisition test set. They compared two protocols: (1) target-held-out (excluding target acquisition from development) and (2) target-exposed (including remaining target acquisition images). SegFormer-B0 was evaluated with RGB, 4-band multispectral (MS), and 7-channel RGB+MS inputs across two fixed-split seeds. A U-Net/ResNet18 replication was also conducted. Additional analyses included corruption tests examining RGB-MS displacement and radiometric scaling sensitivity.

Result: Under target-held-out protocol, RGB achieved the highest performance (0.7317 ± 0.0201 IoU). Under target-exposed protocol, RGB+MS became strongest (0.7822 ± 0.0269). The U-Net/ResNet18 replication confirmed positive exposure gains for all inputs but retained RGB as best modality under both protocols. Acquisition exposure increased measured performance across both backbones, while modality ranking effects were architecture-dependent. Strong near-sequential dependence was found, and early fusion showed substantially higher sensitivity to RGB-MS displacement than to moderate radiometric scaling.

Conclusion: Acquisition-aware same-test evaluation is a necessary complement to ordinary image-level splitting in multimodal UAV benchmarks. The study reveals that exposure to target acquisition data significantly impacts performance metrics and modality rankings, with effects varying by backbone architecture. Current benchmarks may overestimate real-world generalization by not properly accounting for acquisition-level dependencies.

Abstract: UAV image collections contain spatially and temporally related frames, yet semantic-segmentation benchmarks commonly split them at image level. Such splitting can place samples from one acquisition in both model development and testing, obscuring transfer to a genuinely new survey. Using the 734-sample WeedyRice-RGBMS-DB, we fix a 124-image target-acquisition test set and compare two protocols with identical train, validation, and test counts: target-held-out, which excludes the target acquisition from development, and target-exposed, which admits its remaining images. SegFormer-B0 is evaluated with RGB, four-band multispectral (MS), and seven-channel RGB+MS input over two fixed-split seeds. RGB is strongest under complete acquisition holdout ($0.7317\pm0.0201$ IoU), whereas RGB+MS becomes strongest after target exposure ($0.7822\pm0.0269$). A fixed-split U-Net/ResNet18 replication confirms positive exposure gains for all three inputs, but retains RGB as the best modality under both protocols. Acquisition exposure therefore increases measured performance across both evaluated backbones, while its effect on modality ranking is architecture-dependent. A supplied-split audit reveals strong near-sequential dependence, and corruption tests show that early fusion is substantially more sensitive to RGB--MS displacement than to moderate radiometric scaling. These results support acquisition-aware same-test evaluation as a necessary complement to ordinary image-level splitting in multimodal UAV benchmarks. The code and supporting the findings of this study will be publicly released upon acceptance of the paper.

</details>


### [41] [MolParser-Mobile: Ultrafast OCSR System for Large-Scale Chemical Literature Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807)
*Xi Fang,Haocheng Lu,Han Lyu,Chengxiang Luo,Linfeng Zhang,Guolin Ke*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MolParser-Mobile is an AutoML-optimized lightweight end-to-end OCSR framework with only 9.98M parameters, achieving 1,520 molecules per second throughput on NVIDIA RTX 4090D GPU while maintaining competitive/superior recognition accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in deep learning-based OCSR accuracy, inference throughput remains a critical bottleneck limiting web-scale deployment.

Method: MolParser-Mobile uses AutoML optimization to create a lightweight end-to-end framework for Optical Chemical Structure Recognition.

Result: Achieves throughput of 1,520 molecules per second on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090D GPU with only 9.98M parameters, while maintaining competitive and superior recognition accuracy on several benchmarks.

Conclusion: MolParser-Mobile successfully addresses the throughput bottleneck in OCSR for web-scale deployment by providing a compact yet accurate solution.

Abstract: Optical Chemical Structure Recognition (OCSR) is a fundamental component of chemical literature mining, enabling molecular database construction, reaction extraction, and AI-driven scientific discovery. Despite substantial progress in recognition accuracy with recent deep learning-based methods, inference throughput remains a critical bottleneck that limits web-scale deployment. To address this challenge, we propose MolParser-Mobile, an AutoML-optimized lightweight end-to-end OCSR framework. MolParser-Mobile contains only 9.98M parameters, while reaching a throughput of 1,520 molecules per second on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090D GPU. Despite its compact design, it maintains competitive and, on several benchmarks, superior recognition accuracy.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [42] [AhaBench: Do Agents Learn from Prior Experience? A Benchmark for Long-Horizon Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05435)
*Zerui Cheng,Jiawei Xu,Huacan Chai,Jiayang Sun,Pramod Viswanath,Maxm Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AhaBench is a benchmark suite that evaluates whether language agents improve their behavior after receiving useful experience, testing this under conditions where obvious support has been removed, changed, or delayed. It consists of three components: Aha-Puzzle (no-hint exploration after solved puzzles), Aha-Euler (mathematical taught/held-out tasks), and Aha-Vending (simulated vending with delayed feedback). The benchmark uses a three-part scorecard measuring Initial Score, Post-Experience Score, and Learning Lift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current evaluations reset agents after a prompt or score only the final state of one trajectory. The authors argue this fails to capture whether agents actually learn and improve from experience over long horizons when support is later removed or delayed. They seek to answer: when a fixed model receives useful experience, does its later behavior improve under related evaluation conditions?

Method: The benchmark contains three components: (1) Aha-Puzzle tests no-hint exploration after solved hidden-state puzzles; (2) Aha-Euler turns Project-Euler-style mathematical problems into generated taught/held-out tasks with exact validators; (3) Aha-Vending (inspired by Vending-Bench) tests simulated vending agents handling delayed feedback and operational incidents. The three-part scorecard measures: Initial Score (starting competence), Post-Experience Score (later empirical outcome), and Learning Lift (their difference).

Result: On an eight-model panel, Claude Opus 4.6 leads in aggregate Post-Experience Score (64.3) and aggregate Learning Lift (+25.8), with Gemini 3.1 Pro close behind (63.4). Key findings: puzzle traces raise supported scores but often fail to become no-hint exploration behavior; Aha-Euler full teaching reaches 78.6-100.0% while answer-only transfer ranges from 0.0 to 73.9%; Aha-Vending separates profitable incident handling from bankruptcy and no-order failure. The main empirical message is that models excelling in different scorecard dimensions are not always the same.

Conclusion: The decomposition into three scorecard parts (Initial Score, Post-Experience Score, Learning Lift) is the main contribution. Models that use visible support well, reach high post-experience scores, and improve most during a run are not always the same. This reveals the importance of evaluating all three dimensions separately. The authors release benchmark tasks, rubrics, validators, simulator code, and interfaces for evaluating new agents.

Abstract: Modern language agents are expected to operate over long horizons: they ask follow-up questions, reuse worked examples, handle tool feedback, and adapt to delayed consequences. Most evaluations still reset the agent after a prompt or score only the final state of one trajectory. AhaBench asks a more operational question: when a fixed model receives useful experience, does its later behavior improve under a related evaluation condition where the obvious support has been removed, changed, or delayed? The suite contains three components. Aha-Puzzle tests no-hint exploration after solved hidden-state puzzles; Aha-Euler turns Project-Euler-style mathematical ideas into generated taught/held-out tasks with exact validators; and Aha-Vending, an open-source implementation inspired by Vending-Bench, tests whether a simulated vending agent remains profitable while handling delayed feedback and operational incidents. AhaBench reports a three-part scorecard: Initial Score measures starting competence, Post-Experience Score measures the later empirical outcome, and Learning Lift is their difference. This decomposition is the main empirical message: models that use visible support well, models that reach high post-experience scores, and models that improve most during a run are not always the same. On the common eight-model panel, Claude Opus 4.6 leads aggregate Post-Experience Score at 64.3 and aggregate Learning Lift at +25.8, with Gemini 3.1 Pro close behind at 63.4. The component results explain the split: puzzle traces raise supported scores but often fail to become no-hint exploration behavior; Aha-Euler full teaching reaches 78.6-100.0% while answer-only transfer ranges from 0.0 to 73.9%; and Aha-Vending separates profitable incident handling from bankruptcy and no-order failure. We release benchmark tasks, rubrics, validators, simulator code, and interfaces for evaluating new agents.

</details>


### [43] [When Do Options Help? Policy Necrosis and Redundant Coverage in Option-Critic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05508)
*Bingyun Liu,Yuheng Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the option-critic architecture in reinforcement learning, revealing three fundamental problems: (1) the learned termination rule contributes nothing and can actually harm exploration, (2) policies suffer from "necrosis" where states lock onto first actions, and (3) performance gains from multiple options come from reduced joint failure probability rather than improved individual options.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to explain why option-critic performance improves with added options, despite theoretical concerns about its termination learning mechanism.

Method: The paper employs theoretical analysis and experimental validation to examine option-critic's termination learning, intra-option policy behavior, and the effect of multiple options on performance.

Result: Three key findings: (1) When termination test and option-picking policy read the same values, termination fires every step; when they differ, the rule can block exploration, causing Ω(T) regret vs O(log T) for always-terminating. (2) "Policy necrosis" affects ~60% of states in typical options, where states lock onto first action and never update. (3) Extra options don't improve individual options but reduce joint failure probability from 59% to 4%, explaining performance gains.

Conclusion: Option-critic's improvements from multiple options stem from reduced joint failure probability, not improved option quality. The learned termination rule is essentially useless, and policy necrosis is a major issue. Simple fixes like forcing termination at every step or restoring exploration can significantly improve performance.

Abstract: Option-critic learns options: sub-policies together with a learned rule for when each one hands control back. Its headline result is that performance improves as options are added. We explain that result, with theory and experiment. First, the termination rule option-critic learns by maximising return contributes nothing. When the termination test and the policy that picks options read the same values, the test fires at every step, so the learned rule is identical to always terminating. When that policy explores and the test does not, as in option-critic itself, the rule can block the exploration; there are instances where it suffers $Ω(T)$ regret while always terminating holds to $O(\log T)$. Forcing termination at every step leaves the option-count curve intact. Second, the policy inside an option barely explores at all, so a state locks onto the first action that looked good and never updates again. We name this policy necrosis, give a state-level test for it, and find three fifths of states necrotic in a typical option. Restoring exploration repairs those states, and one option then solves the task. Third, extra options improve no option; what falls is the chance that all of them fail in the same state, from $59\\%$ to $4\\%$, and performance follows that joint quantity.

</details>


### [44] [Multi-granularity Adaptive Hypergraph Representation Learning via Granular-ball](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05574)
*Sen Zhao,Yifan Guan,Jinyuan Ni,Gaojie Xu,Zhang Xu,Xiaoyu Lian,Yi Liu,Yi Wang,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MGHRL is a novel hypergraph representation learning framework that addresses limitations of predefined hyperedge methods by adaptively generating hyperedges at multiple granularities through granular-ball splitting and integrating features via hierarchical reversible connections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior hypergraph representation learning methods rely on predefined hyperedge definitions, failing to adapt to diverse graph topological structures and multi-granularity characteristics of hyperedges, thus limiting their ability to effectively discover high-order relationships.

Method: 1) Adaptive Granular Hypergraph Generation strategy: generates hyperedges at multiple granularity levels through adaptive splitting of granular-balls, capturing high-order relationships based on graph topology. 2) Multi-Granularity Hypergraph Network: multiple sub-networks capture features from hyperedges at different granularities, integrated via hierarchical reversible connections.

Result: MGHRL significantly outperforms baseline models on benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed MGHRL framework successfully addresses the limitations of predefined hyperedge methods by adaptively discovering high-order relationships at multiple granularities, demonstrating superior performance on benchmark datasets.

Abstract: Hypergraph representation learning aims to capture high-order information in graphs by constructing hyperedges that simultaneously connect multiple nodes. These hyperedges adapt to the graph's topological features, facilitating the extraction of high-order relationships at multiple granularities. Most prior work relies on predefined definitions to generate hyperedges, overlooking the diversity in graph topological structures and the multi-granularity characteristics of hyperedges. As a result, this limits their ability to effectively and adaptively discover high-order relationships and efficiently process complex structural information. To address this limitation, we propose a novel framework called \underline{M}ulti-\underline{G}ranularity \underline{H}ypergraph \underline{R}epresentation \underline{L}earning (MGHRL). MGHRL introduces an Adaptive Granular Hypergraph Generation strategy, which generates hyperedges at multiple levels of granularity through the adaptive splitting of granular-ball, effectively capturing high-order relationships based on the graph's topological structure. Additionally, we propose a Multi-Granularity Hypergraph Network with multiple sub-networks, capturing features from hyperedges at different granularities and integrating them via hierarchical reversible connections. Experimental results show that MGHRL significantly outperforms baseline models on benchmark datasets.

</details>


### [45] [Capsule Lens: Locating and Tracking Concept Geometry in Model Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05575)
*Yiming Tang,Harshvardhan Saini,Samyak Jha,Huaming Chen,Xufeng Duan,Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Capsule Lens, a framework for analyzing how concepts are encoded in machine learning model representations by matching concept regions with geometric capsules. The method is applied to both static and dynamic representations, revealing different geometric dynamics across various training scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central problem in mechanistic interpretability is understanding how concepts are encoded in neural network internal representations. Existing approaches mainly map representations to interpretable spaces without directly characterizing how concepts occupy representation space. Current hypotheses lack rigorous validation and focus primarily on static representations.

Method: Capsule Lens framework: matches concept regions with geometric capsules (simple, trackable geometric forms) defined by interpretable parameters. These capsules are fitted in closed form to each concept's geometry and validated on held-out samples. Applied to both static representations (analyzing span and norm curves) and dynamic representations (tracking representation drifts).

Result: Three case studies on dynamic representations: (1) CLIP pretraining shows broad network-wide restructuring; (2) RL post-training on visual question answering; (3) RL post-training on mathematical reasoning shows localized, concept-specific changes. Results include both findings aligned with existing literature and novel observations about concept geometry dynamics.

Conclusion: Capsule Lens is a promising tool for locating, analyzing, and tracking concept geometry in both static and dynamic representations. It enables rigorous characterization of how concepts occupy representation space across different model architectures and training paradigms.

Abstract: Understanding how concepts are encoded in the internal representations of machine learning models is a central problem in mechanistic interpretability, essential both for the science of deep learning and for the trustworthy deployment of increasingly capable models. Existing approaches to interpret model representations mainly map representations onto more interpretable spaces and do not directly characterize how concepts occupy representation space; various hypotheses have been proposed, but often lack of rigorous validation and largely focus on static representations. In this work, we introduce Capsule Lens, a framework that matches the region a concept occupies with a simple, trackable geometric form, a capsule, defined by several interpretable parameters, fitted in closed form to each concept's geometry and validated on held-out samples. We apply Capsule Lens in two major settings: static and dynamic representations. On static representations, we demonstrate how to locate concept geometry across various models, and how the span and norm curves uncover important geometric characteristics. On dynamic representations, we present three case studies tracking representation drifts induced by distinct training settings, CLIP pretraining, RL post-training on visual question answering, and RL post-training on mathematical reasoning. These analyses reveal qualitatively different geometric dynamics, ranging from broad network-wide restructuring in CLIP pretraining to localized and concept-specific changes in RL post-training. Our results include findings aligned with existing literature as well as novel observations. We believe Capsule Lens stands as a promising tool for locating, analyzing, and tracking concept geometry in both static and dynamic representations.

</details>


### [46] [HB-PVI: A Hierarchical Bayesian Personalization and Value-of-Information Framework for Complex Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05582)
*Hammed A. Olayinka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study introduces HB-PVI, a hierarchical Bayesian framework for activity recognition that jointly models participant heterogeneity, personalization benefits/harms, and the economic value of calibration labels. The framework uses value-of-information reasoning to decide whether to acquire additional labels or use population inference, demonstrating that population-first deployment is often optimal when personalization gains are small relative to costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalization can improve activity-recognition performance but benefits are heterogeneous across participants, and each calibration label has acquisition cost. The study addresses the need to determine when personalization is economically worthwhile versus when population inference suffices.

Method: HB-PVI combines hierarchical Bayesian modeling with value-of-information analysis. It uses a leakage-safe, leave-one-participant-out evaluation with sequential-Monte-Carlo participant-effect updates, a Student-t hierarchical gain model, and one-step expected-value-of-sample-information (EVSI) stopping rule. Four personalization mechanisms were tested: adapter, adapter-plus-head, and prototype-residual personalization.

Result: Adapter personalization showed small positive mean F1 gains (0.00099 at one label to 0.00198 at ten). The other two mechanisms were negative on average. Under the primary practical-benefit threshold (Δmin=0.01), one-step EVSI was zero at every decision state, resulting in no label purchases and retention of population inference for all 47 participants. This reduced labeling by 100% while keeping posterior mean F1 loss at 0.00217 (95% CI: 0.00048 to 0.00389), with 0.9992 probability of remaining below 0.005 tolerance. HB-PVI was utility-optimal in 199 of 216 cost-threshold settings.

Conclusion: A population-first deployment policy is recommended when personalization gains are small relative to labeling, computation, and harm costs. Value-of-information reasoning, rather than raw predictive accuracy, should drive personalization decisions in health-sensing applications.

Abstract: Personalization can improve activity-recognition performance, but participant-specific gains are heterogeneous, and every additional calibration label has an acquisition cost. This study presents HB-PVI, a hierarchical Bayesian personalization and value-of-information framework jointly modeling participant heterogeneity, the benefit and harm of four personalization mechanisms, and the economic value of an additional label, for the 47-participant MUSIC-CAR complex-activity cohort. A leakage-safe, leave-one-participant-out evaluation combines a sequential-Monte-Carlo participant-effect updater with a Student-$t$ hierarchical gain model and a one-step expected-value-of-sample-information (EVSI) stopping rule. Adapter personalization produced small positive mean F1 gains, growing from 0.00099 at one label to 0.00198 at ten, while adapter-plus-head and prototype-residual personalization were negative on average. Under the primary practical-benefit threshold ($Δ_{\min}=0.01$) and cost setting, one-step EVSI was zero at every decision state, so the policy purchased no labels and retained population inference for all 47 participants, matching always-stop exactly (region-of-practical-equivalence probability $=1$). Relative to fixed ten-shot adapter personalization, this reduced labeling by 100\% while keeping the posterior mean F1 loss at 0.00217 (95\% credible interval, 0.00048 to 0.00389), with posterior probability 0.9992 of remaining below the 0.005 tolerance. HB-PVI was utility-optimal in 199 of 216 cost-threshold settings and in every setting at or above the primary label cost. These results argue for a population-first deployment policy whenever personalization gains are small relative to labeling, computation, and harm costs, and show that value-of-information reasoning, not raw predictive accuracy, should drive personalization decisions in health-sensing applications.

</details>


### [47] [Endogenous Exploration in Reinforcement Learning with Intrinsic Curiosity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05650)
*Armando Vieira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reinforcement learning framework using intrinsic curiosity to drive exploration in non-stationary environments with sparse/absent rewards. The method uses an epistemic motivation mechanism combined with a Liquid State Machine (LSM) substrate, achieving competitive performance on LunarLanderv2 and BipedalWalkerv3 compared to PPO and ICM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address challenges in RL where environments are non-stationary and rewards are sparse, delayed, uninformative, or absent - situations where traditional exploration strategies fail.

Method: Novel framework combining external rewards with an epistemic motivation mechanism that biases agents toward structured exploratory directions, implemented on a Liquid State Machine (LSM) substrate.

Result: Achieves competitive performance on both discrete-action (LunarLanderv2) and continuous-control (BipedalWalkerv3) tasks relative to PPO and ICM. The "curiosity window" (effective exploration at intermediate incoherence) is not observed in Active Inference agents.

Conclusion: The proposed dynamics capture a distinct exploration regime not found in Active Inference, supporting the hypothesis that effective exploration requires intermediate levels of environmental incoherence - not too rigid nor too disordered.

Abstract: We propose a reinforcement learning framework in which exploration is driven by intrinsic curiosity, designed for scenarios where environments are non-stationary and rewards are sparse, delayed, uninformative, or absent. In our model, action selection is guided by a combination of external rewards and an epistemic motivation mechanism that biases the agent toward structured exploratory directions. The central hypothesis is that effective exploration emerges at intermediate levels of incoherence, while performance degrades under both overly rigid and overly disordered dynamics. To test this idea, we implement the framework on top of a Liquid State Machine (LSM) substrate and evaluate it on two standard benchmarks: the discrete-action LunarLanderv2 and the continuous-control BipedalWalkerv3. The proposed method achieves competitive performance on both tasks relative to established deep RL algorithms, including Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and Intrinsic Curiosity Module (ICM). We further show that the curiosity window is not recovered in Active Inference agents under the same analysis, suggesting that the proposed dynamics capture a distinct exploration regime

</details>


### [48] [Robustness of LLM-Generated SystemVerilog Assertions to Semantics-Preserving RTL Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05658)
*FNU Aditi*

Main category: cs.LG
