TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLM-based SystemVerilog Assertion (SVA) generation using metamorphic testing under semantics-preserving RTL transformations. The study reveals that 9.7%-27.0% of correct outputs become incorrect after transformations like operand reordering, identifier renaming, and parenthesization, demonstrating that point accuracy alone is insufficient for assessing LLM reliability in assertion generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLM-based SVA generation only report correctness on a single syntactic representation (point accuracy), which fails to reveal whether correct outputs remain stable when the same RTL behavior is expressed differently. This limitation masks substantial instability in model outputs.

Method: Controlled metamorphic evaluation using the VERT dataset. A quality-filtered conditional-control pool and stratified 40-program evaluation set (295 assignment behaviors) were constructed. Two open code models (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Lite) were evaluated with identical prompts and greedy decoding. Three semantics-preserving transformations were applied: operand reordering, deterministic identifier renaming, and redundant parenthesization. Metrics include baseline/transformed accuracy, conditional robustness, invariance failure, any-flip rate, with 10,000-sample clustered bootstrap intervals.

Result: Across all six model-transformation conditions, 9.7%-27.0% of behaviors correct on original RTL become incorrect after transformation. Under identifier renaming, DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Lite improves from 53.9% to 63.7% accuracy while 19.5% of originally correct behaviors fail. Manual review of 30 sampled correct-to-wrong transitions identified: dropped path predicates, branch-polarity errors, Boolean-structure corruption, and output-contract violations.

Conclusion: Point accuracy alone is insufficient for characterizing LLM reliability in assertion generation. Aggregate accuracy can hide substantial instability. The results motivate the need for robustness-aware evaluation frameworks for AI-assisted hardware verification.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored for automating SystemVerilog Assertion (SVA) generation, yet most evaluations report correctness on a single syntactic representation of an input. Such point accuracy does not reveal whether a model's correct output is stable when the same RTL behavior is written differently. This paper presents a controlled metamorphic evaluation of LLM-based SVA generation under semantics-preserving RTL transformations. Starting from the VERT dataset, we construct a quality-filtered conditional-control pool and a stratified 40-program evaluation set containing 295 assignment behaviors. We evaluate two open code models, Qwen2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Lite, with an identical evaluation prompt and greedy decoding. Three transformations are studied: operand reordering, deterministic identifier renaming, and redundant parenthesization. Beyond baseline and transformed accuracy, we measure conditional robustness, invariance failure, and any-flip rate, with 10,000-sample clustered bootstrap intervals at the RTL-program level. Across all six model-transformation conditions, 9.7%-27.0% of behaviors that were correct on the original RTL become incorrect after a semantics-preserving transformation. Aggregate accuracy can therefore hide substantial instability: under identifier renaming, DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Lite improves from 53.9% to 63.7% accuracy while 19.5% of its originally correct behaviors fail. Manual review of 30 sampled correct-to-wrong transitions identifies dropped path predicates, branch-polarity errors, Boolean-structure corruption, and output-contract violations. The results show that point accuracy alone is insufficient for characterizing LLM reliability in assertion generation and motivate robustness-aware evaluation for AI-assisted hardware verification.

</details>


### [49] [PAC-Private Autoregressive Generation: Calibrating Noise to Ensemble Disagreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05676)
*Mina Mirzadehsarcheshmeh,Amir Keyvan Khandani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends PAC (Probably Approximately Correct) privacy to autoregressive text generation, addressing privacy leakage in language models served via APIs. The method uses m=128 overlapping worlds from a private corpus, with voting mechanisms and posterior-weighted disagreement to calibrate noise. On WikiText-103 with GPT-2-small, they retain 74% of fine-tuning gain at per-token budget of 2^-32, outperforming PMixED.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models adapted on private text are served through APIs, creating privacy leakage through generated outputs rather than exposed weights. Existing methods like PMixED incur privacy cost at each release and increasingly rely on public models over time. PAC privacy offers an alternative by calibrating noise to output variability, adding less noise when predictions are stable. However, PAC-private prediction hadn't been extended from classification to autoregressive generation before this work.

Method: The authors construct m=128 overlapping worlds from the private corpus, with each record appearing in exactly m/2 worlds. They train one adapter per world over a frozen public model, where the realized world is the secret. At each token, the public model defines a candidate set, worlds vote, and posterior-weighted disagreement determines PAC noise. They introduce coupled decoding that preserves privacy accounting while avoiding greedy degeneration. They prove I(S;Y_{1:T}) ≤ I(S;H_T) ≤ bT.

Result: On WikiText-103 with GPT-2-small: (1) Retained 74% of fine-tuning gain at per-token budget of 2^-32; (2) Membership-inference success bounded by 51.08% after 10^6 tokens; (3) Posterior-entropy estimates of leakage roughly 17% of charged budget; (4) Against PMixED, retained 98% of non-private headroom from 10^2 to 10^6 tokens (vs at most 56% for PMixED), with no crossover.

Conclusion: The paper successfully extends PAC privacy to autoregressive generation, handles adaptive self-generated contexts, and introduces coupled decoding that preserves privacy accounting. The method significantly outperforms PMixED across various token generation lengths while maintaining strong privacy guarantees. Even when membership advantage on memorized canaries is indistinguishable from zero, the canary is emitted at the same rate, indicating inference privacy is not content protection.

Abstract: Language models adapted on private text are often served through APIs, so privacy leakage occurs through generated outputs rather than exposed weights. Private prediction protects these releases. Methods such as PMixED incur privacy cost at each release and increasingly rely on the public model over long horizons. PAC privacy instead calibrates noise to output variability across possible secrets, adding less noise when predictions are stable. To our knowledge, PAC-private prediction has not previously been extended from classification to autoregressive generation.
  We construct $m=128$ overlapping worlds from the private corpus, with each record appearing in exactly $m/2$ worlds, and train one adapter per world over a frozen public model. The realized world is the secret. At each token, the public model defines a candidate set, the worlds vote, and their posterior-weighted disagreement determines the PAC noise; unanimity requires no calibration noise. We prove $I(S;Y_{1:T}) \leq I(S;H_T) \leq bT$. Our contributions are extending PAC privacy to autoregressive generation, handling adaptive self-generated contexts, and introducing coupled decoding that preserves privacy accounting while avoiding greedy degeneration.
  On WikiText-103 with GPT-2-small, we retain 74% of the fine-tuning gain at a per-token budget of $2^{-32}$, while membership-inference success is bounded by 51.08% after $10^6$ tokens; posterior-entropy estimates of leakage are roughly 17% of the charged budget. Inference privacy is not content protection: even when membership advantage on a memorized canary is indistinguishable from zero, the canary is emitted at the same rate. Against PMixED under matched membership-inference bounds on the same data universe and test set, we retain 98% of non-private headroom from $10^2$ to $10^6$ tokens, versus at most 56%, with no crossover.

</details>


### [50] [Connecting Score Matching, Maximum Likelihood, and Expectation-Maximization in Mixed Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05688)
*Zhankun Luo,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes variance-preserving diffusion for mixed linear regression with unknown mixing weights, connecting score matching to KL divergence and maximum likelihood estimation, with theoretical guarantees and EM-based gradient analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how variance-preserving diffusion affects parameter estimation in mixed linear regression (MLR) where mixing weights are unknown. The authors aim to separate the statistical guarantees of score matching from loss geometry and optimization dynamics at fixed diffusion noise levels.

Method: The authors use KL divergence to link the denoising score matching objective (integrated over diffusion path) with likelihood and terminal discrepancy. They derive a decomposition connecting score matching loss to cross-entropy and EM operators, yielding an EM-related low-noise gradient expansion with latent variance correction terms. They analyze gradient descent on the limiting loss under isotropic covariance in the high-noise limit.

Result: Under mild regularity conditions and terminal schedule, the estimator converges to the ground truth parameters of MLR, and its scaled error converges to the Gaussian limit of the MLE. At high signal-to-noise ratio rays, both the score matching imbalance gradient and latent-variance term tend to zero pointwise.

Conclusion: The paper provides theoretical guarantees connecting score matching-based diffusion methods to classical MLE for mixed linear regression, with the decomposition revealing EM-like behavior and correction terms. The findings are validated through numerical experiments.

Abstract: We study variance-preserving diffusion of the response in mixed linear regression (MLR) with unknown mixing weights. Our analysis separates the statistical guarantees of score matching from the loss geometry and optimization signal at a fixed diffusion noise level. The KL divergence links the denoising score matching objective integrated over the diffusion path with the likelihood and a terminal discrepancy. Under mild regularity conditions and terminal schedule, the resulting estimator converges up to the ground truth parameters of MLR, and its scaled error converges to the Gaussian limit of the maximum-likelihood estimator. At a fixed scale of the diffusion noise level, we derive a decomposition linking the score matching loss to cross-entropy and Expectation-Maximization (EM) operators. This decomposition yields an EM-related low-noise gradient expansion with additional correction terms of latent variance. In the high-noise limit, we further characterize gradient descent on this limiting loss under isotropic covariance. Along fixed high signal-to-noise ratio rays, the score matching imbalance gradient and the latent-variance term tend to zero pointwise. Numerical experiments illustrate our theoretical findings and statistical guarantees.

</details>


### [51] [GraphNOSE: A Graph Transformer in Olfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05694)
*Mrityunjay Sharma,Sarabeshwar Balaji,Valentina Parma,Ritesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GraphNOSE is an open-source graph transformer framework that predicts multi-label odor descriptors from SMILES strings for single molecules and binary mixtures. It uses positional and structural encodings within a transformer-based architecture, achieving 84% AUROC on out-of-distribution compounds while using six times fewer parameters than standard GNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current linear models for predicting olfactory qualities from molecular structure fail when extrapolating to novel chemical scaffolds, extreme molecular weights, or complex odor mixtures. There is a need for a more scalable and generalizable approach.

Method: GraphNOSE integrates positional and structural encodings within a transformer-based graph architecture. It takes SMILES strings as input for both single molecules and binary mixtures.

Result: GraphNOSE achieves: (1) 6x fewer parameters than standard GNN baselines; (2) 4.52% higher AUROC than linear models, molecular language models, molecular fingerprints, and baseline GNNs (p < 0.01); (3) 84% AUROC on OOD compounds, outperforming Open-POM (81%, p < 0.001); (4) XAI methods identify odor-driving substructures consistent with chemical intuition.

Conclusion: GraphNOSE establishes itself as a scalable and interpretable architecture for olfactory prediction that generalizes to structurally distinct compounds underrepresented in current perceptual databases.

Abstract: Predicting olfactory qualities from molecular structure is an open problem in chemoinformatics. Although linear models can link molecular features to odor descriptors, they often fail when extrapolating to novel chemical scaffolds, extreme molecular weights, or complex odor mixtures. To address this, we introduce GraphNOSE, an open-source graph transformer framework that predicts multi-label odor descriptors from simplified molecular-input line-entry system (SMILES) strings for single molecules and binary mixtures. By integrating positional and structural encodings within a transformer-based graph architecture, GraphNOSE achieves strong performance with six times fewer parameters than standard graph neural network (GNN) baseline while consistently outperforming linear models, molecular language model embeddings, molecular fingerprints, and baseline GNNs by an average area under the ROC curve (AUROC) margin of 4.52% (p < 0.01). GraphNOSE achieves an AUROC of 84% on out-of-distribution compounds (OODs). This exceeds the current state-of-the-art GNN for OOD in olfaction (Open-POM: 81%, p < 0.001), and identifies conditions under which linear models empirically fail. Finally, we apply XAI (explainable AI) methods to identify which substructures and molecular features drive odor predictions, yielding insights consistent with chemical intuition and grounded in the model's learned representations. Together, these results establish GraphNOSE as a scalable and interpretable architecture for olfactory prediction that generalizes to structurally distinct compounds underrepresented in current perceptual databases.

</details>


### [52] [Analysis of Respiratory Sinus Arrhythmia with Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05698)
*Julian Szymanski,Patryk Orkisz,Higinio Mora*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning approach that predicts respiratory waveforms directly from ECG signals by leveraging Respiratory Sinus Arrhythmia (RSA), eliminating the need for manual feature extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-invasive respiratory monitoring is important for healthcare and wearable technology applications, but traditional methods require manual preprocessing and feature extraction, which can be time-consuming and less scalable.

Method: Three different neural network architectures were developed and evaluated to automatically extract relevant features from ECG signals and predict respiratory waveforms without manual preprocessing.

Result: The paper reports successful prediction of respiratory waveforms from ECG input using deep learning models, demonstrating the feasibility of the proposed approach.

Conclusion: The neural network-based method provides a robust and scalable solution for non-invasive respiratory monitoring, with potential applications in healthcare and wearable technology.

Abstract: The paper introduces a neural network-based approach for analyzing ECG signals to estimate respiratory rate by leveraging the phe- nomenon of Respiratory Sinus Arrhythmia (RSA). Our method employs a deep learning model trained to predict respiratory waveforms directly from ECG input data. To achieve this, we developed and evaluated three different neural network architectures capable of automatically extract- ing relevant features from ECG signals without the need for manual preprocessing. The proposed approach offers a robust and scalable solu- tion for non-invasive respiratory monitoring, with potential applications in healthcare and wearable technology

</details>


### [53] [Newton Matching for Generative Modeling: A Unified Framework for Fine-Tuning and Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05727)
*Zeyang Li,Yunan Wang,Paolo Giaretta,Navid Azizan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Newton Matching is a unified framework that combines fine-tuning and sampling in generative modeling through iterative optimization over canonical models. It connects reverse-KL divergence with Fisher-Rao geometry, showing that the Newton direction equals the negative Fisher-Rao gradient. The method provides strict reverse-KL descent, global convergence, and local quadratic convergence properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of bridging isolated loss functions with iterative optimization in generative modeling. The authors aim to develop a unified framework that can handle both fine-tuning (where μ is the pretrained model's terminal density) and sampling (where μ = 1) under a single coherent mathematical structure, moving beyond paradigm-specific approaches.

Method: The method involves: (1) Shifting from isolated losses to iterative optimization over canonical models (population minimizers of standard conditional matching for terminal densities); (2) Transporting the Fisher-Rao metric and mixture connection to the density manifold; (3) Using a canonical retraction that yields exact finite-stepsize density characterization; (4) Constructing sample-wise tangential-update losses via covariance and gradient forms with forward or reverse regression-pair constructions.

Result: Key results include: (1) The reverse-KL Hessian equals the Fisher-Rao metric, so Newton direction coincides with negative Fisher-Rao gradient; (2) Strict reverse-KL descent away from target for 0 < η ≤ τ; (3) Global convergence under mild conditions; (4) Local quadratic convergence for full steps (η = τ); (5) The method recovers representative methods as exact realizations, critical-point-consistent approximations, or objective-altering variants.

Conclusion: The work provides a theoretically grounded framework that advances the understanding of reinforcement learning for generative models. By connecting differential geometry (Fisher-Rao metric) with optimization theory (Newton's method), it enables modular algorithm design and offers rigorous convergence guarantees. The framework's ability to recover existing methods as special cases demonstrates its practical utility.

Abstract: We develop Newton Matching, a unified framework for fine-tuning and sampling in generative modeling. The target is $π\proptoμe^{τr}$, where $r$ is the reward, $τ>0$ the inverse temperature, and $μ$ denotes the pretrained model's terminal density for fine-tuning or the constant $1$ for sampling. We shift the paradigm from isolated losses to iterative optimization over canonical models: population minimizers of standard conditional matching for terminal densities. Under compatible smooth-realization assumptions, canonical velocities form a manifold diffeomorphic to the density manifold. Transporting the Fisher-Rao metric and mixture connection to this manifold, we show that the reverse-KL Hessian equals the metric, so the Newton direction coincides with the negative Fisher-Rao gradient. At terminal density $ρ$, each stage takes a tangential step generated by the regularized reward $r-\frac1τ\log(ρ/μ)$, followed by terminal-density-preserving canonicalization. This canonical retraction yields an exact finite-stepsize density characterization. For the ideal iteration, we prove strict reverse-KL descent away from the target for $0 < η\le τ$, global convergence under mild conditions, and local quadratic convergence for full steps ($η=τ$). Covariance and gradient forms, each with forward or reverse regression-pair constructions, yield sample-wise tangential-update losses with the same population minimizer, without importance sampling or full-trajectory backpropagation. We develop approximate updates and define critical-point consistency as vanishing tangential displacement if and only if $ρ=π$. We recover representative methods as exact realizations, critical-point-consistent approximations, or objective-altering variants, enabling modular algorithm design. Our work advances the theory and algorithms of reinforcement learning for generative models.

</details>


### [54] [A Multi-Source Ensemble Approach to Candidate Generation for Alternative Vacation Rental Property Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05748)
*Syed Mohammed Arshad Zaidi,Eric Rincon,Shayan Hassantabar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a comprehensive study of candidate generation (CG) approaches for vacation rental alternatives, comparing collaborative filtering, shallow embeddings, and GNN methods. A hybrid architecture combining item-based CF with GNN-based retrieval achieves 14.8% Recall@300 improvement over the strongest baseline, with GNN embeddings alone outperforming shallow Hotel2Vec by 48-68%. The study also examines how CG gains propagate to downstream ranking, identifying a "recall-conversion gap" that practitioners must consider.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vacation rental marketplaces need effective alternative property recommendations to help users discover relevant options. However, this domain presents unique challenges including heterogeneous inventory, geographic constraints, rapid availability changes, and long-tail property distributions, making high-quality candidate generation particularly difficult.

Method: The study compares three classes of candidate generation approaches: (1) item-based collaborative filtering, (2) shallow embeddings (Hotel2Vec), and (3) graph neural network (GNN) methods. Experiments were conducted on a large-scale platform with over 2M active properties. A hybrid architecture combining collaborative filtering with GNN-based retrieval was also evaluated. The study examines both retrieval-stage metrics and downstream ranking quality to understand how CG gains carry through the system.

Result: The hybrid architecture combining item-based CF with GNN-based retrieval achieved 14.8% improvement in Recall@300 over the strongest baseline. GNN-based embeddings alone substantially outperformed shallow Hotel2Vec embeddings, with 48-68% relative recall improvement across different K values. The study found that a stronger candidate pool yields higher downstream ranking quality, though attributing this effect cleanly is complicated by the coupling between candidate generation and ranker training.

Conclusion: Combining collaborative filtering with GNN-based retrieval leverages complementary strengths: CF excels at early recall for properties with rich interaction history, while GNNs discover diverse, non-obvious alternatives and handle cold-start scenarios more effectively. The "recall-conversion gap" is an important consideration for practitioners deploying new retrieval methods in two-stage recommendation systems, as CG-stage gains don't automatically translate to proportional improvements in downstream metrics.

Abstract: Alternative property recommendations play a critical role in vacation rental marketplaces, helping users discover relevant options when viewing a specific listing. However, generating high-quality candidate alternatives presents unique challenges: heterogeneous inventory, geographic constraints, rapid availability changes, and long-tail property distributions. We present a comprehensive study of candidate generation (CG) approaches for vacation rental alternatives, comparing collaborative filtering, shallow embeddings, and graph neural network (GNN) methods.
  Our experiments on a large-scale vacation rental platform (over 2M active properties) show that a hybrid architecture combining item-based collaborative filtering with GNN-based retrieval improves Recall@300 by 14.8% over the strongest baseline, by leveraging the complementary strengths of the two sources: collaborative filtering excels at early recall for properties with rich interaction history, while GNNs discover diverse, non-obvious alternatives and handle cold-start scenarios more effectively. As a component result, GNN-based embeddings alone substantially outperform shallow Hotel2Vec embeddings (48-68% relative recall improvement across K), motivating their inclusion in the ensemble.
  Crucially, we examine how CG-stage gains carry through to the downstream ranking stage, and find that a stronger candidate pool yields higher downstream ranking quality, though attributing this effect cleanly is complicated by the coupling between candidate generation and ranker training. This recall-conversion gap is an important consideration for practitioners deploying new retrieval methods in two-stage recommendation systems.

</details>


### [55] [Geodesic-informed Generative Diffusion Model For Topology-preserved Image Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08153)
*Nian Wu,Nivetha Jayakumar,Jiarui Xing,Miaomiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IGG (Image Generation informed by Geodesic dynamics) is a novel framework that integrates topology-preserving geodesic principles into diffusion-based generative models, enabling geometrically consistent and interpretable shape synthesis by learning deformations as smooth, invertible mappings in geodesic space rather than direct image intensity space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative diffusion models have two key limitations: (1) they rely primarily on image intensity/texture without considering underlying object geometry, thus lacking geometric/topological consistency crucial for high-stakes domains like computational anatomy, biology, and robotics; (2) they fail to explicitly learn or represent shape changes, leaving deformation dynamics uninterpretable and physically uninformed.

Method: IGG operates in geodesic deformation spaces instead of conventional image intensity space. It learns and synthesizes diverse samples as smooth and invertible smooth mappings from a given template/source image, integrating topology-preserving geodesic principles into the diffusion-based generative process.

Result: The paper presents IGG framework that enables topology-preserving generation with geometric consistency. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/nellie689/IGG.

Conclusion: IGG addresses the fundamental limitations of existing generative diffusion models by explicitly learning geometric object changes as interpretable geodesic deformations, making the transformation process both physically informed and topologically consistent—critical for applications in computational anatomy, biology, and robotics where preserving object structure is essential.

Abstract: Generative diffusion models have emerged as a class of powerful techniques for various imaging applications, including but not limited to synthesis, reconstruction, and segmentation. Despite their success, current generative models pose two key limitations. First, they primarily rely on image intensity and texture information, with limited attention to underlying object geometry. As a result, they do not guarantee geometric or topological consistency during the generation process, which is a crucial requirement for high-stakes domains such as computational anatomy, biology, and robotics, where preserving object structure is critical. Second, existing models fail to explicitly learn or represent shape changes in the generative process. Such deformation dynamics remain occluded within network parameters; hence leaving the transformation process uninterpretable and physically uninformed. To address these challenges, we introduce IGG (Image Generation informed by Geodesic dynamics), a novel framework that integrates topology-preserving geodesic principles into the diffusion-based generative process. In contrast to conventional methods that operate in image intensity space, IGG learns and synthesizes diverse samples within geodesic deformation spaces, where geometric object changes are learned as smooth and invertible smooth mappings from a given template/source image. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/nellie689/IGG.

</details>


### [56] [Data Scout: Targeted Web Crawling for Domain-Specific Pretraining Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05766)
*Chirag Garg,Eelaaf Zahid,Farhan Ahmed,Jay Pankaj Gala,Eric Butler,Heiko Ludwig*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Data Scout is a novel approach for building domain-specific pretraining corpora that uses targeted crawling instead of filtering web archives. It uses an LLM to generate search queries, groups returned URLs by subdomain, and screens them with a user-supplied classifier to identify relevant content efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current dominant approach of filtering large web archives like CommonCrawl works well for popular domains but fails for specialized domains where relevant content is sparse and not captured by popularity-driven crawlers. There is a need for a more effective method to build domain-specific pretraining corpora for specialized fields.

Method: Data Scout inverts the filtering approach by directing a targeted crawl. The method involves: (1) using an LLM to expand a root topic into a taxonomy and thousands of search queries; (2) grouping returned URLs by subdomain; (3) screening subdomains with a user-supplied classifier (probe) based on small samples. This works because relevance has a sharp boundary at the subdomain level.

Result: With the FineMath classifier as probe, 21.9% of crawled pages are high-quality math content, which is 70x the 0.31% rate from filtering a comparable web sample. Additionally, 63.2% of these pages are missing from CommonCrawl yet equally useful for training. Continued pretraining of Llama-3.2-3B on 1.9B Data Scout tokens matches FineMath corpus performance on GSM8k.

Conclusion: Data Scout significantly outperforms traditional filtering methods for building domain-specific corpora, achieving both higher efficiency (70x improvement) and better coverage (63.2% of content missing from CommonCrawl). Since the probe is the only domain-specific component, Data Scout can in principle apply to any domain with an appropriate classifier, making it a versatile solution for specialized domain corpus construction.

Abstract: The dominant approach to building domain-specific pretraining corpora is to filter large web archives such as CommonCrawl. This works well for popular domains but breaks down for specialized ones, where relevant content is sparse and often beyond the reach of popularity-driven crawlers. We present Data Scout, which inverts this: instead of filtering an archive, it directs a targeted crawl. An LLM expands a root topic into a taxonomy and thousands of search queries; the returned URLs (seeds) are grouped by subdomain and screened with a user-supplied classifier (the probe), admitting each subdomain on the basis of a small sample. This works because relevance has a sharp boundary at the subdomain level: in mathematics, a page is 21x more likely to be relevant than one on a sibling subdomain. With the FineMath classifier as the probe, 21.9% of crawled pages are high-quality math content, 70x the 0.31% rate from filtering a comparable web sample, so the crawl wastes far less effort. But the payoff is not just efficiency: 63.2% of these pages are missing from CommonCrawl altogether, yet just as useful for training. Continued pretraining of Llama-3.2-3B on 1.9B Data Scout tokens matches FineMath corpus on GSM8k. Because the probe is the only domain-specific component, Data Scout can in principle apply to any domain with such a classifier.

</details>


### [57] [RAPTOR: Role-Aware Private Training for Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05770)
*Duc Dm,Khai Le-Duc,Nguyen Do,Minh Son Hoang,Florent Draye,Thai Hoang,Hoang Phuong Dam,Jiarui Liu,Chris Ngo,Terry Jingchen Zhang,Anh Le Duc Tran,Nhat Do Minh,Minh Ngoc Le,My T. Thai,Ran Xu,Silvio Savarese,Mona Diab,Bernhard Schölkopf,Zhijing Jin,Huy L. Nguyen,Daeyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses differentially private (DP) fine-tuning of sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, which conventionally treat MoE as dense blocks. The authors identify three failure modes: global clipping suppressing expert gradients, batch normalization diluting sparse expert updates, and fixed privacy noise degrading signal-to-noise ratio on low-load experts. They propose RAPTOR, a Role-Aware Private Training framework that uses expert-specific clipping/noise, a public expected-owner denominator, and count-independent update schedule. The mechanism achieves (ε,δ)-DP with efficient composition - updating all E experts costs no more than updating one. Experiments on Switch Transformer, OLMoE, and DeepSeek-VL2-Tiny across GLUE tasks show consistent gains over DP baselines, especially at tight privacy budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard DP fine-tuning methods treat sparse MoE models as single dense blocks, ignoring the architectural distinction between shared layers (seeing all data) and expert layers (only seeing routed records). This leads to three critical failure modes that degrade both privacy and utility.

Method: The authors propose RAPTOR framework with four key innovations: (1) Expert-specific clipping and noise instead of global treatment; (2) Public expected-owner denominator for privacy accounting; (3) Count-independent update schedule avoiding conditioning on private expert counts; (4) Alternating shared and expert optimization. The DP guarantee relies on per-expert mechanisms composing in parallel within a layer, while shared and expert streams compose sequentially across training.

Result: Experiments on Switch Transformer and OLMoE fine-tuning across GLUE tasks, and on DeepSeek-VL2-Tiny, show consistent gains over standard DP baselines across several privacy levels (ε), with largest margins at tightest budgets. A bias-variance decomposition of the public-denominator estimator shows bias grows predictably with routing imbalance, yielding a privacy-free rule for layer selection using routing entropy measured on a small public corpus.

Conclusion: RAPTOR successfully addresses the three identified failure modes in DP fine-tuning of sparse MoE models. The key insight is that per-expert mechanisms compose in parallel (costing no more privacy budget than updating one expert), enabling efficient private training. The framework provides theoretical DP guarantees while achieving superior utility, particularly at tight privacy budgets. Code and models are publicly available.

Abstract: Differentially private (DP) fine-tuning methods treat sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models as a single dense block, ignoring that shared layers see all data while experts only see routed records. We identify and formally characterize three resulting failure modes: global clipping suppresses expert gradients, batch-level normalization dilutes sparse expert updates, and fixed privacy noise degrades signal-to-noise ratio on low-load experts. We introduce RAPTOR - a Role-Aware Private Training framework, which alternates shared and expert optimization and targets each failure directly, using expert-specific clipping and noise together with a public expected-owner denominator and a count-independent update schedule that avoids conditioning on private, realized expert counts. We prove the resulting mechanism satisfies $(\varepsilon,δ)$-DP: because each record is assigned to exactly one owner expert, per-expert mechanisms within a layer compose in parallel, so updating all $E$ experts costs no more, in privacy terms, than updating one, with shared and expert streams composing sequentially across training. We further derive a bias-variance decomposition of the public-denominator estimator showing its bias grows predictably with routing imbalance, yielding a privacy-free rule for selecting which layer to protect from routing entropy measured on a small public corpus. Experiments on Switch Transformer and OLMoE fine-tuning across GLUE tasks, and on DeepSeek-VL2-Tiny, show consistent gains over standard DP baselines across several privacy levels ($\varepsilon$), with the largest margins typically at the tightest budgets. Code and models are publicly available: https://github.com/leduckhai/RAPTOR

</details>


### [58] [Nonlinear elliptic homogenization with the parametric Deep Ritz method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05778)
*Conor Rowan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using the Deep Ritz method to solve parametric cell problems in nonlinear homogenization, providing a differentiable representation of cell responses that accelerates macroscale simulations compared to traditional FE² approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In nonlinear homogenization, the homogenized constitutive relation depends on the macroscopic state/gradient, requiring solutions to parametric cell problems. Computing these derivatives in Newton iterations is computationally expensive. The authors seek a differentiable representation of cell problem solutions to enable efficient numerical computations.

Method: The authors use the Deep Ritz method, exploiting the variational structure of the cell problem. They discretize the dependence of cell response on both space and macroscopic state using a neural network, enforcing boundary conditions strongly. The parametric Deep Ritz method solves the cell problem simultaneously over a range of macroscopic states.

Result: The method provides an accurate, efficient, and continuously differentiable representation of the cell response over the macroscopic state and gradient. When integrated into macroscale simulations, it significantly expedites solutions compared to traditional FE² schemes.

Conclusion: The parametric Deep Ritz method is a promising approach for nonlinear homogenization, offering a tractable way to obtain differentiable homogenized constitutive relations that greatly improve computational efficiency in multiscale simulations.

Abstract: Elliptic homogenization is used to determine coarse-grained properties of materials with features on small scales. When these small scale features have rapid, periodic fluctuations, the solution field corresponding to a homogenized constitutive relation closely resembles the true solution based on the heterogeneous material. This homogenized behavior of the material is computed from a cell problem, where a cell is defined to be one period of the fluctuating material. In the context of linear elliptic partial differential equations, the homogenized constitutive relation is defined simply by a constant coefficient tensor, but for nonlinear problems, the homogenized response depends on the macroscopic state and/or its gradient, thus requiring solutions to parametric cell problems. When computing a numerical solution with the homogenized constitutive relation, it is useful to have a differentiable representation of the solution to the cell problem, as derivatives of the homogenized constitutive relation are required in Newton iterations for the macroscopic state field. In this work, we use the Deep Ritz method to solve the parametric cell problems that arise from nonlinear homogenization. First, we exploit the variational structure of the cell problem, then we discretize the dependence of the cell response on both space and the macroscopic state with a neural network. Enforcing boundary conditions on the cell response strongly, we next use the parametric Deep Ritz method to simultaneously solve the cell problem over a range of macroscopic states. We show that this method is accurate, efficient, and offers a continuous and differentiable representation of the cell response over the macroscopic state and gradient. We then show that our parametric representation of the cell response significantly expedites macroscale solutions when compared to a traditional $\text{FE}^2$ scheme.

</details>


### [59] [Online Learning with LLM Experts from Limited Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05820)
*Wang Wei,Soumyabrata Pal,Koyel Mukherjee,Franck Dernoncourt,Ryan A. Rossi,Branislav Kveton,Hoda Eldardiry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies adaptive prompt routing to LLM experts as a bandit problem, proposing algorithms that achieve near-optimal regret bounds (Õ(dT/√m) for full-information and Õ(dT√(K/m)) for bandit settings) with limited feedback, demonstrating effective routing strategy learning across diverse LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem addresses efficient routing of prompts to large language model (LLM) experts in an online setting where feedback is limited. The goal is to maximize response quality while minimizing the number of feedback observations needed to learn high-quality routing strategies.

Method: The paper formulates prompt routing to LLM experts as a bandit problem with K actions (experts) and d features (prompts) over T rounds. Algorithms are proposed that strategically select and observe rewards to minimize regret, with separate analysis for full-information and bandit feedback settings.

Result: The proposed algorithms achieve regret bounds of Õ(dT/√m) in the full-information setting and Õ(dT√(K/m)) in the bandit setting, where m << T is the feedback budget. Experiments demonstrate effective learning of high-quality routing strategies across diverse LLMs from limited feedback.

Conclusion: The proposed algorithms achieve regret bounds that scale favorably with problem parameters. The full-information setting achieves Õ(dT/√m) and the bandit setting achieves Õ(dT√(K/m)). Experiments demonstrate effective learning of routing strategies across diverse LLMs with limited feedback.

Abstract: We study adaptive routing of prompts to large language model (LLM) experts to maximize response quality in an online setting with limited feedback. We formulate it as a bandit problem with $K$ actions that represent experts and $d$ features that encode prompts, over a horizon of $T$ rounds. We propose algorithms that strategically select and observe rewards to minimize regret. In the full-information setting, we achieve a regret of $\tilde{O}(d T / \sqrt{m})$, while in the bandit setting we achieve $\tilde{O}(d T \sqrt{K / m})$, where $m \ll T$ is a budget on feedback. Our experiments show that we efficiently learn high-quality routing strategies across diverse LLMs from limited feedback.

</details>


### [60] [Generalizing HVAC Control With Domain Randomized Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05822)
*Pablo Boitel,Kun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NOMAD-RL is a reinforcement learning controller for HVAC systems that uses physics-informed normalizing flows for adaptive domain randomization, enabling effective transfer across heterogeneous thermal zones without per-site retuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying advanced HVAC controllers at scale is challenging because performance depends on accurate building models or per-site retuning. There is a need for a general-purpose controller that can transfer across heterogeneous thermal zones through a non-invasive interface.

Method: NOMAD-RL uses a recurrent policy for online adaptation under partial observability, acting on temperature setpoints from zone measurements and forecasts. The key innovation is an adaptive domain randomization scheme based on physics-informed normalizing flows that models correlated and multimodal distributions of thermal-zone parameters while maintaining physical plausibility.

Result: NOMAD-RL consistently outperforms PID and non-randomized RL baselines, and approaches the performance of a well-tuned MPC, especially in the more challenging multi-zone case.

Conclusion: Adaptive, physics-informed domain randomization shows significant potential for robust and transferable HVAC control, offering a promising approach to deploy advanced controllers at scale without extensive per-site tuning.

Abstract: Deploying advanced HVAC (Heating, Ventilation and Air Conditioning) controllers at scale remains difficult because performance often depends on accurate building models or per-site retuning. We propose NOMAD-RL (Neural Online Meta-Adaptation for Dynamics), a general-purpose Reinforcement Learning (RL) controller designed to transfer across heterogeneous thermal zones through a universal, non-invasive thermostat interface. The controller acts on temperature setpoints from zone measurements and forecasts, while a recurrent policy supports online adaptation under partial observability.
  Our main contribution is an adaptive domain randomization scheme based on physics-informed normalizing flows, which models correlated and multimodal distributions of thermal-zone parameters while maintaining physical plausibility and controllability. This produces a realistic and progressively adaptive training curriculum that improves transfer across buildings. We evaluate NOMAD-RL against a constant-setpoint PID controller, RL without domain randomization, and MPC in single- and multi-zone settings. NOMAD-RL consistently outperforms the PID and non-randomized RL baselines, and approaches the performance of a well-tuned MPC, especially in the more challenging multi-zone case. These results highlight the potential of adaptive, physics-informed domain randomization for robust and transferable HVAC control.

</details>


### [61] [Scaling Optimal Classification Trees via Adaptive Feature and Sample Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05826)
*Jiancheng Tu,Wenqi Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the computational inefficiency of dynamic programming for optimal classification trees by proposing two methods: Weighted STreeD (which merges duplicate records to reduce sample-dependent computation) and Adaptive STreeD (which iteratively refines candidate feature sets). The approach achieves speedups up to 121x while maintaining comparable predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic programming for optimal classification trees becomes computationally prohibitive as the number of features and training samples increases, limiting practical applicability to larger datasets.

Method: The authors develop a joint feature- and sample-space reduction framework. Weighted STreeD merges duplicate records created after projection onto a fixed candidate set into weighted representatives. Adaptive STreeD repeatedly refines a bounded candidate set, retains features used by the incumbent tree, rebuilds the weighted representation, and solves the resulting reduced problems.

Result: Weighted STreeD achieves speedups of up to 121.41 times over standard STreeD. Adaptive STreeD reduces runtime at depths 2-4 and continues to return feasible trees at greater depths where full-feature methods are limited by time or memory. Predictive performance remains comparable to optimal classification tree baselines.

Conclusion: Joint feature- and sample-space reduction can effectively scale dynamic-programming-based optimal-tree learning to more demanding instances, enabling practical application to larger problems while maintaining optimality guarantees for the current candidate set.

Abstract: Dynamic programming for optimal classification trees becomes computationally expensive as the numbers of features and training samples increase. We develop a joint feature- and sample-space reduction framework based on STreeD. Weighted STreeD merges duplicate records created after projection onto a fixed candidate set into weighted representatives. This reduces sample-dependent computation without changing the fixed-candidate optimization problem. Adaptive STreeD repeatedly refines a bounded candidate set, retains features used by the incumbent tree, rebuilds the weighted representation, and solves the resulting reduced problems. Each certified Weighted STreeD solution is optimal for its current candidate set, while the outer feature search remains heuristic over the full feature space. Experiments on five data sets show that Weighted STreeD achieves speedups of up to 121.41 times over standard STreeD. Adaptive STreeD reduces runtime in matched comparisons at depths 2 to 4 and continues to return feasible trees at greater depths where full-feature methods are limited by time or memory. Under the same computational budget, its predictive performance remains comparable to the evaluated optimal classification tree baselines and is higher in some comparisons. These results show how joint feature- and sample-space reduction can scale dynamic-programming-based optimal-tree learning to more demanding instances.

</details>


### [62] [SAFEGuard: Detect Optimization-Based Jailbreak Attacks Through Harmful Semantic Analysis and Fluency Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05850)
*Quoc Viet Vo,Trung Le,Damith C. Ranasinghe,Ehsan Abbasnejad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAFEGuard is a unified detection framework designed to defend against adversarial jailbreak attacks on Large Language Models by combining hybrid fluency measurement (cross-layer distribution distance and perplexity) with harmful semantic analysis via gradient matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite significant efforts to align LLMs with human values and ensure safe deployment, LLMs remain vulnerable to adversarial jailbreak attacks that can bypass safety guardrails. Existing defense methods have limited effectiveness against optimization-based jailbreak mechanisms that produce highly fluent or harmfully semantic obfuscated prompts.

Method: SAFEGuard incorporates two key components: 1) Hybrid fluency measurement based on cross-layer distribution distance and perplexity, and 2) Analysis of harmful semantics through gradient matching. The approach is grounded in the observation that high fluency prompts maintain malicious intention close to harmful prompts, while harmful semantic obfuscated prompts often inject gibberish token sequences.

Result: SAFEGuard consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines and achieves significant improvement in accuracy across different optimization-based jailbreaks.

Conclusion: SAFEGuard demonstrates effectiveness against evolving jailbreak attacks, providing a robust defense mechanism for LLM safety.

Abstract: Despite the significant efforts devoted to aligning large language models (LLMs) with human values and ensuring safe deployment, recent work has revealed that LLMs remain vulnerable to adversarial jailbreak attacks that can bypass safety guardrails and elicit harmful responses. Many defense methods are proposed to detect jailbreaks but they are limited in their effectiveness to counter wide-range optimization-based jailbreak mechanisms that can yield highly fluency-optimized or harmful semantic obfuscated prompts. To tackle this challenge, we propose a unified detection framework SAFEGuard which incorporates a hybrid fluency measurement based on cross-layer distribution distance and perplexity, and the analysis of harmful semantics through gradient matching. Our method is grounded in a paramount observation: high fluency prompts maintain their malicious intention close to harmful prompts while harmful semantic obfuscated prompts often inject gibberish token sequences. Our evaluation demonstrates that SAFEGuard consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines and achieves significant improvement in accuracy across different optimization-based jailbreaks. This underscores the effectiveness of SAFEGuard against evolving jailbreak attacks.

</details>


### [63] [Selective Posterior Margin Regularization for Forward-Corrected Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05859)
*Zexing Zhang,Jichao Li,Tianyang Lei,XiongYi Lu,Yang Kewei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPMR is a regularization method for learning with label noise that uses disagreement between the reverse posterior and annotations to create graded updates, improving Forward correction by 2.5-7.0 percentage points across five benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-sample networks memorizing corrupted labels despite Forward correction using transition models; the reverse posterior can identify conflicts with annotations but alternatives may remain nearly tied.

Method: Selects leading reverse-posterior class, scales detached pairwise margin by separation between two leading posterior classes, and assigns correspondingly little influence to diffuse conflicts. Uses transition-adjusted pairwise separation and posterior mass carried by leading pair.

Result: Improves full-length Forward by 2.5-7.0 percentage points on five known-transition benchmarks; remains 0.7-2.5 points above Forward with Mixup and early stopping. Transfers to estimated transitions, human annotations, architectural changes, and stronger Forward recipes.

Conclusion: The formulation leverages latent-class evidence already available inside Forward correction without promoting every posterior conflict to a corrected label, providing a principled way to handle label noise through selective regularization.

Abstract: Learning with class-conditional label noise often relies on a transition model from latent clean classes to observed annotations. Forward correction embeds this transition in the likelihood, yet finite-sample networks may still memorize corrupted labels. The corrected likelihood also induces a reverse posterior over the clean classes that could explain each annotation. When its leading class differs from the annotation, the model and transition matrix provide evidence against that annotation, but the leading alternatives can remain nearly tied. We introduce Selective Posterior Margin Regularization (SPMR), which preserves the Forward objective and converts this disagreement into a graded update on the clean classifier. SPMR selects the leading reverse-posterior class, scales a detached pairwise margin by the separation between the two leading posterior classes, and assigns correspondingly little influence to diffuse conflicts. The gap factorizes into transition- adjusted pairwise separation and the posterior mass carried by the leading pair. The active margin follows the locally minimum-norm logit direction that enlarges the selected pairwise margin. Across five known-transition benchmarks, SPMR improves full-length Forward by 2.5-7.0 percentage points and remains 0.7-2.5 percentage points above Forward with Mixup and early stopping. Matched interventions support distinct gains from the posterior-space coefficient, transition-adjusted target, and pairwise action. The same design transfers to estimated transitions, human annotations, architectural changes, and stronger Forward recipes. The formulation uses latent-class evidence already available inside Forward correction without promoting every posterior conflict to a corrected label.

</details>


### [64] [Beyond Arbitrary Geometry: Topology Generalization In neural PDE Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05860)
*Peiyao Chen,Zhouyuan Xu,Jianguo Nie,Jiansheng Fan,Chen Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates how neural operators for PDEs handle changes in domain topology (tunnels and cavities). Using Hodge heat flow as an analytical tool, the study examines six architectures across 45 model-task-topology out-of-distribution cells. The key finding is that topology changes cause significant performance degradation (37/45 cases), primarily through spectral effects rather than harmonic dimension changes. Surprisingly, explicit topological coordinates don't always improve performance, suggesting that global low-frequency structural priors may help organize predictions in the decaying spectral component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural operators claim geometry-general capability but fail to properly handle domain topology changes. The authors identify that topology variations affect both invariant and decaying subspaces of PDE operators in ways that existing methods don't capture. This represents a distinct generalization axis beyond arbitrary-geometry compatibility.

Method: The authors introduce TopoBox-3D, a framework using Hodge heat flow to analyze topology effects. They vary Betti numbers (tunnels and cavities) while using exact Hodge decomposition to separate harmonic kernel from positive spectrum. They tested six architectures and conducted controlled probes to evaluate kernel-identity accuracy.

Result: 1) 37 out of 45 model-task-topology-OOD cells showed performance degradation
2) Changes in harmonic dimension are not more strongly penalized on average
3) Initial Rayleigh quotient is the most stable predictor of error
4) Spectral broadening adds information for edge and face cochains
5) Explicit incidence and harmonic coordinates do not yield best kernel-identity accuracy
6) TNO (Temporal Neural Operator) ranks first in mixed-input nonharmonic accuracy across all six tasks with nontrivial harmonic support

Conclusion: Topology constitutes a distinct generalization axis beyond geometry compatibility. Its influence extends across the entire Hodge spectrum rather than being confined to the harmonic kernel. Global, low-frequency structural priors may help organize predictions in the faster-decaying complementary component, offering a new perspective on neural operator generalization.

Abstract: Neural operators that accept arbitrary meshes are often treated as geometry-general, but unseen domain topology changes both the invariant and decaying subspaces of a PDE operator. We use Hodge heat flow as a controlled lens on this distinction and introduce TopoBox-3D, where tunnels and cavities vary Betti support while the exact Hodge decomposition separates the harmonic kernel from the positive spectrum. Across six architectures, models that infer topology implicitly suffer excess matched degradation in 37 of 45 model--task topology-OOD cells, yet cases that change harmonic dimension are not more strongly penalized on average. The dominant difficulty is instead spectral: the initial Rayleigh quotient is the most stable predictor of error, and spectral broadening adds information for edge and face cochains. Most strikingly, controlled probes show that explicit incidence and harmonic coordinates do not yield the best kernel-identity accuracy; nevertheless, TNO ranks first in mixed-input nonharmonic accuracy on all six tasks with nontrivial harmonic support. Together, these results establish topology as a distinct generalization axis beyond arbitrary-geometry compatibility and show that its influence extends across the Hodge spectrum rather than remaining confined to the harmonic kernel. More broadly, they suggest that global, low-frequency structural priors may help organize predictions in the faster-decaying complementary component, offering a new perspective on how neural operators may generalize across topology as well as geometry.

</details>


### [65] [Budgeted Task-Aware Acquisition of Dynamic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05862)
*Zihe Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scout is a lightweight framework for budgeted task-aware acquisition on dynamic graphs, which learns to select which nodes to query (refresh) to maximize downstream task performance under limited observation budgets. It outperforms 9 baselines in 19/21 settings and demonstrates that task-utility supervision is more effective than structural-change supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning on dynamic graphs is challenging when network changes are only partially observed. Acquiring current graph information incurs costs, making complete updates impractical under limited resources. The paper addresses the problem of optimally allocating有限的观察预算 to refresh stale graph information for downstream tasks.

Method: Scout learns the task value of querying each node from the maintained graph and observation history. The framework uses task-utility supervision (learning directly from downstream task performance) rather than structural-change supervision to allocate observation budgets.

Result: Scout achieves the highest mean downstream performance in 19 of 21 benchmark-budget settings. Task-utility supervision outperforms structural-change supervision in 13 of 16 real-world settings. Task-matched acquisition improves link-prediction AUC by 0.012-0.016 and node-classification accuracy by 0.064-0.09 over task-mismatched acquisition.

Conclusion: Useful graph observations depend on the downstream task, and limited observation budgets can be allocated more effectively by learning directly from downstream utility rather than focusing on structural changes.

Abstract: Learning on dynamic graphs is difficult when changes in the underlying network are only partially observed. Acquiring current graph information incurs observation and computational costs, making complete updates impractical under limited resources. This paper focuses on budgeted task-aware acquisition on dynamic networks, where a model needs to decide which stale graph information to refresh for a downstream task. We propose Scout, a lightweight framework that learns the task value of querying each node from the maintained graph and observation history. Our evaluation covers one synthetic and four real-world dynamic networks, two downstream tasks, nine acquisition baselines, and several query budgets. Scout achieves the highest mean downstream performance in 19 of the 21 benchmark-budget settings. Task-utility supervision also outperforms structural-change supervision in 13 of the 16 real-world settings. On the same dynamic network, task-matched acquisition improves link-prediction AUC by 0.012-0.016 and node-classification accuracy by 0.064-0.09 over task-mismatched acquisition. These results show that useful graph observations depend on the downstream task and that limited observation budgets can be allocated more effectively by learning directly from downstream utility.

</details>


### [66] [A budget-dependent crossover between coverage- and response-based training-set selection for machine-learned interatomic potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05877)
*Jia Bi,Alin-Marin Elena*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates optimal strategies for selecting compact training sets for machine-learned interatomic potentials, comparing structural coverage versus response-guided selection (targeting model disagreement). Through budget-resolved comparisons on GAP-20 Carbon and pooled revised MD17 datasets using MACE models, the study demonstrates that the optimal strategy depends on the retained data budget—at 5% data, coverage outperforms, while at 20% data, response-guided selection yields better results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is that selecting compact training sets for machine-learned interatomic potentials requires deciding between preserving structural diversity or targeting configurations where models disagree. The optimal choice depends on the training-set size retained, making single-point comparisons insufficient. This work aims to resolve which strategy performs better at different data budgets.

Method: The study conducts a budget-resolved comparison of retrained MACE models on two datasets (GAP-20 Carbon and pooled revised MD17). Structural coverage is compared with a response-guided selector that targets disagreement between a coverage-trained model and a full-data reference. A retrospective response witness tests the value of model disagreement for compressing an already labeled pool.

Result: Key findings: (1) At 5% data retention, coverage gives smaller absolute deviations from full-data error than random sampling across four force endpoints in both datasets; (2) The witness (response-guided) selector shows larger deviations than coverage at 1% and 5%, but outperforms at 20%; (3) At 20%, witness-selected models lower direct held-out force errors by 0.46-5.89% relative to coverage, with all eight paired training-seed intervals favoring the witness; (4) Six errors fall below the full-data reference; (5) Mean force-error reductions are 0.164-0.167 meV/Å, with larger gains for tail and masked endpoints; (6) Learned similarity preserves coverage ranking, while selecting by frozen-model error gives higher error than embedding coverage.

Conclusion: The retained-data budget is a decisive variable in atomistic training-set selection. Response-guided compression (witness) improves on structural coverage only at larger data budgets (≥20%), while structural coverage is superior at smaller budgets (1-5%). These findings provide a direct test of when each selection strategy is optimal and establish budget resolution as critical for practical training-set selection.

Abstract: Selecting compact training sets for machine-learned interatomic potentials requires deciding whether to preserve structural diversity or target configurations on which models disagree. The better choice can depend on how much data is retained, making a comparison at one training-set size insufficient. Here we link selection criteria to prediction accuracy through a budget-resolved comparison of retrained MACE models on GAP-20 Carbon and pooled revised MD17. Structural coverage is compared with a response-guided selector that targets disagreement between a coverage-trained model and a full-data reference. This retrospective response witness tests the value of model disagreement for compressing an already labelled pool. At 5\%, coverage gives smaller absolute deviations from the full-data error than random sampling across four force endpoints in both datasets. The witness has larger deviations than coverage at 1\% and 5\%, but the ordering reverses at 20\%. At 20\%, witness-selected models also lower direct held-out force errors by 0.46--5.89\% relative to coverage, with all eight paired training-seed intervals favouring the witness. Six errors fall below the full-data reference. Mean force-error reductions are 0.164--0.167~meV~$\textÅ^{-1}$, with larger gains for tail and masked endpoints. Complementary analyses show that learned similarity preserves the coverage ranking, while selecting by frozen-model error gives higher error than embedding coverage. These findings establish retained-data budget as a deciding variable in atomistic training-set selection and provide a direct test of when response-guided compression improves on structural coverage.

</details>


### [67] [Grounded and Faithful P&ID Reasoning: Constraining Vision-Language Models with Recovered Evidence Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05880)
*Prathamesh Gadekar,Sagar Srinivas Sakhinana,Venkataramana Runkana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical safety issue in using vision-language models (VLMs) to answer questions about Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs). While VLMs can fluently describe these technical drawings, they often hallucinate or miss process connections, which could lead to dangerous plant decisions. The authors propose a graph-grounded approach that extracts an explicit graph from P&IDs (symbols, connections, tags) and requires VLMs to answer topology questions only by querying this graph through seven read-only operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core motivation is safety and reliability in process plant operations. P&IDs are authoritative maps used for isolation, maintenance, and HAZOP (Hazard and Operability) decisions. Current VLMs describe these sheets fluently but "invent or miss process connections" - an invented or missed link can reverse an isolation or reachability call, making plant decisions based on unreliable VLM outputs potentially catastrophic.

Method: The authors propose a Graph-Grounded Harness approach with two main components: (1) Recover an explicit graph from P&ID drawings containing symbols, process connections between them, and tags that name them; (2) Require the VLM to answer topology questions only by querying this recovered graph through seven read-only operators, ensuring any topology claim must cite the query results that support it. They evaluate on TopoPID-VQA, a new dataset of 3000 topology questions, and on the Digitize-PID dataset for graph extraction quality.

Result: The Graph-Grounded Harness significantly improves performance: exact match accuracy rises from 36.7-41.3% (with image-only prompting) to 74.3-76.0% across three models (Qwen3-VL-4B, Qwen3-VL-8B, and Gemma-4-E4B). The recovered graph on Digitize-PID achieves F1 0.742 on exact process connections and 0.801 when symbols and tags are pooled. The residual errors reveal that grounding helps when the graph is correct, but perception errors in graph extraction still break topology questions when the graph is inaccurate.

Conclusion: The graph-grounded approach substantially improves VLM reliability for P&ID topology questions. However, the method's effectiveness is limited by the quality of the underlying graph extraction (F1 0.742 for process connections). The residual errors track this gap - grounding pays off when the recovered graph is right, but perception errors still break topology questions when the graph extraction fails. Future work should focus on improving graph extraction accuracy to further close the performance gap.

Abstract: Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs) are the authoritative maps of process plants: isolation, maintenance, and HAZOP decisions depend on what connects to what. Vision-language models describe these sheets fluently, yet they often invent or miss process connections---and an invented or missed link can reverse an isolation or reachability call, so a plant decision cannot trust a fluent answer that was never checked against the linework. We instead recover an explicit graph of the drawing---its symbols, the process connections between them, and the tags that name them---and then require the model to answer only by querying that graph through seven read-only operators, so a topology claim is returned only when it cites the query results that support it. On TopoPID-VQA, a new suite of 3000 topology questions over these sheets, Graph-Grounded Harness (Ours) raises exact match accuracy from 36.7--41.3% under image-only prompting to 74.3--76.0% for Qwen3-VL-4B, Qwen3-VL-8B, and Gemma-4-E4B. It does so on an imperfect substrate: on Digitize-PID dataset the recovered graph scores F1 0.742 on exact process connections, and 0.801 once symbols and tags are pooled in. The residual errors track that gap---grounding pays off where the recovered graph is right, and perception error still breaks topology questions where it is not.

</details>


### [68] [CALM: Class-wise Agreement and Label-gated Disagreement Modulation for Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05884)
*Yifan Ying,Qing Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CALM introduces smooth trust gates for decentralized federated distillation, weighting teachers by peer agreement, scaling distillation by teacher divergence, and gating by label confidence—outperforming prior methods under severe non-IID skew without additional communication or data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional federated learning requires homogeneous architectures and suffers under non-IID data. Existing decentralized federated distillation methods make hard, all-or-nothing decisions about teacher trustworthiness (outlier rejection), which is problematic when teacher reliability is a matter of degree under severe non-IID skew.

Method: CALM replaces hard trust decisions with three smooth gates: (1) per-class teacher weighting by agreement with peer consensus, (2) per-sample distillation scaling by teacher divergence from consensus target, and (3) label gate scaling by how strongly the target supports the sample's true label. No additional communication or auxiliary data required.

Result: On CIFAR-10, SVHN, OrganAMNIST, and Google Speech Commands with heterogeneous client architectures under Dirichlet label skew, CALM consistently outperforms uniform and hard-filtered distillation and matches or exceeds competing heterogeneous-FL methods.

Conclusion: Smooth, graduated trust mechanisms outperform hard filtering in decentralized federated learning under non-IID conditions, achieving state-of-the-art performance without architecture sharing, server coordination, or public data.

Abstract: Conventional federated learning relies on parameter averaging, which forces clients to be doubly homogeneous: all must run an identical architecture, and accuracy degrades when local data are non-IID. Decentralized federated distillation sidesteps both: each client runs its peers' model snapshots as teachers on its own local data and distills from their soft predictions, with no server, no public data, and no shared architecture. Under severe non-IID skew, however, the trustworthiness of the aggregated teacher target is a matter of degree, yet existing pipelines make hard, all-or-nothing decisions: outlier teachers are discarded by threshold, and whatever target survives is trusted in full. We propose CALM, which replaces every hard decision with a smooth trust gate at three levels: per class, teachers are weighted by agreement with the peer consensus; per sample, distillation is scaled by the teachers' divergence from that target; and a label gate scales it by how strongly the target supports the sample's true label. None of this adds communication or auxiliary data. On CIFAR-10, SVHN, OrganAMNIST, and Google Speech Commands with heterogeneous client architectures under Dirichlet label skew, CALM consistently outperforms uniform and hard-filtered distillation and matches or exceeds competing heterogeneous-FL methods.

</details>


### [69] [One Rate Is Not Enough: Adaptive Anisotropic Learning Rates for LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05885)
*Huiyi Wang,Daijiao Liu,Lina Yao,Dong Gong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AnLR-LoRA proposes an adaptive learning-rate scheme that assigns individual learning rates to each rank-one component in LoRA adapters, addressing the overlooked heterogeneity where different components update at uneven rates and underutilize rank capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LoRA variants use a uniform learning rate across all rank-one components, ignoring substantial within-module heterogeneity where components update at highly uneven rates, causing low-velocity modules to converge to concentrated singular spectra that underutilize the nominal rank budget.

Method: Proposes an adaptive anisotropic learning-rate model that assigns each rank-one component its own effective learning rate, computed online from training-time signals (function-space velocity and Adam SNR) and mean-normalized per module to preserve the global LR budget. Implemented as a lightweight scheme with no extra trainable parameters during AdamW optimization.

Result: Across commonsense reasoning, natural language generation, and visual instruction-tuning benchmarks, AnLR-LoRA consistently improves over LoRA while encouraging broader use of rank capacity. The gains remain robust across a wide range of global learning rates and transfer cleanly to other LoRA variants.

Conclusion: The adaptive per-component learning rate approach effectively addresses the heterogeneity problem in LoRA, leading to improved performance and better utilization of rank capacity. The method is robust, generalizable to other LoRA variants, and requires no additional trainable parameters.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has become the standard for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models. Most LoRA variants follow a uniform-LR convention, applying a single global learning rate across every rank-one component of every adapter. We show that this convention overlooks substantial within-module heterogeneity, where the rank-one components of a LoRA adapter update at highly uneven rates and low-velocity modules converge to concentrated singular spectra that underutilize the nominal rank budget. To address this, we propose an adaptive anisotropic learning-rate model that assigns each rank-one component its own effective learning rate, computed online from training-time signals and mean-normalized per module to preserve the global LR budget. AnLR-LoRA instantiates this model with two signals available during AdamW optimization, namely function-space velocity and Adam SNR, as a lightweight scheme with no extra trainable parameters. Across commonsense reasoning, natural language generation and visual instruction-tuning benchmarks, AnLR-LoRA consistently improves over LoRA while encouraging broader use of rank capacity, with gains that remain robust across a wide range of global learning rates and transfer cleanly to other LoRA variants.

</details>


### [70] [A First-Order Learning Algorithm for Online Resource Allocation with Constant Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05895)
*Menglong Li,Jiawei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a finite-horizon online resource allocation problem where request types arrive with unknown probabilities. The authors propose a primal first-order learning policy that performs gradient ascent updates per period, achieving O(1) expected additive regret independent of the horizon length, without requiring LP solving or nondegeneracy assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop an efficient online learning algorithm for resource allocation under uncertainty. Traditional approaches often require solving linear programs or rely on strong assumptions (like nondegeneracy), which limits practical applicability. The authors aim to achieve constant regret (O(1)) rather than the typical polynomial dependence on horizon T, while removing restrictive assumptions.

Method: The proposed method is a primal first-order learning policy. In each period, upon observing the request type, the algorithm performs one gradient ascent update on the action coordinates associated with that specific request type. This is a stream of single-step stochastic gradient updates, avoiding the need to solve any linear programming problems. The policy operates directly on the action space without maintaining dual variables or solving centralized optimization problems.

Result: The main result is an O(1) expected additive regret bound relative to the hindsight optimum. Critically, this bound is independent of the horizon T, meaning the average regret per period actually decreases to zero as T grows. The bound holds without requiring any nondegeneracy assumption on the fluid linear program, which is a significant improvement over prior work that typically needed such conditions.
