Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that constant regret is achievable in finite-horizon online resource allocation with unknown request probabilities. The primal first-order approach avoids computational bottlenecks (no LP solving) and is more robust (no nondegeneracy assumption). This work bridges online learning and resource allocation, offering a practical algorithm with strong theoretical guarantees. Future directions may include extending to non-i.i.d. arrivals or exploring distributed implementations.

Abstract: We study a finite-horizon online resource allocation problem with initial resource capacities proportional to the horizon. In each period, a request type is observed and one action is chosen from a finite menu. Each action earns a reward and consumes a vector of resources. The arrival types are independent and identically distributed, but their probabilities are unknown. We present a primal first-order learning policy that, in each period, performs one gradient ascent update of the action coordinates associated with the current request type. The policy achieves $O(1)$ expected additive regret relative to the hindsight optimum, with a bound independent of the horizon $T$. It does not solve any linear program, and the regret bound does not require a nondegeneracy assumption on the fluid linear program.

</details>


### [71] [A dictionary learning framework for graphs via filters and optimal transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05919)
*Jinchuan Liao,Dai Hai Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph dictionary learning framework that represents graphs as zero-mean Gaussian distributions from filtered Laplacians, using a tractable surrogate fGOT distance for optimization and connecting it to HSIC for statistical dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph dictionary learning methods lack a metric that captures global structural properties and handles graphs without node correspondence in a tractable way.

Method: 1) Represent each graph as a zero-mean Gaussian distribution from its filtered Laplacian; 2) Approximate observed graphs via barycenter over learned atom graphs under fGOT; 3) Use sfGOT (surrogate fGOT) as tractable approximation for graphs without node correspondence; 4) Interpret sfGOT through HSIC lens - minimizing sfGOT equals maximizing spectral embedding dependence between nodes.

Result: Competitive performance over existing GDL methods on graph clustering and classification benchmark tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed GDL framework with sfGOT provides an effective approach for graph representation learning that captures global structural properties and enables end-to-end optimization.

Abstract: We propose a graph dictionary learning (GDL) framework where each graph is represented as a zero-mean Gaussian distribution derived from its filtered Laplacian. Each observed graph is approximated by a barycenter over learned atom graphs, computed under the filter graph distance (fGOT), a graph comparison metric sensitive to global structural properties. The reconstruction error between the observed graph and its barycenter is measured by the surrogate fGOT (sfGOT) distance, a tractable approximation of fGOT that handles graphs without known node correspondence, and is minimized end-to-end via backpropagation. We further provide a novel interpretation of sfGOT through the lens of the Hilbert-Schmidt Independence Criterion, showing that minimizing the sfGOT distance between two graphs is equivalent to maximizing statistical dependence between the spectral embedding of their nodes. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive performance over existing GDL methods on graph clustering and classification tasks.

</details>


### [72] [Rethinking the Evaluation of Efficiency Methods for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05933)
*Jiamu Zhang,Lingxi Zhang,Pengjun Lu,Qiyue Zhang,Yu-Neng Chuang,Zhengchen Li,Shuai Xu,Vipin Chaudhary,Hanjie Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a controlled diagnostic benchmark to evaluate efficiency methods for LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS), revealing that many reported gains are setup-dependent and may arise from structural collapse or non-MAS-demanding test settings rather than robust improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiency is increasingly important for LLM-based MAS due to substantial execution costs from larger models and more agents. Existing methods aim to improve efficiency through pruning agents, removing communication edges, or searching for compact structures, but the authors argue that existing evaluations may overestimate their true ability due to method-specific prompts, starting topologies, and non-MAS-demanding test settings.

Method: The authors introduce a controlled and MAS-demanding diagnostic benchmark for representative MAS efficiency methods. They evaluate methods under a shared backbone model, agent registry, and runtime, across controlled variations in topology, scale, depth, and tool use.

Result: The analysis shows that many reported gains are setup-dependent and may arise from structural collapse, disabled tool pathways, or starting systems where random pruning already preserves accuracy, rather than robust improvements in MAS efficiency.

Conclusion: Existing evaluations of MAS efficiency methods may be misleading, as gains are often attributable to favorable experimental conditions rather than genuine improvements in multi-agent system efficiency. The proposed benchmark provides a more rigorous evaluation framework.

Abstract: Efficiency is increasingly important for Large Language Model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS), as larger models and more agents introduce substantial execution costs. Recent methods aim to make MAS cheaper by pruning agents, removing communication edges, or searching for compact structures. However, we argue that existing evaluations may overestimate their true ability to improve MAS efficiency. Reported gains are often measured under method-specific prompts and starting topologies, making them difficult to attribute to the proposed structural changes. Moreover, many reported successes appear in non-MAS-demanding settings, where a single agent or a randomly pruned system can already preserve strong performance. To study these issues, we introduce a controlled and MAS-demanding diagnostic benchmark for representative MAS efficiency methods. We evaluate methods under a shared backbone model, agent registry, and runtime, across controlled variations in topology, scale, depth, and tool use. Our analysis shows that many reported gains are setup-dependent and may arise from structural collapse, disabled tool pathways, or starting systems where random pruning already preserves accuracy, rather than robust improvements in MAS efficiency.

</details>


### [73] [Interpretable and Fair Generalized Additive Neural Networks via Multi-objective Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05946)
*Ziming Wang,Changwu Huang,Ke Tang,Yew-Soon Ong,Xin Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the under-explored areas of interpretability evaluation and simultaneous optimization of interpretability and fairness in neural network-based generalized additive models (GAMs). It proposes MONBM, a multi-objective neural basis model framework using evolutionary learning to simultaneously optimize accuracy, interpretability, and fairness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While neural network-based GAMs have been developed as self-interpretable models, existing research has focused primarily on accuracy improvements. The interpretability of these models lacks quantitative evaluation metrics, and the simultaneous optimization of interpretability and fairness remains underexplored.

Method: 1) Introduced explicit quantitative metrics for evaluating interpretability of NN-based GAMs
2) Proposed a Multi-Objective Neural Basis Model (MONBM) framework using multi-objective evolutionary learning
3) Developed a partial retraining strategy for applying evolutionary multi-objective optimization to deep model architectures
4) Conducted experiments to reveal relationships between accuracy, interpretability, and fairness

Result: The paper reveals complex relationships between accuracy, interpretability, and fairness dimensions, demonstrates how multi-objective optimization can be combined with self-interpretable models, and obtains a set of models with different trade-offs between dimensions. The approach is validated against state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: Multi-objective optimization can be effectively combined with self-interpretable models to reveal intricate relationships among trustworthiness objectives. The MONBM framework provides competitive performance while offering multiple model options with different trade-offs between accuracy, interpretability, and fairness.

Abstract: Interpretability and fairness are two of the most emphasized dimensions in trustworthy artificial intelligence (AI). Various explainable AI methods have been introduced to improve interpretability. This paper focuses on neural network (NN)-based generalized additive models (GAMs), a class of self-interpretable models. While most existing research has prioritized improving the accuracy of NN-based GAMs, their interpretability remains largely underexplored. To address this gap, this paper introduces explicit quantitative metrics for evaluating the interpretability of NN-based GAMs, empirically examines their effectiveness, and explores strategies for improving interpretability within these models. In addition, the simultaneous and explicit optimization of both interpretability and fairness, along with their trade-offs and the underlying reasons, remains underexplored. To address this, we propose a multi-objective neural basis model (MONBM) framework based on multi-objective evolutionary learning to consider accuracy, interpretability, and fairness simultaneously. A partial retraining strategy is further developed to facilitate the practical application of evolutionary multi-objective optimization to deep model architectures. Based on MONBM, this paper reveals the complex relationships between these dimensions and the reasons behind these intricate relationships. This analysis demonstrates how multi-objective optimization can be combined with self-interpretable models to reveal relationships among trustworthiness objectives. In addition, MONBM obtains a set of models with different trade-offs between dimensions, and the competitiveness of the approach is validated by comparing it with state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [74] [QGB-W$k$NN: Quantum Granular-Ball Learning for Robust Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05952)
*Suzhen Yuan,Dehang Chen,Lifeng Shen,Shuyin Xia,Jeremiah D. Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes QGB-W$k$NN, a quantum-enhanced weighted K-nearest neighbor classification framework that combines granular-ball representation with hierarchical nearest-neighbor search to improve both computational efficiency and classification reliability in noisy environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nearest-neighbor classification suffers from two key challenges: low computational efficiency and limited robustness in noisy environments. Existing methods struggle with retrieval redundancy and lack reliability-aware decision mechanisms.

Method: The framework integrates three key components: (1) Quantum-kernel granular balls to reduce retrieval redundancy and enhance nonlinear feature representation; (2) A purity-aware HNSW optimization strategy for hierarchical graph construction during neighbor retrieval; (3) A weighted voting mechanism incorporating granular-ball similarity and purity for reliable classification decisions.

Result: QGB-W$k$NN achieves competitive classification accuracy with favorable Pareto trade-offs between performance and computational cost. It also demonstrates improved robustness under various noisy conditions.

Conclusion: Reliability-aware quantum granular-ball learning provides a promising paradigm for efficient and robust nearest-neighbor classification, combining quantum computing advantages with structured neighbor retrieval.

Abstract: Nearest-neighbor classification is widely used in machine learning, yet existing methods often suffer from low computational efficiency and limited robustness in noisy environments. To jointly address these challenges, this paper proposes an efficient and reliable weighted $K$-nearest neighbor classification framework based on quantum granular balls, termed QGB-W$k$NN. The proposed framework improves computational efficiency by integrating quantum-enhanced granular-ball representation with hierarchical nearest-neighbor search, while enhancing classification reliability through a purity-aware weighted decision mechanism. Specifically, quantum-kernel granular balls are constructed to reduce retrieval redundancy and strengthen nonlinear feature representation under limited quantum resources. A granular-ball purity-guided HNSW optimization strategy is developed to exploit structural reliability for hierarchical graph construction during neighbor retrieval, alleviating the local optimality issue caused by conventional random layering. Finally, a weighted voting mechanism jointly incorporating granular-ball similarity and purity is introduced to produce more reliable classification decisions in noisy environments. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that QGB-W$k$NN achieves competitive classification accuracy while exhibiting favorable Pareto trade-offs between classification performance and computational cost. Moreover, the proposed framework consistently improves robustness under various noisy conditions, suggesting that reliability-aware quantum granular-ball learning provides a promising paradigm for efficient and robust nearest-neighbor classification.

</details>


### [75] [LoGIC: Budgeted Context Construction for Node-Level Graph In-Context Learning with Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05955)
*Mingqi Yang,Zidong Guo,Jihui Yang,Wenming Zuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoGIC addresses scalability issues in tabular foundation models for graph learning by introducing a budgeted context construction method with labeled-context and unlabeled-halo budgets. It uses structural, feature-based, and coverage retrieval channels to select relevant nodes, maintaining performance while drastically reducing memory requirements and enabling frozen ICL on million-node graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tabular foundation models (G2T-FM, GraphPFN) use the complete training table as context, causing quadratic attention scaling with labeled pool size and creating preprocessing/memory bottlenecks. There is a need to intelligently select which labeled and unlabeled nodes to include in the prompt for efficient in-context learning.

Method: LoGIC allocates context through two resources: (1) labeled-context budget for predictive evidence, and (2) unlabeled-halo budget for adapter message passing. It retrieves labeled nodes via three channels: structural, feature-based, and coverage. Each context is shared across queries in graph-local clusters. The method incorporates an unlabeled halo for adapter backbones and selects appropriate channels and budget without test labels.

Result: Budgeted contexts maintain locally runnable full-context performance, stay competitive with published large-dataset results, and significantly lower peak memory requirements compared with full-context and whole-graph inference. The method enables frozen graph ICL on million-node graphs without retraining. Analysis identifies when each retrieval channel works best and connects behavior with graph properties.

Conclusion: LoGIC successfully addresses the scalability bottleneck of tabular foundation models for graph learning. By intelligently selecting relevant nodes through budgeted context construction, it achieves comparable performance with substantially reduced memory footprint, enabling practical inference on million-scale graphs.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models have become powerful graph learners. Systems such as G2T-FM and GraphPFN encode each node as a feature row and make predictions through in-context learning (ICL), with labeled rows serving as the prompt. Current protocols employ the complete training table as context, causing attention to scale quadratically with the labeled pool and introducing preprocessing and memory bottlenecks. We investigate context construction for node-level graph ICL: which labeled nodes and auxiliary unlabeled nodes should constitute the prompt for specified queries. We formulate this allocation in terms of two resources: a labeled-context budget for predictive evidence and an unlabeled-halo budget for adapter message passing without using label capacity. We present LoGIC, which retrieves labeled nodes via structural, feature-based, and coverage channels, shares each context across the queries in a graph-local cluster, incorporates an unlabeled halo for adapter backbones, and chooses the channel and context budget without test labels. Across three backbone configurations drawn from two model families on GraphLand, budgeted contexts maintain locally runnable full-context performance, stay competitive with published large-dataset results, and markedly lower peak memory requirements compared with full-context and whole-graph inference. They further permit frozen graph ICL on million-node graphs without retraining. Our analysis identifies when retrieval channels work best and connects their behavior with graph properties.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [76] [Memory as Infrastructure: Reliability Engineering for Persistent Agent Memory in Months-Long LLM-Assisted Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05510)
*Mike Helwig*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents operational experience from a months-scale LLM coding agent project (633,000-line codebase, running since January 2026) and introduces SIx Harness, an open-source memory and continuity infrastructure. The system includes per-project long-term memory with hybrid lexical-vector retrieval, precision-gated context injection, anti-recurrence stores, and reliability engineering specifically designed for memory subsystems. The instrumented record shows 78,933 hook invocations with 85 recorded failures (84 in first 3 weeks, none in final 20 days), zero false fires, and three production incidents traced to structural fixes. Seven design principles are distilled from this N=1 case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM coding agents are transitioning from task-scale to project-scale efforts that run for months across large codebases. However, maintaining coherence across repeated context compactions and massive codebases remains a critical challenge. The authors identify that operational practice lacks reliability engineering for memory subsystems specifically, which they address through this longitudinal study.

Method: The authors conducted a single continuous Claude Code session (N=1) since January 2026 on a 633,000-line codebase, with continuous instrumentation since July 2026. They developed SIx Harness, an open-source MIT-licensed memory infrastructure combining: (1) hybrid lexical-vector retrieval over SQLite for long-term memory, (2) precision-gated context injection, (3) anti-recurrence stores for decisions and dead-ends, (4) compaction-surviving conventions, and (5) reliability engineering including session-start health gates, heartbeat telemetry with enumerated failure modes, and SRE-inspired alert-fatigue budgeting.

Result: The instrumented operational record over months shows: 78,933 hook invocations; 85 recorded failures (84 in subsystem's first three weeks, 1 since, none in final 20 days) - none silent; injection layer with ten-day precision instrument showing zero false fires against intact denominator; three production incidents traced from instrument reading to structural fix. The system achieved high reliability after initial stabilization period.

Conclusion: The authors present the first months-scale instrumented operational record of a persistent agent-memory subsystem. They distill seven design principles for memory infrastructure in persistent LLM agents. Limitations are acknowledged: N=1, no control arm, self-reported data. A tagged pre-registered ablation protocol is published for other teams to replicate using the released MIT-licensed SIx Harness kit. The work addresses a gap in operational practice by providing reliability engineering specifically designed for memory subsystems in long-running LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM coding agents are crossing from task-scale to project-scale: single assisted efforts that run for months, across repeated context compactions, on codebases far larger than any context window. We report operational experience from one such effort (a research project under a single continuous Claude Code session line since January 2026, driving a 633,000-line codebase, with its memory subsystem continuously instrumented since July 2026) and describe SIx Harness, the open-source memory and continuity infrastructure that emerged from keeping it coherent.
  The harness combines per-project long-term memory (hybrid lexical-vector retrieval over SQLite, fully local), precision-gated context injection, anti-recurrence stores for decisions and dead-ends, conventions engineered to survive compaction, and, the part we argue is missing from operational practice, reliability engineering for the memory subsystem itself: a session-start health gate with discriminated failure modes, heartbeat telemetry designed so that no enumerated failure mode can pass unrecorded, and alert-fatigue budgeting borrowed from SRE practice.
  We present what we believe is the first months-scale instrumented operational record of a persistent agent-memory subsystem in production development use: 78,933 hook invocations; 85 recorded failures, none silent: 84 in the subsystem's first three weeks, one since, none in the final 20 days; an injection layer whose ten-day precision instrument shows zero false fires against an intact denominator; and three production incidents traced from instrument reading to structural fix. From the record we distill seven design principles, state our limitations plainly (N=1, no control arm, self-reported), and publish a tagged pre-registered ablation protocol that any team can run with the released MIT-licensed kit.

</details>


### [77] [Look Before You Prompt, and After: Scaffolding Human-AI Collaboration in Software Tutorial Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05563)
*Avinash Bhat,Vy Bui,Jin L. C. Guo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of supporting technical writers in creating software tutorials using LLMs. The authors identified three key requirements through interviews with 17 technical writers, then designed and evaluated a tool called dBlocks that provides blocks for content scoping, a context manager, and inline code execution. A within-subjects study with 16 participants showed significant improvements in confidence and verification workflows compared to participants' preferred LLM-assisted authoring approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs make creating software tutorials easier by generating content, existing LLM tools provide inadequate support for writers in three key areas: (1) assembling and structuring multiple LLM responses, (2) curating the context the model uses, and (3) verifying the generated content. This gap in tooling support represents a significant problem for technical writers working with AI-assisted workflows.

Method: The research employed a human-centered design approach with three phases: (1) Semi-structured interviews with 17 technical writers to identify requirements; (2) Iterative design refinement through a user study with 5 participants; (3) A within-subjects lab study comparing dBlocks with participants' preferred workflows for LLM-assisted authoring (N=16 participants).

Result: Participants reported significantly higher confidence in the tutorials they produced with dBlocks. The tool reduced verification friction by enabling writers to verify code as they drafted rather than deferring or skipping it. Additionally, dBlocks helped writers avoid searching long chat histories by scoping work into blocks that kept each tutorial section and its LLM conversation together.

Conclusion: The work offers implications for designing tools that scaffold human-AI collaboration in software engineering workflows. It demonstrates how human-centered design methodology can effectively guide the development of LLM-integrated tools, providing a model for future research in this space.

Abstract: With LLMs, creating software tutorials now involves steering the model's output and shaping it into a coherent, accurate learning resource, yet existing LLM tools offer writers little support for this work. By analyzing interviews with technical writers ($N=17$), we identify three requirements for how they assemble and structure multiple LLM responses, curate the context the model uses, and verify the generated content. We designed a tool called dBlocks with the following features: blocks to scope content, a context manager to edit context, and inline execution to verify code. Following a human-centered design method, we iteratively refined the design through a user study ($N=5$). In a within-subjects lab study ($N=16$) comparing dBlocks with participants' preferred workflows for LLM-assisted authoring, participants reported significantly higher confidence in the tutorials they produced with dBlocks. In addition, the tool reduced friction in verification, with writers verifying code as they drafted rather than deferring or skipping it, and helped them avoid searching long chat histories by scoping their work into blocks that kept each tutorial section and its LLM conversation together. More broadly, our work offers implications for tools that scaffold human-AI collaboration in SE workflows and shows how human-centered design can guide the development of LLM-integrated tools.

</details>


### [78] [Grounded Skill Synthesis from Code at Scale for Agentic Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05571)
*Yongqi Tong,Pan Wang,Hang Wang,Jianshe Li,Xin Zhang,Jiang-Ming Yang,Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Code2Skill is a fully automated pipeline that transforms source code from GitHub repositories into reusable, verifiable agent skills. It extracts atomic operations, composite workflows, and recurring patterns, then verifies them through source-body-blind reconstruction and source-aware comparison. Applied to 19,769 repositories, it produced CodeSkillBank with 1,006,822 accepted records, improving model performance by 11.7% on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for reusable skills have two key limitations: trajectory-based synthesis requires specific environment interactions, while document-derived skills lack executable evidence and verification. Source code offers a complementary path - it requires no prior agent experience yet provides executable evidence for grounding abstractions.

Method: Code2Skill pipeline transforms selected code units into implementation-anchored records containing atomic operations, composite workflows, and recurring patterns. Each record is verified through source-body-blind reconstruction and source-aware comparison. Applied to 19,769 popular, actively maintained GitHub repositories.

Result: Code2Skill produces CodeSkillBank with 1,006,822 accepted records with workflow, boundary, provenance, and source-evidence metadata. Across 72 protocol-matched evaluations (9 model settings, 8 benchmarks), models augmented with retrieved skills improve by 11.7% on average and outperform baselines in 57 cases. Outperforms trajectory-derived skill banks on all seven shared benchmarks. Skills from AI-generated code achieve 93.50% pass rate vs 93.00% for human-written code.

Conclusion: Code2Skill successfully transforms procedural knowledge embedded in repositories into grounded, verifiable, and transferable agent skills, providing useful procedural knowledge before agents accumulate sufficient interaction experience. The pipeline can also expand with growing AI-generated software.

Abstract: Reusable skills give agents transferable procedural knowledge, making scalable acquisition essential for extending agents beyond prior experience. Existing methods face two limitations: trajectory-based synthesis requires interactions with specific environments, while document-derived skills may lack executable evidence and verification. Source code offers a complementary path: it requires no prior agent experience yet provides executable evidence for grounding abstractions. We present Code2Skill, a fully automated pipeline that transforms selected code units into implementation-anchored records of atomic operations, composite workflows, and recurring patterns, then verifies each record through source-body-blind reconstruction and source-aware comparison. Applied to 19,769 popular, actively maintained GitHub repositories, Code2Skill produces CodeSkillBank, a grounded bank of 1,006,822 accepted records with workflow, boundary, provenance, and source-evidence metadata. Across 72 protocol-matched evaluations covering nine model settings and eight benchmarks, models augmented with retrieved CodeSkillBank skills improve by 11.7% on average over matched baselines and outperform them in 57 cases. Under a unified downstream interface, Code2Skill also outperforms trajectory-derived skill banks on all seven shared benchmarks, showing that repository-derived skills can provide useful procedural knowledge before agents accumulate sufficient interaction experience. Skills synthesized from tested AI-generated code achieve a 93.50% pass rate, compared with 93.00% for human-written code, providing initial evidence that the pipeline can expand with the growing volume of AI-generated software. Overall, Code2Skill transforms procedural knowledge embedded in repositories into grounded, verifiable, and transferable agent skills.

</details>


### [79] [The Impact of GenAI on the Future of Requirements Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05667)
*Travis Breaux,Anmol Singhal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper surveys how AI, particularly large language models (LLMs), is transforming requirements engineering (RE). It examines the shift from code writing to requirements-focused tasks (expressing, curating, verifying, evaluating), reviews advances in prompt programming and generalist SE agents, and explores the future of RE along two axes: interaction with requirements and user experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To inform requirements engineering researchers about navigating the AI transformation and help them select future research priorities, as LLMs are fundamentally changing how software systems are designed and built.

Method: Survey-based research reviewing the state of the art in AI for RE, examining LLM advances, surveying prompt programming (treating LLM instructions as programs), and analyzing generalist SE agents that combine multiple LLM advances to complete SE tasks semi-autonomously.

Result: The paper identifies two future axes for RE: (1) changes in how we interact with requirements throughout the SE process, and (2) changes in how requirements are experienced by developers, stakeholders, and end-users.

Conclusion: As implementation becomes less expensive through generalist SE agents, engineering effort shifts toward requirements-related tasks. The article aims to guide RE researchers in selecting future research priorities amid this transformation.

Abstract: Recent advances in artificial intelligence (AI), particularly large language models (LLMs), are transforming how we design and build systems by increasing access to domain knowledge and by providing automation support to software engineering (SE). As implementation becomes less expensive through generalist SE agents, engineering effort shifts away from writing correct code and toward expressing, curating, verifying, and evaluating requirements. In this paper, we survey the state of the art in AI for requirements engineering (RE) research leading up to the transformation, before reviewing advances in LLMs. We survey two subsequent research areas: prompt programming, which treats LLM instructions as a program in SE vernacular, and generalist SE agents, which combine multiple LLM advances to yield semi-autonomous processes that complete SE tasks. Finally, we explore the future of requirements engineering along two axes: matters changing how we interact with requirements through the SE process, and matters changing how requirements are experienced by software developers and stakeholders more broadly, including end-users. This article aims to inform how RE researchers can navigate this transformation in the selection of future research priorities.

</details>


### [80] [Regret Dominates Surprise: Design-Time Requirements Engineering for Agentic-AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05692)
*Nuwayyir Almohammadi,Rami Bahsoon,Tao Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses safety requirements engineering for agentic-AI systems under epistemic uncertainty by proposing MS-RGR, a Regret-Dominance Mechanism that uses epistemic surprise and cognitive regret signals to decide between routine autonomy, reflective reasoning, or human escalation. The approach reduces silent failures to near-zero and detects risk 17.5x faster than baselines, while improving harmful-task refusal only in models with strong baseline safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Requirements engineers in agentic-AI domains face challenges in evaluating, specifying, and operationalizing safe autonomy under epistemic uncertainty. Mainstream frameworks like Goal-Oriented Requirements Engineering (GORE) lack mechanisms to systematically address these challenges.

Method: The paper introduces MS-RGR (Regret-Dominance Mechanism) with two signals: epistemic surprise for novelty detection and cognitive regret for evaluative risk. It addresses a trilemma: whether an agent should operate in routine autonomy, undergo reflective reasoning, or escalate to human. The mechanism is instantiated in elderly care monitoring and autonomous driving domains, with validation via 100-seed stochastic simulation and retrospective analysis of 208 AGENTHARM scenarios across 7 LLMs using a DRI gate post-hoc.

Result: In stochastic simulation, MS-RGR reduces silent failures to near-zero and detects risk approximately 17.5 times faster than a sensor-only baseline, while remaining formally traceable via LTL safety properties. In the retrospective proxy instantiation across 208 AGENTHARM scenarios, the DRI gate improves harmful-task refusal only for models with strong baseline safety (over 80% pre-gate refusal), with improvements like 84.1% to 90.9%. This indicates MS-RGR amplifies rather than substitutes for model-level safety training.

Conclusion: MS-RGR provides initial feasibility evidence for design-time safety constraints in agentic-AI requirements engineering, though it amplifies rather than replaces the need for robust model-level safety training.

Abstract: Requirements engineers for agentic-AI domains face challenges in evaluating, specifying, and operationalizing safe autonomy. Mainstream frameworks, such as Goal-Oriented Requirements Engineering (GORE), lack mechanisms to systematically address these challenges under epistemic uncertainty. We contribute an approach that builds on GORE to model and simulate safe autonomy in agentic-AI systems. We introduce a novel Regret-Dominance Mechanism (MS-RGR) to operationalize safe autonomy. MS-RGR uses two signals: epistemic surprise (novelty detection) and cognitive regret (evaluative risk) to address the trilemma problem: should the agent operate in routine autonomy, undergo reflective reasoning, or escalate to human? We instantiate MS-RGR in elderly care monitoring and autonomous driving. A 100-seed stochastic simulation shows MS-RGR reduces silent failures to near-zero and detects risk approximately 17.5 times faster than a sensor-only baseline, remaining formally traceable via LTL safety properties. A retrospective proxy instantiation applying the DRI gate post-hoc over execution traces from 208 AGENTHARM scenarios across seven LLMs shows the gate improves harmful-task refusal only for models with strong baseline safety (over 80% pre-gate refusal, e.g., 84.1% to 90.9%), indicating MS-RGR amplifies rather than substitutes for model-level safety training. We discuss threats to validity, positioning MS-RGR as initial feasibility evidence for design-time safety constraints in agentic-AI requirements engineering.

</details>


### [81] [Diffs vs. Whole Files: An Empirical Comparison of Iterative Edit-Based and Direct Generation for Flutter/Dart Code Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05779)
*Andrej Andrejev*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper compares two output regimes for code editing LLMs: direct generation (full file output) vs diff-based generation (iterative search/replace edits). Training two models (100M from scratch and Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B fine-tuned) in both regimes, evaluation on ~1,790 tasks shows direct generation substantially outperforms diff-based across all metrics (compilation rate, bits-per-byte, similarity, LLM-judge ratings). The advantage persists after controlling for task difficulty. Diff-based only competes on short, localized edits, particularly in refactoring and error-handling tasks - the categories with lowest edit-step count. This "task locality" determines when diff-based training is appropriate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The diff-based regime is attractive because it mirrors how developers edit code and should require far fewer generated tokens per turn. The authors investigate whether this assumption holds in practice, as the field lacks systematic comparison between these two output regimes.

Method: Trained two code models (Rainbow-Pony-100M at 100M parameters from scratch, and fine-tuned Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B) in both regimes on a shared Flutter/Dart dataset. Evaluated all four models on ~1,790 held-out tasks per model. Measured: compilation/static-analysis pass rate, bits-per-byte, character-level similarity to reference, and blinded LLM-judge ratings (goal fulfillment, correctness, code quality). Controlled for task difficulty via matched-ID comparison and when restricting to code that compiles on both sides.

Result: Direct generation substantially outperforms diff-based generation on every metric. The gap persists after controlling for task difficulty and when restricting to code that compiles. A single mechanism explains where diff-based wins: it is competitive on short, spatially localized edits, with category-level wins concentrating in refactoring and error-handling/edge-case fixes - the two task categories with the lowest mean edit-step count.

Conclusion: Diff-based generation is only competitive when tasks exhibit "task locality" - short, spatially localized edits. The diff-based regime is the right choice primarily for tasks involving refactoring and edge-case fixes, but direct generation is superior for most code editing tasks. The choice between training regimes should depend on the expected task distribution and the degree of task locality in the target use case.

Abstract: Large language models used for code editing can be trained and deployed in at least two output regimes: direct generation, where the model emits the entire modified file in one shot, and iterative diff-based generation ("steps"), where the model emits a sequence of localized search/replace edits applied one at a time until it signals completion or a step budget is exhausted. The diff-based regime is attractive because it mirrors how developers edit code and should require far fewer generated tokens per turn. We train two code models - a 100M-parameter model trained from scratch (Rainbow-Pony-100M) and a fine-tuned Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B - in both regimes on a shared Flutter/Dart dataset, and evaluate all four resulting models on a held-out set of approx 1,790 tasks per model. Direct generation substantially outperforms diff-based generation on every metric we measure - compilation/static-analysis pass rate, bits-per-byte, character-level similarity to the reference, and blinded LLM-judge ratings of goal fulfillment, correctness, and code quality - and the gap persists after controlling for task difficulty via a matched-ID comparison and when restricting to code that compiles on both sides. We then identify a single, architecture-independent mechanism behind the conditions where diff-based generation does win: it is competitive on short, spatially localized edits, and its category-level wins concentrate in exactly the two task categories - refactoring and error-handling/edge-case fixes - with the lowest mean edit-step count in our dataset. We term this task locality and discuss its implications for when an edit-based training regime is and is not the right choice for a code-editing model.

</details>


### [82] [Correct Tests Are Not Enough: Measuring and Training Oracle Conversion in Specification-Based Test Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05879)
*Yunhao Liang,Chengguang Gan,Ruixuan Ying,Hanjun Wei,Zhe Cui,Shiwen Ni*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies the trade-off between input quality and output correctness in test generation from natural-language specifications. Using reward decomposition and GRPO training with Qwen3.5-9B, the method improves full-test correctness from 28.59% to 42.54% while preserving input-utility signals. Analysis reveals that oracle conversion bottleneck is primarily due to harder input selection rather than output prediction issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generating tests from natural-language specifications requires both input generation (that exposes faulty behavior) and expected output prediction. These two objectives can conflict: a model may increase correctness by choosing easier inputs, or discover useful inputs whose outputs cannot be predicted. The paper aims to study this interaction and identify the bottleneck in oracle conversion.

Method: The authors propose executable reward decomposition with suite-level oracle-conversion measurement. Their generator jointly emits five input-output tests in one response. During training, audited reference programs provide correctness feedback, while a fixed bank of faulty programs provides two utility signals: potential input kill (input exposes fault) and effective kill (after checking generated output). An additive GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) objective preserves both signals without requiring execution at inference time.

Result: On TC-Bench with 506 training and 142 evaluation tasks, three Qwen3.5-9B runs at step 75 achieved: full-test correctness increased from 28.59% to 42.54%, input kill from 24.06% to 25.27%, and effective full kill from 12.23% to 14.15%. Ablations show a trade-off: removing kill rewards yields higher correctness but lowers input kill to 21.60%. Analysis on 64 tasks attributes the NoKill-to-FullKill difference to harder input selection rather than worse output prediction.

Conclusion: Oracle conversion is a measurable bottleneck in test generation. Preserving input-utility feedback in joint test generation provides benefits (higher input kill) but has limits (lower correctness). The principal bottleneck is harder input selection rather than output prediction accuracy on identical inputs.

Abstract: Generating tests from a natural-language specification requires both an input that exposes faulty behavior and a correct expected output. These requirements need not improve together: a model can increase test correctness by choosing easier inputs, or discover useful inputs whose expected outputs it cannot predict. We study this interaction through executable reward decomposition and suite-level oracle-conversion measurement. Our generator jointly emits five input--output tests in one response. During training, audited reference programs provide correctness feedback, while a fixed bank of faulty programs provides two utility signals: potential input kill and effective kill after checking the generated output. An additive GRPO objective preserves both signals without requiring execution at inference time. On an audited TC-Bench split with 506 training and 142 evaluation tasks, three independently trained Qwen3.5-9B runs at step 75 increase full-test correctness from 28.59\% to 42.54\%, input kill from 24.06\% to 25.27\%, and effective full kill from 12.23\% to 14.15\%. Matched 50-step ablations reveal a trade-off: removing kill rewards yields higher correctness and slightly higher full kill, but lowers input kill to 21.60\%. A fixed-input source--oracle crossover on 64 training-pool tasks attributes the principal NoKill-to-FullKill difference to harder input selection rather than worse output prediction on identical inputs. These results identify oracle conversion as a measurable bottleneck and show the benefits and limits of preserving input-utility feedback in joint test generation.

</details>


### [83] [Broken on Arrival: Silently Defective LLM Artifacts in Public Model Registries and How to Catch Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05881)
*Aditi Patodiya*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates defects in quantized GGUF model artifacts distributed through public registries. The authors tested 327 artifacts (305 from Ollama library, 22 from HuggingFace community) using a 15-task smoke suite followed by full 164-task evaluation for suspects. They discovered 5 silently defective official artifacts (1.6%), two backend-dependent failures, and confirmed that some defects are invisible to low-noise heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers increasingly run large language models locally by pulling quantized GGUF artifacts from public registries, yet nothing in the distribution pipeline functionally tests these conversions before they reach users. This lack of acceptance testing creates risks for users who download potentially defective model artifacts.

Method: The authors executed 327 quantized code-capable model artifacts: 305 from the official Ollama library (15 model lines at every eligible quantization level at or under 8 GB) and 22 from the most-downloaded community repositories on HuggingFace. Each artifact ran a 15-task smoke suite calibrated so that healthy artifacts pass while known-broken ones fail. Suspects then faced full 164-task evaluation, a second inference backend, an independent distributor's conversion as referee, and for community files, re-testing under the artifact's own template.

Result: The official library carries five silently defective artifacts: a batch of four Qwen2.5-Coder-3B conversions and one phi3.5-mini conversion that solve zero of 164 tasks and zero of the smoke suite on both backends, while independent conversions of the same models work. This represents 1.6% of official artifacts, or 2 of 29 model-and-size conversion groups. The adjudication chain cleared small-model artifacts that a naive threshold would condemn as broken when they are merely collapsed by extreme quantization, and exposed two older community conversions that degrade badly on CUDA yet pass on Metal—not defective files but backend-dependent failures. Two confirmed defects produce output whose surface statistics sit inside the healthy range, invisible to any low-noise heuristic short of execution.

Conclusion: Model registries need the acceptance gate that package registries already run. The authors release the audit dataset, the quantcheck acceptance-testing tool, and disclosure reports for every confirmed defect at https://github.com/aditi-p31/quantcheck.

Abstract: Developers increasingly run large language models locally by pulling quantized GGUF artifacts from public registries, yet nothing in the distribution pipeline functionally tests these conversions before they reach users. We executed 327 quantized code-capable model artifacts: 305 from the official Ollama library, spanning 15 model lines at every eligible quantization level at or under 8 GB, and 22 from the most-downloaded community repositories on HuggingFace. Each ran a 15-task smoke suite calibrated so that healthy artifacts pass while a known-broken one fails; suspects then faced full 164-task evaluation, a second inference backend, an independent distributor's conversion of the same model and quantization as referee, and, for community files, re-testing under the artifact's own template. The official library carries five silently defective artifacts, a batch of four Qwen2.5-Coder-3B conversions and one phi3.5-mini conversion, that solve zero of 164 tasks and zero of the smoke suite on both backends while independent conversions of the same models work: 1.6% of official artifacts, 2 of 29 model-and-size conversion groups. The adjudication chain cleared small-model artifacts that a naive threshold would condemn as broken when they are merely collapsed by extreme quantization, and it exposed two older community conversions that degrade badly on CUDA yet pass on Metal: not defective files but backend-dependent failures, a third phenomenon no registry currently tests for. Two confirmed defects produce output whose surface statistics sit inside the healthy range, invisible to any low-noise heuristic short of execution. We release the audit dataset, the quantcheck acceptance-testing tool, and disclosure reports for every confirmed defect (https://github.com/aditi-p31/quantcheck), and argue that model registries need the acceptance gate that package registries already run.

</details>


### [84] [VibeCheck: Assessing the Quality of LLM-Generated Unit Tests: A Multi-agent Empirical Study across Heterogeneous Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05978)
*Anika Tabassum,Mushahid Intesum,Md. Fahim Arefin,Tarannum Shaila Zaman*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents VibeCheck, an empirical study evaluating LLM-based IDE agents (Kiro, Antigravity, Cursor) for unit test generation across 15 Python/JavaScript repositories. Using a five-dimensional quality rubric, the study reveals a significant "execution-adequacy gap" where generated tests are runnable but often lack strong assertions and meaningful behavioral coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for LLM-generated unit tests (execution success, coverage) fail to capture deeper quality issues like weak assertions, missing edge cases, poor isolation, and limited maintainability.

Method: Empirical study evaluating three LLM agents on 15 student-developed Python and JavaScript/TypeScript repositories under repository-only, zero-shot conditions using a five-dimensional rubric covering runnability, assertion strength, logic and edge-case coverage, isolation/determinism, and maintainability, with leave-one-out cross-agent peer evaluation.

Result: A clear execution-adequacy gap exists: generated tests are often runnable but frequently lack strong assertions and meaningful behavioral coverage. Weak assertions and missing edge cases occur more often than blocking failures, showing that runnable tests can still be shallow.

Conclusion: VibeCheck provides a reliability-oriented framework for evaluating LLM-generated tests beyond pass/fail outcomes, highlighting the need for deeper quality assessment beyond simple execution success.

Abstract: LLM-based IDE agents are increasingly used to generate repository-grounded unit tests, yet common evaluations often rely on execution success or coverage. These metrics can miss deeper quality issues such as weak assertions, missing edge cases, poor isolation, and limited maintainability. This paper presents VibeCheck, an empirical study of unit test generation across 15 student-developed Python and JavaScript/TypeScript repositories. We evaluate Kiro, Antigravity, and Cursor with Claude Sonnet 4.5 as the underlying agent, under repository-only, zero-shot conditions using a five-dimensional rubric covering runnability, assertion strength, logic and edge-case coverage, isolation/determinism, and maintainability. We also apply leave-one-out cross-agent peer evaluation to compare tools and identify failure patterns. Results show a clear execution-adequacy gap: generated tests are often runnable but frequently lack strong assertions and meaningful behavioral coverage. Weak assertions and missing edge cases occur more often than blocking failures, showing that runnable tests can still be shallow. VibeCheck provides a reliability-oriented framework for evaluating LLM-generated tests beyond pass/fail outcomes.

</details>


### [85] [Replications, Revisions, and Reanalyses: Managing Empirical Evidence in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05983)
*Julian Frattini,Jannik Fischbach,Davide Fucci,Michael Unterkalmsteiner,Daniel Mendez,Robert Feldt,Richard Torkar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel framework for research synthesis in Software Engineering to systematically manage empirical evidence and its evolution. The framework addresses limitations of current meta-analytic approaches by incorporating three types of evidence evolution: replications (external validity), revisions (internal validity), and reanalyses (conclusion validity).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current research synthesis in SE is rare and mostly narrative. Even meta-analyses only produce reliable results when synthesizing exact replications but fail to generalize from variations. The authors aim to extend the frontier of research synthesis beyond the state-of-the-art to systematically manage empirical evidence and its evolution.

Method: The authors apply method engineering to construct a framework from proven, individual method fragments. They explicitly model the relationship between existing pieces of evidence and put three types of evolution into relation: (1) replications investigate the same hypothesis in a new context to improve external validity, (2) revisions challenge an existing hypothesis to improve internal validity, and (3) reanalyses replace analysis methods to improve conclusion validity. The framework includes clear assessment criteria for each dimension of validity.

Result: The framework was demonstrated through application to two fields of research by explicitly modeling the relationship between existing pieces of evidence. It can determine the frontier of a field of research through systematic evolution of evidence and clear assessment criteria for each dimension of validity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a perspective to systematically evolve empirical evidence in SE, supporting more constructive and productive advances in the field. It allows researchers to put new evidence in clear relation to existing body of evidence and systematically expand knowledge about a studied phenomenon.

Abstract: One aspired outcome of empirical research on quantitative data is a variance theory, i.e., a quantification of the effect of an independent on a dependent variables. The validity of variance theories stems from the synthesis of multiple pieces of evidence, which increases its validity beyond the findings of a single study. However, research synthesis in SE is rare and if done mostly limited to purely narrative syntheses. At best, researchers perform meta-analyses to synthesize variance theories from several quantitative results. But even meta-analyses only produce reliable results when synthesizing exact replications yet fail to generalize from variations. We aim to extend the frontier of research synthesis beyond the state-of-the-art to systematically manage empirical evidence and its evolution. We apply method engineering to construct a framework for research synthesis from proven, individual method fragments. The framework allows researchers to put new evidence in a clear relation to an existing body of evidence and systematically expand knowledge about a studied phenomenon. We demonstrate the application of this framework to two fields of research by explicitly modeling the relationship between existing pieces of evidence. The framework puts three types of evolution of evidence into relation: (1) replications investigate the same hypothesis in a new context to improve external validity, (2) revisions challenge an existing hypothesis to improve internal validity, and (3) reanalyses replace analysis methods to improve conclusion validity. Through a systematic evolution of evidence and clear assessment criteria for each dimension of validity, the proposed framework can determine the frontier of a field of research. The framework provides a perspective to systematically evolve empirical evidence in SE, supporting more constructive and productive advances in our field.

</details>


### [86] [SemVul: Semantic-Enhanced Graph Neural Networks for Code Property Graph-based Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06035)
*Younas Affan,Leonardo Regano,Giorgio Giacinto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SemVul is a vulnerability detection pipeline that combines Code Property Graph (CPG) representation with semantic-level enhancement to detect vulnerabilities in source code. It uses pre-trained code embeddings to capture both structural and semantic information, and evaluates multiple GNN architectures on benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Source code vulnerabilities are the root cause of many cyberattacks, enabling unauthorized access, data theft, and service disruption. Existing vulnerability detection approaches lack sufficient generalization and accuracy in learning vulnerable code patterns, necessitating a more effective solution.

Method: The proposed SemVul approach combines Code Property Graph (CPG) representation with semantic-level enhancement. It integrates node-level and edge-level semantic embeddings using pre-trained code embedding techniques. Multiple GNN architectures were systematically evaluated on publicly available benchmark datasets. The approach is language-agnostic and supports multiple architectures.

Result: SemVul outperforms existing approaches in vulnerability detection performance and provides better generalization. The integration of structural information (from CPG) and semantic information (from pre-trained embeddings) improves detection accuracy.

Conclusion: By combining structural flow analysis with semantic understanding of code through Code Property Graph representation and pre-trained embeddings, SemVul achieves superior vulnerability detection performance with improved generalization capabilities compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Vulnerabilities in source code are often the root cause of cyberattacks worldwide, as attackers exploit weaknesses in software to gain unauthorized access, steal data, or disrupt services. In this study, we evaluated existing research approaches and propose SemVul, a vulnerability detection pipeline that demonstrates better generalization and higher accuracy in learning vulnerable code patterns. We propose a Code Property Graph-based vulnerability-detection approach combined with semantic-level enhancement, enabling the model to capture both the program's structural flow and the semantic meaning of the code. Our approach integrates both node-level and edge-level semantic embeddings using pre-trained code embedding techniques. We systematically evaluate multiple GNN architectures on publicly available benchmark datasets. SemVul is generic with respect to the programming language and supports multiple architectures. By integrating structural and semantic information, the proposed approach improves vulnerability detection performance. Our results show that SemVul outperforms existing approaches and provides better generalization.

</details>


### [87] [SpecCoder: Specification-Aware Code Generation with Curriculum Dual-Task Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06041)
*Yixuan Li,Mingxuan Huang,Jiajing Wang,Weidong Yang,Xinyi Liu,Ben Fei,Lipeng Ma*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SpecCoder is a specification-aware two-stage training framework for improving code generation in LLMs by learning to derive structured specifications from natural language requirements and ensuring consistency between specifications and code behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs struggle with challenging programming tasks because they often overlook critical requirements (goals, input/output formats, constraints, examples, edge cases), leading to executable but functionally incorrect code. Existing training-free methods rely on prompting/agents, while training-based methods focus on final code outputs without adequate supervision for learning the intermediate mapping from requirements to structured specifications and grounding them in implementation behavior.

Method: SpecCoder employs a two-stage training approach: (1) Specification-guided SFT trains LLMs to derive structured specification analyses and generate code conditioned on them; (2) Curriculum dual-task GRPO jointly optimizes specification-guided generation and discrimination to encourage stronger correspondence between specifications and code behavior.

Result: SpecCoder consistently improves both standalone code generation and agent-based workflows on APPS, CodeContests, and xCodeEval. Additional evaluations on BigCodeBench-Hard and ClassEval, along with human evaluation and perturbation studies, further validate the role of structured specifications.

Conclusion: Specification-aware training effectively bridges the gap between natural language requirements and code implementation by explicitly learning to derive and adhere to structured specifications, leading to more functionally correct code generation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in code generation but still struggle with challenging programming tasks that require understanding rich natural language requirements. These requirements often specify problem goals, input/output formats, constraints, examples, and edge cases. Overlooking even one may produce executable but functionally incorrect code. Existing training-free methods mainly rely on prompting or agent-based workflows, while training-based methods typically optimize final code outputs. However, existing approaches provide limited supervision for learning the intermediate mapping from raw requirements to structured specifications and for grounding them in concrete implementation behavior. Consequently, models may omit critical constraints, and even when an explicit specification is produced, the implementation may fail to reflect it consistently. Motivated by this gap, we propose SpecCoder, a specification-aware two-stage training framework for code generation. SpecCoder first employs specification-guided SFT to train LLMs to derive structured specification analyses and generate code conditioned on them. It then introduces curriculum dual-task GRPO, which jointly optimizes specification-guided generation and discrimination to encourage stronger correspondence between specifications and code behavior. Experiments on APPS, CodeContests, and xCodeEval demonstrate the effectiveness of specification-aware training, with SpecCoder consistently improving both standalone code generation and agent-based workflows. Additional evaluations on BigCodeBench-Hard and ClassEval, alongside human evaluation and perturbation studies, further validate the role of structured specifications in guiding code generation and discrimination.

</details>


### [88] [SkillSpec: Intent-Masked Specification Reasoning for Agent Skill Correctness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06052)
*Yizhuo Zhang,Bo Kang,Yi Yang,Zhiyu Duan,Zhouteng Ye,Shunkun Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SkillSpec is a Hoare-style framework for verifying correctness of autonomous agent skills by transforming skill repositories into unified graph representations and using specification reasoning to detect defects like intent conflicts and semantic inconsistencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agent systems rely on reusable skill abstractions, but ensuring their correctness is challenging. Failures transcend conventional code defects to subtle semantic inconsistencies like intent conflicts that manifest as silent failures masked by underlying models. Skill correctness must be grounded in task boundaries and generalizability.

Method: SkillSpec transforms heterogeneous skill repositories into unified graph representations aligning descriptions, instructions, and code artifacts. It derives ExpectSpecs from declared intent and infers FactSpecs from encoded behavior under partially disclosed intent. An intent mask regulates access to holistic, lineage, neighborhood, and local views to balance context bias against insufficient context. It jointly reasons over these views to flag candidate defects and validates them in isolated sandboxes.

Result: On 515 real-world skills from SkillsBench and widely downloaded repositories, SkillSpec identified 763 manually confirmed defects across 239 skills, achieving 61.2% precision. Node-level analysis shows specification reasoning is consistently reliable for code nodes but plain-text nodes remain a bottleneck. Most defects arise at boundaries between declared intent and implementation.

Conclusion: Explicit specifications provide a practical foundation for skill quality assurance in real-world agent ecosystems. The approach demonstrates that specification reasoning can effectively detect defects, though plain-text nodes require improvement. Most defects originate at intent-implementation boundaries, highlighting the importance of aligning declared intent with actual behavior.

Abstract: Autonomous agent systems increasingly depend on reusable skill abstractions for consolidating experiential knowledge and domain expertise. These artifacts typically bundle free-form instructions with heterogeneous resources. However, ensuring their correctness remains challenging. Their failure modes transcend conventional code defects to subtle semantic inconsistencies such as intent conflicts, which manifest as silent failures masked by the underlying model. Moreover, skill correctness must be grounded in intended task boundaries and generalizability. We propose SkillSpec, a Hoare-style framework that formulates skill correctness as a specification reasoning problem. It transforms a heterogeneous skill repository into a unified graph representation that aligns descriptions, instructions and code artifacts. For each node, SkillSpec derives an ExpectSpec from the surrounding declared intent, and infers FactSpecs from encoded behavior under partially disclosed intent. An intent mask regulates access to holistic, lineage, neighborhood, and local views to balance the bias introduced by excessive context against unsupported inference caused by insufficient context. SkillSpec jointly reasons over these views to flag candidate defects, and automatically validates them in an isolated sandbox. On 515 real-world skills from SkillsBench and widely downloaded repositories, SkillSpec identified 763 manually confirmed defects across 239 skills, achieving 61.2% precision. The node-level analysis across multiple model families shows that specification reasoning is consistently reliable for code nodes, whereas plain-text nodes remain a major bottleneck. Most defects arise at the boundaries between declared intent and implementation, demonstrating that explicit specifications provide a practical foundation for skill quality assurance in real-world agent ecosystems.

</details>


### [89] [Beyond Lexical Metrics: Sentence-Embedding Detection of Reviewer Habituation in AI Code Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06213)
*Haoran Yu,Lifei Liu,Danping Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Study of 400 reviewers across 11,429 reviews finds that approval rates increase over repeated exposure to AI-generated code (30.5% to 36.6%), but this adaptation is only detectable through sentence embeddings (MLP F1=0.74), not through traditional lexical metrics. Language changes follow approval behavior changes, not precede them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI coding agents submit pull requests at scale, it is unclear whether reviewers reduce scrutiny with repeated exposure and whether review comments reveal this change.

Method: Study of 11,429 reviews from 400 repeat reviewers over 207 days, paired with 10,104 human-authored inline comments. Used Wilcoxon tests, Cohen's d, Spearman correlations, Bonferroni-corrected Mann-Whitney tests, logistic regression classifier, MLP with sentence embeddings, and Granger causality analysis.

Result: Approval rates rise from 30.5% to 36.6% (Wilcoxon p = 8.6 x 10^-8, Cohen's d = 0.25). Four hand-crafted linguistic features (lexical diversity, Shannon entropy, technical specificity, constructive actionability) show no monotonic decline. Logistic regression on these features achieves F1 = 0.485 (below majority-class baseline). However, sentence-embedding structure carries signal: late-period comment centroids shift farther than random permutations (Wilcoxon p < 0.001), and MLP using three embedding statistics achieves F1 = 0.74. Granger analysis shows approval-rate changes predict later shifts in technical specificity (p < 0.001 at all lags), while reverse direction is significant at only one of four lags.

Conclusion: Reviewer adaptation to AI-generated code is detectable in latent distributional structure (sentence embeddings) rather than in classical lexical metrics. Language shifts follow rather than precede changes in approval behavior, suggesting reviewers adapt their judgment implicitly before explicit language changes.

Abstract: Code review is a key quality checkpoint between AI-generated code and production. As AI coding agents submit pull requests at scale, it is unclear whether reviewers reduce scrutiny with repeated exposure and whether review comments reveal this change. We study 11,429 reviews from 400 repeat reviewers over 207 days, paired with 10,104 human-authored inline comments from AIDev. Approval rates rise from 30.5% in reviewers' early periods to 36.6% in late periods (Wilcoxon p = 8.6 x 10^-8; Cohen's d = 0.25). However, four hand-crafted linguistic features - lexical diversity, Shannon entropy, technical specificity, and constructive actionability - show no monotonic decline across exposure deciles (all Spearman absolute rho <= 0.53, p >= 0.11; Bonferroni-corrected Mann-Whitney p >= 0.36). A logistic-regression classifier based on these features reaches F1 = 0.485, below the majority-class baseline. Sentence-embedding structure does carry signal: reviewers' late-period comment centroids shift farther from their early-period centroids than under within-reviewer random permutations (Wilcoxon p < 0.001), and a small MLP using three embedding statistics reaches F1 = 0.74 under 5-fold reviewer-stratified cross-validation. Granger analysis shows that approval-rate changes predict later shifts in technical specificity at all tested lags (p < 0.001), while the reverse direction is significant at only one of four lags. Reviewer adaptation is therefore detectable in latent distributional structure rather than in classical lexical metrics, and language shifts follow rather than precede changes in approval behavior.

</details>


### [90] [SWE-Test: Benchmarking LLM Vulnerability Discovery via Input Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06229)
*Yuanxiang Shi,Jiayi Lin,Xuanyong Lin,Liangcai Su,Yeheng Duan,Wei Wang,Qi Han,Bing Zhao,Wei Hu,Xander Xu,Chenxiong Qian*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces SWE-Test, a new benchmark for measuring LLM agents' vulnerability discovery ability using coverage-guided fuzzing. It decomposes vulnerability discovery into three task modes across 22 real-world C/C++ programs, finding that constraint inference (not code navigation) is the dominant bottleneck, with the best configuration achieving only 55.0% pass rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vulnerability discovery benchmarks are flawed: they are gameable through data contamination, score recall against unknowable vulnerability sets, rely on synthetic bugs, and report single end-to-end verdicts without localizing failures. There's a need to rigorously measure LLM agents' ability to discover real software vulnerabilities.

Method: The paper recasts vulnerability discovery as an input-prediction task with closed, deterministic ground truth. Using coverage-guided fuzzing, they mine deep target branches in real-world C/C++ programs and ask agents to predict inputs that drive execution to given branches. Three task modes are used: (1) Open-loop - fixed-target without feedback, (2) Feedback-enabled - fixed-target with distance oracle, (3) Online Arena - path exploration by coverage gain. They evaluate 15 model-scaffold configurations across 22 programs in 15 domains.

Result: The best configuration achieves only 55.0% pass rate in Feedback-enabled mode. Mean across seven Claude Code configurations is 36.4% with feedback versus 19.3% without. Agents collectively confirmed 13 distinct bugs across six programs in Online Arena. Decomposition reveals constraint inference (not navigation) is the dominant bottleneck.

Conclusion: Vulnerability discovery is a composite ability requiring code comprehension, input constraint inference, input construction, execution, and iterative correction. The dominant failure mode is constraint inference, not navigation. The released SWE-Test benchmark provides a turnkey evaluation environment for rigorously measuring this ability.

Abstract: Vulnerability discovery is becoming an important ability of large language model (LLM) agents: agents that silently miss real defects leave critical software exposed. Rigorously measuring this ability is therefore urgent, but existing benchmarks are gameable through data contamination, score recall against an unknowable vulnerability set, often rely on synthetic bugs, and report a single end-to-end verdict that cannot localize where an agent fails. Vulnerability discovery is a composite ability: an agent must comprehend source code, infer input constraints, construct inputs, execute them, and iteratively correct from feedback. We recast its measurement as an input-prediction task with a closed, deterministic ground truth: using coverage-guided fuzzing, we mine deep target branches in real-world C/C++ programs and ask an agent to predict an input that drives execution to a given branch. This decomposes discovery into three task modes over 22 real-world C/C++ programs spanning 15 domains. Open-loop and Feedback-enabled share 60 fixed-target task instances across 16 of these codebases (13 domains), testing input construction without and with a distance oracle to isolate code comprehension from feedback-driven correction. Online Arena instead removes the predefined target and scores path exploration by coverage gain on a separate, partially overlapping pool of 11 programs; agents collectively confirmed 13 distinct bugs across six programs. Evaluating 15 default-effort model-scaffold configurations, the best reaches only 55.0% pass rate in the Feedback-enabled mode, and the mean across seven paired Claude Code configurations is 36.4% with feedback versus 19.3% without. Decomposing failures, we find constraint inference, not navigation, is the dominant bottleneck. We release SWE-Test with a turnkey evaluation environment.

</details>


### [91] [KG-Commit: A Dynamic Knowledge Graph for Online Just-in-Time Software Defect Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06272)
*Mohsen Hesamolhokama,Mohammad Sina Beyrami Aghbash,Behnam Rohani,Mohammadamin Fazli,Jafar Habibi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: KG-Commit is a novel just-in-time software defect prediction approach that uses a dynamic knowledge graph to maintain repository history, code structure, and commit semantics. It leverages AST-delta tracking for structural changes and lightweight graph inference, achieving state-of-the-art performance across 11 Apache projects while maintaining low latency (~1.33s per commit) suitable for real-time deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current JIT-SDP approaches suffer from two key limitations: (1) reliance on commit-level information only, ignoring broader project context, and (2) lack of efficient methods to incorporate contextual information due to the challenge of both retrieving data quickly for incoming commits and maintaining the context as the repository evolves. This limits the effectiveness of defect prediction in real-world scenarios.

Method: KG-Commit introduces a dynamic knowledge graph that: (1) incrementally maintains repository history, within-file code structure, and commit semantics as the project evolves; (2) uses an AST-delta mechanism to track structural changes between file edits; and (3) employs lightweight graph inference running entirely on CPU. The approach processes commits in real-time while maintaining the graph efficiently.

Result: KG-Commit achieves the highest aggregate performance across 11 Apache software projects: Macro-F1 (0.704), G-Mean (0.706), and AUC (0.809). Under a realistic online protocol, it outperforms LR, HGB, RF, and DeepJIT on all 11 projects, LApredict on 10 projects, and JITLine on 9 projects in Macro-F1, with statistically significant aggregate paired differences. Processing time is approximately 1.33 seconds per commit with stable costs as the graph grows.

Conclusion: Rich project context can be efficiently maintained and exploited for online JIT-SDP. The knowledge graph approach successfully balances prediction accuracy with low latency requirements, making it practical for real-world deployment where timely feedback to developers is critical.

Abstract: Just-in-time software defect prediction (JIT-SDP) aims to identify risky commits as they arrive and provide developers with timely feedback. This need for low latency has led most approaches to rely on commit-level information and overlook the broader project context in which a change occurs. Incorporating this context is challenging because it requires both efficient retrieval for incoming commits and continual maintenance as the repository evolves. We introduce KG-Commit, a dynamic knowledge graph that incrementally maintains repository history, within-file code structure, and commit semantics as the project evolves. It also uses an AST-delta mechanism to track structural changes between file edits and relies on lightweight graph inference running entirely on CPU. Our evaluation on 11 Apache software projects against six baselines shows that KG-Commit achieves the highest aggregate Macro-F1 (0.704), G-Mean (0.706), and AUC (0.809) using our selected inference pipeline.
