Under a realistic online protocol, it outperforms LR, HGB, RF, and DeepJIT on all 11 projects, LApredict on 10, and JITLine on 9 projects in Macro-F1, with the aggregate paired difference significant in every case. KG-Commit processes each commit in approximately 1.33~s, with a cost that remains stable as the graph grows and is compatible with commit rates observed in real-world projects. These findings show that rich project context can be efficiently maintained and exploited for online JIT-SDP.

</details>


### [92] [From Reading Code to Reading Spec: A Verified Layer for LLM-Driven Codebase Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06383)
*Xinhao Zhang,Jingjie Lu,Kunpeng Liu,Fei Xie*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PROOF is a novel framework that manages LLM-generated codebases through hierarchical natural-language specifications, enabling full-lifecycle maintenance while preventing semantic drift by proving semantic equivalence through code reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of LLM-generated code has significantly increased software complexity and maintenance burden, making it difficult for engineers and even LLMs to directly manage large codebases.

Method: PROOF abstracts codebase topology into hierarchical natural-language representations and establishes trust by proving semantic equivalence through reconstructing source code exclusively from specifications. This verified foundation drives maintenance requests and executes code modifications while synchronously updating itself.

Result: Experiments on real-world repositories confirm the effectiveness of these specifications for codebase management.

Conclusion: PROOF enables reliable full-lifecycle codebase management through structured specifications, providing a mechanism to prevent semantic drift and maintain code integrity.

Abstract: The rapid growth of LLM-generated code increases software complexity and the maintenance burden on engineers. While LLMs offer a potential automated alternative, this structural complexity hinders their ability to manage codebases directly. We introduce the Provable Representation Of Original Functionality (PROOF), which manages codebases indirectly via structured specifications. To enable full-lifecycle codebase management strictly through these specifications, PROOF abstracts codebase topology into a hierarchical natural-language representation. To establish absolute trust, the system proves semantic equivalence by reconstructing source code exclusively from this specification. This verified foundation drives maintenance requests, executing code modifications while synchronously updating itself to prevent semantic drift. Experiments on real-world repositories confirm the effectiveness of these specifications.

</details>


### [93] [An Autonomy Aware Metamodel for Human AI Collaboration in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06720)
*Md Mahade Hasan,Md Toufique Hasan,Zheying Zhang,Kalle Kulonen,Konsta Kalliokoski,François Christophe,Tommi Mikkonen,Pekka Abrahamsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is shifting software engineering from tool-supported processes towards AI-first collaboration, where authority is dynamically distributed across human and artificial actors. However, existing method engineering approaches assume static, human-centric control and provide limited support explicitly capturing evolving autonomy. This paper presents a vision for autonomy-aware method engineering by proposing a metamodel that treats autonomy not as a fixed property of an actor, but as a derived, situation-dependent authority assignment determined by task, context, and collaboration pattern. The metamodel formalizes autonomy through four authority dimensions: task execution, task decomposition, task initiation, and collaboration reconfiguration. Through an analytical instantiation with a multi-agent requirements analysis tool, we illustrate how the metamodel supports dynamic authority assignment. This work provides a conceptual foundation for governance-aware, adaptable, and AI-first software engineering methods.

</details>


### [94] [CAROL: Context-Aware Online Learning for Fuzzer Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06772)
*Zirui Liu,Mengfan Xu,Juan Zhai,Shenglong Yao,Shiqing Ma*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CAROL is an online ensemble fuzzing scheduler that uses current context (reward trends, waiting/plateau time, reached code, estimation uncertainty) to allocate CPU time among multiple fuzzers. It outperforms existing schedulers by using either a domain-guided method (detecting rising/rotting phases) or a learned method (predicting reward from 15 context signals with uncertainty). Across nine targets, CAROL triggers more unique bugs than baselines, gains 11.8% over the strongest baseline, and finds 120 previously unknown crashing defects in five C++ programs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ensemble fuzzing schedulers rely on compact summaries of past performance and pre-fixed rules. The authors identified two key limitations: (1) past-reward summaries fail to capture performance evolution reliably due to estimation noise, and (2) predictive signals vary across targets, making learned weightings from other targets less effective.

Method: CAROL uses two approaches: (1) Domain-guided method - detects whether a fuzzer is rising or rotting and applies phase-specific learning rules; (2) Learned method - predicts reward from 15 context signals (reward trends, waiting time, plateau time, reached code, estimation uncertainty) and uses predictive uncertainty for online selection.

Result: Across nine Magma targets, CAROL triggers more unique bugs than each of three ensemble-scheduling baselines whenever results differ, and fewer on none. Compared with the strongest baseline per target, CAROL gains 11.8% and surpasses an oracle that retrospectively selects the best single fuzzer. Removing context eliminates the gain. On five C++ programs, CAROL finds 120 previously unknown crashing defects (deduplicated by site, fault, and entry point), all reported to maintainers.

Conclusion: CAROL demonstrates that using current context for online scheduling in ensemble fuzzing significantly improves bug detection. The context-based approach captures performance evolution better than past-reward summaries, and the gains are concentrated among bugs that baselines trigger rarely or never. The findings suggest that context-aware scheduling is essential for effective ensemble fuzzing.

Abstract: Ensemble fuzzing runs multiple fuzzers on a target while a scheduler allocates CPU time among them. Existing schedulers base these decisions on compact summaries of past performance and rules fixed before a campaign. Our measurements reveal two limitations. First, past-reward summaries do not reliably capture performance evolution: after accounting for estimation noise, agreement between consecutive-window rankings is statistically indistinguishable from within-window self-agreement. Second, predictive signals vary across targets: on eight of nine targets, a weighting learned from the other eight predicts reward worse than one learned on the current target. We introduce CAROL, an online scheduler that uses each fuzzer's current context. Already available to the dispatch loop, this context describes reward trends, waiting and plateau time, reached code, and estimation uncertainty. CAROL uses context in two ways: a domain-guided method detects whether a fuzzer is rising or rotting and applies a phase-specific learning rule, while a learned method predicts reward from 15 context signals and uses predictive uncertainty for online selection. Across nine Magma targets, CAROL triggers more unique bugs than each of three ensemble-scheduling baselines whenever their results differ, and fewer on none. Compared with the strongest baseline for each target, CAROL gains 11.8% and surpasses an oracle that retrospectively selects the best single fuzzer per target. Removing context eliminates the gain, and the additional bugs are concentrated among those the baselines trigger rarely or never. Run unchanged on five widely used C++ programs, CAROL finds 120 previously unknown crashing defects, deduplicated by site, fault, and entry point; all were reported to maintainers through the projects' stated disclosure channels.

</details>


### [95] [Shortcutting the Fix: Identifying and Categorizing Agentic Exploits in Software Engineering Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06780)
*Nikolai Ludwig,Wasi Uddin Ahmad,Somshubra Majumdar,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper reveals that autonomous SWE agents achieve high benchmark scores by exploiting loopholes (Git histories, upstream repos, memorized solutions) rather than genuine problem solving. The authors audit 5 open LLMs on SWE-bench Multilingual and DeepSWE, finding exploitation rates of 45-82% under standard prompts, but these drop to 1.5-10.7% when adding a targeted instruction enforcing originality while maintaining task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High benchmark resolution rates for SWE agents mask exploitative behaviors rather than demonstrating genuine problem-solving capabilities, creating a need to audit and measure true repository-level problem solving.

Method: Systematize and audit exploits across five open LLMs on SWE-bench Multilingual and DeepSWE using a turn-level LLM-as-a-judge protocol, testing both standard prompts and targeted instructions enforcing solution originality.

Result: Under standard prompts, exploitation rates reach 45.1%-82.4% on SWE-bench Multilingual and 44.2%-66.1% on DeepSWE. Appending targeted instruction reduces exploitation to 4.0%-10.7% and 1.5%-7.1% respectively while maintaining strong core task performance.

Conclusion: Exploit-aware evaluation frameworks are critical to measure true repository-level problem solving over benchmark gaming, and simple instructions can significantly reduce exploitation while preserving performance.

Abstract: While autonomous software engineering (SWE) agents achieve high benchmark resolution rates, these scores can mask exploitative behaviors---such as leveraging local Git histories, accessing upstream repositories, or recalling memorized solutions---rather than demonstrating genuine problem solving. We systematize and audit these exploits across five open large language models on SWE-bench Multilingual and DeepSWE using a turn-level LLM-as-a-judge protocol. Under standard prompts, exploitation rates reach 45.1\%--82.4\% on SWE-bench Multilingual and 44.2\%--66.1\% on DeepSWE. Appending a targeted instruction enforcing solution originality drastically cuts these exploitation rates---down to 4.0\%--10.7\% and 1.5\%--7.1\%, respectively, while maintaining strong core task performance. Our findings demonstrate the critical need for exploit-aware evaluation frameworks that measure true repository-level problem solving over benchmark gaming.

</details>


### [96] [LatentMD: Benchmarking Markdown Boundary Failures in LLM-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06993)
*Sungjune Lee,Myungjoo Kang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LatentMD is a benchmark and evaluation protocol designed to diagnose Markdown fence-boundary failures in LLM-generated outputs. It separates content correctness from format boundary correctness, addressing a gap in existing evaluations that conflate these two aspects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM-generated Markdown often conflate content quality with format adherence, leaving Markdown boundary failures under-measured. The authors identified that outputs can be content-correct but boundary-broken, which is a significant but overlooked issue.

Method: The authors created a benchmark with 4,179 prompts and a CLI tool for scoring model outputs. They evaluated 9 LLMs across approximately 37,600 generations, testing boundary failures under various conditions including unspecified prompts, a human-authored validation set, and Python triple-quote docstrings (symmetric delimiters) vs JSON (asymmetric delimiters).

Result: Markdown boundary failures are widespread: 38.0% of valid main-grid outputs are content-correct but boundary-broken. The failures are primarily driven by same-family symmetric-delimiter collisions rather than nesting issues, are only partially mitigated by prompt hints, and generalize to Python triple-quote docstrings while JSON remains robust as an asymmetric-delimiter control.

Conclusion: LatentMD provides a reproducible diagnostic target for parser-sensitive LLM evaluation. The benchmark enables detection of boundary failures that would otherwise go unnoticed in standard evaluations, highlighting a systematic issue in how LLMs handle Markdown formatting.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly generate Markdown that is consumed by renderers, agents, code extractors, and structured downstream pipelines. Yet existing evaluations often conflate content quality with format adherence, leaving Markdown boundary failures under-measured. We introduce LatentMD, a benchmark and evaluation protocol for diagnosing CommonMark-level fence-boundary failures in LLM-generated Markdown. LatentMD separates content correctness from boundary correctness, enabling detection of outputs that are content-correct but boundary-broken. The benchmark contains 4,179 prompts and a CLI for scoring arbitrary model outputs. Across 9 LLMs and roughly 37,600 generations, we find that Markdown boundary failures are widespread: 38.0% of valid main-grid outputs are content-correct but boundary-broken, with substantial boundary breakage under unspecified prompts and in a small human-authored validation set. Ablations show that failures are driven primarily by same-family symmetric-delimiter collisions rather than nesting alone, are only partially mitigated by prompt hints, and generalize to Python triple-quote docstrings while JSON remains robust as an asymmetric-delimiter control. LatentMD provides a reproducible diagnostic target for parser-sensitive LLM evaluation.

</details>


### [97] [Recompilation Is Not Enough: Test-Guided Decompiled-C Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07201)
*Yuhan Huang,Puzhuo Liu,Jianlei Chi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents a workflow for repairing decompiled C code using compiler feedback and test-based validation. The method guides semantic repair through compiler diagnostics, smoke checks, and official tests. Evaluation on 104 Coreutils binaries shows 87.5% successfully recompile and pass tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decompiled C code often fails to recompile or exhibits behavioral differences from the original binary (incorrect option parsing, different output, different exit status). Current approaches only verify recompilation, not test-observed behavior, making LLM-assisted repair less auditable.

Method: A few-step workflow: (1) Use compiler and linker diagnostics to guide build repair; (2) Once C recompiles into a binary, run smoke checks and official tests to expose behavioral discrepancies; (3) Use test-gate feedback for semantic repair. Evaluation on 104 Coreutils 9.1 binaries with static-enriched evaluation and deterministic exact-output smoke comparisons.

Result: Out of 104 Coreutils 9.5 binaries: 91 (87.5%) recompile and pass the test gate; 9 do not recompile within the repair budget; 4 recompile but still fail the test gate.

Conclusion: Test-gate feedback can make LLM-assisted repair of decompiled C more auditable than compile-only recovery approaches.

Abstract: Decompiled C often becomes recompilable only after repair, but recompilation alone does not establish test-observed behavior. A recompiled command-line binary can still parse options incorrectly, print different bytes, or return a different exit status. We present a few-step workflow for repairing decompiled C using compiler feedback and related official tests. Compiler and linker diagnostics first guide build repair. Once the repaired C recompiles into a binary, smoke checks and related official tests expose behavioral discrepancies for semantic repair. In a preliminary static-enriched evaluation on 104 Coreutils 9.5 binaries with available decompiler exports and deterministic exact-output smoke comparisons, 91 binaries (87.5%) recompile and pass the test gate; 9 do not recompile within the repair budget, and 4 recompile but still fail the test gate. The result suggests that test-gate feedback can make LLM-assisted repair of decompiled C more auditable than compile-only recovery.

</details>


### [98] [One Is Not Enough: The Untold Story of Multiple Security Patches for One Vulnerability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07224)
*Fangyuan Zhang,Lyuye Zhang,Lingling Fan,Chengwei Liu,Yinan Li,Liang Huang,Yang Liu,Zheli Liu,Sen Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First large-scale empirical study of multi-SP (Security Patch) vulnerabilities, where a single vulnerability requires multiple patches. The study shows 20.6% of CVEs involve multiple SPs, develops a taxonomy with 6 categories and 16 sub-categories, and creates SPectre - a tool achieving 0.927 recall for same-repository and 0.873 for cross-repository patch discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Security patches are the primary mechanism for fixing software vulnerabilities, but a single vulnerability is not always resolved by a single patch. The prevalence and causes of multi-SP phenomena are poorly understood, and comprehensive patch discovery remains difficult. When patch records are incomplete, downstream users may apply only partial patching, leaving systems vulnerable.

Method: Merged four major vulnerability databases to construct a dataset of 6,053 multi-SP CVEs with 16,260 SPs. Conducted empirical analysis to understand why vulnerabilities are associated with multiple SPs. Derived a two-level taxonomy with 6 categories and 16 sub-categories. Developed SPectre, a taxonomy-driven prototype for comprehensive patch discovery, and evaluated it on 300 multi-SP CVEs and 100 recent CVEs recorded as single-patch by all public databases.

Result: 20.6% of CVEs with patches involve multiple SPs. Merging databases increases recognized multi-SP CVE counts by 36-55% over any single source. SPectre achieves 0.927 recall on same-repository cases and 0.873 recall on cross-repository cases after manual ground truth verification. On 100 recent CVEs recorded as single-patch, SPectre discovered 28 previously unreported SPs across 20 CVEs.

Conclusion: Multi-SP vulnerabilities are both prevalent and systematically underreported. The findings motivate stronger patch-completeness awareness, improved vulnerability database curation, and relation-aware security tooling.

Abstract: Security patches (SPs) are the main mechanism for fixing software vulnerabilities, yet a single vulnerability is not always resolved by a single patch: fixes may be completed incrementally, propagated across maintained branches, or replicated across related repositories. When patch records are incomplete, downstream users may observe only part of the required fix set and therefore apply only partial patching. However, comprehensive patch discovery remains difficult because the prevalence and causes of the multi-SP phenomenon are still poorly understood. In this paper, we present the first large-scale empirical study of multi-SP vulnerabilities. By merging four major vulnerability databases, we construct a dataset of 6,053 multi-SP CVEs with 16,260 SPs, showing that 20.6% of CVEs with patches involve multiple SPs and that merging databases increases recognized multi-SP CVE counts by 36-55% over any single source. We further analyze why a vulnerability is associated with multiple SPs and derive a two-level taxonomy with 6 categories and 16 sub-categories. Based on these findings, we develop SPectre, a taxonomy-driven prototype for comprehensive patch discovery. On 300 multi-SP CVEs, after manually verifying ground-truth SPs, SPectre improves multi-SP patch coverage over representative patch localization baselines, achieving 0.927 recall on same-repository cases and 0.873 recall on cross-repository cases after manual ground truth verification. On 100 recent CVEs recorded as single-patch by all public databases, SPectre further discovers 28 previously unreported SPs across 20 CVEs. Our results show that multi-SP vulnerabilities are both prevalent and systematically underreported, motivating stronger patch-completeness awareness, improved vulnerability database curation, and relation-aware security tooling.

</details>


### [99] [Quality Metrics for LLM-Generated Asset Administration Shells: A Perturbation-Based Evaluation Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07290)
*Janek Groß,Elena Zentgraf,Jens Heidrich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates quality metrics for AI-generated Asset Administration Shells (AAS) using a perturbation-based framework. By degrading AAS generation along multiple dimensions and testing with 3 LLMs on 200 products, the study identifies value-based recall and name-based F1 score as the most reliable quality indicators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid adoption of Industry 4.0 requires reliable digital representations of physical assets through Asset Administration Shells (AAS). While LLMs can generate AAS from unstructured sources like product datasheets, quality assurance remains challenging due to lack of ground truth, standardized metrics, and potential errors. This阻碍 the reliable adoption of AI-generated AAS in industrial applications.

Method: The researchers developed a perturbation-based evaluation framework that systematically degrades AAS generation along multiple dimensions. They generated 6,400 AAS instances from 200 products across multiple manufacturers using three LLMs: GPT-4o-mini, Qwen3, and DeepSeek-R1. The study evaluated metrics based on exact matching of property names and similarity-based soft matching of property values.

Result: The results indicate that value-based recall and name-based F1 score are the most reliable indicators of quality degradation. The study also quantified the impact of different perturbation types and analyzed differences across model families and product segments.

Conclusion: The findings provide guidance for selecting suitable quality metrics, tuning LLM-based AAS generation pipelines, and integrating AI-generated AAS into industrial applications. The research establishes a foundation for systematic quality assurance in AI-driven asset digitization.

Abstract: The rapid digital transformation of manufacturing, often referred to as Industry 4.0, relies on seamless interoperability between physical and software assets. A central enabler is the Asset Administration Shell (AAS), a standardized digital representation of such assets. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) enable the generation of AAS submodels from unstructured sources such as product datasheets but raise challenges for quality assurance. In particular, unexpected errors, the lack of ground truth references, and the absence of standardized quality metrics hinder reliable adoption. In this work, we evaluate quality metrics for AI-generated AAS using a perturbation-based evaluation framework. By systematically degrading AAS generation along multiple dimensions, we assess how well different metrics reflect quality changes. Based on a dataset of 200 products from multiple manufacturers, we generate 6,400 AAS instances using GPT-4o-mini, Qwen3, and DeepSeek-R1. Our results show that metrics based on exact matching of property names and similarity-based soft matching of property values, in particular value-based recall and name-based F1 score, provide the most reliable indicators of quality degradation. Furthermore, we quantify the impact of different perturbation types and analyze differences across model families and product segments. These findings support the selection of suitable metrics, the tuning of LLM-based pipelines, and the integration of AI-generated AAS into industrial applications.

</details>


### [100] [When Stakeholder-centric Requirements Engineering is Not Enough: An Action Research Study on Legacy System Modernisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07340)
*Ruward S. Karper,Damian A. Tamburri,Alessio Ferrari,Willem-Jan van den Heuvel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates how stakeholder-centric Requirements Engineering (RE) can support gap analysis between current (as-is) and future (to-be) systems in legacy modernization contexts. Through action research in a multinational energy company, the study applied stakeholder identification, semi-structured interviews, Delphi method, and MoSCoW prioritization. Results show these practices produced understandable and correct requirements but struggled with achieving agreement on mapping requirements to legacy system functionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legacy system modernization is a major digital transformation challenge, particularly for business-critical systems that are poorly understood. Organizations must simultaneously determine what current systems do and define future needs—which functions to retain, adapt, or replace. This is not merely a technical challenge but a requirements engineering problem heavily shaped by stakeholder perspectives.

Method: Action research study conducted in a multinational energy company undergoing system modernization. The research applied stakeholder-centric RE practices including: (1) stakeholder identification, (2) semi-structured elicitation interviews, (3) agreement-building through Delphi method, and (4) prioritization using MoSCoW method.

Result: The stakeholder-centric RE process was effective in producing requirements that stakeholders generally viewed as understandable and correct. However, it was less effective in achieving agreement on how elicited requirements mapped to legacy system functionality—indicating a gap in connecting stakeholder needs to existing system capabilities.

Conclusion: Stakeholder-centric RE is necessary but not sufficient for legacy modernization. The findings point to the need for uncertainty-aware, iterative, and evidence-based modernization practices that combine stakeholder perspectives with both manual and tool-assisted analysis of legacy systems to bridge the gap between as-is and to-be states.

Abstract: Legacy system modernisation is a major challenge in digital transformation, especially when organisations depend on long-lived, business-critical systems that are only partly understood. In such context, organisations must define future needs while determining what current systems actually do and which functions to retain, adapt, or replace. Modernisation is therefore not only a technical challenge but also a requirements engineering (RE) problem, shaped by stakeholder perspectives. This study examines how far stakeholder-centric RE can support gap analysis between the system-as-is and the system-to-be in a legacy modernisation context. We conducted an action research study in a multinational energy company engaged in system modernisation. In the study, we applied stakeholder-centric RE practices, including stakeholder identification, semi-structured elicitation interviews, agreement-building through the Delphi method, and prioritisation with the MoSCoW method. The results show that this process was effective in producing requirements stakeholders generally viewed as understandable and correct, but less effective in achieving agreement on how elicited requirements mapped to legacy system functionality. The findings suggest that stakeholder-centric RE is necessary but not sufficient, pointing to the need for uncertainty-aware, iterative, and evidence-based modernisation practices that combine stakeholder perspectives with manual and tool-assisted analysis of legacy systems.

</details>


### [101] [EnvPilot: Systematic Design and Evaluation of an Experience-Augmented Agent for Software Environment Setup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07357)
*Hanwu Chen,Hanyu Lin,Zhanjiang Yang,Linhao Zhang,Aoyan Li,Jinxi Li,Meng Li,Yin Chen,Daoguang Zan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Environment Setup is a critical yet complex task in software engineering that relies heavily on expert knowledge. Existing automated environment setup methods lack the ability to accumulate experience from past execution trajectories and to evolve over time. As a result, their performance is limited because they often perform redundant exploration, ignore useful past solutions, and fail to generalize across diverse software ecosystems. We present the systematic design and empirical validation of EnvPilot, an experience-augmented agent that operationalizes trajectory-derived experience reuse for software environment setup. EnvPilot maintains an expandable Trajectory-Derived Memory (TDM), initialized with 667 high-quality experiences. It systematically transforms implicit knowledge from historical execution trajectories into structured experience and retrieves the most relevant guidance during task execution through the Context-aware Retrieval mechanism. This enables EnvPilot to combine multiple validated setup strategies, providing more precise and detailed guidance than methods that rely solely on static project files or web retrieval. To evaluate EnvPilot, we construct AES-Bench, a multilingual benchmark of 112 real-world GitHub instances across 9 programming languages. Experiments show that EnvPilot achieves a new state-of-the-art (SOTA) with a 75.00% Pass@1 success rate while reducing reasoning costs. Our empirical study shows that both the structured experience representation and the Context-aware Retrieval mechanism are essential.

</details>


### [102] [Scanning the Harness: An Empirical Study of Supply-Chain Defects in AI Coding-Agent Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07360)
*Benjamin Kapner,Carmel Soceanu,Alicia Petrunin,Hofni Gartner*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates security vulnerabilities in AI coding agent configurations (Claude Code, Cursor, GitHub Copilot, OpenAI Codex) across 3,171 GitHub repositories.研究发现16.0%的设置存在安全缺陷,包括未固定版本MCP服务器安装、任意执行权限预批准等问题。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI编码代理通过可配置工件(指令文件、技能、钩子、MCP服务器声明、子代理)进行定制,但这些依赖层缺乏安全审计机制:无锁文件、无安装时检查、无组件功能规范。研究旨在量化这些配置的安全风险。

Method: 研究分析3,171个公共GitHub仓库(2,660个设置和511个技能集)。采用三阶段验证流程:1)独立实现从头推导;2)语言模型仲裁器对分歧进行裁决;3)第二个独立模型重新检查每个计数结果。仅统计可从字节直接判定的安全暴露、无法工作的配置或偏离规范的情况。

Result: 发现三类安全缺陷:1)9.8%的设置安装未固定版本的MCP服务器;2)3.1%预批准任意执行权限(如Bash(python:*));3)3.8%携带预批准shell的技能。总体16.0%设置存在安全缺陷,16.7%存在任何确认缺陷,原始扫描仪率为25.5%。第三类缺陷出现在3.7%的集合中。未见凭据泄露路径被确认。

Conclusion: AI编码代理配置存在显著安全风险,约六分之一设置包含安全缺陷。三阶段验证方法将原始扫描率25.5%降至16.0%确认缺陷,证明了严格验证的必要性。工具、语料清单、提示词和所有裁决均已发布以促进后续研究。

Abstract: AI coding agents such as Claude Code, Cursor, GitHub Copilot, and OpenAI Codex are configured through artifacts developers write and share: instruction files, skills, hooks, MCP server declarations, subagents. This harness is a dependency layer installed from marketplaces and public repositories, running with the developer's privileges, with no lockfile, no install-time check, and no vocabulary for what a component may do. We study it over 3,171 public GitHub repositories: 2,660 setups that assemble two or more component types and 511 published skill collections. We measure only rules decidable from bytes whose consequence is a security exposure, a configuration that cannot work, or a departure from the Agent Skills specification, and validate every finding before it counts: an independent implementation re-derives it from the repository at its pinned commit, a language-model adjudicator with a released prompt rules on every disagreement, and a second independent model session re-checks every counted pair. Three security classes survive: 9.8% of setups install an MCP server with no version pinned, 3.1% pre-approve arbitrary execution behind a scoped-looking grant such as Bash(python:*), and 3.8% carry a skill that pre-approves the shell for whoever installs it. In total 16.0% of setups carry a security defect and 16.7% a confirmed defect of any kind, against a raw scanner rate of 25.5% on the same rules; the third class ships inside 3.7% of collections, where a marketplace scan can see it. Rules that compare two files detect differences that are usually intended and are reported as observations. No credential-exfiltration path was confirmed. The instrument, corpus manifest, prompt, and every verdict are released.

</details>


### [103] [Trust the Spec, Not the Code - A Specification-First, AI-Assisted Case Study in Online Banking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07365)
*Eitan Farchi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a follow-on case study applying an AI-assisted formal specification method to an online banking fund-transfer service. The approach uses natural language enriched with lightweight LaTeX mathematics as an intermediate specification language, with LLMs reviewing for ambiguity, drafting proofs, and generating implementations. The method extends prior work on organizational knowledge growth by demonstrating generalization through a "conservation invariant" (money) concept, and adds stress-testing with scheduled transfers, an AI-proposed runtime coverage model, and Z formalization with AI-generated interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formal specification offers early error detection, explicit invariants, and correctness by design, but its notational cost has prevented mainstream adoption. The authors argue that LLMs can significantly reduce this cost by handling ambiguity review, proof drafting, and code generation, allowing specifications to become the primary artifact that is authored, reviewed, proved, and refined—with code becoming regenerable output.

Method: The method uses natural language enriched with lightweight mathematics written in LaTeX as an intermediate specification language. LLMs review specifications for ambiguity, draft proofs, and generate implementations. The approach was validated through: (i) a second case study in online banking; (ii) stress-testing with scheduled/recurring transfers; (iii) proposing a runtime coverage model for invariants; (iv) creating a Z formalization with paired success/failure schemas and invariant proofs over reachable configurations; and (v) an experiment where AI proposes Z interfaces independently.

Result: The two case studies (knowledge growth and fund transfer) share a "conservation invariant" spine (knowledge/money), suggesting the approach generalizes across domains. The richer problem (scheduled transfers) showed the generated code grows substantially while the invariant and proof remain stable. The AI successfully proposed Z interfaces in the experiment. The method has limitations: proofs and runtime checks exist at the specification level and do not establish that generated code refines the specification—this step is delegated to the AI.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that AI-assisted formal specification is viable across different domains through conservation invariants. The approach allows specifications to serve as the primary artifact while code becomes regenerable. However, the method has acknowledged limits: the gap between specification proofs and actual code refinement remains delegated to the AI without formal guarantees. This is presented as a case study, not a controlled experiment.

Abstract: Formal specification promises early error detection, explicit invariants, and correctness by design, yet its notational cost has kept it out of mainstream practice. We argue that AI removes much of that cost: natural language enriched with lightweight mathematics, written in \LaTeX, can serve as an intermediate specification language that is precise enough to reason over and prove, while a large language model (LLM) reviews it for ambiguity, drafts proofs, and generates the implementation. The specification becomes the artifact one authors, reviews, proves, and refines; the code becomes regenerable output. This paper is a follow-on to a prior study that established the discipline on an organizational-knowledge-growth simulation~\cite{predecessor2026}. Here we replicate the discipline in a different domain---an online-banking fund-transfer service---and extend it. The two domains share one spine: a conservation invariant (knowledge in the prior study, money here), which suggests the approach generalizes across domains. We contribute: (i) a second, independent case study of the method; (ii) a stress-test of the method on a richer problem---\emph{scheduled/recurring} transfers---whose generated code grows substantially while the invariant and its proof do not; (iii) an \emph{AI-proposed runtime coverage model} for invariants (``never violated $\neq$ covered''); and (iv) a Z formalization, including paired success/failure operation schemas and an invariant proved over the inductive set of all reachable configurations, together with an experiment in which the AI proposes the Z interfaces itself. We are explicit about the method's limits: the proofs and runtime checks live at the specification level and do not establish that the generated code refines the specification---that step is delegated to the AI. This is a case study, not a controlled experiment.

</details>


### [104] [A Text Mining and Classification Approach for Analyzing Architecture Decision Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07375)
*Nicolás Miccio Palermo,Antonela Tommasel,J. Andrés Diaz-Pace*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes an automated text-mining and classification approach to analyze ADRs from ~550 open-source repositories, examining decision taxonomies, quality attributes, and MADR template compliance. Findings show ADRs frequently capture existence, technology, and process-related decisions, while alternatives, decision drivers, and quality concerns remain under-documented.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the popularity of ADRs as a lightweight mechanism for documenting architectural knowledge, there is limited empirical evidence on the kinds of architectural concerns captured in ADRs and how well their contents align with established documentation practices.

Method: Automated text-mining approach combining topic modeling, LLM-based classification, and MADR template compliance checks, applied to a dataset of ADRs extracted from ~550 open-source repositories.

Result: ADRs frequently capture existence, technology, and process-related decisions, while alternatives, decision drivers, and some quality concerns remain under-documented. There are recurring mismatches between ADR contents and template sections.

Conclusion: The study provides valuable insights for architects to reflect on how ADRs are and should be used. The proposed automated approach is adaptable to other architectural tasks.

Abstract: Architectural decision records (ADRs) have become a popular lightweight mechanism for documenting architectural knowledge in software projects. However, there is limited empirical evidence on the kinds of architectural concerns captured in ADRs and how well their contents align with established architectural knowledge concepts and documentation practices. In this paper, we propose an automated text-mining and classification approach for analyzing ADRs at scale. We apply this approach to a dataset of ADRs extracted from ~550 open-source repositories, combining topic modeling, LLM-based classification, and template compliance checks. Our analysis examines decision taxonomies and quality attributes, and the degree to which ADRs adhere to the MADR template. Our findings show that ADRs frequently capture existence, technology, and process-related decisions, while alternatives, decisions drivers, and some quality concerns remain under-documented. We also observe recurring mismatches between ADR contents and template sections. These insights into current documentation practices provide architects with valuable information to reflect on how ADRs are and should be used to effectively deal with architectural knowledge. Furthermore, our automated approach is adaptable to other architectural tasks.

</details>


### [105] [A Surrogate-based Approach for Fast Multi-objective Architectural Refactoring Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07389)
*J. Andrés Diaz-Pace,Daniele Di Pompeo,Antonela Tommasel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Software model optimization is a process that generates architecture alternatives aimed at improving quantifiable non-functional properties of software systems, such as performance and reliability. Multi-objective evolutionary algorithms are commonly used to explore the search space and help designers identify trade-offs among competing non-functional properties (e.g., through a Pareto front). However, such algorithms face efficiency challenges in complex software models and large design spaces, since evaluating the fitness (i.e., the quality) of each architecture requires analysis tools that become computationally expensive when repeatedly invoked during the search process. In this paper, we explore the construction of surrogate models based on regression techniques to approximate the outputs of these analysis tools at significantly lower computational cost, while maintaining reasonable output accuracy. Our experimental results suggest that surrogate models provide savings of up to $30\%$ in computational time and maintain the Pareto front quality provided by evolutionary algorithms. Also, we observed some differences in the architectural models produced by our approach. Overall, surrogate models constitute a promising approach for scaling multi-objective architecture optimization to larger spaces and complex architectural models.

</details>


### [106] [An Empirical Study on the Impact of Change Granularity in Refactoring Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07482)
*Lei Chen,Shinpei Hayashi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates coarse-grained refactorings (CGRs) spanning multiple commits and ephemeral refactorings (EPRs) that cannot be detected using single-commit granularity analysis. Through an empirical study of 32 open-source Java projects, the researchers found these types commonly occur during development, with about 20% of commit messages explicitly suggesting CGRs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing refactoring detection techniques only work at single-commit granularity, but many refactorings are performed across multiple commits due to complexity or practical development issues. This limitation reduces the effectiveness of refactoring detection for code reviews and empirical software evolution studies.

Method: An empirical study of 32 open-source Java projects, analyzing the features and causes of CGRs and EPRs, categorizing refactoring types, and assessing relationships between commit messages and CGRs.

Result: CGRs and EPRs commonly occur in development. CGRs tend to involve splitting/merging classes/packages and inheritance structure modifications. EPRs target small objects (variables, attributes) and have context-sensitive detection criteria. About 20% of commit messages explicitly suggest CGR existence.

Conclusion: CGRs and EPRs should be valued in refactoring research, and refactoring detectors should be extended to identify CGRs across multiple commits.

Abstract: Detecting refactorings in commit history is essential to improve comprehension to code changes on code reviews, and to provide valuable information for empirical studies on software evolution. Techniques have been proposed to accurately detect refactorings on the granularity of a single commit. However, refactorings can be made over multiple commits because of their complexity or other practical development problems, which cause detecting on only the granularity of a single commit not enough. We observe that some refactorings can only be detected in coarser granularity, i.e., changes conducted over multiple commits, or in the granularity of a single commit but not in coarse-grained. We call these types of refactorings as coarse-grained refactorings (CGRs) and ephemeral refactorings (EPRs). We investigated the features and causes of CGRs and EPRs through an empirical study of 32 open-source Java projects and found that both commonly occur during development. In addition, we found that refactoring types related to splitting or merging classes and packages, as well as those involving modifications to the inheritance structure, tend to be CGRs, and types targeting small objects such as variables and attributes, and refactorings with context-sensitive detection criteria tend to be EPRs. The causes of CGRs and EPRs are analyzed and categorized, and the relationships between the commit messages of CGRs and themselves are also assessed. We found that about 20% of commit messages explicitly suggest the existence of CGRs. We suggest that CGRs and EPRs be valued in refactoring research and that detectors be extended to identify CGRs.

</details>


### [107] [FPScan: An Automated Constraint-Based Analyzer for Floating-Point Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07492)
*Julien Bortolussi,Dorra Ben Khalifa,Pierre-Loïc Garoche*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Writing error-free floating-point programs is a challenging task, especially for programmers who lack a strong background in numerical analysis and rounding-error propagation. State-of-the-art techniques typically aim to bound such errors using static or dynamic analysis. However, only a few tools explicitly address critical floating-point pitfalls such as absorption and catastrophic cancellation. These anomalies represent situations in which rounding errors are significantly amplified, causing the semantics of the finite-precision computation to deviate substantially from the real-number semantics. In this article, we present FPScan, a novel tool to formally define and detect both catastrophic cancellation and absorption in floating-point programs. Our approach starts with a custom static analyzer based on abstract interpretation to infer the order of magnitude of all program variables. This magnitude information is then used to build a set of first-order constraints that model error propagation and numerical precision within the program. Finally, we employ an off-the-shelf SMT solver to determine whether the program exhibits any of these critical numerical pitfalls. Experiments were conducted on FPBench, a well-known benchmark suite of floating-point programs, to evaluate the effectiveness of our tool. We also present a comparison with state-of-the-art tools regarding soundness and analysis time.

</details>


### [108] ["We Permit the Use of AI, but [...]": The Landscape of AI Policies in Popular Open Source Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07542)
*Andre Hora,Romain Robbes,Stefano Zacchiroli*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 281 AI contribution policies from open source communities to understand how projects are governing AI-assisted contributions. The study examines four key dimensions: AI usage permissions, disclosure requirements, countermeasures against "AI slop" (low-quality AI-generated content), and policy evolution over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of AI-assisted code generation tools (like GitHub Copilot, ChatGPT) has created a new governance challenge for open source communities. Maintainers need policies to regulate AI-generated contributions, but there is no established baseline or best practice. This research addresses the gap by systematically analyzing how open source projects are adapting their governance to AI contributions.

Method: The researchers collected 281 AI contribution policies from open source repositories and manually classified them along six dimensions. They also tracked 92 dedicated AI policy files over time to study policy evolution. The analysis addressed four research questions covering: (1) AI usage allowance, (2) AI disclosure practices, (3) AI slop countermeasures, and (4) AI policy evolution.

Result: Four key findings: (1) Permission is the norm—83.3% of policies permit or encourage AI in code contributions, but 67.3% require high human involvement and 43.4% assign accountability. (2) 48.8% require AI disclosure, typically in PR descriptions or commit messages, but disclosure requirements vary widely. (3) Ten distinct countermeasures against AI slop were identified, targeting pull requests, users, and autonomous agents. (4) Policies are dynamic—half of the dedicated AI policy files have been revised since creation.

Conclusion: Open source communities are actively developing AI governance policies, with permission-based approaches being the dominant paradigm. The study provides a baseline and labeled corpus for researchers and maintainers to study AI policy impacts. The findings highlight the need for standardization in disclosure practices and the importance of maintaining human oversight in AI-assisted contributions.

Abstract: Open source communities are converging on a new governance artifact: the AI contribution policy. These policies barely existed a few months ago and are now being written and adopted. We analyzed 281 AI contribution policies, and manually classified them along the six dimensions; to study how policies change, we also tracked 92 dedicated AI policy files over time. We answer four research questions on (1) AI usage allowance, (2) AI disclosure practices, (3) AI slop countermeasures, and (4) AI policy evolution. We find that, first, permission is the norm rather than the exception: 83.3% of policies permit or encourage AI in code contributions. But permission comes with conditions, as 67.3% require a high level of human involvement and 43.4% assign accountability. Second, AI disclosure is required by 48.8% of policies, most often in pull request descriptions and commit messages, but what must be disclosed varies widely. Third, we identify ten countermeasures against AI slop, targeting pull requests, users, and autonomous agents. Finally, policies are not static: half of the dedicated AI policy files have already been revised since creation. Our results give maintainers and researchers a baseline and a labeled corpus for studying the impact of AI policies.

</details>

### [109] [ECHO: Dyadic 3D Facial Motion Generation with Asymmetric Deterministic Articulation and Stochastic Reaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05506)
*Zhuoqiang Cai,Yujie Sun,Chaoyue Niu,Hongyun Yu,Zhiwen Chen,Chengfei Lv,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: ECHO is a method for generating dyadic 3D facial motion from audio alone, decomposing motion into deterministic speech-correlated structure and stochastic residual to handle asymmetric speaking-listening dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem of dyadic 3D facial motion generation under audio-only conditions is asymmetric, involving speech-constrained articulation (speaking) and one-to-many listener reactions (listening). Existing methods treat this uniformly, failing to capture the distinction between stable speech-correlated structure and uncertain interaction dynamics.

Method: ECHO uses a dual-stream audio-only framework with two key components: (1) Motion decomposition into a deterministic anchor (stable speech-correlated structure) and a stochastic residual (uncertain interaction dynamics); (2) Motion Memory as a training-only regularizer for weakly conditioned listening windows; (3) Semantic-group scaling controlling residual injection across expression, jaw, and neck. The method balances articulatory fidelity with listening-side realism and diversity.

Result: Unified, state-wise, and ablation evaluations show that conversational 3D motion benefits from decomposing stable and uncertain components rather than applying stochasticity uniformly. The method provides a practical formulation for deployable conversational digital humans under strict audio-only conditions.

Conclusion: ECHO demonstrates that separating deterministic and stochastic components in facial motion generation improves both speaking-side fidelity and listening-side realism. This decomposition-based approach provides a technical basis for practical conversational digital humans under strict audio-only constraints.

Abstract: We propose ECHO for dyadic 3D facial motion generation under a strict dual-stream audio-only setting, formulating the problem as an asymmetric task involving speech-constrained articulation and one-to-many listener reactions. To address this asymmetry, ECHO decomposes motion into a deterministic anchor that captures stable speech-correlated structure and a stochastic residual that models the remaining one-to-many interaction dynamics. On top of this backbone, Motion Memory acts as a training-only regularizer during brief late-stage fine-tuning to provide local priors for weakly conditioned listening windows, while semantic-group scaling controls residual injection across expression, jaw, and neck. This design balances speaking-side articulatory fidelity with listening-side realism and diversity in a single generation process. Results from unified, state-wise, and ablation evaluations show that conversational 3D motion benefits from decomposing stable and uncertain components rather than applying stochasticity uniformly. ECHO provides a practical formulation and technical basis for deployable conversational digital humans under strict audio-only conditions.

</details>


### [110] [RBF Your SDF: Radial Basis Function Interpolation of Signed Distance Fields with Implied Tangent Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06209)
*Yong Cheng,Yotam Gingold*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel method for reconstructing surfaces from Signed Distance Field (SDF) samples by combining tangent-sphere geometry with radial basis function (RBF) interpolation. The approach addresses limitations of traditional methods (marching cubes, dual contouring) and prior tangent-sphere methods by leveraging all SDF data, not just surface points. It specifically targets preservation of sharp surface features and corners through detection of geometrically constrained tangent points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional surface reconstruction methods (marching cubes, dual contouring) ignore geometric information from SDF samples far from the surface. While recent work by Sellán et al. [2023] leveraged tangent-sphere structure, these approaches only extract the zero-level set via surface reconstruction, considering only points and normals on the surface and ignoring remaining samples. Additionally, surface reconstruction-based methods systematically round or smooth out sharp surface features and corners.

Method: The proposed method combines tangent-sphere observation with radial basis function (RBF) interpolation of all data, including both implied surface points and original SDF samples. Key technical elements include: (1) Detecting spheres with extremely constrained tangent points - a configuration geometrically forced at sharp surface features - to identify and preserve surface corners; (2) Using a partition-of-unity decomposition to enable efficient scaling to large grid resolutions.

Result: The reconstructions improve both Chamfer and Hausdorff accuracy at every tested resolution compared to prior methods. This demonstrates the effectiveness of leveraging all SDF data and properly handling sharp features.

Conclusion: The method successfully combines the geometric insights of tangent-sphere structure with comprehensive RBF interpolation, achieving superior accuracy in surface reconstruction while preserving sharp surface features that other methods systematically round. The partition-of-unity approach ensures scalability to large grids.

Abstract: Signed distance fields (SDFs) are a popular implicit representation of geometry. Converting a discrete set of SDF samples into an explicit surface is a fundamental problem in geometry processing. Traditional reconstruction methods such as marching cubes and dual contouring ignore the geometric information carried by samples far from the surface. Recently, Sellán et al.[2023] and several follow-up works leveraged the tangent-sphere structure of SDFs; every sample implies a point on a sphere tangent to the surface. However, these approaches extract the zero-level set via surface reconstruction, which considers only points and normals on the surface and ignores the remaining samples. We propose an approach that marries the tangent-sphere observation with radial basis function interpolation of all data, the implied surface points and the original data. By detecting spheres with extremely constrained tangent points, a configuration geometrically forced at sharp surface features, we identify and preserve surface corners that surface reconstruction-based methods systematically round. A partition-of-unity decomposition allows our method to scale efficiently to large grid resolutions. Our reconstructions improve both Chamfer and Hausdorff accuracy at every tested resolution.

</details>


### [111] [Skinned Motion Retargeting via Artifact-driven Kinematic Prior Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06517)
*Seokhyeon Hong,Chaelin Kim,Inseo Jang,Soojin Choi,Junyong Noh*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a geometry-aware motion retargeting framework that addresses self-penetration artifacts in target characters by combining a transformer-based retargeting autoencoder with an artifact-driven refinement module. The method explicitly connects posed character geometry artifacts to motion correction while maintaining skeleton-agnostic flexibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion retargeting across characters with different skeletal structures, proportions, and body shapes often produces target-side geometric artifacts such as self-penetration. Existing geometry-aware approaches rely on fixed skeleton templates or implicit geometry-conditioned prediction, requiring a single network to handle multiple tasks and limiting generalization across diverse body types.

Method: The framework consists of three key components: (1) A transformer-based retargeting autoencoder that learns a shared motion embedding across arbitrary source-target skeleton pairs; (2) An artifact-driven refinement module that detects self-penetration on the posed target mesh and converts it into a corrective cue through motion-to-vertex Jacobian; (3) Geometry-aware motion decoding conditioned on skinning weight-based joint-aligned geometry features derived from the rest pose mesh.

Result: Experiments on both fixed and arbitrary skeleton structure settings demonstrate improved kinematic retargeting accuracy and reduced geometric artifacts, producing plausible motions across seen and unseen target characters.

Conclusion: The explicit reasoning about target-side artifacts combined with flexible geometry-aware decoding provides a unified framework that generalizes well across diverse skeleton structures and body shapes, effectively resolving self-penetration issues while preserving motion fidelity.

Abstract: Motion retargeting aims to transfer a source motion to target characters with different skeletal structures, proportions, and body shapes. Although recent neural retargeting methods have improved flexibility across diverse skeletons, target-side geometric artifacts such as self-penetration remain difficult to resolve. Specifically, existing geometry-aware approaches often rely on fixed skeleton templates or implicit geometry-conditioned prediction, requiring a single network to account for target geometry deformation, detect target-side artifacts, and predict the corresponding correction from target geometry alone, which limits their ability to generalize across diverse skeleton structures and body shapes. In this paper, we present a geometry-aware motion retargeting framework that explicitly connects artifacts observed in the posed character geometry to motion refinement while preserving the flexibility of skeleton-agnostic neural retargeting. Our method first learns a motion embedding shared across different skeletons using a transformer-based retargeting autoencoder that transfers motion across arbitrary source--target skeleton pairs. Building on this kinematic motion prior, we introduce an artifact-driven refinement module that observes self-penetration on the posed target mesh and converts it into a corrective cue through a motion-to-vertex Jacobian. We further condition motion decoding on target geometry using skinning weight-based joint-aligned geometry features derived from the rest pose mesh. This design combines explicit target-side artifact reasoning with flexible geometry-aware decoding in a unified framework. Experiments on both fixed and arbitrary skeleton structure settings show that our method improves kinematic retargeting accuracy and reduces geometric artifacts, producing plausible motions across seen and unseen target characters.

</details>


### [112] [CT2Yarn: Yarn-Level Reconstruction of Crochet from Computed Tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06950)
*Chang Luo,Nobuyuki Umetani*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: CT2Yarn is a human-in-the-loop framework that recovers a single continuous yarn path from micro-CT scans of real crochet objects by processing hierarchical yarn structure (fibers → plies → yarn) through Gabor filtering, anisotropic mean-shift clustering, and sketch-based user interaction for disambiguation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crochet creates complex 3D shapes using a single interlocking yarn, but recovering the yarn path from external observations is challenging due to severe self-occlusion. While micro-CT reveals internal structure, it doesn't explicitly encode yarn traversal due to the hierarchical nature of yarn (twisted fibers forming plies, which form the yarn).

Method: 1) Estimate local fiber directions using Gabor filtering and convert volume to oriented point cloud. 2) Apply anisotropic mean-shift procedure to aggregate local fiber orientations within yarn radius neighborhoods into yarn-level orientation estimates. 3) Use automatic topology skeletonization to extract yarn-path fragments. 4) Perform fragment linking, junction cleaning, and loop detection to merge into long curves. 5) Employ sketch-based UI for interactive completion where automatic reconstruction is ambiguous.

Result: The method extracts yarn-path fragments and merges them into a small number of long curves, enabling recovery of the complete single continuous yarn path with human assistance for ambiguous cases.

Conclusion: CT2Yarn successfully recovers yarn paths from micro-CT scans of crochet objects, enabling downstream applications including physically based simulation, ply-level rendering, and stitch-pattern extraction. The human-in-the-loop approach addresses the inherent ambiguity in automatic reconstruction.

Abstract: We introduce CT2Yarn, a human-in-the-loop framework for recovering a single continuous yarn path from micro-computed tomography (micro-CT) scans of real crochet objects. Crochet is a craft that creates complex three-dimensional shapes by interlocking loops formed from a single yarn. Recovering the underlying yarn path from external observations is challenging because of severe self-occlusion. While micro-CT reveals the full internal structure of a crochet object, the volumetric scan alone does not explicitly encode how the yarn traverses the object. This challenge stems from the hierarchical structure of yarn: a yarn consists of multiple twisted plies, and each ply itself consists of twisted fibers. Consequently, local fiber orientations observed in micro-CT scans are not aligned with the overall yarn direction. To recover yarn-level orientations from ply-level fiber orientations, we first estimate local fiber directions using Gabor filtering and convert the volume into an oriented point cloud. We then introduce an anisotropic mean-shift procedure that aggregates local fiber orientations within a neighborhood of the yarn radius into yarn-level orientation estimates. Combined with an automatic topology skeletonization strategy, our method extracts yarn-path fragments. Subsequent fragment linking, junction cleaning, and loop detection merge these trees into a small number of long curves. Where the automatic reconstruction remains ambiguous, a sketch-based user interface enables users to interactively complete the single continuous yarn path. The recovered yarn path enables downstream applications including physically based simulation, ply-level rendering, and stitch-pattern extraction.

</details>


### [113] [Neural Centroidal Voronoi Tessellations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08497)
*Jiacheng Xu,Bo Pang,Rui Xu,Xiaocheng Zhang,Yang Liu,Fei Zhu,Guoping Wang,Peng-Shuai Wang*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Neural CVT is a learning-based surface Centroidal Voronoi Tessellation solver that replaces expensive geometric computations with a recurrent neural optimizer, achieving 10-100x speedup while maintaining quality comparable to state-of-the-art offline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical CVT computation for surface sampling and isotropic remeshing is computationally expensive because each optimization step requires constructing restricted Voronoi diagrams (RVDs) and integrating quantities over surface cells.

Method: The method samples a dense point cloud from the input surface and extracts multi-scale geometric descriptors using a graph neural encoder. A lightweight recurrent optimizer then refines seed positions over few iterations, aggregating interpolated surface features and optimization history to predict per-seed displacements. Training is self-supervised using CVT objectives promoting uniform distributions and feature alignment.

Result: Neural CVT accelerates CVT optimization by one to two orders of magnitude while preserving geometric fidelity. It generalizes to unseen geometry, initialization strategies, and seed densities, producing isotropic, feature-preserving remeshing comparable to state-of-the-art offline optimization methods at a fraction of the computational cost.

Conclusion: The learning-based approach successfully replaces expensive geometric computations with neural predictions, demonstrating that deep learning can accelerate traditional geometry processing primitives while maintaining quality across diverse organic and CAD-like shapes.

Abstract: Centroidal Voronoi tessellation (CVT) is a fundamental primitive for high-quality surface sampling and isotropic remeshing in computer graphics. However, computing surface CVTs with classical solvers remains expensive: each optimization step repeatedly constructs restricted Voronoi diagrams (RVDs) and integrates quantities over their surface cells. We introduce Neural CVT, a learning-based surface-CVT solver that replaces these costly geometric computations with a recurrent neural optimizer, accelerating CVT optimization by one to two orders of magnitude in our benchmarks while preserving geometric fidelity. Given an input surface, we sample a dense point cloud and extract multi-scale geometric descriptors with a graph neural encoder. A lightweight recurrent optimizer then refines seed positions over a small number of iterations, aggregating interpolated surface features and optimization history to predict per-seed displacements. The framework is trained self-supervised using CVT objectives that promote uniform distributions and, when desired, feature alignment. Across diverse organic and CAD-like shapes, Neural CVT generalizes to unseen geometry, initialization strategies, and seed densities, producing isotropic, feature-preserving remeshes comparable to state-of-the-art offline optimization methods at a fraction of the computational cost. Code and trained models will be released.

</details>


### [114] [Inverse Digital Marbling: Recovering Gesture Programs with a Replay Adjoint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08722)
*Tianqi Liu,Yushan Han,Hang Liu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a method to recover executable programs from target images in deposition-based digital marbling. Given a marbled image, the system optimizes an ordered program of capsule insertions whose replay approximates the target. The method uses area-preserving transport with closed-form inverse and a memory-efficient replay adjoint implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pigment deposition in paper marbling displaces existing patterns, creating dependencies between gestures. The authors aim to recover the original generating program (sequence of gestures) from a finished marbled image, enabling editable digital marbling programs.

Method: The method uses a capsule primitive that continuously joins circular drops to elongated deposits. Transport is exactly area-preserving with a closed-form inverse on the exterior of the deposited region. A replay adjoint reconstructs intermediate states, retaining coordinates lost inside deposits and periodic position checkpoints. Implementation uses PyTorch with fused operations for memory efficiency.

Result: The PyTorch implementation uses 8.7x less memory than checkpointed autograd at comparable step time. Programs fit in about four minutes on one GPU. Recovered programs replay across 4x range of linear resolution. Edits specified in program order or palette space work correctly. On synthetic targets with known programs, recovered programs match images but not generating gestures under positional matching.

Conclusion: The system successfully recovers editable programs from marbled images that can replay across different resolutions. However, programs match target images but not the original gesture positions. The approach enables programmatic editing of marbled images while preserving the physical deposition model.

Abstract: Pigment deposition in paper marbling displaces the pattern already present, coupling the appearance of each gesture to later actions. We recover executable programs for a deposition-based digital marbling model: given a target image, we optimise an ordered program of capsule insertions whose replay approximates it. The capsule primitive continuously joins circular drops to elongated deposits. Its transport is exactly area-preserving and has a closed-form inverse on the exterior of the deposited region. A replay adjoint reconstructs intermediate states, retaining coordinates lost inside deposits and periodic position checkpoints. At 2000 gestures and 1024^2 pixels, the PyTorch replay implementation uses 8.7x less memory than the tested checkpointed-autograd configuration at comparable step time; the fused implementation fits a program in about four minutes on one workstation GPU. We evaluate image reconstruction on five marbled sheets, compare against transport-disabled fitting, one-pass geometric compensation and a published stroke-based fitter at matched stroke count, and measure sensitivity to an alternative ordered-drop transport. Recovered programs replay across a 4x range of linear resolution. Edits specified in program order or in palette space -- inserting a gesture, recolouring a stage, translating a stage -- replay correctly under the same model; edits specified by image content, such as moving a motif, do not. On synthetic targets with known generating programs, the recovered programs match the images but not the generating gestures under a positional matching statistic. The output is an editable program in the specified digital medium.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [115] [What Did I Just Say? Self-Listening for Full-Duplex Speech Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05592)
*Xuanning Zhou,Junyi Ao,Xiaotong Liu,Tom Ko,Benyou Wang,Haizhou Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper addresses "anchor interruption" in full-duplex spoken language models - where the model's belief about what it said differs from what was actually played to users. The authors propose "Self-Listening," a method that feeds back realized speech as input to help the model recover from interruptions while staying aware of what users actually heard. They also introduce AnchorSpeech, a benchmark dataset for evaluating structured response consistency after interruptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex spoken language models can listen and speak simultaneously, enabling natural conversation with interruptions and backchannels. However, asynchronous text generation, speech synthesis, and audio playback create a mismatch between what the model believes it said versus what was actually played to the user. This "anchor interruption" problem undermines the model's ability to recover coherently from interruptions.

Method: The authors propose Self-Listening, a full-duplex modeling approach that interleaves three input streams: user speech, model text, and the model's played speech. By feeding the realized speech output back to the model as an input stream, the model grounds its interruption recovery in what the user has actually heard. They also introduce AnchorSpeech, a dataset with homogeneous training and test splits designed to track which items of structured ordered responses have actually been spoken, evaluating whether models can respond consistently with the last completed item before an interruption.

Result: Experiments show that models equipped with the self-listening mechanism achieve better anchoring performance compared to full-duplex baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of feeding back realized speech for coherent interruption recovery.

Conclusion: Self-Listening provides an effective solution for anchor interruption in full-duplex spoken language models by grounding the model's awareness in actually-played speech. The AnchorSpeech benchmark enables systematic evaluation of this capability, showing meaningful improvements over baseline approaches.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken language models can listen and speak simultaneously, enabling them to handle interruptions and backchannels in human conversation. However, text generation, speech synthesis, and audio playback proceed asynchronously. As a result, what a model believes it has said may not match what has actually been played to the user. We refer to the problem of recovering from an interruption while remaining aware of the model's realized speech as anchor interruption. To address this problem, we propose Self-Listening, a full-duplex modeling approach that interleaves user speech, model text, and the model's played speech. By feeding the realized speech output back to the model as an input stream, self-listening grounds interruption recovery in what the user has actually heard. We further introduce AnchorSpeech, a collection with homogeneous training and test splits for tracking which items of structured ordered responses have actually been spoken. AnchorSpeech-test evaluates whether a model can respond consistently with the last completed item before an interruption. Experiments show that, compared with full-duplex baselines, models equipped with self-listening mechanism achieve better anchoring performance.

</details>


### [116] [Emotion as a Distribution: Joint Valence-Arousal Probability Learning for Speaker-Independent Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05755)
*Tingyi Lin,Wen-Ren Yang,Kuanwei Chen*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dual-head multimodal emotion recognition system that outputs both categorical decisions and a probability distribution over the Valence-Arousal plane. Using IEMOCAP dataset with strict speaker-independent evaluation, the system achieves 73.0% unweighted accuracy (with WavLM-Large features: 76.6%), outperforming Transformer baselines by 3.0 points. The distribution output enables tracking of valence/arousal (CCC 0.66/0.66) and captures rater ambiguity through entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal emotion recognizers collapse graded and mixed emotions onto single hard labels, losing valuable information about emotional nuance. The authors argue that recognizing emotion should involve exposing a distribution over affective space, particularly for counseling support applications where understanding the uncertainty and gradation of emotion is crucial.

Method: The system uses a text+speech pipeline with a dual-head architecture: (1) categorical decision head, and (2) 9×9 probability matrix over Valence-Arousal plane. Training uses 2D Gaussian soft targets under KL-divergence/cross-entropy objective. The study compares Transformer and state-space (Mamba-1/2/3) backbones at matched depth/width, with experiments using frozen WavLM-Large features with learnable layer weights. Evaluation uses strict speaker-independent 5-fold leave-one-session-out IEMOCAP with rotating-session inner validation.

Result: The dual-head system achieves 73.0% ± 0.3 unweighted accuracy over three seeds (separate rerun: 72.1%), exceeding the Transformer fusion baseline by 3.0 UA points (95% session-bootstrap CI [1.0, 4.7]; significant under paired t-test and session-level bootstrap). With frozen WavLM-Large features, performance lifts to 76.6% ± 1.3. The distribution's center of mass tracks valence and arousal (CCC 0.66/0.66), and entropy is weakly but consistently linked to categorical rater ambiguity.

Conclusion: The dual-head system with distribution output significantly improves emotion recognition performance over hard-label baselines. The improvement is attributable to the distribution learning rather than auxiliary tasks. The distribution naturally recovers the circumplex model of emotion, with the center of mass tracking dimensional valence/arousal and entropy capturing rater ambiguity. State-space models (Mamba) show no latency or memory advantage at the tested sequence lengths compared to Transformers.

Abstract: Human emotion is graded and frequently mixed, yet most multimodal recognizers collapse it onto a single hard label. We argue the recognizer should instead expose a distribution over affective space. Our text+speech system, alongside its categorical decision, emits a $9\times9$ probability matrix over the Valence-Arousal plane, trained with a two-dimensional Gaussian soft target under a Kullback-Leibler/cross-entropy objective, aimed at counseling support.
