Evaluation is strict: speaker-independent 5-fold leave-one-session-out IEMOCAP with rotating-session inner validation, headline metrics only on the held-out session. Within one fixed encoder-fusion-head pipeline we compare Transformer and state-space (Mamba-1/2/3) backbones at matched depth and width, at two operating points ($T\approx550$, $T\approx2750$). The featured dual-head system reaches 73.0% $\pm$ 0.3 unweighted accuracy over three seeds (separate rerun: 72.1%), exceeding the Transformer fusion baseline by 3.0 UA points (95% session-bootstrap CI [1.0,4.7]; significant under paired t-test and session-level bootstrap), with no latency or memory advantage at these lengths; swapping the ~1M trainable front-end for frozen WavLM-Large features (learnable layer weights) lifts the same architecture to 76.6% $\pm$ 1.3. Pre-specified controls scope the claims honestly: simpler valence-arousal auxiliaries reproduce the classification lift within noise, and a dedicated regression head tracks the continuous ratings slightly better, so the head's specific value is the normalized affect distribution itself. That distribution recovers the circumplex: its center of mass tracks valence and arousal (CCC 0.66/0.66; predominantly between-class structure, weaker within-class tracking), and its entropy is weakly but consistently linked to categorical rater ambiguity, not dimensional spread.

</details>


### [117] [Where Does the Sound Go? Tracing Acoustic Information Loss in Audio-Conditioned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05871)
*Song-ha Jo,Sehyun Lee,Soyoon Kim,Jaesik Choi,Sanghyuk Choi*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates why audio-conditioned language models underuse acoustic cues (prosody, emotion, non-speech sounds), testing whether the frontend encoder is responsible by comparing Whisper and neural audio codecs in a shared Qwen3.5-4B pipeline, and finding that readout alignment rather than encoder-side information loss is the dominant bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-conditioned language models often underuse acoustic cues like prosody, emotion, and non-speech sounds. Researchers questioned whether ASR-supervised frontends discard this information before it reaches the language model, or if the bottleneck lies elsewhere in the pipeline.

Method: The authors compared Whisper-Tiny, Whisper-Small, EnCodec, DAC-VAE, and WavTokenizer encoders in a shared Qwen3.5-4B audio-LM pipeline. They evaluated on ASR, emotion recognition, and sound captioning tasks. They used linear probes, geometric analyses, and LogitLens to trace task-relevant information through encoder, projector, LM layers, and LM head.

Result: Encoder replacement alone does not resolve acoustic underuse; Whisper variants remain strongest overall including on emotion and environmental sound captioning. Linear probes and geometric analyses show discriminative acoustic structure remains recoverable at the final LM layer, even when MCQA accuracy trails probe accuracy by up to 83 points. This task-dependent gap points to content-specific readout failure rather than generic format bias. LogitLens analyses and LM head intervention support that acoustic underuse is not explained solely by encoder-side information loss.

Conclusion: The dominant bottleneck causing acoustic underuse in audio-conditioned language models is readout alignment failure, not encoder-side information loss. This suggests future research should focus on improving the LM head's ability to utilize the acoustic information that is already preserved in the model's representations.

Abstract: Audio-conditioned language models often underuse acoustic cues such as prosody, emotion, and non-speech sounds, raising the question of whether ASR-supervised frontends discard this information before it reaches the LM. We test whether the frontend is responsible by comparing Whisper-Tiny and Whisper-Small with EnCodec, DAC-VAE, and WavTokenizer in a shared Qwen3.5-4B audio-LM pipeline on ASR, emotion recognition, and sound captioning. Encoder replacement alone does not resolve this underuse: Whisper variants remain strongest overall, including on emotion and environmental sound captioning. To localize the failure, we trace task-relevant information through the encoder, projector, LM layers, and LM head. Linear probes and geometric analyses show that discriminative acoustic structure remains recoverable at the final LM layer, even when MCQA accuracy trails probe accuracy by up to 83 points. Because the answer format and decoding procedure are controlled, this task-dependent gap points to content-specific readout failure rather than generic format bias. LogitLens analyses and a targeted LM head intervention support the conclusion that acoustic underuse is not explained solely by encoder-side information loss and that readout alignment can be a dominant bottleneck.

</details>


### [118] [Lead Vocal Separation from Vocal Ensemble Mixtures Using Phoneme Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06488)
*Yuma Narahata,Tomohiko Nakamura,Yuki Saito,Hiroshi Saruwatari*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses voice separation (Vo separation) in contemporary a cappella music, where the goal is to separate the lead vocal (Vo) from accompanying vocal parts. The authors propose using phoneme alignment information as auxiliary input to a BS-RoFormer model via FiLM (feature-wise linear modulation), achieving improved separation performance compared to audio-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that separating lead vocals from accompaniment in a cappella music is challenging because all sources are singing voices with similar acoustic characteristics that overlap in time. Existing methods lack sufficient acoustic cues for this task. The authors aim to leverage phoneme alignment information from the lead vocal part as auxiliary information to improve separation.

Method: The method uses Band-Split RoPE Transformer (BS-RoFormer), a state-of-the-art music source separation model, and introduces frame-level phoneme labels into its intermediate representations using Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM). This allows the model to use phoneme alignment information as conditioning to guide the separation process.

Result: Experimental results show that phoneme-alignment conditioning improves Vo separation performance over an audio-only baseline. The improvement is larger than conditioning only on Vo singing/silence activity. Further analysis reveals that phoneme-label information provides greater advantage when fewer remaining vocal parts share the same phoneme as the lead vocal.

Conclusion: The conclusion demonstrates that incorporating phoneme alignment information as auxiliary conditioning significantly improves voice separation in a cappella music. The approach outperforms both audio-only baselines and simpler conditioning methods (singing/silence activity), with the benefit being most pronounced when there is less phoneme overlap between the lead vocal and accompanying parts.

Abstract: Contemporary a cappella singing often has a lead-and-accompaniment texture, where the lead vocal (Vo) part carries the main melody and the remaining vocal parts provide accompaniment. Owing to their distinct roles, separating the Vo part from the remaining vocal parts, referred to as Vo separation, enables downstream applications such as lyric recognition and minus-one accompaniment generation for vocal ensemble music. Despite these potential applications, acoustic cues for this task are limited because the target and interfering sources are all singing voices with similar acoustic characteristics and often overlap in time, making Vo separation challenging. In this paper, we propose a Vo separation model that uses phoneme alignment of the Vo part as auxiliary information. The proposed model is based on band-split RoPE Transformer (BS-RoFormer), a state-of-the-art music source separation model, and introduces frame-level phoneme labels into its intermediate representations using feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM). Experimental results show that phoneme-alignment conditioning improves Vo separation performance over an audio-only baseline and yields larger average gains than conditioning only on Vo singing/silence activity. Further analysis suggests that the advantage of phoneme-label information is larger when fewer remaining vocal parts share the same phoneme as Vo.

</details>


### [119] [BinauralVAE: Spatial Audio Reconstruction For World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06837)
*Luis Vitor Zerkowski,Luiz Velho*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: BinauralVAE is an open-source pipeline for spatialized audio reconstruction, using Variational Autoencoder architectures (including complex-valued variants) to learn robust latent representations of binaural signals. It aims to establish audio as a complementary modality for spatial knowledge acquisition in embodied AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied AI relies heavily on visual perception, which has limitations in occlusions, low-light, or blackout scenarios. Acoustic information is a critical alternative for spatial awareness but remains under-explored. This work addresses the gap in audio-centric world models for spatial understanding.

Method: The pipeline evaluates various VAE architectures including complex-valued variants to learn latent representations of binaural signals. It uses realistic acoustic data captured during simulated robot navigation in the AudioWorldSim environment.

Result: The pipeline establishes a foundation for state representation in a future audio-based world model that maps the causal connection between navigational actions and resulting acoustic consequences.

Conclusion: This work provides a flexible, open-source pipeline (BinauralVAE) that enables sound as an essential complementary modality for spatial knowledge acquisition, addressing vulnerabilities of vision-centric approaches in challenging environments.

Abstract: Embodied artificial intelligence has historically very much relied on visual perception, leading to a proliferation of multiple vision-centric world models. However, this reliance fails to capture spatial understanding in its entirety and can even present vulnerabilities in environments with visual occlusions, low-light conditions, or blackouts-scenarios, where acoustic information becomes a critical alternative for spatial awareness and navigation. Despite its potential, research into realistic spatial audio and particularly the development of audio-centric world models remains sparse. In this technical report, we introduce BinauralVAE: a flexible, open-source pipeline (https://github.com/Luizerko/BinauralVAE) that explores multiple models for spatialized audio reconstruction, progressing from fundamental baselines to advanced, mathematically grounded architectures. Our approach evaluates various Variational Autoencoder architectures -- including complex-valued variants -- to learn robust latent representations of binaural signals. Developed alongside AudioWorldSim, our methodology leverages realistic acoustic data captured as a simulated robot navigates an environment. This pipeline establishes a foundation for state representation in a future audio-based world model, designed to map the direct causal connection between navigational actions and their resulting acoustic consequences, and helping to enable sound as an essential complementary modality for spatial knowledge acquisition.

</details>


### [120] [AuK Technical Report: An Open-Source Foundational Model for Speech Generation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08936)
*Ziyang Ma,Zhikang Niu,Wenming Tu,Tianrui Wang,Ruiqi Yan,Junxi Liu,Yanru Huo,Nickk Huang,Yang Liu,Qicong Xie,Zeyu Xie,Hui Wang,Haitao Li,Zixuan Jiang,Yalin Li,Jie Fang,Yifan Duan,Zeyue Tian,Guangzheng Li,Haina Zhu,Shuyi Wang,Jinwen Wang,Mingyu Cui,Tian Tan,Auden,Sen Liang,Steve Yves,Shan Yang,Liefeng Bo,Zilong Zheng,Kai Yu,Eng-Siong Chng,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AuK is an open-source foundational model that unifies speech generation and editing through natural-language instructions. It uses 3.03B instruction-audio instances and 1.95M hours of supervision across 5 task families. The architecture combines a multimodal LLM, a VAE, and a hybrid rectified-flow Transformer. A distilled version (AuK-Flash) achieves 4.5x speedup with 4-step inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks a unified model that can handle both speech generation and editing through a common natural-language interface. Existing models typically focus on either generation or editing tasks separately, requiring separate architectures for different speech processing needs.

Method: The model combines: (1) multimodal LLM for semantic conditioning, (2) VAE jointly trained on speech/general audio/music for acoustic conditioning, (3) hybrid rectified-flow Transformer with dual-stream MMDiT followed by unified single-stream DiT blocks. Training progresses from generation-only warm-up to joint generation-editing pre-training. Post-training includes human-feedback preference optimization and reward-based RL. Distillation produces AuK-Flash with consistency initialization and task-routed Decoupled DMD.

Result: AuK achieves leading performance on zero-shot and instruction-controlled speech generation and general instruction-guided editing tasks, while remaining competitive on signal-level restoration tasks. AuK-Flash performs 4-step inference without classifier-free guidance and achieves 4.5x wall-clock speedup.

Conclusion: The proposed AuK model successfully unifies speech generation and editing capabilities through a single natural-language instruction interface, demonstrating strong performance across multiple task families. The distilled version maintains competitive performance while significantly reducing inference cost.

Abstract: We introduce AuK, an open-source foundational model that unifies speech generation and editing through a common interface of natural-language instructions and audio context. To support this broad capability set, we construct approximately 3.03 billion instruction--audio instances and 1.95 million hours of effective supervision across five task families: speech generation, content editing, enhancement and separation, paralinguistic editing, and acoustic editing. AuK combines a multimodal large language model for semantic conditioning, an VAE jointly trained on speech, general audio, and music for acoustic conditioning, and a hybrid rectified-flow Transformer that performs dual-stream MMDiT blocks followed by unified single-stream DiT blocks for generation. Training begins with generation-only warm-up and proceeds to joint generation--editing pre-training. We then apply complementary post-training strategies: human-feedback preference optimization for open-ended editing and reward-based reinforcement learning for speech generation. To reduce inference cost, we further distill the model with consistency initialization and task-routed Decoupled DMD. The resulting AuK-Flash performs 4-step inference without classifier-free guidance and achieves a 4.5 wall-clock speedup over the full model under matched conditions. Experiments demonstrate leading performance on zero-shot and instruction-controlled speech generation and general instruction-guided editing, while remaining competitive on signal-level restoration tasks. We release both the source code and model weights to support reproducibility and further research.

</details>


### [121] [Frequency Estimation Based on SNR-adaptive Frequency Estimator Under Wide SNR Range](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07034)
*Hee-Yang Jung,Dong-Hee Paek,Woo-Jin Jung,Seung-Hyun Kong*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper proposes an SNR-adaptive frequency estimator (SAFE) that combines a time-frequency image neural network (TFINet) for enhancing weak tone components at low SNR with an SNR-based frequency selector (SFS) that chooses between robust and super-resolution estimators. SAFE achieves 13.00% FNR at -10dB to 0dB SNR (13.04% improvement) and reduces NN-RMSE by 56.67% compared to state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing frequency estimation methods struggle in low SNR environments where weak tone components are buried in noise, and exhibit a trade-off between robustness at low SNR and precision at high SNR, making it difficult to achieve consistent superior performance across a wide SNR range.

Method: The proposed SAFE consists of two components: (1) TFINet, a time-frequency image neural network that enhances weak tone frequency components even at low SNR; (2) SFS, an SNR-based frequency selector that estimates the SNR of each tone frequency and selects either a robust frequency estimator or a super-resolution frequency estimator accordingly.

Result: SAFE achieves an FNR of 13.00% over the SNR range from -10 dB to 0 dB, representing a 13.04% improvement over the state-of-the-art method. Additionally, SAFE reduces NN-RMSE by 56.67% compared to the state-of-the-art method. Experiments on real-world data demonstrate robust performance in practical environments with clutter.

Conclusion: The proposed SAFE successfully overcomes the limitations of existing methods by achieving both robustness at low SNR and high precision at high SNR through the combination of TFINet for signal enhancement and SFS for adaptive estimator selection. The method demonstrates superior performance in both simulated and real-world environments.

Abstract: Frequency estimation is the problem of estimating individual tone frequencies from noisy multi-tone sinusoidal signals. Existing frequency estimation methods have difficulty accurately estimating both the number of tone frequencies and the individual tone frequencies in low signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) environments, because weak tone frequency components are buried in noise. In addition, existing methods generally exhibit a trade-off between robustness at low SNR and frequency estimation precision at high SNR, making it difficult to achieve consistently superior frequency estimation performance over a wide SNR range. To overcome these limitations, this paper proposes an SNR-adaptive frequency estimator (SAFE). SAFE consists of a time-frequency image neural network (TFINet), which enhances weak tone frequency components at low SNR, and an SNR-based frequency selector (SFS), which selects an appropriate frequency estimator according to the SNR of the estimated tone frequencies. TFINet enhances tone frequency components even in the low-SNR range, while SFS estimates the SNR of each tone frequency and selects either a robust frequency estimator or a super-resolution frequency estimator according to the estimated SNR. This enables SAFE to achieve robustness at low SNR while preserving high precision at high SNR. Simulation results show that SAFE achieves an False Negative Rate (FNR) of 13.00% over the SNR range from -10 dB to 0 dB, corresponding to an 13.04% improvement over the state-of-the-art method. In addition, SAFE reduces the Nearest Neighbor-Root Mean Squared Error (NN-RMSE) by 56.67% compared with the state-of-the-art method, demonstrating that SAFE performs more accurate frequency estimation. Furthermore, experiments using real-world data demonstrate that SAFE provides robust frequency estimation performance even in practical environments with clutter.

</details>


### [122] [Comparing Self-Supervised and Domain-Invariant Features for Cross-Domain Voice Phishing Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07079)
*Jeongmin Lee,Seung Yun,Minkyu Lee,Ran Han,Yoonkyu Woo,Jinxia Huang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses voice phishing (vishing) detection by comparing domain-invariant prosodic features with self-supervised learning (SSL) models (HuBERT, wav2vec2.0) under data constraints. Training uses actor-based scenario recordings while testing on authentic criminal calls, simulating a challenging cross-domain scenario where real fraud data is unavailable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voice phishing detection faces three critical challenges: (1) real criminal recordings are unavailable due to privacy constraints, (2) when available, only handfuls of samples exist for fine-tuning, and (3) there's a need for lightweight acoustic-only detection as an alternative to large self-supervised models.

Method: The study compares domain-invariant prosodic features against self-supervised representations (HuBERT, wav2vec2.0) through cross-domain evaluation. Training is performed on scenario-based actor recordings, while testing is conducted on authentic criminal calls. Both zero-shot and 5-shot learning scenarios are evaluated.

Result: Domain-invariant prosodic features achieve 69.5% F1 in zero-shot and 71.0% F1 with 5-shot learning. HuBERT achieves the highest performance at 94.2% F1 with 5-shot. wav2vec2.0 exhibits a precision-oriented detection profile, achieving 90.2% F1 with 99.4% precision (5-shot).

Conclusion: A fundamental trade-off exists: domain-invariant features enable zero-shot deployment when no real data exists, making them suitable for privacy-constrained scenarios. However, SSL methods (HuBERT, wav2vec2.0) achieve significantly higher performance but require real samples and greater computational resources. wav2vec2.0 offers a precision-oriented option for applications where false positives are costly.

Abstract: Voice phishing detection faces three critical challenges: real criminal recordings are unavailable due to privacy constraints; when available, only a handful of samples exist, insufficient for fine-tuning; and lightweight acoustic-only detection is needed as an alternative to large self-supervised models. We compare domain-invariant prosodic features and self-supervised representations (HuBERT, wav2vec2.0) through cross-domain evaluation-training on scenario-based actor recordings and testing on authentic criminal calls. Domain-invariant prosodic features achieve 69.5% F1 zero-shot and 71.0% with 5-shot learning. HuBERT achieves highest performance (94.2% F1, 5-shot), while wav2vec2.0 exhibits a precision-oriented detection profile (90.2% F1 with 99.4% precision, 5-shot). These findings reveal fundamental trade-offs: domain-invariant features enable zero-shot deployment when no real data exists, while SSL methods achieve higher performance but require real samples and compute.

</details>


### [123] [KABURI-TTS: Phoneme-Keyed Activity-conditioned Bi-channel Utterance Rendering for Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07200)
*Ryuichiro Higashinaka,Shinnosuke Takamichi,Tetsuji Ogawa*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: KABURI-TTS is a conversational text-to-speech system designed to synthesize full-duplex spoken dialogue with human-like overlap behavior between two speakers. It uses per-speaker phoneme raster input to generate two-channel, one-speaker-per-channel speech, controllable for overlap phenomena like backchannels, interruptions, and simultaneous speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conversational TTS engines are not robust to two-party simultaneous phenomena (backchannels, interruptions, overlaps) that occur during dialogue. There is a need for systems that can synthesize natural-sounding conversational speech with realistic overlap behavior.

Method: KABURI-TTS takes a per-speaker phoneme raster as input and renders speech of two speakers on separate channels. The generation is conditioned on per-frame phonemes and voice activity derived from them. Since the phoneme raster is supplied by a separate module, the method enables controllable generation of one-speaker-per-channel, two-party spoken dialogue.

Result: User evaluation shows that KABURI-TTS attains higher naturalness at both utterance and interaction levels compared to strong baselines. Analysis of voice activity confirms the method produces more overlap and more frequent turn-taking.

Conclusion: KABURI-TTS successfully synthesizes human-like conversational speech with overlap phenomena. The approach of using separate phoneme rasters for each speaker enables controllable dialogue generation with improved naturalness and realistic interaction patterns.

Abstract: Realizing full-duplex spoken dialogue requires large amounts of two-channel, one-speaker-per-channel conversational speech data. Although conversational text-to-speech (TTS) engines have been developed, they are not necessarily robust to two-party simultaneous phenomena such as backchannels, interruptions, and overlaps that occur while the interlocutor is speaking. In this work, aiming at conversational speech synthesis that reproduces human-like overlap, we propose KABURI-TTS. KABURI-TTS takes a per-speaker phoneme raster as input and renders the speech of the two speakers on separate channels, conditioned on the per-frame phonemes and the voice activity derived from them. Because the phoneme raster is supplied by a separate module, the proposed method enables controllable generation of one-speaker-per-channel, two-party spoken dialogue. A user evaluation shows that, compared with strong baselines, the proposed method attains higher naturalness at both the utterance and the interaction level. Furthermore, an analysis of voice activity confirms that the proposed method produces more overlap and more frequent turn-taking.

</details>


### [124] [Iterative Audio Separation with Mixture Consistency via MIMO Model Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07226)
*Yukara Ikemiya,WeiHsiang Liao,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A general framework for stable and effective iterative audio source separation with mixture consistency by extending traditional SISO/SIMO source separation models to a multi-input multi-output (MIMO) configuration, enabling iterative prediction while preserving architectural advantages and mixture consistency properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While iterative approaches like diffusion models achieve perceptually superior results in speech enhancement and user-guided separation, most existing methods focus on single-step separation with SISO/SIMO configurations. Mixture consistency is essential for applications requiring accurate phase and timbral information, but is typically viewed as a regression problem with unique solution, limiting exploration of iterative methods.

Method: Extended source separation architectures to MIMO configuration to enable iterative prediction without compromising architectural advantages or mixture consistency characteristics. Conducted ablation studies combining the framework with discriminators and extending it to a generative model.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements when applying the proposed framework to state-of-the-art separation models.

Conclusion: The MIMO framework successfully enables iterative audio separation while maintaining mixture consistency, providing a general approach that can enhance existing state-of-the-art separation models.

Abstract: This paper proposes a general framework for stable and effective iterative audio separation with mixture consistency by extending source separation models to a multi-input multi-output (MIMO) configuration. In the field of audio separation, mixture consistency is an essential property for many applications that require accurate phase and timbral information of target sources. While iterative approaches such as diffusion models achieve perceptually superior results in speech enhancement or user-guided target source separation tasks, most existing methods focus on single-step separation with a single-input single-output (SISO) or single-input multi-output (SIMO) configuration through architectural improvements, since mixture-consistent audio separation is generally regarded as a regression problem that admits a unique solution. By extending these architectures to a MIMO configuration, we introduce iterative prediction without compromising the architectural advantages or the characteristics of mixture consistency. We conduct a comprehensive ablation study of combining the framework with discriminators and extending it to a generative model. Experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements when applying the proposed framework to state-of-the-art separation models.

</details>


### [125] [TAD: Token-Adaptive Contrastive Decoding with Confidence-Guided Gating for Hallucination Mitigation in Large Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07286)
*Heyu Chang,Nianwen Si,Hao Zhang,Wenlin Zhang,Dan Qu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes Token-Adaptive Decoding (TAD), a training-free strategy to mitigate hallucinations in Large Audio-Language Models (LALMs) that tend to incorrectly answer "yes" to absent sound events in audio question answering tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large audio-language models can hallucinate audio objects by answering "yes" to absent sound events, which undermines their reliability in audio question answering applications.

Method: TAD grounds the initial yes/no decision by contrasting logits under real audio with a matched silent reference. It introduces a token-adaptive, confidence-guided gate that is decision-critical at the first decoding step and class-conditional on affirmative tokens. The method uses the audio-silent margin to avoid overcorrection when evidence is weak or already sufficient.

Result: On AudioCaps-Hallucination, TAD improved F1 for Qwen2 by 0.059 to 0.117 across Popular, Adversarial, and Random splits, and for Gemma by 0.025 to 0.064, compared to Audio-Aware Decoding (AAD). On Clotho-AQA, it raised F1 from 0.810 to 0.816 on Qwen2 and remained comparable to AAD on Gemma.

Conclusion: TAD effectively mitigates hallucinations in LALMs without requiring training, outperforming the contrastive baseline AAD while avoiding overcorrection through its confidence-guided, token-adaptive mechanism.

Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) can hallucinate audio objects, answering "yes" to absent sound events, thus undermining reliability in audio question answering. We propose Token-Adaptive Decoding (TAD), a training-free strategy for hallucination mitigation that grounds the initial yes/no decision by contrasting logits under real audio with a matched silent reference. TAD introduces a token-adaptive, confidence-guided gate that is decision-critical at the first decoding step and class-conditional on affirmative tokens, using the audio-silent margin to avoid overcorrection when evidence is weak or already sufficient. Experiments on AudioCaps-Hallucination show that, relative to Audio-Aware Decoding (AAD), a contrastive baseline with fixed contrast strength, TAD improves F1 for Qwen2 by 0.059 to 0.117 across Popular, Adversarial, and Random splits, and for Gemma by 0.025 to 0.064, while on Clotho-AQA it raises F1 from 0.810 to 0.816 on Qwen2 and remains comparable to AAD on Gemma.

</details>


### [126] [Silent Metronome: Rhythmic Grounding for Live Music Accompaniment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07688)
*Kevin Bretz,Derya Soydaner,Aske Plaat*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Silent Metronome (SiMe) is a system that provides live music accompaniment models with an explicit temporal reference (phase within beat/bar, tempo, time signature) as a conditioning channel, preventing rhythmic drift in causal streaming settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Live accompaniment models must generate music in real-time without future information, causing them to infer tempo, meter, and phase from imperfect past outputs. When input music is ambiguous, compounding errors lead to audible rhythmic drift—the model lacks a temporal reference and cannot correct itself.

Method: SiMe encodes metrical information (phase within beat, phase within bar, tempo, time signature) as periodic functions delivered as a separate conditioning channel independent of generated audio. Complementary auxiliary heads shape the latent representation, including a novel head that predicts the model's own future tokens.

Result: With ground-truth metrical annotations, beat alignment improves by 3.2x over the strictly causal baseline and surpasses a non-causal reference with 1-second look-ahead. Input-accompaniment coherence stays within 1 point of the reference.

Conclusion: Streaming accompaniment systems should treat rhythm as a shared signal (like human ensembles) rather than something to be inferred, leading to significantly better rhythmic accuracy and coherence.

Abstract: Live accompaniment models generate music for an incoming audio stream, committing to each output frame before hearing what comes next. In this strictly causal setting the model must infer tempo, meter, and metrical phase from its own imperfect past, whereby compounding errors quickly become audible as rhythmic drift. Put simply, the model has ears but no temporal reference, so when the ears hear imperfect, ambiguous music, the model will produce a flawed output. We propose Silent Metronome (SiMe), which gives it the temporal reference, encoding the phase within the beat and within the bar as periodic functions, pairing them with tempo and time signature, and supplying the result as a separate conditioning channel. Because this reference is independent of the generated audio, it cannot drift. Complementary auxiliary heads shape the latent representation, including a novel head that predicts the model's own future tokens. With the metrical signal taken from ground-truth annotations, beat alignment improves by a factor of 3.2 over the strictly causal baseline and surpasses a non-causal reference granted a full second of look-ahead. Coherence between input and accompaniment stays within a single point of that reference. These results suggest that streaming accompaniment systems should treat rhythm as a signal to be shared, as human ensembles do, rather than inferred.

</details>


### [127] [Clean Accuracy Does Not Guarantee Provenance Robustness: A Prospective Codec-Stress Evaluation of Audio Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07981)
*Gang Shi*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates how audio codec transcoding affects the ability to identify which AI system produced synthetic speech. While clean benchmarks show near-perfect accuracy, real-world transcoded audio shows significantly degraded attribution performance, with losses varying dramatically (53.5 points for WavLM, 49.8 for W2V2-BERT) depending on codec type and audio representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio provenance attribution achieves high accuracy on clean benchmarks, but real-world audio is typically transcoded through codecs. This gap between benchmark performance and deployment robustness was previously unquantified, creating a critical blind spot for forensic analysts.

Method: Prospectively registered measurement of closed-set attribution after single-stage codec transport, with analysis region fixed from fidelity metadata before any attribution model was trained. Tested on two corpora using WavLM-Base+, W2V2-BERT 2.0, ECAPA-TDNN, and Proxy-Anchor representations across multiple codec conditions.

Result: In-support losses reach 53.5-70.3 Macro-F1 for WavLM and 49.8-61.0 for W2V2-BERT. Degradation is strongly condition- and representation-dependent: WavLM losses range from -0.4 to +53.5 points across conditions. The two encoders differ beyond a ±5-point margin at 6 of 12 conditions. MP3 at 8 kbit/s causes the largest loss but ranks differently on SI-SDR vs PESQ-WB metrics.

Conclusion: Clean accuracy figures do not characterize deployment robustness. Waveform and perceptual measures order codec conditions differently, and the effect is not confined to one representation family or weak linear heads. A clean-qualified ECAPA-TDNN and Proxy-Anchor head degrade comparably.

Abstract: Audio provenance attribution - which system produced a synthetic utterance - is reported at near-ceiling accuracy on clean benchmarks, yet audio reaching an analyst has usually been transcoded. We report a prospectively registered measurement of closed-set attribution after single-stage codec transport, with the analysis region fixed from fidelity metadata before any attribution model was trained. On two corpora, in-support losses reach 53.5 [43.5, 63.6] and 70.3 [63.0, 77.5] Macro-F1 points for WavLM-Base+, and 61.0 [56.8, 65.1] and 49.8 [41.6, 57.9] for W2V2-BERT 2.0, under simultaneous component-level bands. Degradation is strongly condition- and representation-dependent: within one in-support grid WavLM losses run from -0.4 to +53.5 points, and the two encoders differ beyond a prespecified +/-5-point margin at six of twelve conditions. A clean-qualified ECAPA-TDNN and a Proxy-Anchor head degrade comparably, so the effect is not confined to one representation family or a weak linear head. The registered matched-fidelity comparison was not estimable on this grid, and waveform and perceptual measures order the conditions differently: MP3 at 8 kbit/s ranks mid-grid on SI-SDR but last on PESQ-WB while causing the largest loss. For the tested tasks, corpora, representations and codec grid, a clean accuracy figure does not by itself characterise deployment robustness.

</details>


### [128] [Geometry-Informed Distributed Acoustic Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08026)
*Yiyuan Yang,Shitong Xu,Niki Trigoni,Andrew Markham*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A geometry-informed distributed acoustic scene understanding framework that uses distributed microphones, audio spectrogram transformers, topology-aware GNNs, and a frozen LLM to generate spatially consistent narratives of multi-room environments, outperforming centralized baselines under occluded conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing acoustic scene understanding systems rely on single centralized microphone arrays, which fail in multi-room environments because walls and doors block sound signals. There is a need for distributed acoustic sensing that can overcome occlusion and provide comprehensive spatial understanding.

Method: The framework uses distributed microphones combined with an audio spectrogram transformer and a topology-aware graph neural network to fuse spatio-temporal acoustic features. These features are decoded into discrete semantic triplets, which are then combined with environmental geometry through a frozen large language model to perform spatial understanding and infer missing transitions.

Result: The framework outperforms centralized baselines and improves spatial consistency under simulated occlusion, as demonstrated on a custom multi-room simulator.

Conclusion: The proposed geometry-informed distributed approach effectively addresses the limitations of centralized acoustic scene understanding systems in multi-room environments, enabling spatial understanding, inference of plausible missing transitions, and generation of physically consistent narratives.

Abstract: Acoustic scene understanding in multi-room environments is a difficult task. Most existing systems use a single centralized microphone array, and they often fail because walls and doors block sound signals. To address this challenge, we propose a geometry-informed distributed acoustic scene understanding framework. Our system leverages distributed microphones and uses an audio spectrogram transformer and a topology-aware graph neural network to fuse spatio-temporal acoustic features. Then, these features are decoded into discrete semantic triplets. Finally, a frozen large language model combines these symbolic observations with the environmental geometry. This allows the system to perform spatial understanding, infer plausible missing transitions, and generate a physically consistent narrative of the scene. Experiments on a custom multi-room simulator demonstrate that our framework outperforms centralized baselines and improves spatial consistency under simulated occlusion.

</details>


### [129] [Stabilizing Instruction Supervision for Instruct-TTS via Controllable Diversification and Drift Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08204)
*Yizhong Geng,Kecan Mao,Qifei Li,Cong Wang,Yingming Gao,Ruimin Wang,Chunfeng Wang,Hao Li,Ya Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper addresses "instruction supervision instability" in Instruct-TTS systems, where LLM rewriting of style labels into training instructions causes semantic drift (corrupting supervision). Over 40% of unconstrained rewrites contain this drift. The authors propose a data-centric stabilization recipe with three mechanisms: controllable instruction diversification, LLM-based drift filtering, and attribute-aligned supervision. Their method achieves 56.4% instruction-following (vs. 34.5% without fine-tuning and 51.0% with naive fine-tuning) and reduces drift from 40.4% to 15.4% on the Chinese split of InstructTTSEval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruct-TTS systems use LLMs to expand structured style labels into natural-language training instructions. However, the authors discovered that over 40% of unconstrained rewrites contain semantic drift, which corrupts supervision signals and weakens generalization. This problem was formalized as "instruction supervision instability," representing a significant challenge in instruction-driven TTS generation.

Method: The proposed solution is a data-centric stabilization recipe with three complementary mechanisms: (1) Controllable instruction diversification for systematic expansion of instruction coverage; (2) LLM-based drift filtering for quality control to identify and remove drifted instructions; (3) Attribute-aligned supervision that grounds prosody control in acoustic perturbations to ensure alignment between instruction semantics and acoustic features.

Result: On the Chinese split of InstructTTSEval: (1) Instruction-following improved from 34.5% (no fine-tuning) to 51.0% (naive fine-tuning) to 56.4% with the proposed recipe; (2) Constrained rewriting reduced semantic drift from 40.4% to 15.4%. Ablation studies confirmed that the three mechanisms are complementary and each contributes to the overall improvement.

Conclusion: The three proposed mechanisms (controllable diversification, drift filtering, and attribute-aligned supervision) work synergistically to address instruction supervision instability. The drift taxonomy developed in this work may generalize to instruction-driven generation beyond TTS, suggesting broader applicability for instruction-following systems in other domains.

Abstract: Instruct-TTS systems expand structured style labels into natural-language training instructions through LLM rewriting, yet we find that over 40% of unconstrained rewrites contain semantic drift that corrupts supervision and weakens generalization. We formalize this problem as instruction supervision instability and propose a data-centric stabilization recipe that jointly improves coverage and fidelity through three mechanisms: controllable instruction diversification for systematic expansion, LLM-based drift filtering for quality control, and attribute-aligned supervision that grounds prosody control in acoustic perturbations. On the Chinese split of InstructTTSEval, our recipe raises instruction-following from 34.5% without fine-tuning and 51.0% with naive fine-tuning to 56.4%, while constrained rewriting reduces drift from 40.4% to 15.4%. Ablations confirm the three mechanisms are complementary, and the drift taxonomy may generalize to instruction-driven generation beyond TTS.

</details>


### [130] [Noise Adaptive Streaming Audio-Visual Speech Token Enhancement for Robust Full-Duplex Spoken Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08390)
*Bella Godiva,Yeonju Kim,Yong Man Ro*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AV-STE is a modular streaming audio-visual front-end that restores corrupted semantic speech tokens from noisy audio and lip video before they reach a speech LLM, enabling improved response coherence in full-duplex dialogue systems without requiring multimodal training of the downstream model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex spoken dialogue systems face challenges with audio-only perception under background noise and overlapping speech, leading to incoherent responses. While audio-visual approaches using lip movements improve robustness, existing methods require costly multimodal training of large speech dialogue models.

Method: AV-STE is proposed as a modular streaming audio-visual front-end that restores corrupted semantic speech tokens from noisy audio and lip video before they reach the speech LLM. The downstream dialogue model remains entirely frozen, preserving its pretrained conversational capabilities.

Result: When integrated with frozen Moshi, AV-STE improves average GPT-4o-judged response coherence from 1.42 to 1.91 under same-dataset speaker interference while largely preserving turn-taking behavior. Gains also transfer to out-of-domain Seamless Interaction.

Conclusion: The proposed approach demonstrates that modular audio-visual front-ends can effectively restore corrupted speech tokens without modifying the downstream LLM, achieving significant improvements in response coherence under noise while preserving the model's original capabilities and transferring to out-of-domain scenarios.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue systems enable simultaneous listening and speaking, but their audio-only perception often fails under background noise and overlapping speech, leading to incoherent responses. Recent audio-visual dialogue approaches show that incorporating visual cues such as lip movements improve robustness under audio corruption. However, existing approaches often adapt the large speech dialogue model itself to process visual input, requiring costly multimodal training. We propose AV-STE, a modular streaming audio-visual front-end that restores corrupted semantic speech tokens from noisy audio and lip video before they reach the speech LLM. The downstream dialogue model remains entirely frozen, preserving its pretrained conversational capabilities. When integrated with frozen Moshi, AV-STE improves average GPT-4o-judged response coherence from 1.42 to 1.91 under same-dataset speaker interference while largely preserving turn-taking behavior. Gains also transfer to out-of-domain Seamless Interaction.

</details>


### [131] [X2Streaming-ASR: wait when uncertain, emit when ready for streaming ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08672)
*Zhiwei Lin,Kaiqi Fu,Rime Wen,Zehan Liu,Shawn Qin,Roy Gan,Hao Wang,Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: X2Streaming-ASR is a novel streaming automatic speech recognition system that optimizes both when to commit and what to commit in real-time voice agents. It uses a three-stage training procedure to achieve low commit latency (27-84ms) while maintaining recognition accuracy, outperforming baseline systems on multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming ASR systems use fixed chunk sizes, look-ahead, or target delay approaches that do not directly optimize the additional context to use at each output position under a single-pass, hard-commit constraint. Real-time voice agents and full-duplex dialogue require accurate partial transcripts with low commit latency, but current methods achieve 409-585ms latency, which is too high for natural dialogue.

Method: The proposed X2Streaming-ASR decomposes streaming recognition into two sub-problems: when to commit and what to commit. It employs a three-stage training procedure: (1) establish streaming recognition ability, (2) warm-start the commit policy with automatically probed trajectories, and (3) refine the policy using character-level, segment-assigned group-relative rewards for recognition accuracy and latency.

Result: X2Streaming-ASR achieves a mean character-level commit latency of 27-84 ms relative to forced-aligned character endpoints, compared with 409-585 ms for evaluated streaming baselines. It achieves the best streaming CER among evaluated systems on AISHELL-1 and AISHELL-3 with substantially lower latency, across AISHELL-1/2/3 and WenetSpeech datasets.

Conclusion: The decomposition approach of X2Streaming-ASR into "when to commit" and "what to commit" with the three-stage training procedure effectively addresses the latency-accuracy tradeoff in streaming ASR. The significant latency reduction (5-20x improvement) while maintaining or improving recognition accuracy demonstrates the effectiveness of directly optimizing the commit policy rather than using fixed parameters.

Abstract: Streaming automatic speech recognition (ASR) for real-time voice agents and full-duplex dialogue must provide accurate partial transcripts with low commit latency. Existing systems commonly use a fixed chunk size, look-ahead, or target delay, or encourage emissions near estimated acoustic boundaries. These approaches do not directly optimize how much additional context to use at each output position under a single-pass, hard-commit constraint. We propose X2Streaming-ASR, which decomposes streaming recognition into when to commit and what to commit. Its three-stage training procedure first establishes streaming recognition ability, then warm-starts the commit policy with automatically probed trajectories, and finally refines the policy using character-level, segment-assigned group-relative rewards for recognition accuracy and latency. Across AISHELL-1/2/3 and WenetSpeech, X2Streaming-ASR achieves a mean character-level commit latency of 27-84 ms relative to forced-aligned character endpoints, compared with 409-585 ms for the evaluated streaming baselines. It achieves the best streaming CER among the evaluated systems on AISHELL-1 and AISHELL-3 with substantially lower latency.

</details>


### [132] [TontaubeV1: Streaming Text-to-Speech with Hierarchical Codec Modeling and Bounded Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08703)
*Fritz Cremer,Jonathan Cremer*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: TontaubeV1 is a text-to-speech system that achieves natural prosody while enabling streaming inference on a single consumer GPU (RTX 5090). It uses a hierarchical DualCodec representation separating semantic and acoustic streams, with one 1.7B parameter transformer predicting the semantic stream and three 0.6B transformers adding progressive acoustic refinements. The model achieves ~200ms latency to first audio, matches ElevenLabs Flash v2.5 on prosody, and outperforms several commercial TTS systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-speech systems typically face a trade-off between natural prosody and efficient inference: higher perceptual quality requires increased computational cost and latency. The authors aim to preserve natural prosody while enabling streaming from a single consumer GPU.

Method: The model uses hierarchical DualCodec representation at 12.5 Hz, separating semantic stream from acoustic refinements. A Qwen3-1.7B-derived transformer predicts the semantic stream (and utterance duration), while three progressively smaller Qwen3-0.6B-derived transformers each add one acoustic refinement. Text is tokenized per character. Overlapping DualCodec reconstructions are mapped into VibeVoice acoustic latent space and decoded causally for streaming. The model accepts up to one minute of reference audio for voice conditioning.

Result: The four predictors total 2.9B parameters. On a single RTX 5090, streaming path reaches approximately 200 ms to first audio. End-to-end real-time factor (RTF) is 0.08 for one input and 0.02 across eight concurrent inputs. On the LLM-as-a-judge audiobook-reading benchmark, TontaubeV1 matches ElevenLabs Flash v2.5 and outperforms Fish Audio S2 Pro, the April 2026 Gradium API, and Cartesia Sonic 3 on prosody.

Conclusion: TontaubeV1 successfully achieves natural prosody with efficient streaming inference on consumer hardware. It matches or outperforms several commercial TTS systems on prosody while maintaining low latency. The model weights are released under Tontaube Community Model License 1.0.

Abstract: Text-to-speech systems often face a trade-off between natural prosody and efficient inference: higher perceptual quality typically comes at increased computational cost and latency. We present TontaubeV1, a model that preserves natural prosody while enabling streaming from a single consumer GPU. Speech is encoded by the hierarchical DualCodec representation at 12.5 Hz, which separates a semantic stream from successive acoustic refinements. Our design assumes that prosodic structure is largely established when the semantic stream is generated, and allocates capacity accordingly: a Qwen3-1.7B-derived transformer predicts that stream and thereby the utterance duration, while three progressively smaller Qwen3-0.6B-derived transformers each add one acoustic refinement. Text is tokenized per character rather than by subword. Paired text and audio markers at shared positions support long-form generation with bounded context, and overlapping DualCodec reconstructions are mapped into the VibeVoice acoustic latent space and decoded causally, enabling streaming despite DualCodec's noncausal decoder. The model accepts up to one minute of reference audio for voice conditioning and is designed primarily for English and German, with additional multilingual support. The four predictors total 2.9B parameters; on a single RTX 5090 the streaming path reaches approximately 200 ms to first audio. In separate non-streaming measurements, the end-to-end real-time factor (RTF) is 0.08 for one input and the aggregate RTF is 0.02 across eight concurrent inputs. On our LLM-as-a-judge audiobook-reading benchmark, TontaubeV1 matches ElevenLabs Flash v2.5 and outperforms Fish Audio S2 Pro, the April 2026 Gradium API, and Cartesia Sonic 3 on prosody. The model weights are released on Hugging Face under the Tontaube Community Model License 1.0.

</details>


### [133] [From Scores to Evidence: Auditable Decisions Can Improve Speech Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08899)
*Mengzhe Geng,Yujia Lu,Patrick Littell,Manuela Kunz,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes an auditable decision record for speech deepfake detection that preserves provenance information (passive detector score, keyed-probe score, retrieval support, and speaker-profile margin) while still producing a single operating score for thresholding. The system achieves 8.43% EER through late calibration and exposes 82.85% of errors at a 33.75% review budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current speech deepfake detectors output only a single score per utterance, which is useful for ranking but provides no explanation for why borderline cases should be trusted, deferred, or reviewed. Different utterances can fall in the same score band for different reasons (e.g., passive and retrieval evidence disagreement, unavailable keyed probe).

Method: The authors introduce an auditable decision record carrying four aligned cues into a late calibration step: (1) passive detector score, (2) conditional keyed-probe score on a marked derivative, (3) retrieval support, and (4) speaker-profile margin, together with explicit disagreement coordinates. Evaluation was performed on the ASVspoof 5 Track 1 matched subset (4,080 examples).

Result: Fixed retrieval-augmented rule improved EER from 15.84% to 11.91% compared to retrieval-only evidence. Late calibration over the full record achieved 8.43% EER. At a 33.75% review budget, the exposed cue union covered 82.85% of the calibrated model's errors. The best passive WavLM run achieved 6.71% EER.

Conclusion: The decision record is not intended as a stronger standalone detector (best passive WavLM at 6.71% EER is better), but serves to preserve the evidence behind each surfaced utterance while still producing one operating score for thresholding and review. The system enables auditors to understand why decisions were made rather than just receiving a scalar score.

Abstract: Speech deepfakes can mimic a speaker's voice convincingly enough to deceive listeners and automated systems. This has driven strong progress in speech deepfake detection, but most detectors still end with one score per utterance. That score is useful for ranking systems, yet it says little about why a borderline item should be trusted, deferred, or reviewed. Two utterances can fall in the same score band for different reasons, for example because passive and retrieval evidence disagree or because the keyed probe is unavailable. We ask whether the final decision can remain scalar without discarding that provenance. We answer this question with an auditable decision record that carries four aligned cues into a late calibration step: a passive detector score, a conditional keyed-probe score on a marked derivative, retrieval support, and a speaker-profile margin, together with explicit disagreement coordinates. On the 4,080-example ASVspoof 5 Track 1 matched subset, the fixed retrieval-augmented rule improves on retrieval-only evidence, from 15.84 percent to 11.91 percent EER, and late calibration over the full record reaches 8.43 percent EER. At a 33.75 percent review budget, the exposed cue union covers 82.85 percent of the calibrated model's errors. The best passive WavLM run still reaches 6.71 percent EER, so we do not present the decision record as a stronger standalone detector. Its contribution is to preserve the evidence behind each surfaced utterance while still producing one operating score for thresholding and review.

</details>


### [134] [Disentangled Global-Local Feature Learning with E-Branchformer for Audio Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08948)
*Phuong Tuan Dat,Ho Bao Thu,Nguyen Tran Trung,Pham Viet Hoang,Nguyen Thi Thu Trang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes an E-Branchformer-based audio deepfake detection system that uses self-supervised learning representations with a dual-branch architecture combining global self-attention and local convolutional processing, achieving state-of-the-art results on ASVspoof 2021 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid advancement of voice synthesis technologies (text-to-speech and voice conversion) poses significant threats to speech-based authentication systems, necessitating robust deepfake detection methods to protect biometric security.

Method: The proposed E-Branchformer architecture employs parallel branches to capture both global contextual dependencies (via multi-head self-attention) and local temporal patterns (via convolutional processing). It integrates depthwise convolution (DWConv) and Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) modules to enhance classification token with refined patch token information after feature merging, leveraging self-supervised speech representations.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on ASVspoof 2021 LA, DF, and In-the-Wild datasets with Equal Error Rates (EER) of 0.88%, 1.85%, and 6.30% respectively, substantially outperforming existing methods. The model demonstrates strong generalization capability to diverse acoustic conditions and unseen spoofing attacks in real-world scenarios.

Conclusion: The dual-branch architecture provides complementary discriminative information; Squeeze-and-Excitation Aggregation significantly improves SSL feature integration; and the combination of DWConv and SE modules is critical for effective class token enhancement. The approach shows superior performance in real-world scenarios with strong generalization capability.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of voice synthesis technologies such as text-to-speech and voice conversion poses significant threats to speech-based authentication systems, necessitating robust deepfake detection methods. In this work, we propose a novel E-Branchformer-based architecture that effectively leverages self-supervised speech representations for audio deepfake detection. Our model employs parallel branches to simultaneously capture global contextual dependencies through multi-head self-attention and local temporal patterns through convolutional processing. To enhance discriminative capability, we integrate depthwise convolution and Squeeze-and-Excitation modules that enrich the classification token with refined patch token information after feature merging. Extensive experiments on ASVspoof 2021 LA, DF, and In-the-Wild datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance with equal error rates of 0.88%, 1.85%, and 6.30% respectively, substantially outperforming existing methods. Comprehensive ablation studies validate that the dual-branch architecture provides complementary discriminative information, Squeeze-and-Excitation Aggregation significantly improves SSL feature integration, and the combination of DWConv and SE modules is critical for effective class token enhancement. The superior performance on real-world scenarios demonstrates strong generalization capability to diverse acoustic conditions and unseen spoofing attacks.

</details>


### [135] [TASTE2: Text-Aligned Speech Modeling and Deployment toward Full-Duplex Voice Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08956)
*Yi-Chang Chen,Chun Wei Chen,Dien-Ruei Wu,Jie Lin,Yu-Kuan Fu,Yang-Hsien Lin,Eddie TC Huang,Simon See,Hung-yi Lee,Da-Shan Shiu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: TASTE2 transforms utterance-level speech tokenization into an incremental dialogue system for full-duplex voice interaction, achieving 98.2% text-only reference accuracy retention while enabling streaming synthesis and barge-in handling. The system identifies natural conversation robustness, latency, and paralinguistic control as remaining open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex voice interaction requires more than utterance-level conversion - it needs to process streaming speech, manage turn-taking and interruptions, while preserving pretrained linguistic competence and acoustic paralinguistic cues. The goal is to determine if TASTE (Text-Aligned Speech Tokenization and Embedding) provides a viable path toward this goal.

Method: TASTE2 transforms utterance-level TASTE into an incremental dialogue stack with: (1) Shared text-token vocabulary removing word-level averaging; (2) Modality-aligned dialogue training predicting one continuous audio latent per text token without interleaving heterogeneous token streams; (3) Incremental Speech Detokenizer enabling streaming synthesis through CosyVoice2. The system was evaluated on LLaMA-Questions and Full-Duplex-Bench v1.0.

Result: TASTE2 (Merge) achieves 56.3% on LLaMA-Questions against a 57.3% Qwen2.5-7B text-only reference (98.2% accuracy retention), while TASTE2 (Direct) reaches 53.0% (92.4% retention). TASTE2 VoiceBot handles interruptions well while maintaining high conversational coherence on Full-Duplex-Bench v1.0. Deployed mean time to first audio is 2.701s on two NVIDIA RTX A6000 after TensorRT acceleration. Paralinguistic control shows fast speaking rate works as cross-strategy proof of concept, while emotion control is strategy-dependent.

Conclusion: TASTE-based modeling establishes a practical route toward full-duplex systems. Remaining open challenges include natural conversation robustness, speech generation latency, and feature general paralinguistic control.

Abstract: Full-duplex voice interaction requires more than utterance-level conversion. It must process streaming speech, manage turn-taking and interruptions, while preserving pretrained linguistic competence and acoustic paralinguistic cues. We ask whether TASTE (Text-Aligned Speech Tokenization and Embedding) provides a viable path toward this goal. We present TASTE2, which transforms utterance-level TASTE into an incremental dialogue stack. A shared text-token vocabulary removes word-level averaging, while modality-aligned dialogue training predicts one continuous audio latent per text token without interleaving heterogeneous token streams. An incremental Speech Detokenizer enables streaming synthesis through CosyVoice2. After speech and dialogue training, TASTE2 (Merge) reaches 56.3% on LLaMA-Questions against a 57.3% Qwen2.5-7B Instruct text-only reference (98.2% accuracy retention), and TASTE2 (Direct) reaches 53.0% (92.4% retention). We build TASTE2 VoiceBot, which processes user speech incrementally, streams synthesized audio, and stops generation on barge-in. On Full-Duplex-Bench v1.0, TASTE2 and TASTE2 VoiceBot handle interruptions well while maintaining high conversational coherence. Natural conversation remains challenging, and deployed mean time to first audio is 2.701 s on two NVIDIA RTX A6000 after TensorRT acceleration. Finally, to our knowledge, we provide the first systematic characterization of explicit paralinguistic control in a TASTE based model. Fast speaking rate serves as a cross-strategy proof of concept after dialogue SFT, while emotion control is strategy dependent and the remaining attributes stay weak. Together, these results establish TASTE based modeling as a practical route toward full-duplex systems while identifying natural conversation robustness, speech generation latency, and feature general paralinguistic control as open challenges. Explore TASTE2 online.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [136] [Beyond Right and Wrong: Evaluating Second-order Social Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05437)
*Sunny Rai,Jinyi Kuang,Reyhan Jamalova,Annie Lou,Cristina Bicchieri,Niyati Malhotra,Victor Hugo Orozco-Olvera,Ana Maria Munoz-Boudet,Lyle H Ungar,Sharath C Guntuku*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel framework for evaluating metanorm reasoning (second-order social expectations about norm enforcement) in LLMs, along with a new dataset NormReact. Current LLMs overestimate negative sanctions and underrepresent tolerance in actual human norm enforcement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous AI alignment efforts focused only on first-order social norms (what is acceptable), but ignored second-order expectations about who enforces norms and how. This gap limits AI's ability to understand social intelligence, which is critical for applications in conflict mediation and policy simulation.

Method: The authors propose a framework evaluating metanorm reasoning along two dimensions (emotional appraisal and behavioral response), with two classification tasks: predicting self-regulation in violators and other-regulation in observers. They created NormReact, a dataset of 450 norm violation scenarios annotated for emotions and behavioral responses across gender and social closeness variables.

Result: Across six tested LLMs, models overpredict negative sanctions where humans would expect inaction. Alignment with human judgments deteriorates as social distance increases. LLMs portray a harsher social world than actual human behavior.

Conclusion: AI systems in norm-sensitive domains may produce distorted pictures of social regulation—over-representing punishment while under-representing tolerance, restraint, and relational calibration that characterize real-world norm enforcement. This highlights the need for better alignment with human social expectations.

Abstract: Previous AI alignment efforts have focused primarily on first-order social norms -- teaching models what is socially acceptable or unacceptable (e.g., `do not steal'). However, social intelligence depends not only on norm recognition, but also on anticipating who will enforce it and how (e.g., public shame or even imprisonment). These second-order expectations, known as metanorms, govern how people respond when social rules are broken. We introduce a novel framework for evaluating metanorm reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) along two dimensions: emotional appraisal and behavioral response, and propose new classification tasks, namely, predicting self-regulation in violators, and other-regulation in observers. We release a multi-perspective dataset, NormReact, of 450 norm violation scenarios, hand-annotated for emotions and behavioral responses across norm violators' gender and observers' social closeness. Current LLMs portray a harsher social world: across six models, they overpredict negative sanctions where humans would expect inaction, and alignment with human judgments deteriorates as social distance increases. These findings suggest that AI systems in norm-sensitive domains from conflict mediation to policy simulation, may risk producing a distorted picture of social regulation: one that over-represents punishment and under-represents the tolerance, restraint, and relational calibration that characterize actual norm enforcement in real world.

</details>


### [137] [CriticGen: Generation-Aware Evaluation as Actionable Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05439)
*Huifang Du,Zecheng Zuo,Sen Wang,Chenghao Fan,Haofen Wang,Yehui Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CriticGen is a fine-grained, generation-aware evaluation framework for large language models that creates sample-specific evaluation dimensions and scoring criteria to produce actionable feedback including scores, reasons, executable refinement suggestions, and refined answers. It addresses the limitation of coarse-grained, generic evaluation methods that fail to provide actionable improvement feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation methods are coarse-grained and decoupled from generation, producing generic explanations that fail to provide actionable feedback for model improvement. There's a need for evaluation frameworks that are both instance-specific and actionable.

Method: CriticGen generates sample-specific evaluation dimensions and scoring criteria under high-level categories (subjective, objective, and self-derived constraints). These criteria serve as a dynamic rubric for jointly producing a score, a reason, an executable refinement suggestion, and a refined answer. This rubric-conditioned refinement process enables models to diagnose flaws and perform targeted answer improvement.

Result: CriticGen improves relevance/coverage from 3.33/4.03 to 3.97/4.24. Achieves best score correlations with 0.9556 Pearson and 0.9560 Spearman. Raises F1 of criterion-grounded reasons and executable suggestions from 0.6369/0.5994 to 0.7554/0.7900. Its feedback improves 73.17% of answers with a 93.28% non-degradation rate.

Conclusion: Fine-grained evaluation should be both instance-specific and actionable. CriticGen induces higher-quality rubrics and its feedback translates into reliable answer improvement, demonstrating the value of generation-aware evaluation frameworks.

Abstract: Current evaluation methods for large language models are coarse-grained and decoupled from generation, producing generic explanations that fail to provide actionable feedback for model improvement. We propose CriticGen, a fine-grained, generation-aware evaluation framework that turns evaluation into actionable control for answer improvement. CriticGen first generates sample-specific evaluation dimensions and scoring criteria under high-level categories such as subjective, objective, and self-derived constraints. These criteria then serve as a dynamic rubric for jointly producing a score, a reason, an executable refinement suggestion, and a refined answer. This rubric-conditioned refinement process enables models to diagnose flaws and perform targeted answer improvement. Experimental results show that fine-grained evaluation should be both instance-specific and actionable. CriticGen induces higher-quality rubrics, improving relevance/coverage from 3.33/4.03 to 3.97/4.24. CriticGen also achieves the best score correlations, with 0.9556 Pearson and 0.9560 Spearman, and raises the F1 of criterion-grounded reasons and executable suggestions from 0.6369/0.5994 to 0.7554/0.7900. Crucially, its feedback translates into reliable answer improvement, improving 73.17% of answers with a 93.28% non-degradation rate.

</details>


### [138] [RAFM-SER++: A Lightweight Multimodal Emotion Recognition Framework for Real-Time Behavioral Monitoring in Surveillance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07409)
*Ngo Truong Dinh,Tung-Lam Bui,Chi-Trung Duong,Vien Nguyen Thi,Viet-Anh Nguyen,Phuc-Lu Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAFM_SER++ is a lightweight multimodal Speech Emotion Recognition framework that uses an asymmetric Residual Attention Fusion Mechanism to inject affective speech cues into semantic text representations through a one-directional pathway, achieving superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off compared to state-of-the-art methods while reducing trainable parameters by over 60%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal SER systems achieve high accuracy but require computationally expensive bidirectional cross-modal transformers, making them unsuitable for latency-sensitive and resource-constrained surveillance systems. There is a need for lightweight alternatives that maintain performance while reducing computational overhead.

Method: The proposed RAFM_SER++ framework features: (1) an asymmetric Residual Attention Fusion Mechanism (RAFM) that injects affective speech cues into semantic text representations through a one-directional residual attention pathway (avoiding expensive bidirectional interactions), (2) a BYOL-inspired cross-modal alignment objective, and (3) attention-guided pooling for improved multimodal representation learning.

Result: On IEMOCAP and ESD benchmarks, RAFM_SER++ consistently outperforms the HuBERT-Base baseline. Compared to state-of-the-art MemoCMT, it reduces trainable parameters by more than 60%, achieves faster inference (79.60 it/s), and attains BACC scores of 81.10% on IEMOCAP and 95.39% on ESD.

Conclusion: Lightweight asymmetric multimodal fusion is an effective alternative to interaction-heavy cross-modal transformers for real-time surveillance applications, offering a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Abstract: Recent multimodal Speech Emotion Recognition (SER) systems achieve high accuracy through interaction-heavy cross-modal transformers, but their computational cost limits deployment in latency-sensitive and resource-constrained surveillance systems. To address this challenge, we propose RAFM_SER++, a lightweight multimodal SER framework featuring an asymmetric Residual Attention Fusion Mechanism (RAFM). Rather than relying on computationally expensive bidirectional interactions, RAFM injects affective speech cues into semantic text representations through a one-directional residual attention pathway. Combined with a BYOL-inspired cross-modal alignment objective and attention-guided pooling, the proposed framework improves multimodal representation learning while maintaining low computational overhead.
  Experiments on the IEMOCAP and ESD benchmarks demonstrate that RAFM_SER++ consistently outperforms the HuBERT-Base baseline and achieves a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off compared with the state-of-the-art MemoCMT. Specifically, RAFM_SER++ reduces trainable parameters by more than 60%, achieves faster inference (79.60 it/s), and attains BACC scores of 81.10% on IEMOCAP and 95.39% on ESD. These results indicate that lightweight asymmetric multimodal fusion is an effective alternative to interaction-heavy cross-modal transformers for real-time surveillance applications.

</details>


### [139] [When Does Memory Help? A Cost-Aware Evaluation of Long-Term Memory in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05441)
*Shweta Mishra,Shashank Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MERIT is a benchmark that evaluates long-term memory for LLM agents by measuring whether remembered facts actually change agent behavior in tool-use tasks, not just conversational recall. It provides episodic tasks with explicit cost accounting and finds that memory implementation choice significantly impacts task success (up to 60 points), while embedding retrieval fails on updated facts and update-on-write stores outperform hybrid approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory benchmarks (LoCoMo, LongMemEval) only measure question answering over dialogue history, failing to assess whether remembered facts actually change what a tool-using agent does in real tasks.

Method: MERIT provides: (1) episodic tool-use tasks in three domains with leak verification; (2) difficulty ladder ending in updated-fact recall; (3) controlled memory corruption; (4) full token and dollar metering. Tested on 23,440 episodes across GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Haiku 4.5, and Claude Sonnet 5 with various memory implementations including embedding retrieval, structured fact stores, LLM summarization, and hybrid approaches.

Result: Memory lifts task success from leak-verified floor of 0.00 to 0.55-1.00. On updated facts: embedding retrieval collapses unpredictably (0.30-0.95, max seed gap 0.45); agents act on correctly retrieved value only 55% of time; update-on-write stores (structured fact store and LLM summarization) maintain 0.70-1.00; hybrid is worse than fact store alone. Full replay never economical: best condition delivers 2.7-3.9x marginal utility per dollar.

Conclusion: Memory implementation choice significantly impacts agent performance (up to 60 points). Update-on-write stores outperform embedding retrieval on updated facts. Full replay is never cost-effective. The benchmark and traces are released for further research.

Abstract: Long-term memory for LLM agents is evaluated today by conversational recall benchmarks (LoCoMo, LongMemEval), which measure question answering over dialogue history, not whether remembered facts change what a tool-using agent does. We present MERIT (Memory Evaluation for Realistic Instrumented Tasks), a benchmark and harness that measures the marginal utility of memory for task-executing agents under explicit cost accounting. MERIT provides episodic tool-use tasks in three domains whose dependence on earlier-episode facts is verified by an automated leak check; a difficulty ladder ending in updated-fact recall; controlled memory corruption; and full token and dollar metering of every memory operation. Across 23,440 scored episodes ($42.57), a two-generation pilot on gpt-4.1-mini and a preregistered 3-model x 3-seed grid (GPT-4.1, Claude Haiku 4.5; memory side held fixed), memory lifts dependent-task success from a leak-verified floor of 0.00 to 0.55-1.00. On updated facts, embedding retrieval collapses unpredictably (0.30-0.95 across models; max seed gap 0.45), and agents act on a correctly retrieved value only 55% of the time, while update-on-write stores (a structured fact store and, notably, LLM summarization) remain at 0.70-1.00; the hybrid is worse than the fact store alone. A latest-generation spot-check (Claude Sonnet 5, gated on a clean full-replay control) reproduces the pattern. Swapping a memory's implementation moves task success by up to 60 points, and full replay is never economical: the best condition per domain delivers 2.7-3.9x its marginal utility per dollar. We release the benchmark, harness, and all traces.

</details>


### [140] [AutoFyn Technical Report: Non-Parametric Expert Iteration for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05446)
*Adib Hasan,Daniel Schaffield,Akashnil Dutta,Tarik Adnan Moon*

Main category: cs.AI
