TL;DR: AutoFyn is an agent harness inspired by Expert Iteration that adapts frozen language models across multiple rounds by updating persistent state from verified reward signals rather than model weights. It uses an orchestrator for exploration/planning, specialized agents for building alternatives, and a task-grounded verifier for objective reward signals that update the effective policy for subsequent rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improve AI model performance on complex tasks by creating a feedback loop that doesn't require modifying the underlying model weights. The system aims to leverage persistent state (memory files, reports, repository state) to accumulate knowledge across rounds while starting fresh each round from a baseline model session.

Method: AutoFyn uses a multi-agent architecture with: (1) an orchestrator that explores, plans and builds alternative approaches with specialized agents, (2) a task-grounded verifier that validates work and supplies objective rewards, (3) persistent state that distills rewards back for the next round. Each round begins from a fresh model session, with durable information reintroduced through explicit interfaces (persistent memory, reports, repository state).

Result: Three domain demonstrations: (1) Olympiad mathematics: On six fresh problems of the 2026 IMO, every model with room to improve achieved higher scores under AutoFyn than with provider's own coding agent. (2) Data science: Built the top-ranked agent on Spider 2.0 dbt benchmark. (3) Cybersecurity: Produced 16 maintainer-confirmed vulnerability advisories in Next.js, MetaMask, pnpm, Warp, LiteLLM, Langflow, and Open WebUI.

Conclusion: AutoFyn demonstrates that adapting frozen models through persistent state updates and verified reward signals can significantly improve performance across diverse domains (mathematical reasoning, data science, security). The approach successfully scales to real-world tasks including competitive mathematics (IMO), benchmark performance (Spider 2.0), and practical security vulnerability discovery.

Abstract: We introduce AutoFyn, an agent harness inspired by the Expert Iteration algorithm, adapting a frozen model across many rounds by updating persistent state from verified reward signals rather than model weights. Each round begins from a fresh model session, and durable information is reintroduced only through explicit interfaces such as persistent memory files, reports, and repository state. Within a round, an orchestrator explores, plans and builds many alternative approaches with specialized agents, while a task-grounded verifier verifies the work and supplies an objective reward for measuring progress. This reward is distilled back into the persistent state, which updates the effective policy for the next round. In this technical report, we formalize this loop and describe its persistent state and verification interfaces. We then demonstrate its use in three domains, namely olympiad mathematics, data science, and cybersecurity. On the six fresh problems of the 2026 International Mathematical Olympiad, every model with room to improve scores higher under AutoFyn than in its provider's own coding agent. AutoFyn also built the top-ranked agent on the Spider 2.0 dbt benchmark, and has produced $16$ maintainer-confirmed vulnerability advisories in Next.js, MetaMask, pnpm, Warp, LiteLLM, Langflow, and Open WebUI.

</details>


### [141] [Damage-Aware Bandit Pruning for Vision and Language Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05448)
*Salem Ameen,Sunil Vadera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates structured post-training pruning of transformers (attention heads and MLP channel groups) as a damage-aware multi-armed bandit problem. It uses paired damage measurement and UCB-style or Thompson Sampling policies to sequentially select units for pruning. Experiments across multiple language and vision transformer models show the method generally outperforms baseline selection methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured pruning of transformers requires selecting functional units whose removal causes minimal performance degradation. Existing methods either lack budget awareness or don't properly account for batch-to-batch variation in damage estimation. The authors aim to develop a principled approach that efficiently allocates a fixed budget to evaluate candidates and select pruning units.

Method: The method models structured-unit selection as a multi-armed bandit problem. Key innovations include: (1) Paired damage measurement = masked loss - base loss on the same calibration batch to reduce variation; (2) Smooth bounded reward for UCB-style policy or fractional-Beta Thompson Sampling; (3) Sequential mask construction adding one unit per step. Attention heads and MLP channel groups are temporarily masked on calibration batches to measure their importance.

Result: Across five seeds, bandit methods generally reduce degradation relative to budgeted greedy in paired language-model comparisons. Of 28 highlighted comparisons, 23 bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero, 11 have p < 0.05, and 6 have q < 0.05 after Benjamini-Hochberg correction. Matched-evaluation shows gains for ViT-B/16 and Swin-Tiny are not explained by larger candidate-evaluation budget alone.

Conclusion: The bandit-based approach provides a principled framework for structured pruning of transformers, effectively balancing exploration and exploitation under budget constraints. The method shows consistent improvements over baseline selection strategies across diverse models and datasets, with statistical significance in a majority of comparisons.

Abstract: Structured post-training pruning of transformers requires selecting complete functional units whose suppression causes limited degradation. We formulate structured-unit selection for language and vision transformers as a damage-aware multi-armed bandit problem under a fixed candidate-evaluation budget. Attention heads and MLP channel groups are temporarily masked on calibration batches. Paired damage is the masked loss minus the base loss on the same batch, reducing batch-to-batch variation. A smooth bounded reward drives either a UCB-style policy or fractional-Beta Thompson Sampling, and the final mask is constructed sequentially by adding one unit at each step. The selected units are functionally zeroed in the original dense checkpoint; therefore, the reported parameter effects represent effective structural suppression rather than physical compression or measured speedup. Experiments on WikiText-2, LAMBADA, and Imagenette cover GPT-2, OPT, Pythia, Qwen2.5, SmolLM2, ViT-B/16, DeiT-Tiny, and Swin-Tiny, with comparisons against random, magnitude, static-saliency, and budgeted-greedy selection. Across five seeds, the bandit methods usually reduce degradation relative to budgeted greedy in the paired language-model comparisons. Of 28 comparisons highlighted in the paper, 23 bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero and 11 paired tests have p < 0.05; six have q < 0.05 after Benjamini-Hochberg correction across the full family of 116 dataset-wise tests. Matched-evaluation results for ViT-B/16 and Swin-Tiny indicate that their gains are not explained solely by a larger candidate-evaluation budget.

</details>


### [142] [Compiling VGDL into Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05459)
*Mohit Jiwatode,Bodo Rosenhahn,Alexander Dockhorn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a deterministic framework that compiles video games specified in VGDL (Video Game Description Language) into Dynamic Structural Causal Models (DSCMs), addressing limitations of RL agents relying on spurious correlations and LLMs hallucinating game rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reinforcement learning agents tend to rely on spurious correlations, while large language models are prone to hallucinating game rules. Although causal reinforcement learning improves interpretability, there is currently no formal methodology to map complex game mechanics directly into causal models.

Method: The methodology directly translates game components (sprite dynamics, interaction rules, and termination conditions) into explicit structural equations. Each game tick represents a causal transition from state variables at time t to t+1, establishing a grounded mapping that guarantees absolute causal fidelity to the ground-truth game mechanics.

Result: The resulting models offer transparent causal pathways that support counterfactual reasoning, causal reinforcement learning agent training, and procedural content validation. This framework provides a principled bridge between symbolic game descriptions and causally grounded game AI.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables formal causal modeling of video games with guaranteed fidelity to ground-truth mechanics, supporting applications in counterfactual reasoning, causal RL training, and procedural content validation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning and large language models often struggle to accurately capture the causal mechanics of game environments. Standard reinforcement learning agents tend to rely on spurious correlations, while large language models are prone to hallucinating game rules. Although causal reinforcement learning improves interpretability, there is currently no formal methodology to map complex game mechanics directly into causal models. To address this, we propose a deterministic framework that compiles games specified in the Video Game Description Language into Dynamic Structural Causal Models. Rather than inferring causal structures from gameplay traces or noisy large language models' outputs, our methodology directly translates game components, including sprite dynamics, interaction rules, and termination conditions, into explicit structural equations. Each game tick represents a causal transition from state variables at time $t$ to $t+1$. By establishing this grounded mapping, the approach guarantees absolute causal fidelity to the ground-truth game mechanics. The resulting models offer transparent causal pathways that support counterfactual reasoning, causal reinforcement learning agent training, and procedural content validation. This framework provides a principled bridge between symbolic game descriptions and causally grounded game AI.

</details>


### [143] [ARC-Bench: Closed-Loop Replanning Masks Broken Action Ranking in Frozen JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05461)
*Zhengshu Zhang,Zhiyuan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper exposes a critical flaw in reward-free latent world models that plan by scoring actions using latent space distances: these models systematically rank suboptimal actions as top choices. This defect is masked by closed-loop replanning, making it invisible to standard evaluation protocols. The authors introduce ARC-Bench, a proper auditing protocol, and demonstrate that the assumption of "action-rankability" in frozen latent spaces fails severely across multiple domains and encoder architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of latent world models (JEPA-WM) relies on closed-loop success rates, which silently mask a fundamental flaw: the assumption that latent distance correlates with action quality. The authors sought to directly audit whether frozen JEPA-style objectives can correctly rank candidate actions - a property they term "action-rankability."

Method: The authors introduce ARC-Bench, a no-leak, fixed-candidate protocol that directly measures action ranking accuracy. They test official JEPA-WM checkpoints across navigation and manipulation domains, include controlled experiments with different encoders (DINOv2, V-JEPA 1, V-JEPA 2 at ViT-L/ViT-G scale), and conduct ablation studies varying replanning frequency to expose how closed-loop settings mask the underlying defect.

Result: The assumption fails severely and structurally: on official manipulation audits, the top-scored candidate is almost always suboptimal. The same ranking inversion appears in maze domains. This defect persists across all tested encoders. When replanning frequency is reduced, success collapses in both navigation and manipulation domains, and episodes rescued by frequent replanning are enriched for severe first-plan ranking failures.

Conclusion: Closed-loop success rates systematically overstate the rankability of frozen latent representations. The defect remained invisible because closed-loop replanning masks it. ARC-Bench provides proper measurement, and the replanning mechanism provides the explanation, for methods that adapt, amortize, or replan around latent-space planners without directly auditing JEPA-WM action rankability.

Abstract: Reward-free latent world models plan by scoring candidate actions with distances in a frozen latent space: an action is preferred if its predicted future embedding lands closer to the goal embedding. This silently assumes that latent closeness is action-rankable, i.e., that ordering candidates by latent distance agrees with ordering them by true cost. We audit this assumption directly. We introduce ARC-Bench, a no-leak, fixed-candidate protocol that measures whether frozen JEPA-style objectives rank candidate actions correctly, and apply it to official released JEPA-WM checkpoints across navigation and manipulation-style control. The assumption fails, severely and structurally: on the official manipulation audits the top-scored candidate is almost always suboptimal, and the same inversion appears in the maze domains. A controlled visual-backbone extension shows that the defect persists when DINOv2 is replaced by video-pretrained V-JEPA 1 and V-JEPA 2 encoders at ViT-L/ViT-G scale. Provenance, undertraining, matched-budget backbone controls, and metric-circularity controls rule out trivial explanations. We then explain why this defect has stayed invisible: closed-loop replanning masks it. When we reduce the planner's replanning frequency, success collapses in both a navigation and a manipulation domain, and the episodes rescued by frequent replanning are enriched for severe first-plan ranking failures in the PointMaze first-plan diagnostic. Closed-loop success rates therefore systematically overstate the rankability of frozen latent representations. ARC-Bench supplies the measurement, and the masking mechanism the explanation, for methods that adapt, amortize, or replan around latent-space planners without directly auditing released JEPA-WM action rankability.

</details>


### [144] [RAPID: Reliability-Aware Pair Importance Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05481)
*Ali Mahdavi,Azadeh Zamanifar,Amirfarhad Farhadi,Omid Kashefi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAPID introduces reliability-aware relational distillation for knowledge distillation, separating target reliability from evaluation priority to efficiently use limited relation budgets in text classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing all pairs in relational distillation has quadratic complexity in batch size, and uniform subsampling may inefficiently use limited relation budgets. The paper aims to address this inefficiency by separating target reliability from evaluation priority.

Method: RAPID proposes two modular components: (1) A reliability gated relational target that determines which teacher relations are emphasized based on calibrated teacher entropy and detached student-teacher residuals; (2) A full support adaptive pair proposal that determines which relations are evaluated. Exact inverse proposal correction ensures unbiased loss and gradient estimators with respect to the gated mini batch target.

Result: On AG News (BERT-to-DistilBERT, budget=256): gated target achieves 94.285±0.054%, RAPID achieves 94.241±0.025%, baseline achieves 94.154±0.124%. On SST-2 (DistilBERT-to-DistilBERT, budget=64): gated target achieves 88.800±0.532%, RAPID achieves 88.685±0.462%, baseline achieves 87.271±0.162%. The gated target yields highest mean accuracy in both settings.

Conclusion: The gated relational target achieves the highest mean student accuracy on both datasets. The results support the modular view that target reliability and evaluation priority are separable design dimensions, enabling efficient use of limited relation budgets in relational distillation.

Abstract: Inter example relational distillation transfers a teacher's representation geometry by matching relations among examples within a mini batch. Computing all pairs has quadratic complexity in the batch size, whereas uniform subsampling may use a limited relation budget inefficiently. We introduce Reliability Aware Pair Importance Distillation, or RAPID, which separates a reliability gated relational target from a full support adaptive pair proposal. Reliability determines which teacher relations are emphasized, while calibrated teacher entropy and detached student-teacher residuals determine which relations are evaluated. Exact inverse proposal correction makes the loss and gradient estimators conditionally unbiased with respect to the gated mini batch target. We evaluate RAPID in two text classification settings: AG News with BERT-to-DistilBERT distillation using three paired seeds and a relation budget of 256, and SST-2 with DistilBERT to DistilBERT distillation using three paired seeds and a relation budget of 64. Reliability gated relational distillation achieves the highest observed mean student accuracy on both datasets: 94.285 plus or minus 0.054 percent on AG News and 88.800 plus or minus 0.532 percent on SST-2. RAPID ranks second, achieving 94.241 plus or minus 0.025 percent and 88.685 plus or minus 0.462 percent, respectively, compared with 94.154 plus or minus 0.124 percent and 87.271 plus or minus 0.162 percent for the cross entropy baseline. Pilot evaluations are counted toward the same total budget as the main relation evaluations. Across both settings, the gated target yields the highest mean accuracy, while the adaptive proposal remains within seed-level variation. These results support the modular view that target reliability and evaluation priority are separable design dimensions.

</details>


### [145] [PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN: Prior-Guided Joint Probabilistic Forecasting of Clinical Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05488)
*Weizhi Nie,Rihao Chang,Weijie Wang,Yuting Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN is a trajectory-first framework for joint probabilistic forecasting of multivariate physiology, combining missingness-aware temporal encoders, soft organ-system prior, patient-specific relations, nonlinear Kolmogorov-Arnold messages, and low-rank multivariate Student-t head. It achieves second-lowest normalized MAE (0.37727) and lowest RMSE (0.52656) on MIMIC-IV data, but a derived risk score (AUROC 0.603) underperforms a dedicated GRU-D classifier (AUROC 0.650).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical deterioration unfolds through coupled, partially observed trajectories rather than a single diagnostic label, necessitating a trajectory-first approach to capture multivariate physiology dynamics.

Method: PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN integrates: (1) missingness-aware temporal encoders to handle incomplete data, (2) soft organ-system prior for physiological domain knowledge, (3) patient-specific relational structure, (4) nonlinear Kolmogorov-Arnold message passing, and (5) low-rank multivariate Student-t head for probabilistic forecasting. Evaluated on 24-hour histories with six-hour forecasts on 6,882 patients (54,694 windows) from MIMIC-IV.

Result: Among 13 models across 5 seeds, PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN achieves second-lowest normalized MAE (0.37727 ± 0.00029) and lowest RMSE (0.52656 ± 0.00034), reducing MAE by 0.52% vs deterministic TimeKAN. Probabilistic metrics: marginal NLL 0.66380, CRPS 0.27301. Empirical coverage: 0.533 (50%), 0.831 (80%), 0.958 (95%). Ablation: relational structure provides largest gain; higher covariance rank improves likelihood but not point accuracy. Trajectory-derived risk score (AUROC 0.603) trails dedicated GRU-D classifier (AUROC 0.650).

Conclusion: Joint trajectory forecasting provides an inspectable intermediate task for clinical monitoring, but accurate physiology forecasts alone do not ensure a calibrated event detector. The gap between forecasting accuracy and clinical utility (AUROC 0.603 vs 0.650) highlights that predictive performance on physiological trajectories does not directly translate to reliable deterioration detection, limiting immediate clinical applicability.

Abstract: Clinical deterioration unfolds through coupled, partially observed trajectories, not a single diagnostic label. We introduce PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN, a trajectory-first framework for joint probabilistic forecasting of multivariate physiology. It combines missingness-aware temporal encoders, a soft organ-system prior, patient-specific relations, nonlinear Kolmogorov-Arnold messages, and a low-rank multivariate Student-t head. We evaluate 24-hour histories and six-hour forecasts on a frozen MIMIC-IV-derived cohort of 6,882 patients and 54,694 windows. Across five seeds and 13 models, PGP-Clinical-TimeKAN obtains the second-lowest normalized MAE (0.37727 +/- 0.00029) and the lowest RMSE (0.52656 +/- 0.00034). It reduces MAE by 0.52% relative to deterministic TimeKAN. For probabilistic forecasting, it reaches a marginal NLL of 0.66380 and a CRPS of 0.27301. Empirical coverage is 0.533, 0.831, and 0.958 for nominal 50%, 80%, and 95% intervals. Removing relational structure causes the largest ablation loss. Increasing covariance rank improves joint likelihood but has little effect on point accuracy. A trajectory-derived risk score remains weaker than a dedicated GRU-D classifier (AUROC 0.603 versus 0.650), which limits the present clinical claim. Joint trajectory forecasting therefore provides an inspectable intermediate task, but accurate physiology forecasts alone do not ensure a calibrated event detector.

</details>


### [146] [SciLitBench: Benchmark and Design Principles for LLM-Powered Systematic Literature Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05505)
*Miguel Zabaleta,Baihan Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciLitBench is a multi-stage benchmark for evaluating LLMs across systematic review tasks, covering title/abstract screening, full-text screening, and data extraction, with 42,981 records and 1,012 full texts. The study evaluates 22 open-weight LLMs and finds that explicit criteria improve screening performance, while data extraction shows declining accuracy across different fields, with models recovering only 30% of evaluation evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations for systematic reviews examine review stages in isolation, but systematic reviews require sustained human judgment across thousands of records. The researchers identified a need for a comprehensive benchmark that spans multiple stages of the systematic review process.

Method: The researchers created SciLitBench, a multi-stage benchmark with 42,981 retrieved records, 1,012 full texts, and annotations for 888 included papers. They evaluated 22 open-weight LLMs from six model families across three stages: title and abstract screening, full-text screening, and schema-guided data extraction. They tested the impact of explicit inclusion/exclusion criteria and researcher-authored rationales on performance.

Result: Explicit inclusion and exclusion criteria improved title and abstract screening F2 by 28.8%. Researcher-authored rationales improved full-text screening by 15%. For data extraction, performance declined from 0.97 accuracy for publication year to 0.37 Jaccard overlap for computational approach. The strongest models recovered only 30% of annotated evaluation evidence and 25% of limitations.

Conclusion: SciLitBench identifies a practical boundary between high-recall screening and evidence-complete extraction. It provides a reproducible resource for evaluating LLM-assisted evidence synthesis and reveals that different reliability regimes exist across systematic review stages.

Abstract: Systematic reviews require sustained human judgment across thousands of records, yet existing evaluations of large language models (LLMs) typically examine review stages in isolation. We introduce SciLitBench, a multi-stage benchmark spanning title and abstract screening, full-text screening, and schema-guided data extraction, with 42,981 retrieved records, 1,012 full texts, and annotations for 888 included papers. Across 22 open-weight LLMs from six model families, explicit inclusion and exclusion criteria improve title and abstract screening $F_2$ by 28.8\%, while researcher-authored rationales improve full-text screening by 15\%. Data extraction reveals a different reliability regime: performance declines from 0.97 accuracy for publication year to 0.37 Jaccard overlap for computational approach, while the strongest models recover only 30\% of annotated evaluation evidence and 25\% of limitations. SciLitBench identifies a practical boundary between high-recall screening and evidence-complete extraction and provides a reproducible resource for evaluating LLM-assisted evidence synthesis.

</details>


### [147] [SCAFFOLD: Self-Improving Web Agents via Recursive Parametric Skill Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05511)
*Bowei He,Xiaokun Zhang,Meng Ding,Xue Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scaffold is a self-improving framework for visual web agents that induces parametric executable skills from successful trajectories, maintains a recursively composed skill hierarchy, compacts the library via MDL criterion, and periodically distills knowledge back into model weights. It achieves 11.1-17.2% absolute improvement over baselines across three benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Web agents need to navigate visually rich, long-horizon interfaces across different sites, but previous agents learn each task in isolation and discard procedural knowledge. Existing skill-augmented frameworks treat the skill library as flat/two-tier cache without principled mechanisms for compressing redundancy or composing skills recursively.

Method: Four key components: (i) Induces parametric, executable skills from successful trajectories under multi-instance abstraction constraint; (ii) Maintains recursively composed hierarchy where higher-level skills invoke lower-level ones; (iii) Compacts library via minimum-description-length (MDL) criterion and behavioral equivalence checking; (iv) Periodically distills skill-augmented trajectories back into model weights.

Result: Across WebArena, VisualWebArena, and held-out Online-Mind2Web, Scaffold improves success rate by 11.1-17.2 absolute points over the strongest skill-augmented baseline, with monotonic gains across five self-improvement iterations without library collapse.

Conclusion: Scaffold demonstrates effective self-improvement for visual web agents through hierarchical skill composition, MDL-based compression, and knowledge distillation, achieving significant improvements over baselines while maintaining stability across iterations.

Abstract: Web agents need to navigate visually rich, long-horizon interfaces that change across sites, yet most previous agents still learn each task in isolation and discard the procedural knowledge they accumulate. Recent skill-augmented frameworks take an important first step, but they treat the skill library as a flat or two-tier prompt-side cache and offer no principled mechanism for compressing redundancy or composing skills recursively. We introduce \textsc{Scaffold}, a self-improving framework for visual web agents that (i) induces parametric, executable skills from successful trajectories under a multi-instance abstraction constraint, (ii) maintains a recursively composed hierarchy in which higher-level skills invoke lower-level ones, (iii) compacts the library via a minimum-description-length (MDL) criterion and behavioral equivalence checking, and (iv) periodically distills skill-augmented trajectories back into model weights to internalize the abstractions. Across WebArena, VisualWebArena, and a held-out split of Online-Mind2Web, \textsc{Scaffold} improves success rate by $11.1$--$17.2$ absolute points over the strongest skill-augmented baseline and shows monotonic gains across five self-improvement iterations without library collapse. We release the code and documents in the Github \href{https://github.com/BokwaiHo/SCAFFOLD}{repository}.

</details>


### [148] [Reasoning-Aware Compression: Identifying and Protecting Vulnerable Reasoning Circuits for Energy-Efficient LLM Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05512)
*Leonard Twagirayezu,Prasenjit Mitra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A reasoning-aware compression framework that selectively quantizes Large Reasoning Models to preserve critical reasoning circuits while reducing energy consumption, validated across five reasoning benchmarks with hardware-level GPU energy measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) impose substantial energy costs during deployment, but current uniform quantization methods risk damaging critical reasoning circuits.

Method: The framework benchmarks quantization conditions across five reasoning benchmarks (GSM8K, FOLIO, MATH-500, ProofWriter, MuSiQue) with hardware-level GPU energy measurement; profiles per-module INT8 vulnerability across 196-224 (layer, projection) pairs via perturbation sweep on calibration data; then selectively restores the most sensitive circuits to FP16.

Result: Three key findings: (1) INT4 quantization can increase energy by extending reasoning chains—a 25% power reduction becomes net energy increase on GSM8K; (2) Vulnerability is task-dependent—attention projections more critical for mathematical reasoning, with sensitivity patterns differing by architecture in logical inference; (3) Selective compression achieves Pareto-optimal points—R1-Qwen-7B Top-10% on ProofWriter gains +12 pp over FP16 at -9.7% energy.

Conclusion: The framework achieves superior performance-energy tradeoffs than uniform quantization methods, validated on held-out data across five reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) impose substantial energy costs during deployment, yet current compression methods apply uniform quantization across all components, risking damage to critical reasoning circuits. We present a reasoning-aware compression framework that benchmarks quantization conditions across five reasoning benchmarks, GSM8K, FOLIO, MATH-500, ProofWriter, and MuSiQue, with hardware-level GPU energy measurement; profiles per-module INT4 vulnerability across all 196-224 (layer, projection) pairs via a perturbation sweep on a held-out calibration split, then selectively restores the most sensitive circuits to FP16. Three findings emerge. First, INT4 quantization can increase energy by extending reasoning chains; a 25% power reduction becomes a net energy increase on GSM8K. Second, vulnerability is task-dependent: attention projections are more critical for mathematical reasoning, and sensitivity patterns differ by architecture in logical inference. Third, selective compression achieves Pareto-optimal points inaccessible to uniform methods: R1-Qwen-7B Top-10% on ProofWriter gains +12 pp over FP16 at -9.7% energy, validated on held-out data across five reasoning benchmarks.

</details>


### [149] [When and What to Teach: Budget-Aware Online Adaptation for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05513)
*Jianwei Zhang,Sihan Cao,Pengcheng Zheng,Ya Wen,Pei Ke,Kuien Liu,Shen Gao,Wei Dong,Yang Yang,Chaoning Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of deploying web agents in real-world environments using lightweight local models that can adapt online via teaching from a stronger teacher model. The key problem is that standard interactive feedback is prohibitively expensive, and conventional trajectory-level preference optimization wastes budget on unresolvable episodes and redundant execution turns. The proposed solution is Score-Guided Online Teaching with Budgeted Trajectory Trimming, which uses a solvability-aware teacher gate to decide when to query the teacher and a score-guided turn selection mechanism to decide what informative turns to retain. Experiments on MiniWoB and TimeWarp show comparable first-pass success while reducing teacher calls by 22.6% and student training compute by 52.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying web agents in real-world environments requires continuous online adaptation, but using powerful proprietary models is commercially cost-prohibitive. Practitioners must rely on lightweight local models that evolve post-deployment via online teaching from a stronger teacher. However, standard interactive feedback imposes prohibitive costs, and conventional trajectory-level preference optimization wastes budget on both unresolvable episodes and redundant execution turns.

Method: The proposed framework, Score-Guided Online Teaching with Budgeted Trajectory Trimming, integrates two key components: (1) a solvability-aware teacher gate to dictate when to query the teacher model, and (2) a score-guided turn selection mechanism to decide what informative turns to retain. This budget-aware framework systematically orchestrates when and what to teach.

Result: Extensive experiments on MiniWoB and TimeWarp demonstrate that the method achieves comparable first-pass success while reducing teacher calls by 22.6% and student training compute by 52.1% on average.

Conclusion: The proposed Score-Guided Online Teaching with Budgeted Trajectory Trimming framework effectively addresses the inefficiencies of conventional trajectory-level preference optimization by intelligently deciding when to query the teacher and what informative turns to retain. This results in significant reductions in teacher calls and training compute while maintaining comparable performance, making it a practical solution for deploying web agents in real-world environments.

Abstract: Web agents have achieved significant success in automating complex internet tasks but deploying them in real-world environments requires continuous online adaptation. Given that deploying powerful proprietary models remains commercially cost-prohibitive, practitioners must rely on lightweight local models that evolve post-deployment via online teaching from a stronger teacher. However, standard interactive feedback imposes prohibitive costs. We show that conventional trajectory-level preference optimization wastes budget on both unresolvable episodes and redundant execution turns. To resolve these inefficiencies, we propose \textbf{Score-Guided Online Teaching with Budgeted Trajectory Trimming}, a budget-aware framework that systematically orchestrates \textbf{when} and \textbf{what} to teach. Specifically, our framework integrates a solvability-aware teacher gate to dictate \textbf{when} to query the teacher model and a score-guided turn selection mechanism to decide \textbf{what} informative turns to retain. Extensive experiments on MiniWoB and TimeWarp demonstrate that our method achieves comparable first-pass success while reducing teacher calls by 22.6\% and student training compute by 52.1\% on average. Our code is available at https://github.com/zjw131f1fc/budgeted-online-teaching.

</details>


### [150] [The Failure Happens Before the Drift: The Social Dynamics of Values in LLM Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05514)
*Farah Atif,Sougata Saha,Monojit Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether LLM-based agents can faithfully simulate diverse human value systems in social science research. Using a WVS-grounded simulation framework with ~4,000 conversations across 1,200 personas, 15 topics, and 3 models, the study finds significant limitations: over 50% of personas fail to express their assigned WVS profiles initially, 2-7% drift over time, and simulated value distributions systematically deviate from assigned profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical question in social science methodology: whether LLM agents can serve as valid proxies for human participants in studying diverse and conflicting human value systems. This is increasingly important as researchers adopt LLM-based agents in social science experiments.

Method: The study employs a World Values Survey (WVS)-grounded simulation framework where culturally diverse agents with different communication styles engage in longitudinal, value-laden discussions. They conducted approximately 4,000 conversations involving 1,200 personas, 15 topics, and three models (GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-Flash, and Gemma-4-E4B), evaluating value faithfulness, value drift, and conversational realism through ablation studies removing demographic details.

Result: Over 50% of personas fail to express their assigned WVS profiles from the outset. 2-7% drift after repeated conversations. Removing demographic details improves faithfulness for some models but doesn't change the broader trend. Simulated value distributions systematically deviate from assigned WVS profiles. Compared to human discussions, simulated dialogues show different trade-offs between stylistic consistency and semantic diversity—often producing content-wise varied but stylistically repetitive exchanges.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents can generate plausible conversations but remain limited proxies for representing and preserving diverse human value profiles over time. They cannot faithfully simulate diverse and conflicting human value systems as required for social science research.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents are increasingly used as proxies for human participants in social science research, yet it remains unclear whether they can faithfully simulate diverse and conflicting human value systems. We present a World Values Survey (WVS)-grounded simulation framework where culturally diverse agents with different communication styles engage in longitudinal, value-laden discussions. Across approximately 4,000 conversations involving 1,200 personas, 15 topics, and three models (GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-Flash, and Gemma-4-E4B), we evaluate value faithfulness, value drift, and conversational realism. We find that more than 50\% of personas fail to express their assigned WVS profiles from the outset, while 2-7\% drift after repeated conversations. Ablations removing demographic details improve faithfulness for some models but do not change the broader trend: simulated value distributions still systematically deviate from the assigned WVS profiles. Compared to human discussions, simulated dialogues show a different trade-off between stylistic consistency and semantic diversity, often producing content-wise varied but stylistically repetitive exchanges. These findings suggest that current LLM agents can generate plausible conversations, but remain limited proxies for representing and preserving diverse human value profiles over time.

</details>


### [151] [Beyond "AI Helps Humans": Decision-Targeted Evaluation Design for Human-Agent Teams in the Agentic Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05527)
*Hamed Khosravi,Xiaoming Huo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TEAM-Design is a method for deciding which tasks to replay under human-only versus AI-only baselines when evaluating human-AI collaborative workflows, optimizing under fixed replay budgets by raising replay probabilities where baseline outcomes are hard to predict and comparisons are difficult to establish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When deploying human-AI workflows, we need to compare them against human-only and AI-only alternatives. However, once deployed, we don't observe these counterfactual outcomes. Replaying tasks under alternative workflows is expensive (costs expert time or compute). Under a fixed replay budget, existing methods don't directly address which tasks should receive which type of replay.

Method: TEAM-Design assigns each task two replay probabilities (one per baseline). It raises replay probability where: (1) the missing baseline outcome is hard to predict from available task information, and (2) that comparison is harder to establish. It lowers probability where replay is expensive. The method includes theoretical guarantees that random drawing from recorded probabilities controls false positive rates when declaring a workflow beats both baselines.

Result: Reanalysis of 6 clinical settings showed no human-AI workflow beats both alternatives; one coding benchmark does. TEAM-Design performs best when one comparison is clearly harder than the other, but can underperform variance-based allocation when both comparisons are similarly difficult.

Conclusion: TEAM-Design provides a principled approach to budgeted deployment decisions for human-AI workflows, with theoretical guarantees. It is most valuable when comparisons have asymmetric difficulty, though simpler variance-based methods may suffice when comparisons are equally challenging.

Abstract: Wherever a coding agent works under engineer supervision, or a clinical model assists a radiologist, the deployment question is whether to keep the human-AI workflow or replace it with the human alone or the agent alone. The human-AI workflow is worth keeping only if it beats both of those alternatives. Yet once it is deployed, neither alternative outcome is observed: recovering one means replaying the task under that alternative, and every replay costs expert time or compute. Under a fixed replay budget, the design question is therefore which tasks should be more likely to receive a human-only replay, and which an agent-only replay. Existing methods do not directly target this decision. Agent benchmarks do not choose which missing baseline to measure, variance-based sampling ignores which of the two comparisons is closer to failing, and Bayesian information methods focus on learning model parameters instead of making the deployment decision. We propose TEAM-Design, a rule that gives every task two replay probabilities, one per baseline. It raises a probability where the missing baseline outcome is hard to predict from what is already known about the task and where that comparison is harder to establish, and lowers it where replay is expensive. We prove that the rule solves this budgeted design problem, and that drawing the replays at random from recorded probabilities still controls the chance of wrongly declaring that the workflow beats both. We reanalyze 6 clinical settings, where no human-AI workflow beats both alternatives, and a coding benchmark, where one does, then evaluate TEAM-Design on synthetic designs and on a semi-synthetic design built from a real chest X-ray reader study. TEAM-Design works best when one of the two comparisons is clearly harder to settle than the other, and can do worse than variance-based allocation when the two are similarly difficult.

</details>


### [152] [EdgeMem: LLM-Free Agent Memory Construction and Retrieval via Evidence-Preserving Multi-Anchor Hypergraph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05553)
*Zeyang Cui,Jiannong Cao,Zhiyuan Wen,Bo Yuan,Junlan Feng,Shengyuan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EdgeMem is an agent-memory method that preserves original interaction turns and organizes them through multi-anchor hypergraphs with content, temporal, and episodic cues, avoiding generative LLM calls for memory management while achieving strong retrieval and QA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent memory methods compress interaction histories into LLM-generated summaries, which adds cost and risks discarding answer-bearing details before knowing what future queries will require.

Method: EdgeMem constructs a multi-anchor hypergraph using lightweight local processing with complementary content, temporal, and episodic cues. Retrieval directly returns source evidence without generative LLM calls.

Result: On LoCoMo, EdgeMem achieves the highest strict-judge score (61.01) among seven reproduced systems under shared prompt (vs 58.70). On LongMemEval-S, strong retrieval and memory-grounded QA performance is demonstrated. Construction and retrieval require no generative-LLM calls.

Conclusion: Preserving and organizing source evidence provides an effective and efficient foundation for agent memory without generative memory management, demonstrating that lightweight local processing can replace costly LLM-based summarization.

Abstract: Agent memory allows LLM agents to use earlier interactions when answering new queries. Existing methods often compress interaction histories into summaries or other LLM-generated representations. Repeated generation adds cost and can discard answer-bearing details before the system knows what a future query will require. We propose EdgeMem, an agent-memory method built around a simple principle: preserve original interaction turns and organize them through complementary content, temporal, and episodic cues. EdgeMem realizes this principle with a multi-anchor hypergraph constructed by lightweight local processing. Retrieval directly returns source evidence and reserves LLM use for final answer generation, combining structured access to multi-session histories with faithful retention of the original conversation. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S show strong retrieval and memory-grounded question answering; on LoCoMo, EdgeMem achieves the highest strict-judge score among seven reproduced systems under a shared prompt (61.01 versus 58.70), while construction and retrieval require no generative-LLM calls. Overall, EdgeMem shows that preserving and organizing source evidence provides an effective and efficient foundation for agent memory without generative memory management.

</details>


### [153] [Deep belief networks are exact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05572)
*Gleb Smirnov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proves that any strictly positive probability distribution on binary vectors {-1,1}^n can be exactly represented by a sigmoid belief network with finite parameters, resolving an open question by Sutskever and Hinton.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sutskever and Hinton had proposed a probability-sharing approximation for representing probability distributions using sigmoid belief networks, but whether exact representation was possible remained an open question. This paper answers that question in the affirmative.

Method: The proof employs Brouwer's fixed-point theorem to upgrade the probability-sharing approximation to exact representation, demonstrating that finite-parameter sigmoid belief networks can capture any strictly positive probability distribution on the hypercube.

Result: Every strictly positive probability distribution on {-1,1}^n can be represented exactly by a sigmoid belief network with finite parameters.

Conclusion: The representational capacity of sigmoid belief networks is complete for strictly positive distributions on binary domains, settling the theoretical question posed by Sutskever and Hinton.

Abstract: We prove that every strictly positive probability distribution on \(\{-1,1\}^n\) is represented exactly by a sigmoid belief network with finite parameters. This answers a question of Sutskever and Hinton. The proof upgrades their probability-sharing approximation to exact representation using Brouwer's fixed-point theorem.

</details>


### [154] [EnvCraft: Synthesizing Executable Environments in Agentic RL for Claw-like Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05576)
*Yirong Zeng,Shen You,Jinhang Feng,Yufei Liu,Xiao Ding,Yutai Hou,Hao Cong,Yuxian Wang,Wu Ning,Wang Xu,Bibo Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EnvCraft is an automated framework that synthesizes executable environments and training data to address the scarcity of interactive training environments for Agentic RL. It creates 139 environments with ~20K tasks, achieving up to +11.9% improvements on Claw-style benchmarks and +8.0% on general tool-use benchmarks while reducing inference costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paradigm of LLMs has shifted to autonomous claw-like agents, but Agentic RL scaling is bottlenecked by severe scarcity of interactive training environments. Existing synthetic environments are limited to tool-calling endpoints and cannot meet end-to-end real-world demands of claw-like agents.

Method: EnvCraft employs two key components: (1) an environment synthesis engine that builds sandbox-isolated workspaces, and (2) a topology-aware data generation engine that produces coherent task trajectories. The framework synthesizes 139 interactive environments comprising approximately 20K complex tasks for Agentic RL training.

Result: Experiments on Qwen3/3.5 models (8B-32B) show gains of up to +11.9% on Claw-style benchmarks and +8.0% on general tool-use benchmarks, with concurrent reductions in inference token cost.

Conclusion: Synthesized executable environments provide robust and generalizable learning signals for training LLM agents, effectively addressing the environment scarcity problem in Agentic RL.

Abstract: The paradigm of LLMs has rapidly shifted from passive language interfaces to autonomous Claw-like agents that execute long-horizon tasks across stateful workspaces. While Agentic Reinforcement Learning (Agentic RL) provides a promising path to optimize these agents, its scaling is heavily bottlenecked by the severe scarcity of interactive training environments. Existing synthetic environments are strictly limited to tool-calling endpoints, rendering them insufficient for accommodating the end-to-end real-world demands of claw-like agents. To bridge this gap, we introduce EnvCraft, an automated framework for synthesizing executable environments and scalable training data. Specifically, EnvCraft employs an environment synthesis engine to build sandbox-isolated workspaces, alongside a topology-aware data generation engine to produce coherent task trajectories. Overall, we synthesize 139 interactive environments comprising approximately 20K complex tasks for Agentic RL training. Experiments on Qwen3/3.5 models (8B-32B) show that our method yields gains of up to +11.9% on Claw-style benchmarks and +8.0% on general tool-use benchmarks, with concurrent reductions in inference token cost. The results confirm that synthesized executable environments provide robust and generalizable learning signals for training.

</details>


### [155] [Planning and Scheduling Business Processes under Control-Flow Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05578)
*Michel Kunkler,Stefanie Rinderle-Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses scheduling activities in business processes with uncertain execution paths. The authors formulate the problem as a chance-constrained optimization problem and propose two approaches: a decomposed two-stage method (planning then scheduling) and an integrated approach. Evaluation on three datasets shows the integrated approach achieves better makespans but doesn't scale, while the decomposed approach scales to large settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Business process scheduling can improve efficiency (reduce makespan), but is challenging because the exact sequence of activities is often uncertain due to data-driven decisions during execution. Probabilistic information about decisions can be estimated from historical logs, and planning with particular execution paths affects both feasibility (probability of successful completion) and the expected number of superfluous activities (planned but never executed).

Method: The authors frame the problem as a chance-constrained optimization problem and present two formulations: (1) A decomposed approach with two stages - a planning stage that minimizes the expected number of superfluous activities subject to a feasibility constraint, and a scheduling stage that minimizes the makespan over the planned activities; (2) An integrated approach that combines planning and scheduling into a single formulation.

Result: Evaluation on two real-world and one synthetic dataset shows that the integrated approach yields superior makespans but is intractable at scale, while the decomposed approach scales to large settings.

Conclusion: The paper provides a comprehensive solution for scheduling uncertain business processes, offering both an optimal but non-scalable integrated approach and a scalable decomposed approach. The trade-off between makespan optimization and computational tractability is clearly demonstrated through empirical evaluation.

Abstract: Scheduling activities in business processes can improve efficiency (e.g., reduce makespan), but is challenging because the exact sequence of activities required to complete a case is often uncertain due to decisions based on data that emerges during execution. Nevertheless, probabilistic information regarding such decisions can often be estimated or derived from historical execution logs, and can help anticipate which execution paths are likely to lead to successful completion. Planning with particular execution paths affects feasibility, i.e., the probability of successful completion, and the expected number of superfluous activities that are planned but never executed. We frame the problem as a chance-constrained optimization problem and present two formulations: A decomposed approach with two stages, a planning stage that minimizes the expected number of superfluous activities subject to a feasibility constraint, and a scheduling stage that minimizes the makespan over the planned activities; and an integrated approach that combines planning and scheduling into a single formulation. Evaluation on two real-world and one synthetic dataset shows that the integrated approach yields superior makespans but is intractable at scale, while the decomposed approach scales to large settings.

</details>


### [156] [Agents Trust Tools Too Much: Measuring Reliance on Unreliable Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05587)
*Hoyeol Yang,Woojung Song,Taewon Kim,Jonghyun Song,Seoyeon Park,Yohan Jo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how 14 LLMs respond to unreliable tool returns across three tools (web search, LLM sub-agent delegation, code execution). The study reveals that agents exhibit high overtrust, adopting corrupted tool outputs in over one-third of cases (up to 68% for web search). A particularly concerning finding is that agents often recognize conflicts internally but still present only the corrupted answer. Interventions at prompting, metadata, and post-training levels fail to consistently mitigate this overtrust problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of tool-using agents assume tools return reliable information, but real-world tools can return plausible yet incorrect outputs. This discrepancy between evaluation assumptions and real-world reliability motivates the investigation of how agents handle unreliable tool returns.

Method: The researchers evaluated 14 LLMs using three tools (web search, LLM sub-agent delegation, and code execution). For each tool, they corrupted its returns and measured whether agents adopted the corrupted content in their final answers. They analyzed reasoning traces to understand agent behavior. Three intervention levels were tested: user prompting, metadata from tool provider, and post-training by agent builder.

Result: Agents exhibit high overtrust across all three tools: mean adoption rate exceeds one-third for every tool and reaches 68.0% for web search. Analysis reveals a critical failure mode: agents often recognize conflicts and even recover correct answers internally, yet present only the corrupted answer without warning users. None of the three intervention levels consistently mitigates overtrust across all tools.

Conclusion: Overtrust in unreliable tools is a serious and persistent failure mode for tool-using agents. The findings motivate the need for evaluations and interventions that enable agents to validate tool outputs and transparently communicate unresolved conflicts to users.

Abstract: Existing evaluations of tool-using agents primarily measure whether an agent can successfully complete diverse tasks with tools. These evaluations generally assume that tools return reliable information. However, tool returns in real-world systems can be plausible yet incorrect. We investigate how agents respond to unreliable tool returns by evaluating fourteen LLMs using three tools-web search, LLM sub-agent delegation, and code execution. For each tool, we corrupt its returns and measure whether agents adopt the corrupted content in their final answers. Agents exhibit high levels of overtrust across all three settings: the mean adoption rate exceeds one third for every tool and reaches 68.0% for web search. Analysis of reasoning traces reveals a particularly concerning failure mode: agents often recognize conflicts and even recover the correct answer internally, yet present only the corrupted answer without warning the user. To mitigate agents' overtrust in tool returns, we intervene at three levels: prompting by the user, metadata from the tool provider, and post-training by the agent builder. Although some interventions help for particular models or tools, none consistently mitigates overtrust across tools. These findings identify overtrust in unreliable tools as a serious and persistent failure mode, motivating evaluations and interventions that enable agents to validate tool outputs and transparently communicate unresolved conflicts.

</details>


### [157] [The convergent laboratory: when AI reasoning, autonomous experiments, high performance and quantum computing reshape chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05643)
*Eliu Huerta,Xiaoyun Wang,Geetika Gupta,Edward H. Sargent,Cameron J. Owen,Victor Fung,Abhishek Mitra,Austin Cheng,Emma Bouchard,Shams Mehdi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This Comment paper discusses the transformative convergence of AI, autonomous agents, self-driving laboratories, high-performance computing, and quantum computing in materials science discovery, based on perspectives from TPC26 conference leaders from academia, national laboratories, and industry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to capture the firsthand experiences of researchers at the frontiers of materials science and provide a reference at what they believe is a tipping point of transformative advances in the chemical sciences.

Method: This is a conference-based commentary synthesizing perspectives from academic, national laboratory, and industry leaders exploring the intersection of multiple advanced technologies in materials science.

Result: The paper presents a collective perspective on how AI-driven reasoning, autonomous agentic frameworks, self-driving laboratories, and fault-tolerant quantum processors are maturing simultaneously to potentially revolutionize materials science discovery.

Conclusion: The authors position the current moment as a tipping point where these converging technologies will drive transformative advances and productive disruption in the chemical sciences.

Abstract: This Comment emerges from TPC26 (https://tpc26.org), a conference convening leaders from academia, national laboratories, and industry who are reshaping materials science discovery. The meeting explored how AI, autonomous agents, self-driving labs, higher performance and quantum computing converge to amplify their individual impact on materials science discovery. The perspectives here reflect the firsthand experiences of researchers at these frontiers and capture the essence of this global endeavor. As AI-driven reasoning, autonomous agentic frameworks, self-driving laboratories, and fault-tolerant quantum processors mature simultaneously, we offer this Comment as a reference at what we believe is a tipping point of transformative advances and productive disruption in the chemical sciences.

</details>


### [158] [What LLM Trading Agents Actually Do in Production: A Six-Month, Population-Scale Record from Two Fleets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05663)
*T. J. Barton,Chris Constantakis,Patti Hauseman,Annie Mous,Alaska Hoffman,Brian Bergeron,Hunter Goodreau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study of autonomous LLM-based trading agents operating in production environments (DeFi markets). The authors analyze ~7.5M model invocations across two systems over 6 months, finding that operating infrastructure (not strategy design) predominantly determines agent behavior, that position sizing ignores volatility, that agents fail to capture their reached upside, and that neither fleet shows directional trading edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to understand how autonomous LLM trading agents behave in real production environments at scale, moving beyond backtesting to empirical analysis of actual onchain trading behavior and performance.

Method: Empirical analysis of production data from two DeFi trading systems over ~6 months, covering 7.5M model invocations and ~300K onchain actions. Statistical tests include day-clustered inference, permutation nulls, and fee restatement. Also includes paired-replay league of frontier models on 416 captured production scenarios.

Result: Four key findings: (1) Operating layer determines behavior—risk slider explains leverage (+0.425 per level), agent fixed effects absorb 60% variance, leaderboard boundary causally routes selection (1.75x RDD at top-3); (2) Sizing is volatility-blind—median leverage 5.0x in all volatility sextiles, one posture-slider cell holds 62% of liquidations; (3) Agents capture almost no upside—43.2% had +300 bps favorable excursion, yet 49.3% closed negative; mechanical bracket recovers +39.0 bps; (4) No directional edge—DXAP not profitable, trails benchmark (41% vs 50% win rate), frontier models show indistinguishable decision quality but differ in choice stability.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLM trading agents in production show no directional edge, fail to capture upside despite favorable price movements, and that infrastructure/operating parameters (not strategy design) dominate behavioral outcomes. The paper provides a 17-rule methodology canon based on the authors' own retractions.

Abstract: We present a continuous, population-scale measurement record of autonomous language-model trading agents operating in production across two systems with one design lineage: DX Terminal Pro (3,505 user-funded vaults trading real ETH in Base memecoin markets for 21 days, February to March 2026) and the DXAP live alpha fleet (500 to 599 user-created agents all-history, 91 to 117 concurrently active, trading Hyperliquid perpetuals, June to August 2026). The record spans roughly six months, 7.5M single-model invocations with about 300K onchain actions, and a further 231,638 multi-tool turns producing 14,596 fills. Four findings carry the paper. First, the operating layer determines behavior more than anything written in strategy text: a risk slider explains leverage (+0.425 per level), agent fixed effects absorb 60% of variance, and a leaderboard render boundary causally routes selection (regression discontinuity 1.75x at the top-3 cut). Second, sizing is volatility-blind: median leverage is 5.0x in every volatility sextile, and one posture-slider cell (11% of the book) holds 62% of liquidations. Third, agents capture almost none of the upside they reach: 43.2% of positions saw at least +300 bps of favorable excursion within 24h, yet 49.3% of those closed with a negative trade return; a mechanical bracket recovers +39.0 bps per position. Fourth, neither fleet shows a directional edge. The DXAP fleet is not profitable and trails a matched Hyperliquid retail benchmark (41% vs. 50% roundtrip win rate). A paired-replay league of frontier models on 416 captured production scenarios finds decision quality statistically indistinguishable at this horizon, while choice stability differs sharply across model families. Every headline survives day-clustered inference, permutation nulls, and a common-fee restatement; the paper closes with a 17-rule methodology canon bought with our own retractions.

</details>


### [159] [CUSP: Decomposable Collective Uncertainty for Multi-Agent Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05708)
*Chung-En Johnny Yu,David Garcia,Brian Jalaian,Nathaniel D. Bastian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CUSP is a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for aggregating heterogeneous vision-language models (VLMs). It maps multiple VLM responses to a shared semantic response space, pools them into a pooled semantic opinion, and reports two complementary system-level signals: collective uncertainty (dispersion of pooled opinion) and Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD, conflict among model-level opinions). It works without token logits or calibration labels, applicable to both open-weight and commercial VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregating heterogeneous VLMs improves multimodal reasoning, but neither individual model confidence nor aggregated answer confidence measures reliability at the system level. There was a need for a framework to quantify system-level uncertainty in VLM ensembles.

Method: CUSP maps multiple VLM responses to a shared semantic response space, pools them into a pooled semantic opinion, and reports two complementary system-level signals: (1) collective uncertainty - the dispersion of the pooled opinion, and (2) Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD) - the conflict among model-level opinions. The unnormalized collective entropy decomposes exactly into the mean of models' individual semantic entropies plus the JSD, separating total dispersion from model conflict.

Result: - Collective uncertainty is strongest in small-model regime: 0.764 AUROC for prediction-error detection, 0.889 AUARC for abstention (outperforms baselines by 4.7 to 15.8 points)
- JSD is strongest in commercial regime: 0.819 AUROC, 0.910 AUARC
- JSD ranks hard-answer model conflict with AUROC up to 0.982
- Pooled prediction improves accuracy over average single model by 5.6 to 13.0 points
- In multi-step multi-agent systems: subagent collective uncertainty ranks system failures at 0.619 AUROC, best abstention ordering at 0.699 AUARC

Conclusion: CUSP provides effective system-level uncertainty quantification for VLM ensembles without requiring token logits or calibration labels. It successfully separates total dispersion from model conflict, with collective uncertainty performing best for small models and JSD best for commercial models. The framework also effectively ranks system failures in multi-agent scenarios.

Abstract: Aggregating heterogeneous vision-language models (VLMs) can improve multimodal reasoning, but neither an individual model's confidence nor that of the aggregated answer measures reliability at the system level. We present CUSP (Collective Uncertainty through Semantic Opinion Pooling), a training-free uncertainty quantification framework that maps multiple VLM responses to a shared semantic response space, pools them into a pooled semantic opinion, and reports two complementary system-level signals: collective uncertainty, the dispersion of the pooled opinion, and Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD), the conflict among the model-level opinions. Within this pooled semantic opinion, the unnormalized collective entropy decomposes exactly into the mean of the models' individual semantic entropies and the JSD, separating total dispersion from model conflict. Requiring neither token logits nor calibration labels, CUSP applies to open-weight and commercial VLMs alike. In static multi-VLM ensembles, collective uncertainty is the strongest signal in the small-model regime (0.764 AUROC for prediction-error detection, 0.889 AUARC for abstention), outperforming uncertainty baselines majority voting and naive selection by 4.7 to 15.8 points and widening its margin as the ensemble grows; JSD is strongest in the evaluated commercial regime (0.819 AUROC, 0.910 AUARC) and ranks hard-answer model conflict with AUROC up to 0.982. The pooled prediction also improves accuracy over the average single model by 5.6 to 13.0 points. Over the full trajectory of a multi-step, multi-agent system, subagent collective uncertainty ranks system failures above chance (0.619 AUROC) and gives the best abstention ordering among the evaluated signals (0.699 AUARC).

</details>


### [160] [Recovering Temporal and Geographic Signals from Language Model Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05721)
*Esteban Feuerstein,Victoria Klimkowski,Juan Manuel Ortiz de Zarate,Federico Hernán Suaiter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a simple projection-based method to analyze whether language model embeddings encode temporal and geographic information, using only output embeddings without access to model internals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding whether language-model embeddings encode structured real-world information (temporal and geographic signals) is important for representation analysis and information retrieval applications.

Method: A projection-based method that: (1) uses a small set of seed examples to define an axis in embedding space, (2) ranks texts/entities by their projection onto that axis, (3) operates directly on output embeddings, (4) is fully black-box and model-agnostic requiring only embeddings without access to weights, internal activations, or training.

Result: Embedding projections successfully recover meaningful chronological and spatial structure, providing evidence that output embeddings encode signals relevant to time and space.

Conclusion: Output embeddings encode temporal and spatial dimensions. The method provides a practical tool for interpretability analysis and enables downstream tasks such as temporal ordering, geographic ranking, and tagging.

Abstract: Understanding whether language-model embeddings encode structured real-world information is important for both representation analysis and information retrieval. We study this question for temporal and geographic signals using a simple projection-based method that operates directly on output embeddings. Given a small set of seed examples, the method defines an axis in embedding space and ranks texts or entities by their projection onto that axis. Our approach is fully black-box and model-agnostic: it requires only embeddings, without access to model weights, internal activations, auxiliary probes, or additional training. This makes it applicable to modern embedding models available only through APIs and provides a lightweight way to analyze whether temporal and spatial dimensions are present in their representation spaces. We apply the method to temporal and geographic datasets and find that embedding projections recover meaningful chronological and spatial structure. These results provide evidence that output embeddings encode signals relevant to time and space, while also offering a practical tool for interpretability and for downstream temporal and geographic information retrieval tasks, such as temporal ordering, geographic ranking, and tagging.

</details>


### [161] [Beyond Prompts: Measuring and Optimizing LLM Tool-Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05736)
*Cen,Zhao,Haibo Ruan,Wenjie Chen,Pei-fen Tu,Usman Abbasi,Joel Hesch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes improving LLM tool agents without retraining by modifying the runtime harness (prompts, tool interfaces, middleware) rather than the model itself. The authors introduce a protocol for evaluating harness selection with multiple metrics including RelLift95(B), and present PRISM, an optimizer that clusters failures and routes repairs to different edit surfaces. PRISM achieves 10.1-14.9 percentage point mean held-out lifts across three benchmarks, with ablation showing failure-surface routing and edit-pattern constraint as key contributors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to improving LLM tool agents typically require retraining, which is expensive. This work explores whether modifying the runtime harness around a fixed model can achieve meaningful improvements. The key insight is that the "harness" (prompts, tool interfaces, middleware, state handling, recovery logic) represents an underutilized optimization surface that doesn't require model retraining.

Method: The authors define a resource-bounded harness selection problem for fixed-model multi-turn tool agents. The search surface is scoped to prompts and tool-boundary middleware with edits as guarded intercepts at the tool boundary. They propose an optimizer-agnostic evaluation protocol reporting: (1) mean held-out lift, (2) worst-condition lift, (3) repeatability, (4) logged cost diagnostics, and (5) RelLift95(B), a conservative estimate of held-out gain under budget B. PRISM is instantiated as a specific optimizer that clusters failures and routes repairs to prompt, tool-boundary middleware, or joint edit surfaces within a Pareto search framework.

Result: On BFCL multi-round, tau2-Retail, and tau2-Telecom benchmarks, PRISM achieves mean held-out lifts of 14.2, 14.9, and 10.1 percentage points respectively. Positive empirical RelLift95 is achieved on all three benchmarks. Component ablation attributes the performance margin primarily to failure-surface routing and the edit-pattern constraint. The study also reveals that some search procedures can find large gains but choose brittle updates, highlighting the importance of reporting reliability alongside average held-out lift.

Conclusion: The runtime harness represents a viable and effective optimization surface for improving LLM tool agents without retraining. PRISM's success demonstrates the value of failure-surface routing (routing different failure types to appropriate fix surfaces) and edit-pattern constraints (guarded intercepts at tool boundaries). The paper emphasizes that evaluation protocols should report both average performance and reliability metrics, as some optimization procedures may find large but brittle improvements. The proposed RelLift95(B) metric provides a conservative estimate of practical gains under budget constraints.

Abstract: LLM tool agents can be improved without retraining by modifying the runtime harness around a fixed model: prompts, tool interfaces, middleware, state handling, and recovery logic. We study this setting as resource-bounded harness selection for fixed-model multi-turn tool agents, with the search surface scoped to prompts and tool-boundary middleware: edits are guarded intercepts at the tool boundary, not arbitrary rewriting of agent execution logic. Our optimizer-agnostic protocol reports mean held-out lift, worst-condition lift, repeatability, logged cost diagnostics, and RelLift95(B), a conservative estimate of the held-out gain of the harness selected under budget B. We instantiate the protocol with prompt-only and prompt-plus-middleware optimizers, including PRISM, which clusters failures and routes repairs to prompt, tool-boundary middleware, or joint edit surfaces within a Pareto search. On BFCL multi-round, tau2-Retail, and tau2-Telecom, PRISM obtains mean held-out lifts of 14.2, 14.9, and 10.1 percentage points and positive empirical RelLift95 on all three benchmarks, and a component ablation attributes the margin chiefly to failure-surface routing and the edit-pattern constraint. Across optimizers, the results show that some search procedures can occasionally find large gains but still choose brittle updates, so the reliability of the chosen harness should be reported alongside average held-out lift.

</details>


### [162] [The Normalization of Deviance in AI Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05749)
*Emilio Barkett,Alexander Kimpton,Daniel Graham,Yusuf Kundgol*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Work on the risks of artificial intelligence has focused predominantly on capability risk: the danger that systems become too powerful, too autonomous, or too misaligned with human values. Far less attention has been paid to the organizational level---to whether the institutions building these systems are themselves predisposed to drift toward failure. This paper argues that they are. Regardless of how capable AI systems become, the organizations building them face the same structural dynamics that preceded past major technological disasters. Drawing on case studies of the Space Shuttle Challenger, the Three Mile Island accident, and the Boeing 737 MAX crashes, this paper identifies the common structural mechanisms preceding each failure and maps them onto contemporary AI development. The findings suggest that existing safety infrastructure may provide less protection than it appears, as organizations can complete safety processes in full compliance and still produce catastrophic outcomes. The pre-disaster period of AI development is still underway; the purpose of this paper is to make these dynamics legible while they can still be interrupted.

</details>


### [163] [From Monolithic Blending to Agentic Orchestration: Dynamic Response for Conversational Assistants at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05758)
*Cen,Zhao,Peng Wang,Chuan Shi,Yufeng Zhang,Ying Lyu,Wanmeng Ren,Robert Xue,Claire Na Cheng,Yashar Mehdad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reports a production migration of a customer-support assistant at a large accommodation marketplace (millions of conversations/month, 11 languages). The system replaced a single large blended model (Qwen3-235B-A22B) with a bounded ReAct orchestrator over typed tools plus a smaller generator. Key improvements include: precision increased from 8.3% to 89.1% for entity selection, structured-action hallucination eliminated (2.14% to 0.0%), escalation rates significantly reduced (hard: 5.60%→3.08%, soft: 9.56%→2.49%), latency reduced by 42% (3.87s→2.24s), and costs reduced by over 10x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation was to improve a production customer-support assistant that faced challenges with: 1) low precision in entity selection (8.3%), 2) structured-action hallucination (2.14%), and 3) high escalation rates to human agents. Additionally, the system needed to meet strict latency requirements (10-second P90) while managing high operational costs from serving a large 235B parameter model.

Method: The migration replaced a single Qwen3-235B-A22B blended responder with a bounded ReAct orchestrator over typed tools plus a smaller generator that writes from a backend-validated context contract. The architecture uses: 1) typed entity selection for reservation selection, 2) typed action IDs with membership checks to prevent hallucination, and 3) self-hosting for cost reduction. Effects were isolated by measuring on identical replayed turns to attribute changes to specific architectural changes.
