Result: Key results: (1) Typed entity selection moved precision from 8.3% to 89.1% (recall 75.2%→67.3%); (2) Typed action IDs with membership check eliminated structured-action hallucination (2.14%→0.0%); (3) A/B test showed hard-escalation reduced from 5.60% to 3.08% and soft-escalation from 9.56% to 2.49%, while self-solve improved by +5.1 points (95% CI [-2, +12]); (4) Orchestrator P90 latency reduced from 3.87s to 2.24s; (5) GPU footprint reduced by ~1/3; (6) Estimated annual model-serving cost reduced by more than 10x.

Conclusion: The migration demonstrates that separating retrieval, action selection, escalation, and wording into a bounded ReAct orchestrator with typed tools significantly improves precision, eliminates hallucination, reduces escalation rates, and cuts costs by over an order of magnitude while maintaining latency requirements. The architecture change—rather than prompt/alignment tweaks—is the primary driver of improvements, as validated by controlled replay experiments.

Abstract: Conversational assistants can blend retrieval, action selection, escalation, and wording in a single model path, or separate those roles. We report a production migration of a customer-support assistant at a large accommodation marketplace (millions of conversations per month, 11 languages, 10-second P90). Dynamic Response (DR) replaces a single Qwen3-235B-A22B blended responder with a bounded ReAct orchestrator over typed tools plus a smaller generator that writes from a backend-validated context contract. Because the migration also changed prompts, alignment, and serving, we attribute each effect to its cause and claim as architecture effects only those measured on identical replayed turns: typed entity selection moves the reservation selector to a precision-first operating point (precision 8.3% to 89.1%, recall 75.2% to 67.3%), and typed action IDs with a membership check remove observed structured-action hallucination (2.14% to 0.0%). A low-ramp A/B test reproduces the replay escalation reductions: hard-escalation responses fall from 5.60% to 3.08% and soft-escalation responses from 9.56% to 2.49%, while production handoff volume holds roughly steady; self-solve is directional (+5.1 points, 95% CI [-2, +12]). Serving optimizations cut orchestrator P90 latency from 3.87s to 2.24s on a GPU footprint reduced by roughly one-third, and self-hosting reduces estimated annual model-serving cost by more than an order of magnitude.

</details>


### [164] [Inference-Time Graph Engineering for Multi-Agent LLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05774)
*Katherine Tieu,Dongqi Fu,Yinglong Xia,Hong Li,Hong Yan,Jingrui He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReActNet is a training-free multi-agent LLM framework that synthesizes task-conditioned temporal workflow graphs to coordinate specialized agents. It compiles a query and role-specialized agents into a sequence of directed communication graphs, where each graph snapshot represents a reasoning stage and edges carry natural-language instructions. Execution occurs through structured message passing without requiring reinforcement learning or gradient-based topology optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-agent LLM systems rely on graph-structured communication but typically use static topologies. The authors argue that effective orchestration depends not just on which agents communicate, but also on engineering executable workflow graphs that encode when, why, and how information should flow during reasoning. Existing approaches require expensive reinforcement learning or gradient-based topology optimization.

Method: ReActNet compiles queries and role-specialized agents into a sequence of directed communication graphs. Each graph snapshot corresponds to a reasoning stage, and each edge carries a natural-language instruction specifying the message that a source agent should provide to a target agent. Execution involves structured message passing where agents update their reasoning states by integrating previous states with messages from controller-assigned neighbors, and a final aggregator synthesizes the results into an answer.

Result: ReActNet consistently improves over fixed-topology and learned-topology baselines across knowledge reasoning, mathematical problem solving, code generation, and GAIA-style assistant tasks while maintaining competitive inference cost.

Conclusion: Effective multi-agent orchestration depends on engineering executable workflow graphs that encode when, why, and how information should flow during reasoning, not just on which agents communicate. The separation of graph compilation from execution makes multi-agent coordination explicit, inspectable, and task-conditioned without requiring reinforcement learning or gradient-based topology optimization.

Abstract: Recent multi-agent LLM systems increasingly rely on graph-structured communication to coordinate specialized agents. We revisit multi-agent orchestration from a graph-engineering perspective: rather than optimizing a static topology, we synthesize a task-conditioned temporal workflow graph that jointly specifies agent connectivity and edge-level communication semantics. We introduce ReActNet, a training-free framework that compiles a query and a set of role-specialized agents into a sequence of directed communication graphs. Each graph snapshot corresponds to one reasoning stage, and each edge carries a natural-language instruction specifying the message that a source agent should provide to a target agent. The compiled temporal graph is then executed through structured message passing: agents update their reasoning states by integrating their previous states with messages from controller-assigned neighbors, and a final aggregator synthesizes the resulting states into the answer. This design separates graph compilation from graph execution, making multi-agent coordination explicit, inspectable, and task-conditioned without requiring reinforcement learning or gradient-based topology optimization. Across knowledge reasoning, mathematical problem solving, code generation, and GAIA-style assistant tasks, ReActNet consistently improves over fixed-topology and learned-topology baselines while maintaining competitive inference cost. These results suggest that effective multi-agent orchestration depends not only on which agents communicate, but also on engineering executable workflow graphs that encode when, why, and how information should flow during reasoning.

</details>


### [165] [DI-Bench: Systematically Generating In-Domain Data Intelligence Benchmarks for Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05776)
*Jiangyun Zhang,Kristen Surrao,Torpong Nitayanont,Yupei Zhang,Roopali Singh,Zhiyu Chen,Julia Huang,Zhou Tang,Shayan Ali Akbar,Omar Alonso,Erwin Cornejo,Yuan Li,Yi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DI-Bench is a pipeline for generating realistic benchmarks for data intelligence (DI) tasks that require both business knowledge retrieval and analytical computation. It uses an artifact linkage graph to create questions involving structured data and documents, and produces a 731-task benchmark from two public datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public benchmarks rarely evaluate whether enterprise agents can integrate business knowledge with analytical computation. Manually constructing such benchmarks is costly. There is a need for automated methods to create realistic DI benchmarks that test both computation and knowledge retrieval capabilities.

Method: DI-Bench builds an artifact linkage graph over data tables, dimensions, metrics, and documents to form questions involving structured data and associated knowledge. Ground truth answers are derived via query execution, followed by LLM question generation and validation.

Result: The pipeline produces a 731-task benchmark covering three task types: knowledge retrieval, analytical computation, and rule-grounded reasoning. Evaluation of four models reveals only 32% accuracy on computational tasks where retrieved business rules modify the computation.

Conclusion: DI-Bench successfully generates realistic DI benchmarks. The benchmark proves discriminatory and difficult, with current models struggling significantly (32% accuracy) on tasks requiring integration of business rules with computations, highlighting a key gap in current enterprise agent capabilities.

Abstract: Evaluating enterprise agents on domain-specific benchmarks is critical, yet public benchmarks rarely evaluate whether agents can integrate business knowledge with analytical computation, and constructing such benchmarks manually is costly. We present DI-Bench, a pipeline for generating realistic benchmarks for data intelligence (DI), the practice of extracting insights from large volumes of enterprise data. To emulate realistic DI tasks that require both computation and knowledge retrieval, DI-Bench builds an artifact linkage graph over data tables, dimensions, metrics, and documents to form questions involving structured data and associated knowledge. Ground truth answers are derived via query execution, followed by LLM question generation and validation. Applied to two public datasets, the pipeline produces a 731-task benchmark covering knowledge retrieval, analytical computation, and rule-grounded reasoning. To show the discriminatory capability and difficulty of the benchmark, we evaluate four models, revealing a substantial finding: models achieve only 32% accuracy when doing computational tasks where retrieved business rules modify the computation.

</details>


### [166] [Distilling Vision-Language Models for On-Device Fire Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05782)
*Mohammad Kazzazi,Zixuan Liu,Siavash Khajavi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of deploying large vision-language models (VLMs) for fire detection on resource-constrained embedded sensors by developing a teacher-student knowledge distillation framework to compress domain-specialized VLMs into lightweight models suitable for fully on-device deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models can reduce false alarms in fire detection by reasoning about semantic context, but their large size makes deployment on embedded fire sensors impractical. There is a need to compress these models while preserving safety-critical fire detection capabilities.

Method: The authors develop a teacher-student knowledge distillation framework where large VLMs fine-tuned for fire understanding are distilled into lightweight student models. The compressed models are deployed on commercial Detectium fire detection sensors for evaluation.

Result: Compact student models preserve most of their teachers' fire-understanding capability. Qwen2.5-0.5B provides the strongest overall deployment trade-off considering reasoning accuracy, latency, and memory usage. Compression affects both accuracy and model failure modes.

Conclusion: The findings provide guidance for deploying domain-specialized VLMs in resource-constrained, safety-critical settings, demonstrating that knowledge distillation is a viable approach for compressing VLMs for embedded fire detection applications.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) offer a promising alternative to conventional fire detection systems by reasoning about the semantic context of a scene and thus reducing false alarms, yet their large model size makes deployment on embedded fire sensors impractical. In this paper, we study how domain-specialized VLMs can be compressed for fully on-device deployment without losing the safety-critical behavior required for fire detection. We develop a teacher-student knowledge distillation framework in which large VLMs fine-tuned for fire understanding can be distilled into lightweight students. Experiments across multiple VLM families and model scales show that compact students preserve most of their teachers' fire-understanding capability. We further deploy the distilled models on our commercial Detectium fire detection sensor and jointly evaluate reasoning accuracy, latency, and memory usage. The results show that compression and deployment affect not only accuracy but also model failure modes, with Qwen2.5-0.5B providing the strongest overall deployment trade-off. Our findings provide broader guidance for deploying domain-specialized VLMs in resource-constrained, safety-critical settings.

</details>


### [167] [More Than Mimicking Reviewers: Evaluating LLMs for Pre-Submission Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05788)
*Pouya Parsa,Amin Rezaei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM-based system generates pre-submission peer-review feedback by creating a broad pool of atomic concerns and compressing them into a short report. The system achieves 84.9% weighted coverage of historical review issues but requires significant computational resources (3.6× more requests, 5.2× more tokens), revealing a compression gap between broad coverage and concise reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Peer-review feedback typically arrives too late for meaningful author revisions. This paper addresses this timing problem by moving the "stress test" before submission—using an LLM to generate preemptive feedback that helps authors improve their papers before formal review.

Method: Evaluated on 3,398 ICLR 2026 manuscripts with accessible pre-review versions. Tested independent sampling, deduplication + refill strategies, and compared oracle selectors (Top-32) against paper-only selectors. Measured coverage of historical review issues.

Result: Independent sampling covers 44.9% of historical issues. Deduplication and refill achieves 78.7% strict and 84.9% seriousness-weighted coverage at 3.6× more requests and 5.2× more tokens. A hidden Top-32 Oracle maintains 79.3% weighted coverage from a 256-candidate pool, while paper-only selectors only achieve 40-44% coverage.

Conclusion: LLM review provides broad coverage when using a large candidate pool but compresses poorly into concise reports. The main sources of the compression gap are representative selection and matcher sensitivity. Paper-only selectors significantly underperform oracle methods.

Abstract: Peer-review feedback often arrives too late for authors to make meaningful revisions. We study an author-facing LLM system that moves part of this stress test before submission: it generates a broad pool of atomic concerns and compresses them into a short report. We evaluate agreement with historical reviews and, separately, the possible validity of concerns they omit.
  From 10,000 ICLR 2026 submissions, we use 3,398 manuscripts with accessible versions that predate review. On a ten-paper diagnostic, independent sampling covers 44.9% of historical issues; deduplication and refill reaches 78.7% strict and 84.9% seriousness-weighted coverage, at 3.6$\times$ more requests and 5.2$\times$ more tokens. A hidden Top-32 Oracle preserves the full 79.3% weighted coverage of a 256-candidate pool, but paper-only selectors retain only 40--44%. LLM review therefore provides broad coverage with a large candidate pool but compresses poorly; ablations identify representative selection and matcher sensitivity as the main sources of this gap.

</details>


### [168] [Spillover-Aware Multi-Value Steering for Pluralistic LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05800)
*Weici Pan,Xander Barron,Jiawei Zhou,Zhenhua Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of pluralistic alignment in LLGs, where different stakeholders require different value emphases. The authors identify that naive activation steering causes "spillover" effects between value dimensions, and propose a zero-cost correction method that decouples steering directions using geometric analysis of their Gram matrix. Their pipeline automatically discovers value dimensions and applies corrected steering without fine-tuning, reward models, or manual prompt engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current activation steering methods handle one concept at a time, but real-world pluralistic alignment requires steering multiple value dimensions simultaneously. Existing methods produce substantial "spillover" where effects intended for one value leak into others, creating a fundamental limitation for practical deployment.

Method: The authors trace spillover to geometric entanglement of steering directions, captured by their Gram matrix. They derive a zero-cost correction from an activation-norm-penalized objective that decouples each direction's contribution exactly. The end-to-end pipeline requires no fine-tuning, reward models, or manual prompt engineering - it automatically discovers value dimensions from domain questions, extracts directions, diagnoses entanglement, and applies corrected steering.

Result: On climate discourse, the correction improves the net steering effect from +5.9% to +14.0%, validated over 100,000 pairwise judgments.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully decouples multiple steering directions, eliminating spillover effects in pluralistic alignment scenarios. This enables effective multi-dimensional value steering in LLMs without requiring fine-tuning or additional training, with significant practical improvements demonstrated in real-world applications.

Abstract: Activation steering controls LLM behavior at inference time by adding learned directions to hidden states, but existing methods handle one concept at a time. Pluralistic alignment, where different stakeholders need different value emphases, requires steering multiple dimensions simultaneously. We show that naive steering produces substantial spillover: the effect intended for one value leaks into others. This parallels the treatment-versus-spillover decomposition in causal inference. We trace spillover to geometric entanglement of steering directions, captured by their Gram matrix, and derive a zero-cost correction from an activation-norm-penalized objective that decouples each direction's contribution exactly. Our end-to-end pipeline requires no fine-tuning, no reward model, and no manual prompt engineering: given only domain questions, it automatically discovers value dimensions, extracts directions, diagnoses entanglement, and applies corrected steering. On climate discourse, the correction improves the net steering effect from +5.9% to +14.0%, validated over 100,000 pairwise judgments.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [169] [MVWeaver: A Hierarchical Music Video Generation Agent with a Learned Song-to-Visual Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06478)
*Sifei Li,Minyan Luo,Xu Li,Guodong Qi,Xincan Wang,Hanwen Wang,Chen Zhang,Pengfei Wan,Oliver Deussen,Weiming Dong*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: MVWeaver is a music video generation agent that integrates hierarchical planning with a learned song-to-visual bridge to translate song understanding into executable shot plans. It addresses limitations of existing systems in long-form coherence and song-grounded visual development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automatic music video generation systems can generate visually plausible shots but struggle with long-form coherence and song-grounded visual development. There is a need for a system that can translate song understanding into coherent, song-grounded visual narratives.

Method: The system comprises three main components: (1) a comprehensive song analysis module, (2) a visual planner that constructs hierarchical plans, and (3) downstream image and video generation models. To equip a general-purpose LLM with MV-specific knowledge, the authors perform LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a large language model using 1,861 real-world song-MV pairs with structured annotations including song-side, MV-side, and teacher-inferred song-to-visual rationale.

Result: The system demonstrates stronger song-grounded visual translation, richer visual development, and greater conceptual and shot-to-shot coherence. Ablations support the benefits of learned bridge conditioning.

Conclusion: MVWeaver successfully integrates hierarchical planning with a learned song-to-visual bridge to generate more coherent and song-grounded music videos. The learned bridge conditioning, trained on real-MV-derived supervision, is crucial for the system's performance.

Abstract: Music videos are an important form of audiovisual expression in contemporary culture. They translate and extend the expressive content of songs through deliberate visual design. Existing automatic music video (MV) generation systems can generate visually plausible shots, yet often struggle with long-form coherence and song-grounded visual development. We present MVWeaver, a music video generation agent that integrates hierarchical planning with a learned song-to-visual bridge that translates song understanding into executable shot plans. The MVWeaver architecture comprises a comprehensive song analysis module, a visual planner that constructs hierarchical plans, and downstream image and video generation models that render the planned content. To equip a general-purpose LLM with MV-specific song-to-visual knowledge, we learn a bridge between song analysis and visual planning from real-MV-derived supervision and curate 1,861 real-world song--MV pairs with structured song-side, MV-side, and teacher-inferred song-to-visual rationale annotations. Using these annotations, we perform LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of a large language model to predict song-to-visual bridges that guide hierarchical visual planning. Our experiments demonstrate stronger song-grounded visual translation, richer visual development, and greater conceptual and shot-to-shot coherence, while ablations support the benefits of learned bridge conditioning.

</details>


### [170] [Vision-Guided Text Prompt Tuning for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06497)
*Xiaoran Kou,Jingyi Wu,Peng Sun,Yang Liu,Hong Chen*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: VG-TPT proposes vision-guided text prompt tuning for multimodal sentiment analysis, using layer-wise adaptive prompts to inject visual affective cues into a frozen BERT encoder via a co-guided router, achieving competitive performance with only 2.4M trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal sentiment analysis requires effective fusion of textual and visual cues. Current approaches face challenges: 1) indiscriminate fusion introduces visual noise and distorts textual semantics, 2) full fine-tuning of large encoders is costly and prone to overfitting on limited benchmark data. The authors seek a parameter-efficient method that calibrates text understanding with visual evidence in an adaptive, controlled manner.

Method: VG-TPT formulates visual-text sentiment modeling as controllable visual calibration of frozen text representations. It uses layer-wise adaptive prompts injected into a frozen BERT encoder (rather than late-stage fusion or full backbone tuning). A co-guided router composes prompts from a trainable prompt bank according to both evolving text states and visual guidance features, enabling sample-specific and layer-specific modulation.

Result: Experiments on CMU-MOSEI and CMU-MOSI datasets show that VG-TPT: 1) consistently improves over text-only baselines, 2) achieves competitive or superior performance compared with several full-modality methods, while updating only 2.4M trainable parameters.

Conclusion: VG-TPT successfully addresses the parameter-efficiency and overfitting challenges in multimodal sentiment analysis. By using visual cues to guide prompt tuning in a frozen text encoder rather than full fine-tuning or late fusion, the method achieves strong performance with minimal trainable parameters, demonstrating the effectiveness of visual calibration for text-centered sentiment understanding.

Abstract: Multimodal sentiment analysis requires effective modeling of both verbal semantics and non-verbal affective cues. A central challenge is to calibrate text-centered sentiment understanding with visual facial evidence in a controlled, adaptive, and parameter-efficient manner. Text usually serves as the semantic anchor, whereas visual cues provide complementary evidence for ambiguous or implicit expressions; however, indiscriminate fusion may introduce visual noise and distort textual semantics. Moreover, fully fine-tuning large visual and textual encoders is costly and prone to overfitting on limited and scenario-dependent MSA benchmarks. To address these issues, we propose Vision-Guided Text Prompt Tuning (VG-TPT), which formulates visual-text sentiment modeling as controllable visual calibration of frozen text representations. VG-TPT injects visual affective cues into a frozen BERT encoder through layer-wise adaptive prompts, rather than relying on late-stage feature fusion or full backbone tuning. A co-guided router composes prompts from a trainable prompt bank according to both the evolving text state and the visual guidance feature, enabling sample-specific and layer-specific modulation. Experiments on CMU-MOSEI and CMU-MOSI show that VG-TPT consistently improves over text-only baselines and achieves competitive or superior performance compared with several full-modality methods, while updating only 2.4M trainable parameters. The code is available at https://github.com/ma-tubu/VG-TPT.

</details>


### [171] [AdoDAS: A Privacy-Preserving Multimodal Challenge for Adolescent Depression, Anxiety, and Stress Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07038)
*Zhaojie Luo,Junkun Wang,Tianhua Qi,Yuxuan Wu,Xin Zhao,Tetsuya Takiguchi,Tomoko Matsui,Kun Qian,Fei Wang,Shuqiong Wu,Zhengjun Yue,Hiroshi Ishiguro,Xinyuan Qian,Haizhou Li*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: The AdoDAS Grand Challenge aims to develop scalable tools for screening adolescent depression, anxiety, and stress (D/A/S) using audio-visual data and ASR-derived text. The challenge provides anonymized data from 6,000 participants across 4 sessions, with two tracks: binary D/A/S screening and ordinal prediction of 21 DASS-21 items. Leading submissions achieved 0.5921 mean F1 and 0.2776 mean Quadratic Weighted Kappa, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adolescent mental health issues require scalable screening tools that complement professional evaluation while preserving privacy. The challenge addresses this need by providing a privacy-preserving dataset with anonymized audio-visual representations and ASR-derived text.

Method: The challenge collected 24,000 segments from 6,000 participants across one scripted-reading and three open-response sessions. Two tracks were evaluated: (1) multi-task binary D/A/S screening, and (2) ordinal prediction of 21 DASS-21 item responses. 95 teams participated in screening and 64 in item prediction. Representative systems used cross-session modelling, temporal multimodal fusion, psychometric structure, and task-aware calibration.

Result: Audio-visual baselines achieved 0.4604 mean F1 and 0.2675 mean Quadratic Weighted Kappa. Leading submissions reached 0.5921 mean F1 and 0.2776 mean QWK, showing significant improvement over baselines.

Conclusion: The challenge demonstrates the feasibility of automated D/A/S screening from adolescent audio-visual data, with leading approaches leveraging multimodal fusion, psychometric modeling, and task-aware calibration. The privacy-preserving dataset enables research without compromising minor participants' data security.

Abstract: Adolescent depression, anxiety, and stress (D/A/S) call for scalable tools that complement, rather than replace, professional evaluation. Under a privacy-preserving policy, the AdoDAS Grand Challenge withholds minors' raw recordings and distributes anonymized audio-visual representations and ASR-derived text. Its 6,000 participants provide 24,000 segments across one scripted-reading and three open-response sessions. Two tracks assess multi-task binary D/A/S screening and ordinal prediction of 21 DASS-21 item responses. From 191 registrations, the final leaderboards included 95 eligible screening teams and 64 item-prediction teams. Audio-visual baselines achieved 0.4604 mean F1 and 0.2675 mean Quadratic Weighted Kappa; leading submissions reached 0.5921 and 0.2776. Representative systems emphasize cross-session modelling, temporal multimodal fusion, psychometric structure, and task-aware calibration.

</details>


### [172] [Can Agents Win the Video Browser Showdown?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07311)
*Bastian Jäckl,Zuzana Vopálková,Daniel A. Keim,Jakub Lokoč*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether modern Vision Language Models (VLMs) and agentic systems can autonomously perform interactive video search tasks from an initial search goal description, without human intervention in the search loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional video search is inherently interactive, requiring users to both hold search intent and operationalize it through iterative query refinement. The researchers aim to explore if modern AI agents can replace this human-driven interactive process by autonomously operating existing video search systems.

Method: The approach combines a VLM-based agent with an existing interactive video search system. The search system provides indexing and efficient querying capabilities, while the VLM agent analyzes top-ranked results and makes decisions about next actions. The key challenge is that VLMs cannot perceive the entire large video dataset upfront, requiring effective integration between the agent and the search system.

Result: The results demonstrate that modern VLM agents can autonomously operate interactive video retrieval systems to solve many search tasks from an initial intent description. The agents achieve performance competitive with strong historical expert-operated systems in several settings.

Conclusion: Modern AI agents have the capability to autonomously handle traditionally interactive video search tasks, potentially reducing or eliminating the need for human involvement in the search loop while maintaining competitive search effectiveness.

Abstract: Searching large video collections is typically an interactive process in which users play two roles. First, they hold the search intent: the underlying goal that determines what content they seek and why. Second, users must operationalize this intent through an iterative search loop. Users translate their intent into queries, browse the retrieved candidates, and refine their queries based on the results. In this paper, we investigate the capabilities of modern Vision Language Models (VLM) and agentic approaches to reach search goals interactively and fully autonomously. Specifically, we study whether a provided initial specification of a search goal might be sufficient to solve traditionally interactive search tasks with an agentic system. Provided that the involved VLMs are not aware of the whole large video dataset in advance, the key challenge lies in the effective combination of an existing interactive video search system and a smart VLM agent controlling the system. While the search system provides indexing and efficient querying, the VLM-based agents analyze top-ranked items and make decisions about next actions. Our results show that modern agents can autonomously operate interactive video retrieval systems to solve many search tasks from an initial intent description, achieving performance competitive with strong historical expert-operated systems in several settings.

</details>


### [173] [Concept-Level Risk and Calibration for Governance in Diffusion Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08517)
*Kun Xu,Yushu Zhang,Tao Wang,Shuren Qi,Barbara Carminati,Elena Ferrari,Yuming Fang*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: CLRC is a concept-level probabilistic audit framework for diffusion models that formalizes governance-relevant concept behaviors as Bernoulli semantic events and enables systematic comparison across models, conditioning channels, and deployment conditions. It reveals that embedding-based access and obfuscated prompts pose higher risks than standard-prompt evaluation suggests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluation methods for diffusion models rely on heuristic audits, adversarial probing, or task-specific erasure benchmarks, providing limited support for systematic comparison across models, conditioning channels, and deployment conditions. As semantic control extends beyond natural-language prompts to learned embeddings and intervention pipelines, safety and governance become increasingly difficult to evaluate in a unified manner.

Method: The authors formalize governance-relevant concept behaviors as Bernoulli semantic events induced by stochastic generation, and define a Concept Risk Operator mapping model-channel configurations to structured risk profiles. They apply sample-level post-hoc calibration and configuration-level risk aggregation, demonstrating that probability error can change thresholded actions near policy boundaries.

Result: Experiments on SD1.5, SD2.1, and SDXL reveal consistent yet non-uniform operational risk patterns across concept families, channels, recorded conditions, and shifted protocols. Embedding-based access and obfuscated prompts expose risks often understated by standard-prompt evaluation. A pooled multi-protocol calibrator improves held-out probability reliability.

Conclusion: The paper introduces CLRC as a unified audit schema for probabilistic and decision-aware governance of multimedia generation systems. The framework reveals consistent but non-uniform operational risks across models and channels, with embedding-based access and obfuscated prompts exposing often understated risks. A pooled multi-protocol calibrator improves probability reliability, though transfer from standard-only calibrator is not claimed.

Abstract: Diffusion models have become a core paradigm for multimedia generation, offering powerful concept-driven controllability for personalization, semantic editing, and selective unlearning. However, as semantic control extends beyond natural-language prompts to learned embeddings and intervention pipelines, the safety and governance of these systems become increasingly difficult to evaluate in a unified manner, especially for safety-sensitive, identity-linked, and other privacy-relevant concepts. Existing studies mainly rely on heuristic audits, adversarial probing, or task-specific erasure benchmarks, and therefore provide limited support for systematic comparison across models, conditioning channels, and deployment conditions. We present a concept-level probabilistic audit and reporting framework for diffusion models. We formalize governance-relevant concept behaviors as Bernoulli semantic events induced by stochastic generation, and define a Concept Risk Operator that maps model-channel configurations to structured risk profiles, enabling comparison across prompting interfaces, learned embedding channels, models, and recorded conditions. We apply sample-level post-hoc calibration and configuration-level risk aggregation, and show that probability error can change thresholded actions near policy boundaries. Experiments on SD1.5, SD2.1, and SDXL reveal consistent yet non-uniform operational risk patterns across concept families, channels, recorded conditions, and shifted protocols. In particular, embedding-based access and obfuscated prompts expose risks often understated by standard-prompt evaluation. A pooled multi-protocol calibrator improves held-out probability reliability, but we do not claim transfer from a standard-only calibrator. CLRC provides a common audit schema for probabilistic and decision-aware governance of multimedia generation systems.

</details>
